## B: bridle track–BYOG

### bridle track
 
 <i>n.</i> a path or track for horses, the preferred NZ 19C. use, and still extant, a track fit or designed only for horse rather than wheeled traffic. [[nf OED2; EDD 1879 Wiltshire, (1890) Scotland); W3]]

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> A bridle-track to the stations of Mason and Taylor. <i>(1859,Journal of Two Expedns West Coast,294</i>)

>  ... a practicable dray-track ... a good bridle-track. <i>(1862,AJHR,E-9/24</i>)

>  We at last got to the Bridle track which we followed to the top of the hill ... <i>(May 14, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,38,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CARTER</b> There was at this time only a `bridletrack.' <i>(1866,Life & Recollections of an Early Colonist,2/106</i>)

>  The road would be longer ... than the present bridle-track over the Maungatapu, but then it would be a dray-road ... <i>(Jun 6, 1868,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> I was soon trudging away along the winding bridle track which wound up the gorge, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,85</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> There is only a bridle-track between Masterton and the east coast. <i>(Oct 1872,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,26,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> From Drury to the Waikato, ... there existed but a short time before only a rough bridle track over the densely wooded Pokeno Ranges ... <i>(1879,Waikato War,8</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> In the meantime, a good bridle-track had been formed along the rocky banks of the Upper Wilberforce, which here is simply a mountain torrent. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,119</i>)

>  It is connected with Coromandel by a bridle-track ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/360</i>)

>  At the fringes of settlement, where the pioneers are hewing their homes out of the wilderness, the history of road-making is as follows: First comes the bridle-track, that gradually grows into the dray-road; and then comes the metal, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,33</i>)

>  <i>[Path]</i> is the word that is most often used by Englishmen for we call a track, but its use in this sense survives here only in <i>bridle path,</i> and that has a rival in <i>bridle track. </i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,24</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> During the early days ..., the only access to Waihi was by bridle track over the range. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,6</i>)

>  Moved by Mr. Shand ... That the bridle track be made practicable from the bridle-path to Christchurch. <i>(Apr 12, 1851,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> There is also a communication [with Lyttelton] by means of a bridle track over the hills, ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),197</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [see also bridle path]

>  <b>HARSANT</b> There were only bridle tracks in lieu of road, but throughout the farm were several "Taranaki" gates to be opened and shut, if one wanted to go further afield. <i>(1979,They Called Me Te Maari,17</i>)

>  In the countryside conditions were even worse, with roads - often little more than bridle tracks - at the mercy of weather and geography. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/77</i>)

>  In the countryside conditions were even worse, with roads - often little more than bridle tracks - at the mercy of weather and geography. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/77</i>)

>  The road has been begun and I am told nearly two miles of it made, and a bridle-track cut the whole distance. <i>(Feb 21, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

>  Many settlers were to-day in practically the same isolated state with a "six-foot bridle track" instead of a good dray road. <i>(Apr 14, 1919,Stratford Evening Post,3</i>)

>  The farm was along a bridle track that became known as Field's Track, later to be known as Norsewood Road... <i>(Aug 2003,NZ Memories,No. 43/22</i>)

>  The road proper had been pushed through to Drury by 1855, and a bridle track cut from there to the Waikato. <i>(Jan 2003,NZ Geographic,59</i>)

>  A mounted trooper in glittering uniform was espied riding down the bridle-track from the saddle of Mount Pisa. <i>(Jun 10, 1924,Otago Witness,68</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a primitive track accommodating a saddle or pack horse.

>  First comes the bridle-track that gradually grows into the dray-road. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,33</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> It [Bermuda grass] has survived changes necessitated by the widening of the pack-trail into a bridle-track and that again into a dray-road. <i>(1921,Tutira,285</i>)

### bridled
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] having lower incisors caught in neck wool while trying to remove lice or keds.

>  <b>NELSON</b> bridled sheep - those caught, often starving to death, with their incisors caught in neck wool while scratching or removing lice or keds. <i>(1999,Eugene Nelson Glossary,2</i>)

### Bridlington cheese cakes
 
 <i>n.</i> (recipe)

>  <b>BRANDON</b> <i>BRIDLINGTON CHEESE CAKES</i> ... Butter ... sugar to a cream ... two beaten eggs ... ground rice ... vanilla; put this moisture [sic] into patties of puff paste, with or without jam, and bake ... <i>(1905,'Ukneadit'. Home for Incurables Bazaar,62</i>)

### briefs of evidence
 
 <i>phr.</i> a police summary of evidence in a criminal case. [Jim writes : "...his solicitors requested from the police the briefs of the evidence of the witness..." In New Zealand the expression "briefs of evidence" is commonly used to describe what are usually called "proofs" in England. Cooke P. (1988) 1 <i>New Zealand Law Reports</i> p. 387]

### brier
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [briar](bla-bri.md#briar))

### brier shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [Not a NZ term; one ref;]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, brier 71 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### bright
 
 <i>adj.</i> [kauri gum digging]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Some of the classifications under which gum was exported give an indication of the appearance of some of the grades: bright, rescraped bush, bright chips, black flour dust, chalk, bright seeds and black sugar are some of the names. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,70,1960 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> as an epithet for gold [mining]

>  The gold [from Waipori] is very fine, but very bright and pure. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and ReSources,20</i>)

>  Gold, gold, fine bright gold,/ Tuapeka, Wangapeka, bright red gold. <i>(1862,Canterbury Rhymes,129,1888 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> One-a-pecker, two-a-pecker, bright fine gold, / Spend it in the summer and you die in the cold. <i>(1957,One-a-pecker, Two-a-pecker,149</i>)

### bright one
 
 <i>exclam.</i> good!, excellent!

>  <i>bright one:</i> good! <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)

### brill
 
 <i>n.</i> a marine flatfish

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] brill 2221, 2235, 2239-40, 2252, 2297 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?=megrim

>  <i>The Brill </i>(Caulopsetta scapha). The fish known by this name is occasionally taken by the trawlers outside Otago Heads. <i>Ibid.</i> 551 <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i>, Forster. Known as the `brill'; ... <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/543</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson Otago)]

>  ... where in two hours 48 dozen of flat-fish, brill, and other good fish were trawled. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/574</i>)

>  <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i> Forster. This species referred to as the `brill' in my former list, is a totally different fish. This specific name belongs to the megrim, or witch, a common form, not sold as a food fish on account of its lean, bony character. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/232</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  PELOTRETIS FLAVILATUS Waite. <i>Lemon Sole</i>. Sold as `brill' in Wellington. Mr. Adams informs me that it is common in Dunedin markets during midsummer, when examples are secured off Otago Heads in about 15 fathoms. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/122</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[?syn. of some above].]

>  <i>Ammotretis guntheri</i> Hutton. This is the species sold as `brill' in the south. AMMOTRETIS GUNTHERI Hutton. <i>Brill</i>. Received in Wellington from Timaru and sold as `lemon sole.' The supply is small, increasing towards the end of summer. ... Distribution: South Island and Cook Strait. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/232</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  The principal fishes caught in the trawl [off Otago] are soles, ... flounders, and brill among the flat fishes; ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/100</i>)

>  <b>61/142</b> 20. <i>Ammotretis guentheri</i> Hutton. Brill. <i>(1929,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: Young]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Colistium guntheri</i> Hutton. BRILL. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,28,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> These two are the Turbot (<i>Colistium nudipinnis</i>) with broadly blotched upper surface, and the Brill (<i>C. guntheri</i>) (Pl. II. fig. 30) which is marked with spots forming lines along the body. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,202</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 202]

>  <i>Colistium guntheri</i>: Brill. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> These much sought-after but relatively rare flatfish [flounder and sole] are generally found on sandy bottoms of shallow inshore areas. There are eight commercial species in New Zealand : the sand flounder, yellowbelly flounder, greenback flounder, black flounder, lemon sole, common sole, brill and turbot. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,73</i>)

>  <i>Colistium guntheri</i> Hutton. Brill. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/407</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Brill <i>Colistium guntheri</i>(Hutton) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,404</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 404]

>  <b>WILSON</b> BRILL Oval shaped flatfish, brownish-grey in colour with the unusual features of having each scale with a black mark on its outer edge. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,74</i>)

>  Brill is a handsome, well-proportioned flatfish with long black lines down its brownish-grey body. <i>(Dec 8, 1982,Evening Post,37</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BRILL Colistium guntheri</i> ... The brill is an oval shaped flounder that averages 25 to 40cm in length ... It can be distinguished from other flounders by having a short rostral hook on the upper jaw ..., very small eyes ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,311,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] brill .. [scientific name] <i>Colistium guntheri</i>.. [other name(s)] colours <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Brill Colistium guntheri</i> Restricted to New Zealand. ... Not particularly common, and restricted to southern coasts. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,144</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[?syn. C. scapha]]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 82.PSEUDORHOMBUS SCAPHUS. Forst. Brill. <i>(1872,Fishes,51</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> BRILL (<i>Pseudorhombus scaphus</i> ). ... [26] Of this fish, Dr. Hector writes: `It has been termed brill on account of its being the only one of our flat fish with the eyes on the left side of the head; but it is small in size, and so full of bones, that it cannot be eaten with comfort.' <i>Ibid.</i> 304 <i>Pseudorhombus scaphus</i> Frost. Brill <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,25</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Brill</i> ... a small and very bony rhomboidal fish of New Zealand, <i>Pseudorhombus scaphus</i> , family <i>Pleuronectidae</i>. The true <i>Brill</i> of Europe is <i>Rhombus levis.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,56</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  21. <i>Waipapapa Point</i> - Forster's brill, herrings, conger-eels, skate <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/204</i>)

### brillo
 
 <i>adj.</i> brilliant, also a popular term [1984 14 F E137Wgton Girls C. 4 BRILLO]

### brim
 
 <i>n.</i> young or small marine foodfish, esp. snapper; variant of bream

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> In Nelson, small snapper were called 'brim' and some fishermen insisted they were a different species. <i>(2004,Hooked,309</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>BREAM OR BRIM</i> - A loose term for Black Bream (Auckland); small snapper at Nelson; and small Groper at Dunedin. <i>Ibid.</i> 45 <i>SNAPPER</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Chrysophrys auratus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Golden Snapper (c) Other common names: Schnapper; Brim (small snapper at Nelson); Tamure. (d) Principal localities where taken: From Nelson northwards to both coasts of the North Island. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,12</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] brim 1455 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The smaller speciments of Snapper are frequently called `Brim'. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,99</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> A sea bream (family Sparidae), not closely related to the true snappers of tropical and subtropical seas. <i>Snapper Chrysophrys auratus </i>... Snapper is often spelt schnapper; strictly, neither is correct. Small snapper are often called bream or brim, and the brightly coloured, medium-sized fish caught during the spring spawning season are often called school snapper. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,96</i>)

>  [species name] brim (small snapper) .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] now coded as snapper <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### brindle-britch
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cow difficult to handle.

>  a difficult cow - a brindle-britch. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### bring back
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to restore pasture to its former potential or production.

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> H.G.-S. had embarked on the three years' plan for bringing back the Opouahi block, but he too must consider how to reduce the cost of running the station without wronging stock or land ... <i>(1959,Guthrie-Smith of Tutira,203</i>)

### Bring Back Buck
 
 <i>phr.</i> [Referring to All Black Buck Shelford after he was dropped from the team. Originally used only in football terms with banners bearing the words appearing at rugby matches, now also in transferred usage e.g. "I wouldn't say "Bring back Buck" yet but.. in relation to suggestions that Ron Brierly should be brought back to replace Roger Douglas as head of BIL.]

>  The obvious answer for New Zealand now to get its administration back on track is to Bring Back Brian [Lochore]. <i>(Aug 1, 2002,Contact (Wgtn),4</i>)

>  [Hdg] Forget Mitchell, it's time to bring back Buck <i>(Oct 6, 2001,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Many people would have picked Frano Botica instead of Grant Fox, for example. The bring-back-Buck brigade made as much noise as we are about Cully. <i>(Jul 11, 2003,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  You can always rely on a cricket crowd to put a new spin on an old situation. "Bring back buck" has had a shelf life much longer than that of another All Black captain, Wayne "Buck" Shelford...Spotted at Sunday's one-day international at Napier, a banner reading "Bring Back Judy". <i>(Jan 13, 2006,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Even today, "Bring Back Buck" signs can be seen at sports grounds around the world. <i>(Jun 12, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  To New Zealanders, who have debated whether the selectors should Bring Back Buck, whether Terry Wright should be fullback or winger,.. the World Cup is everything. <i>(Sep 30, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  So to the main event: "Bring Back" Buck Shelford against Mark "Sharko" Graham. <i>(May 5, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  No, of course Jonah Lomu is not the only halfway decent All Black. But bring back Buck just in case. <i>(Dec 29, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  The single thing that would do most to improve life in New Zealand is jobs, jobs and more jobs. Equally heartfelt but perhaps less practical suggestions were : Bring back Buck, blow up the Beehive, abolish all laws to cut the crime rate, drain Lake Rotorua, send the water to the South Island and paint the lake blue so it can be used as a swimming pool, get rid of the rest of the world, move the country closer to the equator, put cocaine in the water supply and have more dogs like Spot. <i>(Jul 17, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  Although it's not quite up there with Bring Back Buck, the campaign to stop the extinction of the Mowat - slow moving and somewhat anachronistic native fauna of National Radio's week-day afternoons - is gathering momentum. <i>(May 21, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

>  We refer, of course, to Bring Back Buck, that unofficial Kiwi ensign with the slogan painted in black, usually on the backdrop of an almost-white bedsheet taken from someone's flat. All other suggestions for a new flag are meaningless compared with Bring Back Buck. It speaks to us of Maori and Pakeha striving together for an outcome that will make the nation proud. <i>(Jan 24, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  The decision has sparked a Bring Back Buck-style campaign in the Australian community, with petitions and protests demanding Young's reinstatement. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F2</i>)

### bring down
 
 <i>v.</i> to bring in, introduce (legislation) into Parliament

>  It was clear at this stage, that many people had not yet prepared their detailed views on the new proposals and the Minister stated that, if necessary, he would bring down a further Bill later in the year, thus allowing time for further representations to be considered. <i>(1953,Te Ao Hou,5: 61</i>) [NOTES: Spring 1953 number]

>  I would like to compliment the honourable Member for Napier on again bringing down the measure. <i>(1935,NZ Parliamentary Debates,242/237</i>)

>  I would like to congratulate the member.. for bringing this bill down. <i>(1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,299/728</i>)

>  I cannot expect that the Government themselves will be in a hurry to bring down here a Bill, the passing of which must rob them of support of that kind. <i>(1876,NZ Parliamentary Debates,21/459</i>)

### bring forward
 
 <i>v.</i> introduce, submit (a parliamentary bill)

>  The Minister has not given us a very satisfactory reason for bringing this bill forward. <i>(1953,NZ Parliamentary Debates,299/279</i>)

>  I recall a member of this House bringing forward a Bill year by year until he was finally rewarded by its acceptance. <i>(1953,NZ Parliamentary Debates,299/553</i>)

>  She should stand proud in the House for bringing forward a Bill that is aimed at reducing violence on television. <i>(1993,NZ Parliamentary Debates,535/15853</i>)

>  The question was whether the Bill, as now brought forward, would act beneficially or not. <i>(1876,NZ Parliamentary Debates,21/417</i>)

### bring in
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> One objective.. was to ensure that private members and the Opposition as a group should have more opportunity for initiating debates, bring in bills, asking questions and so forth. <i>(1966,Good Food,108</i>)

>  He had moved for a Committee of the whole House to lay this Bill before them, and to ask leave to bring in the Bill. <i>(Jun 21, 1854,NZ Parliamentary Debates,116</i>)

>  Mr. Hall and Mr. Stevens were ordered to prepare the Bill, which was brought in, read a first time, ordered to be printed, and the second reading ordered for Friday. <i>(Jul 22, 1868,NZ Parliamentary Debates,II/9</i>)

>  Another of those [seven] was a gentleman who brought in the Bill which now appears before us in a slightly-altered form. <i>(1892,NZ Parliamentary Debates,77/246</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> In accordance with its promises, the Liberal Government brought in several labour Bills in the session of 1891. <i>(1907,Life & Work of Seddon,234</i>)

>  One of the most useful measures brought forward by Weld during his term of office was one for the amendment of the existing Militia Act. <i>(1914,Life of Sir Frederick Weld,110</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to bring land into cultivation or production (See [break in](bla-bri.md#break-in))

>  <b>CARGILL</b> Open land is covered with either fern or grass, ... and is easily brought in. <i>(1860,Otago, New Zealand,29</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> ... approved method of `bringing in' bushed country. <i>(1933,NZ Holiday,111</i>)

>  Swedes and turnips ... their usefulness as feed for dairy cows and in the bringing in of new land. <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,371/1</i>) [NOTES: [OED2]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bring in</i> in the process of introducing land to cultivation. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,21</i>)

>  ... - the operation of `bringing in' the bush-country, or in other words of quickly converting forest-land to a sheep and cattle-grazing pasture. <i>(1929,TrNZI,59/406</i>) [NOTES: (Aston)]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere, but first in NZ] to develop or tame land. (See [break in](bla-bri.md#break-in))

>  Blocks of about 10 to 12 ha were brought in each year, starting with a burn, followed by stick raking and one giant discing. <i>(Jun 11, 1981,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

>  <b>CARGILL</b> Open land is covered with either fern or grass .. and is easily brought in. <i>(1860,Otago, New Zealand,29</i>)

>  A co-operative scheme for bringing in farm areas .. was adopted by the Whangarei RSA. <i>(Apr 20, 1944,NZ Farmer,5</i>)

>  The aim of the development programme is to bring in the easily worked wheel tractor country at about 30 acres per year. <i>(Dec 1964,NZ Journal of Agriculture,463</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Most of this area is manuka country and during recent years they have been bringing in anything from 200 to 600 acres annually. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,164</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> break in or bring in: to clear land of its original flora, cultivate it, and sow it down with something more productive such as a pasture of exotic grasses .. land was brought in; they worked hard to bring in the land (colloquial usage). <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,10</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to muster sheep or cattle in to a station, homestead block or shearing shed.

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> When I first came here 14 years ago we went from one place to the other mustering. We didn't do any yard work or shed work, we just brought the sheep in and went straight on to the next place. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,10</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Some shepherds carry a shearing blade with them so they can shear any woollies they come across, rather than bring them in. Sheep which miss more than three musters are completely wild and just about impossible to bring in, but they're very rare. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,81</i>)

### bring in the wool
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to muster sheep into a station.

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> For we shared good times, you and I, bringing in the wool. <i>(2000,Country Bloke and Other Verse, The,183</i>)

>  To muster: to bring in the wool. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  .. while mustering they are doing a dog job, taking up the burnt chops or bringing in the wool. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### bring your own
 
 <i>phr.</i> i.e. liquor (a suggestion that donations of liquor at a party would not be amiss; or, to patrons of a restaurant with a restricted license, that they may bring their own liquor to be consumed on the premises) (See [BYO](bri-byo.md#byo))

>  Few are likely to revert to bring-your-own, which they see as an anachronism, a hangover from the old days. <i>(Dec 23, 1999,Dominion,8</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> On the fifth I rested and threw a bring-your-own-booze party. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,151</i>)

### bringing in
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the mustering of stock to a station or central point.

>  We had our pipe-opener for the year's mustering with the bringing-in of the ewes for pre-lamb shearing. <i>(Sep 10, 1961,NZ Farmer,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the developing of land from its natural state into pastoral or agricultural production.

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> It is little wonder that station holders complain of the deterioration of their swards .. that despite the bringing in of new areas from fern and scrub ... <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,58</i>)

>  Swedes and turnips .. as feed for dairy cows and in the bringing in of new land. <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,371</i>)

### briquette
 
 <i>n.</i> a small block of compressed coal dust, used for fuel.

>  He maintains that briquettes can be successfully made from brown-coal slack. <i>(Aug 10, 1900,Taranaki Herald,1</i>)

### brisket
 
 <i>n.</i> the (human) chest; diaphragm. [ca1914-18 at least: W.H.B. Orsman: also by boxers You'll get one in the brisket if you don't look out.]

>  <b>HYDE</b> The men formed a ring. ... Larry Kirst said, `Sock 'im in the brisket, Kid, 'e's all wind downstairs,' and Timothy nodded. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,129,1970 ed.</i>)

### brisket-puncher
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a chain slaughterman who parts skin from the brisket of a carcass.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> brisket puncher: the chain slaughterman who parts the skin from the brisket. This used not to be done with the fist but with the tips of all four fingers. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,10</i>)

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> Wombat was a brisket puncher at the Patea freezing works from March through to September each year. <i>(1985,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,47</i>)

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> <i>Brisket-puncher, -punching</i>: punching or pulling pelts off briskets. [check] <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

>  His one ... companion was a freezing-works chain slaughter-man (with appropriate gestures he explained his job as a brisket-puncher) ... <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories III,4</i>) [NOTES: 19? Sargeson `A Final Cure' in 1975 NZ Short Stories III 4]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... their fullback, a brisket-puncher on number one chain, kicks it into the stand ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,85</i>)

### brisket-punching
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> <i>Brisket-puncher, -punching</i>: punching or pulling pelts off briskets. [check] <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

>  The slaughter-man withdrew - ... but this was no occasion for brisket-punching <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories III,7</i>) [NOTES: 19? Sargeson `A Final Cure' in 1975 NZ Short Stories III 7]

### Briskula
 
 <i>n.</i> a Dalmatian card game

>  There was a time when the game of cards, known as Briskula was king, If you did not play <i>Briskula</i>, you were not a Dalmatian, as it went with the breed. Every house and boarding hosue and club had its Briskula dens... In Briskula there were some distinctions - you led or were led. There was the traditional banging of fists and the cursing by all. If you won, you were great, if you lost, you were a fool and were abused by all, including your own... T heir fathers [50] didn't care much - they were out back drinking and playing cards - <i>Briskula and Treseta</i>, match-making, after all was women's work. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/49</i>)

### bristle
 
 <i>adj.</i> as an epithet; bristle grass, bristle moss, bristle tussock (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass),[moss](../M#moss),[tussock](tot-tyr.md#tussock))

### bristle fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> For our growing collection, we must now add specimens of the 'bristle ferns', <i>Trichomanes. </i> <i>(1882,Out in the Open,66</i>)

### bristle moss
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Yet another common moss on forest soils is the BRISTLE MOSS (<i>Echinodium hispidum</i>). <i>(1961,Flora NZ,69</i>)

### bristle tussock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARK & ADAMS</b> Some of the smaller tussock (<i>Poa colensoi</i>), alpine fescue tussock (<i>Festuca matthewsii</i>) and bristle tussock (<i>Notodanthonia setifolia</i>), have become aggressive in this way. Bristle tussock now dominates in parts of Marlborough and North Canterbury while the other two are more important in inland areas of South Canterbury and Otago. <i>Ibid.</i> 256 <i>Notodanthonia setifolia</i> (Hook. f.) Zotov BRISTLE TUSSOCK <i>(1973,NZ Alpine Plants,22</i>)

### bristle-back
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild pig.

>  <b>CARR</b> I peered hard among the neighbouring vegetation, hoping to catch a glimpse of the bristle-back as he snuck quietly away. <i>(1997,Wild Boars of Oruaiti, The,11</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> I knew that Philip's pig hadn't been responsible and there was yet another bristle-back to chase around the bush. <i>(1997,Wild Boars of Oruaiti, The,92</i>)

### brit
 
 <i>n.</i> fry; a small fish

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... along with their [sc. Right Whales'] food; which is principally spawn of a pabulous nature, of a red and yellow hue, called by the fishermen brit; which is sometimes seen supernatant on the surface of the ocean, many miles around. <i>(1838,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/402</i>)

>  Megrim ...Brill, Mahoa, Witch <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i> <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/197</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

### Britain of the South (Seas)
 
 <i>phr.</i> New Zealand

>  It became common in colonial times for New Zealand to be designated the <i>Britain of the South</i> (or <i>Great Britain of the Southern Hemisphere</i> or <i>South Seas</i>, etc.). Analogous formations with more idyllic reference include <i>Eden of the Southern Sea</i> and <i>Wonderland of the Pacific</i>. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,2</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Albions [sic] enterprising sons ... are wont to call [New Zealand] `the Great-Britain of the South Sea'. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),iii</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  From the hour when Captain Cook first sighted new Zealand, it was destined to become the Britain of the South; and though it might yet have much to encounter it, it would assuredly overcome all difficulties ... <i>(Sep 21, 1844,Nelson Examiner,1</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... the lord had ... talked to him about the `strong young Britain of the South Seas.' He had told the lord of its promise, its staunchness, and chiefly, of its inborn loyalty. <i>(1935,Little Country, The,150,1937 ed.</i>)

### Britannia
 
 <i>n.</i> the name to be given to Wellington, but dropped in 1840, applied to the original (and proposed) settlement at the mouth of the Hutt River.

>  <b>1/14/194</b> Britannia as our town is called. <i>(1840,NZ Journal</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from `Port Nick')]

>  Britannia, (or Wellington as it is now called by the NZ Company) <i>(Nov 3, 1840,NZ Journal,2/34/119</i>) [NOTES: (Hanson)]

### britch
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a woolly area of a sheep's rear and rear legs.

>  <b>BELL</b> These were strong-woolled ewes with coarse britches which had been crossed with the Corriedale by Jack Nairn to eradicate this defect. <i>(1952,Roundaway: Story of a Sheep Farm,32</i>)

### britch strike
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an infestation of a sheep's back end with blow-fly maggots.

>  On hill country, back and neck strike are a greater menace than britch strike and are more difficult to treat ... <i>(Dec 26, 1940,NZ Farmer,20</i>)

>  Crutching is of undoubted value in preventing britch strike. <i>(1940,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

### britchy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] having hairy wool around hind legs.

>  <b>PETER</b> Britchy: having hairy wool around back legs of sheep. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,7</i>)

### British treasury note
 
 <i>n.</i> a (thin) army blanket [WW1]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>British treasury note</i>. A blanket: NZ soldiers': in G.W. Ex Ex thinness of many Army blankets. <i>(1938,Dictionary of Slang,94</i>)

### Briton
 
 <i>n.</i> be a Briton: said to encourage a child to put a brave face on a hurt or setback (hence a (little) Briton, a brave child) [ca1935 Havelock said to children who have hurt themselves: also `Be a soldier, now. Don't cry.']

>  <b>HYDE</b> She opened her eyes and saw ... Mr Brennard in the act of being fond of her. `A little Briton,' he said, patting Simone's shoulder, `game as a regular little Briton'. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,105,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Mother dried his eyes ... And she told Arnold to take his hand, and father wheeled his bicycle out of the woodshed and told him to be a Briton, ... <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,5,1974 ed Rhodes ed.</i>)

### Britten motorcycle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRIDGES & DOWNS</b> The Britten motorcycle... Britten bikes have set four world speed records in ordinary bike trim, and hold the outright speed record for Daytona. <i>(2000,No 8 Wire: The Best of Kiwi Ingenuity,120</i>)

### bro
 
 <i>n.</i> (chiefly as a form of address, especially in Maori English; compare the US use especially in Black English) brother, friend.

>  <b>MARRINER</b> When addressed by one of these guys, so long as he refers to you as 'bro', you can rest assured you're in his good books. On the other hand, if he starts calling you 'beau', initiate a salvage rapidly. <i>(2001,Stonedogs,63</i>)

>  He lunches in the local RSA / They all know him there. He's a mate, a bro', a staunch bloke. <i>(Nov 27, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  They like to keep their celebrations simpler, so I get the call: "Come and have a meal with the bros." <i>(May 8, 2005,Sunday Star Times,E1</i>)

>  <b>Grimshaw</b> 'How bad are they?' James asked. 'How bad? They're like the Sopranos, bro!' Don said, and went off into a big laugh. <i>(2007,Opportunity,148</i>)

>  "Kia ora, bro," says the gardener. <i>(Jul 31, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  We have more to come, Bros. <i>(Nov 14, 1991,TV3,00</i>) [NOTES: In a "Nightline" story about gangs]

>  Hey Milt! C'mere! Hurry up bro. <i>(1989,Awekotuku Tahuri,58</i>)

### bro time
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The best thing I learned from travel was how unique New Zealand was. Our land, culture, "bro time" way of life. <i>(Oct 30, 2005,Sunday Star Times,11</i>)

### bro'ocracy
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal)

>  The tried and true NZ First vote winners are there - immigration, the Treaty of Waitangi, "bro'ocracy" and law and order - but this time there is also a more obvious pitch to Mr Peters' large grey constituency with a promise to raise superannuation to 72.5% of the average wage and a golden age card cutting pensioners' rates, transport, phone and power bills. <i>(Aug 1, 2005,Dominion Post,00</i>)

>  Gripping the podium, he slams Labour, National, the news media, the "bro'ocracy" that provides unnecessary jobs and privileges for Maori. <i>(Jul 8, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### bro'rocracy
 
 <i>n.</i> government policies and staffing that favour particular principles of the Treaty of Waitangi. (See [also bro'ocracy](bri-byo.md#broocracy))

>  NZ FIRST leader Winston Peters is promising to weed out the 'bro'rocracy' - and 'token jobs for Maoris' .. "[Te Puni Kokiri] has become the opiate of the Bro'rocracy - the never-ending trough of government money which keeps them in a permanent trance and mystic reality." <i>(Jun 22, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  Strategists had also coined the phrase "the bro'rocracy" to highlight those cashing in on Treaty settlements, but Mr Peters lost interest in it and dropped it. <i>(Dec 8, 2005,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

### bro'Town
 
 <i>n.</i> groundbreaking Simpsons-type TV cartoon featuring Polynesian characters

>  BRO'TOWN: Not even aoh! The best indigenous comedy to hit TV screens since Fred Dagg ... <i>(Dec 26, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

>  To its credit, <i>bro'Town</i> doesn't just skid over the settling pond of superficial textual referencing; there's also nutrient in the lumps beneath... Ultimately, <i>bro'Town</i> is fishing in its own lagoon so it knows the territory and is able to catch much more than just the ratings fish. <i>(Nov 7, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)

>  <b>ed. James H. Liu ... et al..</b> <i>bro'Town</i> is very much a boy's world - the product of the all-male theatre ensemble known as 'The Naked Samoans.' [223] With a notion of open-ended kinship embedded in its very title - you don't have to be 'blood' to be a 'bro' - <i>bro'Town</i> points to the ways that art can serve as a much needed bridge from cultural specificity to universal humanism. <i>(2005,NZ Identities: Departures & Destinations,221</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  Gleefully anti-PC - the boys are described as "dumb-ass P-heads and no-hopers" by Carol Hirschfeld in a cameo - <i>bro'Town</i> revels in skewering Polynesian culture, Auckland, single parents, smug Pakehas, gays, and Indian dairy owners in equal measure. <i>(Oct 17, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),29</i>)

>  <i>bro'Town</i> is a primetime animated sitcom that's going to hit New Zealand in a few months on TV3. <i>(Apr 17, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

### broad acres
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an extensive run or landholding.

>  While the agriculturists of Canterbury may be said to be enjoying a fair season, the opposite is the position of the small grazier and the pastoralist with his broad acres. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,218</i>)

### broad-axe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE</b> Two other tools used were the broad-axe, with one flat side and a face 10-11 inches long used for squaring timber for stringers ..., and the `frow' or paling knife. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,39</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### broad-billed petrel
 
 <i>n.</i> [The Maori name oi comes from the high-pitched call the birds make during flight.] (See [oi](o-on.md#oi))

>  The grey-faced petrel / great-winged petrel / North Island muttonbird / oi is not endangered - not by any means. <i>(Mar 2005,NZ Geographic,No 72, p.18</i>) [NOTES: The Maori name oi comes from the high-pitched call the birds make during flight.]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> <i>Prion Vittatus </i> - Broad-billed Petrel - Parara or Parera. The latter word is elsewhere applied to the Grey Duck - Anas Superciliosa. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,201</i>)

### broad-billed prion
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pachyptila vittata</i>, parara

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Parara, ...Pachyptila vittata, the broad-billed prion. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,264</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> ... I ... came upon Arthur , who'd just dug out two adult parara, another species of petrel, also called the broad-billed prion. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,53</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Prion, broad-billed .. .. parara, pekeha .. .. <i>Pachyptila vittata </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BROAD-BILLED PRION <i>Pachyptila vittata Other names</i>: Parara, Blue Billy (Chathams). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,38</i>)

>  <i>47. Pachyptila vittata vittata</i> (Forster, 1777) <i>Broad-billed Prion(Parara)</i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,24,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Broad-billed Prion Pachyptila vittata vittata</i> (FORSTER, 1777) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Whalebird, scooper, blue-billy, parara. ... Body blue-grey with conspicuous black open-M pattern across upper wings.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,85</i>)

>  The greatest enemy of the Chatham petrel is not a predator, but a competitor, the broad-billed prion (<i>Pachyptila vittata</i>). <i>(Aug 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 52/88</i>)

### broad-leaved cabbage tree
 
 <i>n.</i> [also broad-leaf cabbage tree]

### broadbill
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish (See [spearfish](spa-sys.md#spearfish); [swordfish](spa-sys.md#swordfish))

>  Landing a dozen broadbill worth almost $3000 improves the mood, but after six days of hard slog the Caroline did not land any big tuna to send to Tokyo. <i>(May 15, 2004,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] broadbill 2117 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### broadleaf
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  A hunt among the thick pristine undergrowth would uncover old gnarled broadleaf logs, doubtless discarded when the earliest settlers had felled large trees to saw and split the straightest portions for fence posts. <i>(Sep 2000,North & South,138</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> On December 8 I found a peculiarity of these islands - a tree which, so the botanists assured me, was only to be found here. It is some 12 feet high, has leaves some 12 inches long, and is of a dark green colour. It is called the `broadleaf' tree by the colonists <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,97,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> When complete Mana will have 76 hectares of coastal broadleaf forest, 57 hectares of grassland, and 84 hectares of wetlands in flax and sedge. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,33</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Hen and Chicken Islands) ?spp. ?=puka. [broadleaf tree]



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Coprosma</i> spp.

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> [<i>Coprosma lucida,</i> Forst.] The `Broad-leaf' or `Orange-leaf' tree of the settler; terms, however, which are probably not only applied to other species of the genus <i>Coprosma, (e.g. C. robusta,</i> Raoul,) but to species of genera belonging to very different families - and especially to <i>Griselinia lucida, (N.O. Corneae,) </i>whose foliage closely resembles that of <i>Coprosma lucida ... C. lucida</i> is also known to the Otago natives as `Kalamou' (Hector); and to the North Island Maoris as `Karamu', [fn] `Kakaramu,' or `Karangu,' [fn] (Colenso.) [fn] Also applied to <i>C. foetidissima. </i> <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,71</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) <i>Griselinia littoralis</i>, with foliage palatable to stock and with timber commonly used for fenceposts or battens. [occ. used for Gr lucida and some native coprosmas.] [aka kapuka, papauma, pukatea]

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The broadleaf had grown tall before the deer got thick and survi ved only because these animals could not reach the higher branches. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,85</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Broadleaf, .. kapuka, papauma .. <i>Griselina littoralis</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>broadleaf n. </i>small NZ tree or shrub with large glossy leaves. <i>(1968,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,89</i>)

>  Perhaps the commonest tree [near Waikouaiti] is one that I was not aware of having seen before. It grows to a considerable size, but in a very twisted, irregular shape. Its leaf is of a smooth, glossy, green colour, about the size of the leaf of a Portugal laurel, but more heart-shaped. The native name of the tree is kapuka. It is commonly called broad-leaf by the white men there. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 242]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Broad-leaf</i> ... a settlers' name for <i>Griselina littoralis</i>, Raoul; Maori name, <i>Paukatea. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,56</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Two or three hundred yards beyond begins an irregular fringe of native bush, broadleaf, veronica, fuchsia, pittosporum, and tangled supplejack; beyond these rise the lofty pines and other forest giants. <i>(1898,Contributions to NZ Botany,79</i>)

>  Broadleaf [fn <i>Griselina littoralis</i>.], five-fingered Jack [fn <i>Panax arboreum.</i>] ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>G[riselinia] Littoralis, Raoul. Kapuka; Papaumu; Broad-leaf.</i> Timber strong, close-grained and durable; ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,239</i>)

>  <i>pa[a]pa[a]uma</i>, a tree (Griselinia littoralis), broad-leaf. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Broadleaf (<i>Griselinia littoralis</i>) is the only other species of its genus in New Zealand. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,81</i>)

>  <b>BLAIR</b> There are few trees in the [Otago] bush so conspicuous or so well known as the broad-leaf....It grows to a height of fifty or sixty feet, and a diameter of from three to six; the bark is coarse and fibrous, and the leaves a beautiful deep green of great brilliancy. <i>(1879,Building Materials of Otago,155</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  88 Papauma (broadleaf) slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,17</i>)

>  Broadleaf are trees of medium height with a smooth white-grey bark and rather large, thick, glossy leaves. They don't usually grow in stands, mostly randomly spread throughout beech forest. <i>(May 28, 2008,Daily Post (Rotorua),A18</i>)

>  Bob was negotiating the branches of a broadleaf, up which he was climbing with a speed which did not promise to abate till he had reached the topmost branches. <i>(Jan 2, 1890,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Later, broadleaf was the timber chiefly used for fencing, because it was much less inflammable than totara. Black pine (matai) was used on the front country but it was not as good as broadleaf, because it rotted away in a few years. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,104</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> Our farm supplies manuka blocks, ironwood and broadleaf. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,95</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> .. generations of fallow had fallowed, up into the low-hanging broadleaf. It doesn't really matter where you encounter broadleaf, or what type of deer ranges in it, the same story applies - those large, oval-shaped, shining green leaves, often tinged with yellow, are a year-round delight. [broad-leaf] <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,213</i>)

>  Some big broadleaf and kowhai overhang the roadway. <i>(Jan 29, 1924,Otago Witness,69</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> There is also the rata tree, the prince of firewoods, ... miki-miki, kamahi, or so-called red-birch, white and black birches (which are really beeches), the mountain broom, ribbon wood, broad leaf, totara, and many others, ... and always available in the Ranges [sc. Alps]. <i>(1986,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>) [NOTES: [wood]]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Griselina littoralis (The Broadleaf)</i>. A tree, sometimes 60 ft. in height, differing from <i>Griselina lucida</i> in its terrestrial habit, and smaller, less glossy leaves ... One of the best known and commonest of New Zealand trees. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,316</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Later, broadleaf was the timber chiefly used for fencing, because it was much less inflammable than totara. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,104,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  Broad leaf (<i>Griselina littoralis</i>). A large tree, with large ovate, shining leaves. <i>Ibid.</i> 43 Broad leaf. Griselinia littoralis, Raoul. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/3/37</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Grisilinea littoralis Broad leaf <i>Ibid.</i>2/123 Griselinia lucida Broad leaf <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> The native laurel (the `broadleaf' of the colonists). <i>(1874,George Washington Pratt,42</i>)

>  The broadleaf (<i>Griselina littoralis</i>) is abundant in the district [of Banks' Peninsula], and produces a hard red wood of a durable nature. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/328</i>) [NOTES: (J. B. Armstrong)]

>  <b>POTTS</b> The rough trunks and limbs of the broadleaf ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,103</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Griselina littoralis</i>, Raoul. PUKATEA, BROADLEAF. - An erect and thickly-branched bush-tree, 50-60 feet high; 3-10 feet in diameter. Grows chiefly in the South Island and near the coasts. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,128</i>)

>  [Native name] Kapuka .. [Settlers' name] Broadleaf .. [Family] Corneae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  Papauma (broadleaf) .. Griselinia littoralis .. Timber heavy, dense, and of great stength, grain prettily marked, but timber liable to warp; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)

>  <i>broadleaf</i> A common New Zealand tree which grows chiefly on the sea-coast <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),51</i>)

>  <i>broadleaf n.</i> one of two similar N.Z. trees, the other being the<i> shining broadleaf.</i> The Maori name for both is <i>puka. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 60]]

### broadleafed
 
 <i>n.</i> broadleaf [?typo or tree omitted]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> You remember how old Andy Keogh brought that big broadleafed down right on top of his own kitchen. Only the real bushmen can do it properly. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,34</i>)

### brob
 
 <i>exclam.</i> ?Brother as address [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 22B BROB]

### broccolini
 
 <i>n.</i> a cross between broccoli and Chinese kale

>  Broccolini: This is one sexy vegetable. It's a cross between broccoli and Chinese kale producing a long, elongated green that has become popular even with broccoli stalk haters and children. <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

### Brogdenite
 
 <i>n.</i> labourer imported by Brogden and Co. to work on the rail works under the Vogel policy.

>  In the autumn of 1874 ... Mr M.Grenfell was busy with a staff of employees erecting the Immigrants Barracks for the Government ... The first immigrants consisted of a number of workers with others, known as Brogdenites, who had been selected by Messrs Brogden and Co., the contractors for the Oamaru-Moeraki section of the railway. <i>Ibid.</i> 107 [At the Oamaru sports meeting c1880s] there were Chinese, Cingalese, Germans, ... shearers, gold miners, Brogdenites, sailors and swaggers; ... [108] COMMENCEMENT OF RAILWAY LINE/ The Brogdenites./ The [railway building] contractors, Mrssrs Brogden and Sons, had a bullock prepared [for the ceremony of turning the first sod]. The Brogdenmen were all there, and a very fine type of navvy they were. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,48,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> ... in their absence a party of Brogdenites (railway construction gangs) broke in to the Maoris' huts [at Waimate] and used them as their own. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,11</i>)

### broken
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of pieces of fleece wool) from which the short and dirty ends have been removed.

>  <i>Broken:</i>Pieces of fleece wool from which the short and dirty ends have been removed. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,53</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] scored with gullies and steep faces.

>  <b>WELD</b> Pasture lands (i.e. hilly, broken and unsuited for tillage ) ... <i>(1860,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <b>GRANT & FOSTER</b> On broken runs there is always a certain percentage of these. <i>(1880,New Zealand: Report on its Agric. Conditions,42</i>)

>  The land being a little broken, it was found judicious to increase the original area. <i>(Feb 1894,NZ Farmer,63</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Left early this morning in several small canoes in which we proceeded about 3 miles up the river Wangaroa and then continued our course overland through a most broken country ... <i>(Mar 5, 1840,Williams Journal,88</i>) [NOTES: Journal March 5 in Porter (1970) Turanga Journals 88]

### broken arse
 
 <i>n.</i> an emotionally distressed person; a prisoner of low status (Paremoremo prison)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> His answer never varied, and we all used to laugh at Ted and call him 'broken arse'. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,103</i>)

>  [A psychiatrist discussing the difficulties of drug therapy for disturbed prisoners] Drug therapy can give the inmate the status of a `broken arse' - if you'll forgive the expression - at the bottom of the [status] heap. <i>(Apr 12, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: (Spectrum)]

### broken country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that is tamed or developed from native vegetation.

>  In the days when fertilisers were freely available, the handling of newly broken country was relatively easy. <i>(Apr 1, 1944,NZ Dairy Exporter,5</i>)

### Broken Hill square-set
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Broken Hill square-sets: </i>A special method of timber support for wide stopes brought to Waihi by R.E. Williams, associate mine manager, in the early 1900s. ... <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),313</i>)

### broken mouth
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep that has lost some, but possibly not all, of its teeth.

>  For Sale - 3000 half-bred ewes, mixed ages, no old broken mouths. <i>(Nov 29, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> John Wallace from Takapau called and bought our last years 4 Lincoln rams. One of them a 4 tooth, one a broken mouth, 2 good mouths. <i>(Feb 4, 1908,Brabazon Diaries</i>)

### broken-down swell
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> an `upper-class' emigrant reduced through circumstances (usu. liquor) to manual or menial labour (See [cf. paper-collared swell](p-per.md#paper-collared-swell))

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> There was another shepherd of a very different type; he came under the category known as `broken-down swells' - a numerous class in the country at that time [ca1850-1860s]. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,6</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an emotionally distressed person

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BROKEN DOWN SWELL (n) Emotionally distressed person. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)

### broken-in
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] tamed for pasturing stock.

>  <b>COWAN</b> The experience he gained in work on the newly broken-in settlements was useful ... when he came to take up a pastoral block in Hawke's Bay ... <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,3</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This broken-in country is mainly in the vicinity of the Mount Possession homestead. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,228</i>)

### broken-mouthed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of aged sheep] having few or no remaining teeth.

>  There was only one of them [sheep] broken mouthed. <i>(Mar 1895,NZ Farmer,99</i>)

>  No broken-mouthed ewes should be put to the ram. <i>(Apr 1908,NZ Farmer,296</i>)

>  Customarily, broken-mouthed ewes are turned in to eat off the tops and the roots are then harrowed out in breaks some days before the sheep are given access to them. <i>(May 20, 1933,NZ Journal of Agriculture,300</i>)

>  Cereals are also used for wintering broken-mouthed ewes ... <i>(Apr 15, 1944,NZ Journal of Agriculture,245</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> .. even broken-mouthed ewes will winter quite well and rear a good lamb. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,13</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> broken -mouthed: by its fifth year an adult sheep has erupted eight incisor teeth and is termed full-mouthed. The teeth then begin to deteriorate and the animal steadily becomes broken- or failing-mouthed. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,11</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Sheep at eight years old will be in their old age; ... an eight year old sheep would be what is called a broken-mouthed [39] creature, that is to say it would have lost some of its teeth from old age, ... <i>(Feb 10, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/38-39</i>)

>  Ewe lambs 32 two tooth ewes 402 four & six tooth 638. full mouthed 1322 broken mouthed 11, wedders 290 total 2695. <i>(Mar 14, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [Hocken TS]]

>  <b>BELL</b> Has nobody a line of broken-mouthed ewes that could be palmed off to him as four-tooths; .. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,26</i>)

### broll
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bran-like by-product of the wheat milling process, used for animal feed.

>  Daily News Sept 22 14 we have gone on to broll, which is a by-product from the wheat mills. It's a sort of bran. We had been looking for something like a brewer's grain or broll for a long time. The main difference between broll and maize is that while they are the same in energy, broll has the highest protein content, and that energy is starch energy .. broll, if fed correctly, can have a huge impact on your protein test. <i>(Sep 22, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,Farming 2000 Supp 14</i>)

### bronze cabbage tree
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Designers are searching for a flowering bronze cabbage tree to complete the garden. <i>(Nov 5, 2004,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

### bronze cuckoo
 
 <i>n.</i> = shining cuckoo

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> Once, hearing the chirp of a chick in a tree, I climbed up and saw a young bronze cuckoo in the nest of one of these warblers, grown so big that he could not force his way out. In this bush I also can [sic] on many nests of pigeon, green-parrot, and tui. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,72,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Shining Cuckoo Chrysococcyx lucidus lucidus </i>(GMELIN, 1788) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Pipiwharauroa, bronze cuckoo, whistler. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,254</i>)

### bronze shag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Hypoleucus chalconotus</i> Gray. Bronze Shag; Pink-footed Shag. Guthrie-Smith (1914) records this with the other Stewart Island species, with which he thinks it probably conspecific; at least, he found them almost certainly interbreeding. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/75</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The bronze and Stewart Island shags ... which interbreed, are confined to the Foveaux Strait area; .. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,260</i>)

### bronze whaler shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka bronzewhaler shark]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, bronzewhaler 238-43, 250 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BRONZEWHALER SHARK ... <i>Other names: Carcharinus brachyurus</i>; New Zealand whaler, whaler shark, toiki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,177</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BRONZEWHALER [<i>Carcharinus brachyurus</i>] ... Other names are whaler, New Zealand whaler, and toiki (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,12</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BRONZE WHALER <i>Galeolamna ahenea</i>: A shark that grows to 11 feet (3.35cm) and gets its name from the bright bronze colour of the whole of the top portion of the body, which has been described as `like a new penny'. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,215</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BRONZE WHALER SHARK Carcharinus brachyurus (C. ahenea, C. remotus, C. improvisus)</i> (Bronze shark) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,60,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Mayor Island was great for diving, for kingfish, for bronze whalers and black coral, right down very deep. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,59</i>)

>  [species name] bronze whaler shark .. [scientific name] <i>Carcharhinus brachyurus</i> .. [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

 <i>n.</i> = shining cuckoo

>  How true is the Bronze-winged Cuckoo to his appointment ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/65 CHRYSOCOCCYX LUCIDUS, Gml. Pipiwharaupa. [sic for -wharauroa] Bronze-winged Cuckoo, Whistler. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/45</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

You may walk knee-deep in Mountain Lilies, you may pick Edelweiss [fn] by the handfull [sic]; the air at a certain season of the year is full of the fragrance of the Alpine Broom [fn 2]. [fn] The Mountain Lily is ... a buttercup, and the Edelweiss does not belong to the same genus as its European namesake. [fn 2] <i>Carmichaelia grandiflora</i>, var. <i>alba. </i> <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,203</i>) [NOTES: (L.Cockayne)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> We have climbed above the pines [on the West Coast]; ... and up here on the mountain side the vegetation is the flowering <i>rata</i>, the <i>akeake</i>, the native alpine broom, the white blossomed ribbonwood, ... the flax-like<i> kiekie</i>, and the semi-tropical <i>neinei</i>, half-shrub, half-palm, and whole forests of fern trees. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 95</i>)

### broom hand
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a woolshed worker who sweeps up scraps of wool [known as being ''on the broom.''] (See [also broomie](bri-byo.md#broomie))

>  <b>BIBBY</b> We had plenty to do as there were no broom hands or set sheepo, and cutting dags was done in spare time. <i>(1990,Lunesdale: A Bush Farm Community from 1871,36</i>)

### broom-handle
 
 <i>v.</i> to sodomise with a broom handle

>  The cadet, who was at the school for 15 months from the start of 1962 said he had also seen other cadets being raped and "broom-handled". <i>(Oct 14, 2004,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

### broomey
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of basic jobs] relatively easy.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He was a bit useless, and seems to have got the broomey jobs around the places. <i>(1976,Swagger Country,35</i>)

### broomie
 
 <i>n.</i> a worker who sweeps the shearing board clean during shearing (also called sweepo).

>  <b>BOWEN</b> This is why a lot of tally pens and yards holding freshly-crutched sheep present the appearance of a fall of snow, and very often shearers are blamed for not making clean blows when it is the fault of the `Broomie'. <i>Ibid.</i> 155 BROOMIE. The broom hand on the board. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,65</i>)

>  <i>Broomie</i>. A sweeper on the shearing board. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing Gloss)]

>  <i>BROOMIE</i>: The sweeper on the shearing board. <i>(Sep 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>)

>  <i>Broomie:</i> Person who sweeps the shearing board clean during shearing (also called sweepo). <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,53</i>)

>  <i>broomie n. NZ & Austral. colloq.</i> person who sweeps floor in shearing shed. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,90</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>broomie</i>: A shedhand who uses a broom. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/22</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  <i>Broomie</i>.- A boy who keeps the board swept of locks, etc., at shearing. (C.)[c1890-1910] <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  At shearing time about 55 men were employed in the shed; 28 to 30 shearers, 2 penners-up, 4 pickers-up, 2 broomies, 6 rollers, 1 classer, 1 fleece carrier, 4 piece sorters, 3 pressers, 1 bale brander, 1 butcher and brander. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,208,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> BROOMIE: A shed hand who sweeps the board. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person who works on the broom in a woolshed, sweeping the shearing board clean between sheep being shorn. (See [sweepo; rousie](../S#sweepo;-rousie))

>  Broomie, a boy who keeps the board swept of locks, etc at shearing. <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> It seemed a wrench to leave the crowd, to wear a broomie's crown .. as shearer, roller, picker-up and broomie all in one. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,9</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As in the Lagoon Hill shed the fleecies and broomies take turns about doing the bottom end ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,210</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> In the smaller sheds he [the fleece-o] may also act as a broomie. If so, he will enjoy a lower social status in the shed, for a broomie sometimes acts as penner-up. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,31</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Broomie: Person who sweeps the shearing board clean during shearing (also called sweepo) <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,53</i>)

### Brorepairs
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I recommend the sister who brushes suitors' tears from her shoes, and her doctor hubby who sews up wounded gang members and calls his work "Brorepairs". <i>(May 1, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

### brose
 
 <i>n.</i> oatmeal water as a thirst-quencher for shearers or harvest workers.

>  Sailors [sic] word for porridge. Here the word is applied to the oatmeal and water taken to the woolshed or harvest field to be drunk between smoke-ohs. I have also heard it called <i>beer</i> and <i>brose</i>. (C.)[c1890-1910] <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

### Brothers Island tuatara
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a specific tuatara ( <i>Sphenodon guntheri</i> ) [ORIGIN: blend]

>  The Matiu/Somes hatchlings are just the second and third confirmed tuatara produced by Brothers Island tuatara (<i> Sphenodon guntheri </i> ) transferred to offshore islands ... These were the only two transfers of Brothers Island tuatara, directly from the island and from eggs hatched at Victoria University and raised at Nga Manu Nature Reserve in Waikanae. <i>(Oct 2007,Footnotes,29/1</i>)

### brown
 
 <i>n.</i> anus

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> The concrete truck driver ... informed us that a woman had thirty-two muscles and nerves in her bottom ... every time he saw us he would yell `Rember [sic] what I told you Tim - whack her up the brown!' <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a situation or mood unpleasing or unpleasant. [1984 17 M E103 Pakuranga Coll. 5 WHAT A BROWN [indicating annoyance]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>brown</i> in phr. <i>do a brown</i> shy or sulky or ashamed; eg `What's wrong with Jill?' `Nothin', Miss. She does a brown for no reason, eh.' <i>[Alison Gray]</i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,21</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> a distinguishing epithet in the names of plants and animals, see beech, birch, bittern, creeper; daisy, mountain; dogfish, duck; fern, hypolepsis; grass, mountain poa; kiwi linnet, lizard(tree), mudfish, parrot, pine, robin, shark, teal, trout, tussock.



 
 <i>n.</i> a penny, a copper coin [also brownie c1950s p.c. G.J.Griffiths notes: a copper coin; Aust; used very occasionally in NZ in my hearing - `toss him a brown' - and that was some years ago] (See [also brownie](bri-byo.md#brownie))

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And said they scorned to earn a brown;/ <i>Ibid.</i> 18 There are lots of little swindles/ In all New Zealand towns,/ Got up by folks for easing you/ Of your superfluous browns;/ <i>(1864,Songs of the War,10</i>)

>  You can't turn an honest brown (sc. penny) anyhow. <i>(1896,Tom Bracken's Annual,41</i>) [NOTES: [check quotation]]

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> ... but 'twere even half a crown/ You'd get it if I had it, but I haven't got a `brown'. <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,64</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> In New Zealand Slang a penny is a <i>brown. </i> <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... <i>brown</i>: penny <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> Half penny [:] half copper, brown, magpie, bawbee <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,151</i>)

>  For the penny nicknames used were<i> copper </i>and <i>brown</i> (sometimes<i> Brownie</i>), while the halfpenny was called a <i>half-copper</i> a <i>mag</i>, or a <i>rap. </i> <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,16</i>)

### brown as a Maori
 
 <i>phr.</i> on form of <i>brown as a berry</i> (14c Chaucer) [ca1930 R Mason - Auckland]

### brown backs
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Senecio eleagnifolius [?elaeagnifolium]</i>, a short scrub plant.

>  *brown backs<i> Senecis [?=Senecio] elaeagnifolium</i> [Hetley Native Flowers 1888] [fn]*Because of the brown hairs on the backs of the leaves <i>(1927,TrNZI,57/913</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Popular Names]

>  <b>HETLEY</b> The short scrub [at New Plymouth] ... was greatly composed of <i>Senecio eleagnifolius</i> or `brown backs.' <i>(1888,Native Flowers of NZ,3</i>)

### brown beetle
 
 <i>n.</i> grass grub [[Should this go to beetle or is brown collocated cf blackbeetle: prob. place at beetle.]]

>  There are twenty-eight native species belonging to the genus <i>Odontria,</i> but all observers agree that the principal grass grub is <i>Odontria zealandica.</i> In the larval stage this is known as `grass grub,' `white grub,' or `curl grub,' and the adult stage is known as `grass grub beetle,' `brown beetle.' or `turnip fly.' <i>Ibid.</i> 23/322A One of the most destructive pests in New Zealand is the grass-grub or brown beetle (<i>Odontria zealandica</i>). This insect, together with the caterpillar of the Porina moth, has been responsible for the destruction of ... crops. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/306A</i>)

### brown birch
 
 <i>n.</i> [?see cit. also at red pine]

>  [<i>Notho]fagus menziesii</i>, Hook. f. ... Silver Beech. Brown Birch, White Birch, Red Birch. Silver Birch. Although this species exhibits a considerable amount of variation in the shape, toothing and texture of the leaves, it is the most easily recognized of all the New Zealand beeches, especially when the fruit is fairly developed: <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/298</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

>  <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/307</i>) [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner) see cit. 1 b. above.]



 
 <i>n.</i> Nothofagus solandri [(Canterbury)]

>  <i>[Notho]fagus solandri</i>, Hook. f. ... <i>Myrtilloides cinerascens</i>, Banks & Sol., MS.<i> Cliffortiordes oblongata</i>, Dryander. Entire-leaved Beech. Black Birch of Wellington, Canterbury, and in part of Otago and Southland. White Birch of Nelson, and in part Otago. Black-heart Birch of Otago. White, Black, Red, and Brown Birch of Oxford and Alford Forests. A fine tree, sometimes attaining the height of 100 feet, but usually 60-80 feet, with a trunk from 2 to 4 feet or more in diameter. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/301</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Nothofagus Solanderi </i> <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/307</i>) [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner) see cit. 1 b. above.]

>  <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/307</i>) [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner) see cit. 1 b. above.]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Nothofagus Solanderi </i> <i>(1905,Tree Culture in NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### brown bomber
 
 <i>n.</i> an Auckland beer, DB bottled brown.

>  <i>brown bomber n. (Coll.)</i> name given to N.S.W. parking police from their brown uniforms; in N.Z., beer. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  By then, we were growing up , and the temptations of surfies, brown bomber (beer) and late night beach parties were there for the taking. <i>(Dec 28, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

### brown bread
 
 <i>adj.</i> dead [rhyming slang]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Feeling my body so cold ... they thought I was brown bread <i>Ibid.</i> 245 BROWN BREAD (adj) Dead. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,55</i>)

>  A man who talked too much about his involvement in one of New Zealand's largest bank robberies became `brown bread with two in the head', the High Court was told yesterday. ... The woman said that in May or June 1983 she overheard Oldham teling some men not to worry about Grace not turning up. ... He said, `Brown bread with two in the head.' <i>(May 5, 1984,Dominion,3</i>) [NOTES: [?1984, ?1987]]

### brown coalition
 
 <i>n.</i> an alliance of interested Maori parties

>  Journalists probed for chinks in the brown coalition. <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  He fished for the brown cod / and had a name for those / who thought it inferior eating. <i>(Jan 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Kahlua and milk. [1984 Nov 3 Wellington Teachers' Club]

### brown derby
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>brown derby</i>: A CONE, usually OF plain (vanilla) ICE CREAM, with the ice cream part DIPPED IN hot CHOCOLATE which quickly hardens around it. Very tasty. This term is used only in the South Island. It is a chocolate dip in the North Island. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,18</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>brown derby</i> South Island version of a chocolate dip ice cream. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,21</i>)

### brown dogfish
 
 <i>n.</i> = northern dogfish

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> NORTHERN DOGFISH ... <i>Other names: Squalus blainvillii</i>; Griffin's dogfish, common spined dogfish, brown dogfish; pioke, okeoke (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,186</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] dogfish, brown 66, 98, 115 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### brown duck
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Elasmonetta chlorotis</i>, a scarce NZ wild duck [aka brown teal, see]

>  A teal duck with a brood of young ones was seen in the Te Ngutu Domain on the 10th of March. <i>(Jun 27, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

>  This concentration of ducks affords a good opportunity of ascertaining if any of the rarer species are present in the district but among the concourse of water-fowl in this sanctuary not a single specimen of black teal, brown duck, or grey teal could be seen. <i>(May 25, 1940,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Flights of the common brown duck [fn 1 Anas superciliosa.], and of the blue-breasted red-billed water-hen [fn 2 Porphyrio australis.], people these swamps. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/276</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANAS CHLOROTIS. (BROWN DUCK.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Tarawhatu, Patete, {Tete,} and Tete-whero. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,248,[1888 2nd edn 2/257] ed.</i>)

>  The brown duck [<i>Anas chlorotis</i>] ... is not so commonly seen as the grey duck and the `black teal' or pochard. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/373</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The species we have identified in the Kaipara and Hokianga are - the Putangi, or `paradise-duck' (<i>Casarca variegata</i>); the grey duck, or Parera (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>); the brown duck, an allied species or variety of the last; the Papanga, or `teal,' or `widgeon' (<i>Fuligula N.Z.</i>), and some other varieties that may be imported birds, or crosses, or other native species. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/225</i>)

>  <i>Anas chlorotis</i>. - Pateke, Brown Duck. Both Islands and Chatham Islands. A very indifferent flier. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  ANAS CHLOROTIS, Gray. - Brown Duck. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/127</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>PARKER</b> There are eleven species of Native Ducks belonging to nine genera, all found elsewhere, except two--the little Flightless Duck of the Auckland Islands (genus <i>Nesonetta</i>) and the Blue Mountain Duck (<i>Hymenolaemus</i>). Among the most interesting of the non-endemic forms, are the Paradise Duck or Sheldrake (<i>Casarca variegata</i>), the Brown Duck (<i>Anas chlorotis</i>), the Shoveller or Spoonbill duck (<i>Rhynchaspis variegata</i>), and the Scaup or Black Teal (<i>Fuligula Novae-Zealandiae</i>). <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,117</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Prof. Parker `Catalogue of New Zealand Exhibition' 117 in 1898 Morris Austral-English 128]

>  As regards the duck family we are also well off, with our paradise ducks, brown ducks, grey ducks, white-winged ducks and blue mountain ducks - the last belonging to a genus (Hymenolaemus) peculiar to New Zealand. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,196</i>)

>  T.W. Kirk records a similar instance in the case of the brown duck (<i>Elasmonetta chlorotis</i>). <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/145</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>tete. 1. n. Spatula rhyncotis, spoonbill duck. 2. Elasmonetta chlorotis, brown duck. ... 3. Nettion castaneum, grey teal. ... 4. Fuligula novae-zealandiae, black teal. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,479</i>)

>  <i>Elasmonetta chlorotis</i> Gray. Brown Duck. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/83</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Knowing the extraordinarily furtive habits of the Brown Duck I am not prepared to state that it is altogether ... gone from Tutira. <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,14</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Tata (usually spelt tataa to show emphasis is on second vowel). This is the brown duck, now very rare. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Elasmonetta ...chlorotis</i> ... Brown Duck. PATEKE. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,91</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Paateke, n. ... 2. Anas chlorotis, brown duck... <i>Ibid.</i> 409 Tete ... Anas chlorotis, brown teal ... <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,270</i>)

>  <i>brown duck n 2</i> : a rather rare New Zealand duck (Anas chlorotis or Elasmonetta chlorotis) grayish brown above with rufous markings and breast <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,284</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BROWN DUCK (BROWN TEAL) <i>Anas chlorotis Maori name</i>: Pateke. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,88</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Brown duck .. pateke, patake .. <i>Elasmonetta chlorotis</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> lycopodium, a beautiful [143] wiry green ground-creeper is lush; the now rare native brown duck is still to be seen <i>(1970,Stewart Islanders,142</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERA</b> There were putakitaki (paradise ducks), parera (grey ducks), tataa (brown ducks) and whio (blue ducks). ... The pukeko (swamphen) were caught only as a last resort, because their flesh was stringy and tough. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,75</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> ... The gray [sic] Duck [Anas superciliosa] <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,235</i>) [NOTES: c1899 (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 235 (Birds S. Westland)]

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> In the smaller water-courses [in Southern Alps] were numbers of Mountain Ducks (<i> Hymenolaemus malacorhyncus </i>) which with Paradise ducks (<i> Casarca variegata </i>), and Scaups (<i> Fuligula Novae Zealandiae </i>) and others obtained ... formed a welcome addition to our ... provisions. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,216</i>)

### brown eye
 
 <i>n.</i> (a display of) the anus (as an expression of contempt)

>  Principal Rod Widdon took action after his pupils started imitating Bart [Simpson] bending over with his trousers down., They had seen the image on Tazo hologram discs in Eta chip packets. "From having no children doing "brown eyes" at school., we now have a spate of it," he wrote. <i>(Sep 4, 1998,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <i>brown eye</i>: bare bum display <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>MIHAKA</b> But not only that, you [Sergeant Rattenbury] said that I clasped, or this Maori gentleman clasped, the cheeks to his ... - you know, that area of his body and pulled them apart? [Sergeant] Yes, what I would call a `brown eye'. [Mihaka] Is that what you call it? And you are positive of that are you? <i>(1984,Whakapohane,51</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>brown eye</i> an active verb here, in that it involves exposure of the anus; elsewhere simply a noun for the anus. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,16</i>)

>  [British] Good for you [NZ] good on yu[sic] ... [Bri.] Royal greeting [NZ] brown eye <i>(Jun 10, 1989,Dominion,10</i>)
