 <i>n.</i> <i>Pteridium esculentum</i>

>  <i>(1869,TrNZI,1</i>) [NOTES: [check cit.]]

### brown flat
 
 <i>n.</i> a large flat brown (portobello) mushroom

>  Button mushrooms still dominate about 75 per cent of our market, but having arrived just four years ago, the brown giant with a cap the size of a mini-pizza is now muscling for space on every supermarket shelf... Actually, these "brown flats", "gourmet browns", "portobellos" (call them what you will) are nothing more than brown button mushrooms which have been allowed to grow up. <i>(Oct 17, 2000,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  These big brown mushrooms... are an American strain of the standard cultivated mushroom <i>Agaricus bisporus</i>. In the United States, they are known as portobello. Here, Clive Thompson [of Parkvale Mushrooms in Carterton] chose to give them the traditional New Zealand name "brown flats". <i>(Jul 16, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

### brown flight
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  While Lange's Alma Mater was suffering from "brown flight" brain drain, the merits of a trucking firm sponsoring a local primary school were debated. <i>(Jul 5, 1998,Sunday Star Times,F11</i>)

>  Years of white and brown flight, and a poor academic record have contributed to Petone College losing three-quarters of its roll. <i>(Jun 30, 1998,Dominion,11</i>)

>  The poor standard of some schools denied them a fair chance and led to `brown flight' as parents directed their children to high achievement schools, many of them Catholic. Senior lecturer in Maori studies at Auckland University Pat Hohepa rejects Mr Ioane's comments and suggests Islanders involved in `brown flight' should go home. <i>(Aug 10, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),A1</i>)

>  Porirua secondary school principals say `brown flight' is not a significant problem in their schools, and has been largely contained by strict enforcement of zoning schemes. <i>(Aug 12, 1986,Dominion,3</i>)

### brown gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> general term for the <i>Hoplodactylus</i> genus of geckos [not to be confused with the brown gecko / Canterbury gecko]

>  NEW ZEALAND is home to two types of geckos, which have generally been described as green (<i>Naultinus</i> genus), and brown (<i>Hoplodctylus</i> genus) geckos. Genetic work may yet reveal the taxonomy to be more complicated than this, as brown gecko species have proved to be unexpectedly diverse. <i>(Sep 2010,NZ Geographic,49</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) alternative name for the Canterbury gecko; associated with scrubby coastal areas from Banks Peninsula as far north as Marlborough. [common gecko <i>Hoplodactylus "Canterbury"</i>] (See [common gecko](chi-con.md#common-gecko))

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Papa, ... 2. Dactylocnemis pacificus, tree-lizard. = moko-papa, narara-papa, teretere. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,284</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Papa ... Hoplodactylus pacificus, brown gecko</i> a lizard = <i>moko-papa. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,244</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Brown or Common Gecko moko-papa, moko-pirirakau, moko-tapiri, ngarara papa - <i>Hoplodactylus pacificus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  'So for the extensive survey, we're using a basic method of counting how many lizards are captured in a 24-hour period. It's a quick index of population size for species such as common skinks and McCann's skinks and common (brown) geckos. Unfortunately we're unlikely to detect less abundant species such as green skinks, cryptic skinks or jewelled geckos.' <i>(Mar 2009,Open Space,75/11</i>)

### brown kiwi
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) one of the two species Apteryx australis and A. mantelli, found in specific regions. [eg North Island brown kiwi, South Island brown kiwi, Okarito.]

>  ...the essence of Hauturu is.. encapsulated in experiences like these: watching brown kiwi feeding at night under pohutukawa on the foreshore, then several hours later having a pod of orca working the shallows along the same coastline... <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/31</i>)

>  The rarest kiwi, the Okarito brown kiwi, has been saved from extinction, the Conservation Department says. <i>(Nov 10, 1999,Dominion,14</i>)

>  56. <i>Apteryx mantelli</i>, Bart. North Island Kiwi, Brown Kiwi. - Still to be found in many parts of the North Island, it is also met with on some adjacent islands. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,505</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>Apteryx australis</i> Shaw and Nodder. Brown Kiwi. The brown kiwi is one of the species which Guthrie-Smith considers will always maintain itself in the broken country on his run in Hawke's Bay. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/81</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <i>56. Apteryx mantelli,</i> Bart. North Island Kiwi, Brown Kiwi. - Still to be found in many parts of the North Island, it is also met with on some adjacent islands. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,505</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BROWN KIWI <i>Apteryx australis Maori name</i>: Kiwi; tokoeka (Stewart Island). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,18</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> APTERYX MANTELLI. Bart. Brown Kiwi <i>(1971,Birds of New Zealand,59</i>)

>  It was thought less than 5% of young North Island brown kiwi were surviving to adulthood because they were being killed by predators. <i>(Dec 1, 2000,Daily News,1</i>)

>  Even the brown kiwis of the Waitangi pine forest managed to thrive and keep ahead of the bulldozers (although they aren't so lucky dealing with rogue german shepherd dogs). <i>(Jul 18, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

>  The Conservation department had consulted Ngai Tahu and proposed to adopt nationally the tribe's traditional name of "tokoeka" for the southern species, he said. Tokoeka, literally translated as weka with a rod or walking stick, will be the name of the Haast, Stewart Island and Fiordland varieties of brown kiwi and the other [sic] will be the North Island and Okarito. <i>(Apr 23, 1993,Dominion,3</i>)

>  A fourth species of kiwi had been discovered... Till now scientists had recognised three species - the great spotted kiwi or roa in the northwest of the South Island, the little spotted kiwi on Kapiti and a few other islands, and the brown kiwi, scattered from Northland to Stewart Island. <i>(Apr 23, 1993,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Instead he tells of the efforts being made to help the rare Okarito brown kiwi, which has a population of only 60-100 birds. <i>(Aug 6, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  Five kiwi sanctuaries were created, covering 43,000 hectares and protecting critically endangered Okarito brown kiwis, the Haast Tokoeka and some areas of North Island brown kiwis... About 10,000 North Island brown kiwis live there [sc. in Whanganui National Park] and DoC has no specific programmes aimed at saving these. <i>(Jun 8, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

>  There's also a thriving population of little spotted kiwi and South Island brown kiwi, and visitors must keep to the paths to avoid disturbing the burrows. <i>(Dec 10, 2001,Dominion,17</i>)

### brown skink
 
 <i>n.</i> a well camouflaged skink which lives in damp habitats in the lower North Island and the northern tip of the South Island; a sunbasker but secretive. [<i>Oligosoma zelandicum</i>, previously <i>Leiolopisma zelandicum</i>]

>  At first glance the food mass in the weasel's stomach appeared blackish with numerous tiny yellow flecks. This proved to be the remains of a juvenile Whitaker's skink, which made up about 70% of the stomach contents. The rest of the stomach's contents were comprised of the remains of a juvenile brown skink Leiolopisma zelandicum and three tiny invertebrates (a spider, an isopod and a fly) that may have come from the skinks' stomachs. <i>(1997,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.doc.govt.nz/upload/documents/science-and-technical/casn146.pdf 1997 Department of Conservation, P.O. Box 10-420, Wellington, New Zealand. Reference to material in this report should be cited thus: Miskelly, C.M., 1997. Whitaker's skink Cyclodina whitakeri eaten by a weasel Mustela nivalis. Conservation Advisory Science Notes No. 146, Department of Conservation, Wellington.]

>  We carried out captive-based behavioural comparisons between striped skinks and the common terrestrial brown skink (Oligosoma zelandicum). Brown skinks are of similar mass to striped skinks and overlap in geographic range and habitat, but are easily captured by hand and pitfall trapping in the wild. <i>(2004,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: New Zealand Journal of Ecology (2004) 28(2): 267-278 (C)New Zealand Ecological Society http://www.nzes.org.nz/nzje/abstract.php?volume_issue=j28_2&pdf_filename=NZJEcol28_2_267.pdf]

>  Since then four other species of skinks and geckos have been re-introduced to the island, the brown skink has been re-discovered and the numbers of gold stripe gecko and the equally threatened MacGregors skink were thought to have grown markedly. <i>(Dec 31, 2007,Internet - see Notes,3</i>) [NOTES: http://www.manaisland.org.nz/Newsletter35.PDF Annual Report for the AGM of Friends of Mana Island Inc (FOMI) for year ending 31 December 2007]

>  Brown skinks are now present in the Taranaki area and the western half of the southern North Island, on Kapiti Island, Mana Island .. and possibly on islands in Wellington Harbour. Brown skinks are also found in the northern and north-western South Island. Brown skinks frequently co-exist with common skinks .. and at a few localities with spotted skinks. Brown, common, spotted and speckled skinks co-exist in Marlborough Sounds on Stephens Island/Takapourewa and are likely to have once done so in the Wellington area ... <i>(2002,Internet - see Notes,11</i>) [NOTES: http://www.doc.govt.nz/upload/documents/science-and-technical/TSRP48.pdf North Island Oligosoma spp. skink recovery plan 2002-2012]

### Brown Table
 
 <i>n.</i> the Maori establishment (play on Roundtable)

>  He developed a public profile with assaults on what he has called Maoridom's "brown table", sparking fury in their boardrooms. <i>(Aug 15, 2002,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  <i>(1998,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>) [NOTES: Issue of June 1998?]

>  They talked disparagingly of "the brown table" - the old Maori power base of Sir Graham Latimer and the Maori Council. <i>(Mar 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Alliance Maori activist Willie Jackson, a media colleague and friend, says Tuku and his brother-in-law Tau Henare were shrewd in setting themselves apart from the iwi power networks, and offering to represent ordinary Maori against the "Brown Table" elite. <i>(Jul 28, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  He's made an issue of challenging the older Maori bureaucracy, "the Brown Table". <i>(Sep 19, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  John Tamihere.. attacks the "BMW boys", the Browntable which he says seeks to hog Waitangi money for their own iwi. <i>(Sep 26, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  Few of the so called "Brown Table" at the centre of the Peters storm.. would claim to have emerged unscathed. <i>(Jun 24, 1992,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  This is not a report that will be welcomed by either Government of the so-called "Brown Table" group, who have made an industry out of exploiting grievances, but it is an important document and must be addressed by politicians and policy makers. <i>(Sep 27, 2000,Dominion,12</i>)

>  Mr Tamihere's intervention in the process is likely motivated by a determination to protect his urban constituents' slice of the action and to prevent the "Brown Table" from wetting its beak too liberally. <i>(Feb 28, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  He and NZ First deputy Tau Henare were among the new wave of Maori politics that was going to rock the foundations of the "Brown Table" - the powerful male tribal elders such as Sir Graham Latimer, Sir Robert Mahuta and Sir Tipene O'Regan. <i>(May 31, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### brown tussock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Then an awful falling - the brown tussock leapt up to meet them. <i>(Jun 27, 1931,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  For miles and miles they beheld one unbroken series of plains, covered with brown tussock, with here and there a few clumps of native flax and a species of wild palm-lilies, or cordyline, familiar to us now as the cabbage tree. <i>(Dec 10, 1900,Star, The,4</i>)

### brownie
 
 <i>n.</i> a penny (coin or money); a copper [also brown ca1950 (an occasional use;? esp. in hotels)] (See [also brown](bri-byo.md#brown))

>  Following are other local money-names ... 1d. - <i>Maori half-crown, brownie or copper ...</i> <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,(Red Page)</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from <i>Loafer</i>, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

>  For the penny nicknames used were <i>copper</i> and <i>brown</i> (sometimes <i>Brownie</i>), while the halfpenny was called a <i>half-copper</i>, a <i>mag</i>, or a <i>rap. </i> <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small sultana cake, also known as station brownie.

>  <b>JONES</b> We also made a sweet loaf called a brownie ... <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,55</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> brownie. ii Small sultana cake, almost ubiquitous in the shearers' smoko ... <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,11</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> brownie: originally cocoa damper or bush bread, currant loaf among shearers. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a currant or raisin loaf sweetened with (brown) sugar, an old word for a currant or raisin loaf; [also station brownie cf. spotted Tommy] (See [also station brownie](spa-sys.md#station-brownie))

>  <i>brownie:</i> Currant-loaf, as made in camps, etc. in Australia and New Zealand. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),51</i>)

>  <i>browny, brownie n. </i>unleavened cake containing currants baked in a camp oven; any plain cake. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <i>brownie ... NZ & Austral</i>. loaf baked in camp oven. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,90</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 139]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>brownie</i> originally cocoa damper or bush bread, currant loaf among shearers, treacled gingerbread cake among swaggers The Shiner knew; <i>brownie gorger </i>shearing shed hand. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,21</i>)

>  <i>brownie</i>: A bun baked with fruit and sugar. In the past it was typical shearer's food. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/22</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  <b>PARTINGTON</b> It was an amusing sight to see the three of us, each with a huge hunch of `brownie' (bread sweetened with brown sugar and currants) in one hand, and a lump of ice in the other. <i>(1883,Random Rot,312</i>)

>  <i>Brown&#769;.ie</i> (broun&#769;.&ibreve.), <i>n. [Add.] 2.</i> A rough cake made of flour and water mixed with currants. <i>[Australian bush cant] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2019</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> The rations for musterers in my time were mostly bread and meat. At times dried apples and apricots were supplied together with rice. Now and again a duff was boiled or a brownie cooked in the camp oven. <i>(1969,Molesworth,102</i>) [NOTES: 1907-14 Mowatt's Reflections in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 102]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Cook apparently not doing his work properly. Men wanted brownie at night which cook refused to give ... <i>Ibid </i>176 [note] Brownie: `Bread baked with currants and sugar, sometimes called `Tommie' and now known as currant loaf.' L.G.D.Acland. <i>(Feb 8, 1908,Coldstream,96,1985 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1908 Feb 8 in Studholme Coldstream (1985) 96]

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Then with this eternal mutton, sodden brownie he will spread,/ An' black tea with a little sugar, ... <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,44</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  Sugar, bread, meat, and brownie disappear as if by magic when the cook's back is turned, and are hid underneath their coats. <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/4/5</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Anyone who can bake, make a decent `brownie' (as the plain cake is called) and a plum duff is fit for a shearers' cook. <i>(1913,Country Work,17</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> [Shepherds speak] Here, jerk the brownie along, Pots. Think you've got the only guts at the table. <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,17</i>)

>  <i>Brownie</i>. - Bread baked with currants and sugar. Sometimes called <i>Tommy</i> and now, usually, <i> currant loaf. </i> <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Two other specialties were brownies (a scone loaf) and boiled rice. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,68</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... demanding that [the cook] produce the `Brownie' which they vowed he'd have hidden in the tucker-box. <i>(1949,High Country Days,13</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> The digger's fare ... included an occasional duff or pudding, and a current [sic] or raisin cake risen with soda and acid, which was usually the full width of the camp oven and termed a brownie. <i>(1952,Faring South,166</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The packmen produced tea and `brownie' and we all sat round the fire enjoying it. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,39</i>)

>  We also provided ... very strong tea and a slice of brownie with butter and jam ... for all and sundry passers-by ... <i>(Aug 1954,Here & Now,17</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Doug still remembers the hungry stations where they ate old ewe, potatoes, rice pudding and, at the weekend, one pound of `brownie' per man. ... The boss said `brownie' was cake. We knew it only as bread dyed with bullocky's joy (treacle), and sparingly dotted with currants. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,97</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> A good camp cook could ... turn out beautifully light bread, scones or `brownie' in his camp oven. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,62</i>)

>  <b>MARRIOT</b> Brownies were the bushman's staple diet. Made in a camp oven, they were like a big brown scone with a nice crisp crust. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,59</i>)

>  May there never be less than cold meat and "browny" (currant bread) in their tucker boxes. <i>(Jan 26, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> brown trout.

>  <b>MANNERING</b> As far as I remember they were all rainbows, but now and then we used to get a `brownie' in daylight. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,175</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Dried-up) dwarf-dock

>  <b>DON</b> On these Rough Ridge foothills the light olive-brown dwarf-dock is in great abundance ; so there will be many `brownies' next Autumn. <i>(1907,Chinese Mission Work,22</i>) [NOTES: 1907-8 Don Chinese Mission Work(?) 22]



 
 <i>n.</i> a ?local or idiolectal name for a young saddleback.

>  The plumage of the yearling [saddleback] was the hue of blended chocolate and black ... these first-season birds, or `brownies' as we called them ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/337</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver) [quoting Guthrie-Smith's Bird Life on Island and Shore]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The sweet station "brownie," rich in brown sugar and heavy with Merino dripping and a few rare raisins is being cut up for supper. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,256</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  I remember the times when pannikin tea and "brownie" with the shearers was the joy of my life. <i>(Dec 14, 1897,Otago Witness,57</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small portable whare, used for sleeping quarters on a harvesting train

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> They'd put ten tons of cement on each of half a dozen waggons and pull a brownie too - that's a whare with a couple of bunks and food. <i>(1976,Water Joey,40</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Small, wheeled whare (of only two or three bunks) which is used as sleeping quarters for a small team of, say, chaff-cutters. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,11 i</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (informal) a person with 'brown' skin

>  SPORTS ON THE WEST COAST .. Sixth Race: 300 yards flat race, nine competitors, open to natives. Robert (native) 1; private C. Bater, 2. This was closely contested, the brownie only beating Bater by a yard. <i>(Jun 29, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,6</i>)

>  In his column in yesterday's <i> Northland Age </i>, the Maori Party MP says he and wife Hilda stopped off in Hawaii "to do some indigenous networking - hanging out with the brownies". <i>(Nov 11, 2009,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  "It's based on a lot of things I've experienced as a brownie, where I've often been the lone 'brownie' and white people have kind of held me responsible for all the bad things brown people have done," Rodger says. <i>(Mar 5, 2002,Independent Herald (Wellington),7</i>)

>  <b>Fiu</b> 'Dude, only us brownies come up and hug people, tell the maids something nice and only fellas like you thank fellas like me ... <i>(2006,Purple Heart,262</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Charlie and the 3 girls played with Hakopa's little daughter. This is the first day we have managed to get the little browny in the slightest degree sociable. <i>(Nov 1, 1850,Auckland Journals,34,1971 ed.</i>)

>  He is taking on what he says are racial stereotypes, such as that of the "nasty brownie". "As a brownie, you get this, and I almost sound too victimy on it and it's not a victimy play, but sometimes [the hostility] is just a look, or crossing the street." <i>(Mar 6, 2002,Dominion,17</i>)

>  The White Power stuff that exists down in Christchurch just wouldn't happen here, purely because of the number of 'brownies' you've got. <i>(Jun 4, 2002,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  However, my experience from last Friday night is a positive story about the lovely brownies .. Then I saw this guy (didn't look carefully to tell if he is Maori or Islander, but definitely browned), holding my purse ... <i>(Sep 21, 2009,Capital Times,4</i>)

### brumby
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THORNTON</b> I've got that there brumby in; he's a beauty. <i>Ibid </i>256 These [Taupo] plains were the habitation of hundreds of wild horses, or `brumbies', as they were generally termed. <i>(1916,Wowser, The,111</i>)

>  `Them brumbies on the plain out there, I see 'em yesterday up near the Boomin' Creek. ... bald-tailed brumbies, campin' round a bunch of scrub ... <i>(Oct 1919,Quick March,33</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> BRUMBY: Australian name for wild horse. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,185</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 185]

>  <i>brumby n.</i> a wild horse. [origin uncertain] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [The Australian terms] <i>Never never, brumbee</i> ..., for instance, will have little meaning for most New Zealanders. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,60</i>)

>  Brumby to Represent NZ[sic] /... One of the five New Zealand riders to compete at the Sydney Royal Show ... on her mount Rum. A brumby is a common description of a wild horse, and Rum was formerly among the many running round the Galatea district [of the central North Island plateau.] <i>(Feb 1953,Sports Post,1</i>)

>  Brumby to Represent NZ[sic] /... One of the five New Zealand riders to compete at the Sydney Royal Show ... on her mount Rum. A brumby is a common description of a wild horse, and Rum was formerly among the many running round the Galatea district [of the central North Island plateau.] <i>(Feb 21, 1953,Sports Post,1</i>)

>  <i>brumby</i>. The Australian name for a wild horse. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),52</i>)

>  <i> ~ n. </i>a wild horse [origin doubtful, perhaps Abo]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <i>brumby ... n</i>. a wild horse [Prob. from Major Brumby, a . Tasmanian breeder and breaker in of horses]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> Duff, who took us chasing brumbies through the manuka scrub [at Tokaanu], old Bob who cut our hair ... <i>(1969,Wild Irishman,84</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> Duff, who took us chasing brumbies through the manuka scrub [at Tokaanu], old Bob who cut our hair ... <i>(1969,Wild Irishman,84</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Often a weedy, long tailed brumby was grazing in the paddock. Or it just stood there with its head down. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,8</i>)

### brush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... burning away the brush and underwood. <i>(1839,Information Relative to NZ,9</i>) [NOTES: (?W. White)]

>  <b>MATHEW</b> (Letter) ... after scrambling for ten minutes through the brush, we came upon a full view of the fall. <i>(1840,Journal,2/s</i>) [NOTES: 1840 F & S Mathew [TS] Journal 2]

>  <i>brush n.</i> brushwood; forest. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <i>brush ... NZ & Austral.</i> thick undergrowth, small trees and shrubs, land covered with these. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,91</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 140]]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> First we notice the broken down swells,/ Once wealthy, but now very needy,/ <i>(1862,Auckland Vocalist,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a girl or woman. esp. as a sexual object (See [cf. crow](con-cyp.md#crow))

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I don't go looking for trouble with brushes that are under age. <i>(1941,When the Wind Blows,55</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> What comes along but an Iti bint, a real grouse brush she was, with bonzer black eyes and nice charlies. <i>(1947,Gorse Blooms Pale, The,200</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Wait a minute, the man said. How old do you reckon she is? ... [49] Because, the man said, I don't go looking for trouble with brushes that are under age. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,48,1974 ed.</i>)

>  There were about a dozen brush in the place and about three times as many jokers. <i>(1951,Landfall,17/21</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton)]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> It's the good-looking brush that give a man all the trouble. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,ix/239</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> What a grouse looking brush. She's asking for it. I'd like to slam a length into that. <i>(1974,Predicament,182,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> <i>brush</i> girl, woman <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,95</i>)

>  <i>brush 2 n. NZ & Austral. sl.</i> a young woman or girl. [orig. unkn.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,91</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  GRASS GRUB (<i>Costelytra zealandica</i>). The adult of this grub is a beetle which is commonly called the grass-grub beetle or the brown beetle. This species is the most serious insect pest in New Zealand. ### brush hook
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDER</b> Will not these islanders convert anything and everything they can into war instruments? Even axes, hatchets, reaping hooks, brush hooks, or anything else that has a sharp edge or is heavy enough to break a man's skull, they will use as weapons of war when they cannot procure European arms. <i>(Sep 27, 1821,Marsden's Lieutenants,174,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1821 Sep 27 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 174]

### brush yard/brushwood yard
 
 <i>n.</i> a makeshift sheepyard made of brushwood (scrub).

>  <b>AYSON</b> Thomas got his assistants to erect a brushwood yard on the bank of the river, well above the ford, so that the lambs could be forced to jump over the bank into the deep water. <i>(1937,Thomas,121</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> We built a yard for the sheep - that is, we chopped down a lot of scrub, and made what is termed a `brush' yard - and then my `boss' left me ... <i>(1881,Chequered Career,74</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> ... having made a small brush yard, Dent and `old Lass' were put in charge of the main mob of sheep ... <i>(1898,Reminiscences and Experiences,20</i>)

>  A brush yard will do well enough for a small flock which does not require to be very often in it. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,281</i>)

### brush-harrow
 
 <i>v.</i> to harrow with a rough or makeshift harrow made of brushwood (See [cf. bush-harrow](bri-byo.md#bush-harrow))

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> Seed should be brush harrowed in, that is wild Irishman should be interlaced in the crossbars of the harrows. <i>(1949,Sheep and Sheepmen of Canterbury,45</i>) [NOTES: 1860 August Duppa in 1949 Crawford Sheep and Sheepmen 45]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to harrow pasture using manuka or other scrub cuttings.

>  Brush-harrow paddocks to spread the cattle droopings [sic] <i>(1885,NZ Farmer,5 130</i>) [NOTES: NZ Farmer 5 130 Brush-harrow paddocks to spread the cattle droopings [sic]]

>  .. the grass seed is sown, brush harrowed and rolled. <i>(Mar 15, 1943,NZ Journal of Agriculture,166</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

Rockies stick to the Kawau cliffs, occasionally making their way up coastal gullies to forage. This striking orange and chocolate brown-coloured wallaby even scuttles up sloping puriri trunks. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### brushwood
 
 <i>n.</i> in early occas. use for scrub: it appears to have been the early word for `scrub'. (See [cf. brush](bri-byo.md#brush))

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> ... the land is generally clear from timber, cepting small brushwood and fern, which can with little trouble be cut down and burnt off. <i>Ibid.</i> 269 We reached before dark the spot on the plain where we had lodged before and remained there all night, have made a screen of brushwood and fern to shelter us from the rain which now began to fall. <i>(1819,Letters & Journals,176,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> ... we passed `Three Kings' which are steep ... and covered with brushwood and ferns. <i>(1820,Journal,12,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> As to the natural productions into the interiors the low grounds are full of brush wood from 6 to 20 feet high principally of the Tea tree or Diocema and nearly the same sorts of tree which surround the harbour. <i>(Mar 22, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 22 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 362]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The road, being now unfrequented, is quite tedious to pass over, on account of brushwood. <i>(Nov 28, 1832,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1832 Nov 28 W.Williams's Journal in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 25]

>  ... covered ... with timber and brushwood <i>Ibid </i>1/9/121 [Dieffenbach's Report] The beach was grown over with brushwood <i>(1839,NZ Journal,1/4/39</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Col. Wakefield's despatch in (1840) NZ Journal 1/4/39]

>  ... near the beach, a thick brushwood grows <i>Ibid.</i> 2/45/251 ... now grown over with brushwood <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/29/50</i>) [NOTES: Dieffenbach]

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> We walked round the little valley, ... the hills rising ... at the back, covered with thick fern brushwood, and shut in on one side by a mangrove flat covered with water at high tide, ... <i>Ibid.</i> Apr. 7 We traversed the site of the intended town ... [sc. Russell]; the country seems everywhere the same, hills and dales covered with thick brushwood, the real scrub of New South Wales, and divided by deep ravines and gullies full of luxuriant vegetation, ... <i>(Apr 2, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 2 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 103]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... you cannot walk through a wood without cutting your way through the brushwood before you. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,178</i>) [NOTES: 1844 F.A.Molesworth to the House of Commons Committee on NZ Matters in 1848 Wakefield Handbook for NZ 178]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> [Clematis] anon descending in chains of bloom, wreathing fantastic garlands around the brushwood. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/15</i>) [NOTES: [Angas from South Australia]]

>  <b>ALLOM</b> Grass everywhere makes its appearance whenever fern and brushwood are burnt off, ... <i>(1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: [does not use scrub] 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 126]

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> It frequents the brushwood surrounding the forest ... <i>(1971,Mr Surveyor Thomson</i>) [NOTES: c1850s in 1971 Hall-Jones Mr Surveyor Thomson (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> In a few moments we were ... collecting dry fern and brushwood for a fire ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,120,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> ... the shells bursting amongst the brushwood close to where the natives were concealed <i>(1879,Waikato War,40</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> No sign of human life that morning; the shrill cry of the curlew on the beach and the full rich carol of the tui or parson-bird from the brushwood skirting the shore fell faintly upon the ear. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/5/99</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Through tangled boughs and brushwood crashed,/ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/127</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> One man had to go in front of us, and with an axe chop a way through masses of supplejack and brushwood, so rich was the vegetation. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,113</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Alex Mounter ... helped Starkie to build a big board and brushwood shack at Waikaremoana; ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,167</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> ... a continued chain of fertile hills, producing on their sides a great deal of brushwood and small trees, with a rich covering of varied herbage. ... the land swelling up in curious hillocks, covered with [line missing] <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/225</i>) [NOTES: [does not use scrub]]

### buck
 
 <i>v.</i> (of horses) to buck violently, buckjump, buck a town down.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Some of the sailors ... amused themselves by trying to ride some horses brought in from a wild mob running in the back-blocks. These horses would `buck a town down,' as the saying is. I saw one sailor get a cropper; he regained his feet quickly, ... and caught hold of the stirrup; whereupon the neddy `cow-kicked,' and caught him in the stomach. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,70,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Buck</i> (b&ubreve.k), <i>n</i>. The plunge of a horse in bucking. See BUCK,<i> v. i</i>., 2, in the Dictionary. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2019</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a try, attempt, go; esp. give it a buck

>  <b>BELL</b> If I had a couple of hundred acres of good bush land, and a wife like that girl down there would make, I wouldn't call the Queen my aunt; and, by Jove! I mean to have a buck at some of those sections in the Whakatangi Block that'll be thrown open shortly, ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,84</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> I've a mind to have a buck at this new rush myself. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,71</i>)

>  <i>buck ... NZ & Austral.</i> act of bucking by horse; <i>NZ & Austral. </i>an attempt, a try (<i>give it a buck, have a buck at</i>) [latter sense nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,91</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 141]]

>  <b>ALLEY & HALL</b> One of the most striking features of modern New Zealand farming is its flexibilty, and the readiness to `try anything once' or to `give it a buck' when some innovation swims into its ken. <i>(1941,Farmer in New Zealand, The,104</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to object.

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Buck</i>. 3. Also, v.i., to object, be reluctant (v.t. with at): coll, from ca1890; mainly Aust and NZ. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,99</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a male crayfish

>  <b>FISHER</b> We found that when there is a group of crayfish in a cave it is usually the biggest that stands vigil at the opening. We have witnessed how a big buck would face up to a fully grown conger eel, ... <i>(1977,Angels Wear Black,40</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (of a reef) barren

>  <b>McARA</b> [Prospectors in the 1870s] ... seem to have ignored the Martha outcrop entirely - describing it as a `buck', or barren, reef. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a young male, esp. a Maori (See [cf. buckaroo](bri-byo.md#buckaroo))

>  <b>BAUME</b> Four bucks from Raglan came in drunk and there was a fight. <i>(1933,Half Caste,23</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The Maori buck did the shearing, with machines, boys penned, and did pick-up work. Wahines ... did the sorting, and Maori girls did the sweeping on the shearing board. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,110</i>)

### Buck Buck
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> At school, however, there was little place for Duck Stones, with its dangerous stone-throwing; Tipcat, with `cats' a danger to eye and window in a crowded area; Buck Buck, with its backbreaking potential, particularly on asphalt; punching forms of Bar the Door; Cap On and Fly the Garter, also a danger on hard grounds and in crowded areas; hoops, Whip Top, and kites, for which there was less space. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,217</i>)

### buck fever
 
 <i>n.</i> a hunter's attack of shakes at the sight of a prize stag

>  <b>CRUMP</b> For the first time since I'd shot my first deer I was stricken with buck-fever; I knew I was going to miss him [the stag] but I fired anyway. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,138</i>)

>  Hunters should keep an eye on their companions to watch for the onset of `buck fever' - a condition which affects hunters when they sight a roaring stag. ... hunters get excited, they become confused and may make irrational decisions leading to accidents. <i>(Mar 11, 1981,Evening Post,16/1</i>)

>  Buck fever has broken out - and it is a disease which can kill! Each year at this time hunters swarm into the high country aiming to bag the perfect stag. <i>(Apr 3, 1988,Sunday News,9</i>)

### buck hop
 
 <i>n.</i> a stag dance

>  It may not be out of place to mention Jack Burns' `buck hop' on the top floor of the old bacon factory. ... There were no lady partners, ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,127,1978 ed.</i>)

### buck in
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>LELAND</b> The Mayor of Greymouth was on TV recently, praising the townsfolk for the way they bucked in (PITCHED IN) to help the city and each other during a flood. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,19</i>)

### buck Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a male Maori (See [cf. buck](bri-byo.md#buck))

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Finally the College Fifteen wound up a close second in the season's fixtures, ... for a team with several big `Buck Maories' from Leeston settled all other teams in that year, and they were just too heavy, good and impossible to defeat. ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,67</i>)

>  <i>Buck Maori</i> (slang) a male Maori, especially a well-built man. Recorded post-1920, but possibly used before. (On the American <i>buck-nigger</i>, it may be noted that <i>buck-nigger</i> has been used to describe a male Australian aborigine - 1870 ...) <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/231</i>) [NOTES: (S.J. Baker)]

### buck on
 
 <i>v.</i> (obsolete)

>  A porter at Palmerston had the duty of clearing the car, but he apparently bucked on the job and on the morning of August 29 the car was returned to Dunedin without being cleaned. <i>(Sep 17, 1910,NZ Truth,273/5/4</i>)

### buck out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to practise bareback riding on stock.

>  <b>MCRAE</b> We had been 'bucking out' his precious heifers as practice for the upcoming rodeo <i>(1998,On the Run,41</i>)

### buck quartz
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining] (See [cf. buck reef](bri-byo.md#buck-reef))

>  The only change is that the vein of buck quartz has now changed to payable ore. <i>(Dec 18, 1896,Thames Star,4</i>)

>  <b>BALE</b> The colour varied according to the impurities present ... Quartz that contained no impurities was termed by the goldminers `buck' quartz. <i>(1971,Maratoto Gold,23</i>)

>  In the face of the drive at the 160 feet level the buck quartz in the footwall is cutting out and a better class of stone taking its place. <i>(Jun 12, 1880,Thames Star,2</i>)

### buck reef
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Buck Reefs.</i> The explanation of these occurrences is difficult. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/84</i>)

>  The great "blow" or "buck-reef" of the Tokatea is found in the slates at the western end of the lower or No. 7 adit. <i>(1898,AJHR,C-9/51</i>)

>  There is evidence to show that occasionally they [sc. small branching veins] lead into large veins of nearly barren quartz called "buck reefs." <i>(1898,AJHR,C-9/31</i>)

### buck saw
 
 <i>n.</i> a hand frame-saw [c1935 used occas. Havelock instead of usual frame-saw]

>  <b>FEATON</b> Try the /CHEAP HARDWARE HOUSE/ FOR/ ... /COLONIAL OVENS, &C. ... Hand, Rip. Tennon, Cross-cut, Pit, and Buck Saws. <i>(1879,Waikato War,frontispiece advertisement</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

### buck scoop
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a horse-drawn implement used in making irrigation systems.

>  A modification of the ordinary scoop is called the "buck scoop". <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,117</i>)

### buck-a-head
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  You started in Auckland playing Radio Hauraki buck-a-head concerts... <i>(Dec 7, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

### buck-dance
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> BUCK-DANCE: All men dancing together. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,185</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 185]

### buck-dancing
 
 <i>n.</i> dancing between men only; `stag' dancing

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... on Saturday nights ... [the shearers] had `buck' dancing in the sheds. <i>(1922,Happy Wanderer,143</i>)

### buck-jumping
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> ... a few tumbles from a buck-jumping brute. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,43</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> [In Canterbury c1850] The Australian coo-ee, the Australian buck-jumping horse, the Australian stockwhip and wideawake hat came into New Zealand pastoral life. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,242</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the violent bucking of horses

>  <b>W H S ROBERTS</b> BUCKJUMPING [title] ... August 23, 1856. I mounted my good mare `Lassie,' with the intention of leading the two packed horses, when to my astonishment her ladyship started buckjumping, and after a long fight, finding she could not throw me, suddenly stood on her hind legs and fell over backwards. ... Buckjumping is a horrid vice, although it is a very amusing performance - to the spectator. The horse tries to roll itself up into a ball, with all four feet close together, head down as near the ground as possible between the forelegs, tail well down, back stuck up like the hump of a camel when in good condition. Attitude is everything. Then commences the performance. With an active spring and rather a forward motion up you go in the air, and down again with a jerk, then up and down like an india-rubber ball, occasionally varying the action by jumping forward and kicking out behind with both legs at once, returning to the oval position as rapidly as possible, then turning round like a teetotum at lightning speed. <i>(Aug 23, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Aug 23 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 35]

>  <b>CAIRD</b> Buck-jumping is, I believe, an art only known by the colonial horses at the Antipodes. I imagine it is generally caused by the horse being broken in too quickly, and backed too soon. <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,29</i>)

>  <b>COTTLE</b> I had never experienced the sensation of buck-jumping, which in its true significance is peculiar to colonial horses. <i>(1891,Frank Melton's Luck</i>)

>  <i>Buck&#769;.jump&#769;.ing</i> (-&ibreve.ng), <i>vb. n.</i> The act of bucking. See BUCK, v. i., 2, in the Dictionary. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2019</i>)

>  In Australia, the majority of horses are vicious, and given to the trick of buck-jumping. <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,137</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> The faculty of buckjumping is ... almost confined to Australian horses...it was a fact that eight out of every ten horses could and did buckjump, and with many of them the vice was incurable. <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,50</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the habit of some horses to attempt to throw their riders.

>  <b>CAIRD</b> Buck-jumping is, I believe, an art only known by the colonial horses at the antipodes .. it [the horse] soon gets its head between its legs, and begins the series of bounds and flings in every imaginable direction, which constitutes buck-jumping. <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,29</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The athletic games of strength include Bull in the Ring, Buck Jumping, Cock-Fighting, Honey Pots, Heave-Ho, King of the Castle, No Man Standing, ... and Lazy Stick. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/744</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Apart from simple climbing there was, for example, `crossing' the plantation on the treetops, and Rough-riding, in which one player sat on a fir tree branch while the others took the end of it and pulled it up and down and then let it go flying up in the air. The player who could stay on the longest was the rough-riding champion. Of a similar nature was Buck-jumping, in which individual players rode a macrocarpa or gum branch [113] to see who could rise the highest. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,112</i>)

### buck-jumping show
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CHARLES</b> The term `buck-jumping show' has given way to the American `rodeo', although in fact the only real similarity is in the riding of bucking horses. <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,10</i>)

### buckaroo
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [pakaru](p-per.md#pakaru),[puckeroo](pop-pyo.md#puckeroo))



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. US buckaroo `cowboy' f. vaquero](../U#us-buckaroo-`cowboy'-f.-vaquero))

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... <i>buckaroo:</i> farmhand <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a young male Maori [cf. buck ca1951 F. O'Brien (Wellington)] (See [cf. buck](bri-byo.md#buck))

### buckaroos
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) money

>  Beans, bread, buckaroos, cash, coin, dough, loot, green stuff, lucre, mazuma, moolah, want some? <i>(Dec 2005,Tearaway,48</i>)

### bucker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>bucker, buckjumper n.</i> vicious horse, given to bucking or buck-jumping [orig. Australia or America]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Open up the gate now and let the bucker go,/ And see another jackaroo come down covered in snow!/ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,11</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,28</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> [He] could skin a sheep or back a bucker] <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,22,1950 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [1889 P.Reeves (1950) A.E.Woodhouse NZ Farm and Station Verse 22]

### buckeroo
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pakaru](p-per.md#pakaru),[puckeroo](pop-pyo.md#puckeroo))

### bucket dredge
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Spoon dredges were used in this and other countries for many years, but the employment of bucket dredges to work the auriferous gravels in the beds of rivers was first introduced in New Zealand. <i>(1906,NZ Mining Handbook,34</i>)

### bucket man
 
 <i>n.</i> a homeless person - refer notes [Orig. Robert Jones, a well-known transient on Wellington streets in the years 1980-2003]

>  [They] carry the same status as Robert Jones, the bucket man, and train guards we come to know. <i>(Jan 28, 2006,Dominion Post,E9</i>)

>  Maurice Gee's moving <i>Blindsight</i> reminded me of the late 'bucket man' and helped me think better of my 'plastic bag' man. <i>(Feb 11, 2006,Dominion Post Indulgence,17</i>)

>  They called him bucket man before he kicked the bucket, dying in the gutter waiting for an ambulance. <i>(Jul 9, 2007,Salient,4</i>)

>  Few knew his name, referring to him as the Bucket Man, but everyone knew transient Robert Jones, who died on Monday. <i>(Jul 2, 2003,TV1</i>) [NOTES: recorded also on TVNZ.co.nz/view/News_national story_skin]

>  The Demon Post editorial featured the Bucket Man again as they commented on this bylaw [making it illegal to sleep in public]. <i>(Jul 7, 2003,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www. stonesoup.co.nz/incremental/archives/2003]

### bucket of bolts
 
 <i>phr.</i> a dilapidated car.

>  <i>bucket of bolts</i> dilapidated car <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,22</i>)

### bucket-conveyor
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Bucket-conveyor</i>: A chain of buckets sometimes fastened to a belt, used for elevating finely broken rock or sand at a high angle. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),313</i>)

### bucket-of-water-wood
 
 <i>n.</i> fuchsia; any tree or wood rich in sap and useless for firewood, also bucket-of-water-tree [ca1935 Marlborough (because abundant sap makes it useless for firewood)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1910,NZ Plants</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> honeysuckle, rewarewa

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Knightia excelsa (The [Maori] Honey-suckle)</i>... Maori name, <i>Rewa-rewa</i>. It is sometimes called the <i>Bucket-of-water-tree, because it is so slow of combustion</i>. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,146,1940, p. 150 ed.</i>)

>  Her ladyship started buckjumping ... <i>(1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 35]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  `Bucket-of-water-wood', <i>P. tenuifolium</i>, Maori Kowhiwhi or Maupariki - despised as firewood. ... PITTOSPORUM TENUIFOLIUM (<i>Banks & Sol</i>.) ... This small evergreen tree, known by the native names of `Kowhiwhi' and `Maupariki', abounds on the eastern coasts of both Islands. ... Even as firewood it is despised, and bears the all too suggestive name of `Bucket of Water Wood', so little is it calculated by its combustive qualities to enliven the domestic hearth. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,32</i>)

### bucketeer
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pukatea (variant bukatea)](pop-pyo.md#pukatea)))



 
 <i>n.</i> the pukatea tree

>  <b>REEVES</b> Even in the North Island the average bushman will speak of the pukatea tree as "bucketeer", and not to call the poroporo shrub a "bull-a-bull" would be considered affectation. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,404</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> Even in the North Island the average bushman will speak of the pukatea tree as "bucketeer", and not to call the poroporo shrub a "bull-a-bull" would be considered affectation. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,404</i>)

### buckjump
 
 <i>n.</i> a buck of a horse.

>  <b>BARKER</b> The process called `gentling' was a complete misnomer for the series of buck-jumps, of bites and kicks, with which [the mare] received the slightest attempt to touch her. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,233</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> I used to go to every show/ Where they ran a buck-jump ring./ The wildest moke I thought a joke,/ I really had a fling/ <i>Ibid.</i> 18 A bullock-busting sideshowman/ Who knew the buck-jump stunts./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,9</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to buck

>  <b>McKENZIE</b> Saddled my horse. She got away and buckjumped fearfully. <i>(1853,Journal,45</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Journal in 1893 Capt F.W. Mackenzie (Journal) Overland from Auckland in 1853 45]

>  I had scarcely remounted my horse before the animal began to buck-jump most violently ... <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/99</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> The faculty of buckjumping is ... almost confined to Australian horses...it was a fact that eight out of every ten horses could and did buckjump, and with many of them the vice was incurable. <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,50</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Perhaps the most recurrent and universal of rough games in the early period was <i>Cockfighting</i> (K-78). This was a free-for-all contest in which a smaller boy mounted on the back of a bigger boy and the two endeavored [sic] to dislodge all other riders by pushing or [202] pulling them over. ... Sometimes the game was known as a Piggy-back Contest or as Pulling Off. `One trick of the game was to "buck-jump" your rider, which consisted in jerking your elbows so that the rider was lifted over your head' (Naseby, Otago, 1905). <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,201</i>)

### buckjumper
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse which bucks violently

>  Clarke's horses are notorious buck-jumpers. <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,18[fn]</i>)

>  <b>INNES</b> My dear fellow, can you sit a buck-jumper. <i>(1879,Canterbury Sketches,94</i>)

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> (Griffiths Collection) <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,165</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> It was in 1875 I ... proposed to a girl, and was rejected. ... I felt as if I had been thrown from a buck-jumper, or fallen from the roof of a house - <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,28</i>)

>  <i>Buck&#769;.jump&#769;.er </i>(-j&ubreve.mp&#769;.&etilde.r),<i> n</i>. A horse given to buckjumping. See BUCK, v. i., 2, in the Dictionary. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2019</i>)

>  <b>BOREHAM</b> At Wellington he won the undying admiration of the Maoris by the way in which he rode a certain horse along the beach. At every step he was greeted with shouts of <i>`Tena korua ko!'</i> (`There you go, you and the buck-jumper!'). ... the chief ... had deliberately placed at his disposal an [92] animal of ... vicious propensities, and the natives were applauding the skill with which he was managing the worst buck-jumper in the country! <i>(1911,Selwyn,91</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> According to bush custom it was usual to dub all fresh arrivals 'new chums' until they had satisfactorily passed certain ordeals in bush life. They should be able to ride a buckjumper... <i>Ibid.</i> 50 The faculty of buckjumping is ... almost confined to Australian horses...it was a fact that eight out of every ten horses could and did buckjump, and with many of them the vice was incurable. <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,15</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> Thomas soon realized that riding a buckjumper meant more than just sticking to the saddle and allowing oneself to be jerked up, down, here, there and everywhere. <i>(1937,Thomas,66</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Years afterwards when I was up at the Cardrona down from the hills came an old white horse, a noted buck-jumper known as tinker. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,63</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> When all is fixed and the big tent up, there's a hoop-la stall, a shooting gallery, a bearded lady, a line of piebald ponies and wicked buckjumpers ... <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,100,1979 ed.</i>)

>  ~ A horse addicted to `bucking'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),52</i>)

>  <i>bucker, buckjumper n</i>. vicious horse, given to bucking or buck-jumping [orig. Australia or America]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> buckjumper <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,53</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> This is where we struck trouble - half the horses were `brumbies' [54] not used to a pack-saddle, and the pack-saddles ... were not of a class to stand up to a buckjumper determined to dump his load. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,53</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> There were just rough buck-jumpers, in an A. & P. sideshow. <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,11</i>)

>  <i>buckjumper n. Austral.</i> an untamed horse. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,141</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 91]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a low, springy bough, to straddle and ride up and down like a bucking horse (the fun was to swing up and down as far as possible without falling off). [ca1935 children's; Havelock also `Play buckjumpers' (probably suggested by Western picture craze)]

### buckle
 
 <i>v.</i> to arrest

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BUCKLE (v) Arrest. British prison slang since 1880 or earlier. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)

### buckle me
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>buckle me!</i> exclamation of frustration; a Shepard example: `Buckle me, look what I've done - packed the whole ruddy parcel and left out the card!' Obsolete C19 Australian `buckle', mood; more likely euphenism. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,22</i>)

### buckley
 
 <i>n.</i> a wet day off work [kauri bushmen]

>  The working hours are 10 per diem, and the weather must be very inclement before any stoppage of work ensues, and a 'buckley', or wet holiday, is declared. <i>(Feb 12, 1913,Otago Witness,79</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> These wet-day holidays were known as 'buckleys', but, says an old bushman, "under some contractors and most 'subbers', they were a bit hard to come by". The term "buckleys" is said to have originated from a place called Buckley's Gully, which was always heavily shrouded in mist when a storm was approaching. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,135</i>)

>  Only the very wet days - known as "buckleys", after Buckleys Gully, where the weather had traditionally always been bad - were paid days off. <i>(1986,World of the Kauri, The,94</i>)

### Buckley's
 
 <i>n.</i> short for Buckley's chance, little or no chance at all, a forlorn hope

>  The [Boxing] Association is making a `dead set' against the professional talent which has come into such prominence in Wellington of late, and the stoushers' chance of fixing up `shleinter goes' is `Buckley's'. <i>(Jul 28, 1906,NZ Truth,58/3/3</i>)

>  To oust Hereditary Ward's Government, Massey will have to hold all the seats that he has got and capture fifteen more. He has got two chances of doing this - his own and Buckleys. <i>(Aug 11, 1911,Maoriland Worker,23/8/2</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Buckley's chance</i> or<i> Buckley's</i>. No chance whatever. Does anyone know who Buckley was? <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,358</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Buckley's </i>short for <i>Buckley's chance</i>, which is no chance; after mid-C19 Australian outlaw who was reckoned to have no chance of escape, though other explanations exist; eg `We haven't a Buckley's of winning the cup now Honk's had to drop out of the team.' ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,22</i>)

### Buckley's chance
 
 <i>n.</i> no chance

>  <i>Buckley's chance, Buckley's</i>.-No chance whatever. Does anyone know who Buckley was? <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> We've Buckley's Chance of getting there first. <i>(1944,Troop Target,38</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> `You haven't got Buckley's chance.' (Buckley's chance was a colloquialism for an impossible hazard.) <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,140</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> No possibility. .`You've got Buckley's chance of doing that - it's an impossible task.' `You'll have your own and Buckley's chance of doing this.' (i.e. no chance) <i>Ibid.<i>1118 The odds against you are far too heavy. .;`Carrying heavy loads and setting out on that track at this time of the year, they got their own and Buckley's chance of getting as much as half way. They'll never be able to do it.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],465</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> I added a little more for good measure. `Borrower's Buckley's. Borrower's Buckley's.' I amplified that and made it read, `Borrower has Buckley's chance. Lender has a bad attack of memory.' <i>(1965,Rhetoric at the Red Dawn,95</i>)

>  <i>Buckley's chance ... (Phr.)</i> one chance in a million, no chance at all. Derived from name of convict who escaped into bush and survived all hazards. Used commonly in <i>haven't Buckley's chance.</i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <i>Buckley's chance n. Austral. sl.</i> no chance at all. Often shortened to Buckley's. [C19: <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,141</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 91; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 62]]

>  <b>CLAPPERTON</b> I don't suppose for a moment I have Buckley's chance. <i>(1936,Lauder Brothers,10</i>)

>  <i>Buckley's chance.</i>-A correspondent from Kirwee, the same as explained <i>Jimmy Woodser</i> for me, writes that Buckley was one of the earliest convicts (perhaps the first) to escape from Botany Bay and take to the bush. It was then thought impossible to do this and live, as apart from the problem of food and water, the blacks were numerous and savage and reported to be cannibals. Extensive search was made, but no trace was found of Buckley; and it was concluded that the natives had got him. Any other convict who talked of escaping was invariably told that he would have `Buckley's chance' - hence the saying. However, many years afterwards Buckley was found to be still living. He had managed to make friends with a small band of natives who were moving to a remote part of the country, married one of their gins, and lived their life. The convict system had by that time been abolished, so the Government granted him a free pardon. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

### Buckley's show
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of Buckley's chance

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> Personally I wouldn't give you Buckley's show against a third-form team with a couple of girls on the wing. <i>(1985,Shuriken,47</i>)

### buckrake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was Rex Paterson who invented the buckrake. <i>(Jul 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,45</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to use a buckrake to harvest hay.

>  In the 1970s, hay was buckraked and backed up on the stack. <i>(Nov 4, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

### buckreefy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>[WICKHAM]</b> It presents itself to me as a "buckreefy," "mullocky leadery" sort of country. <i>(1897,Casual Ramblings,116</i>)

### buckshee
 
 <i>n.</i> a trip to England or free return trip; a superficial wound meriting a return home; `a blighty'; `a homer' (prob from adj.). to NZ [also backsheesh]

>  <b>ANZAC</b> They [sc. the Egyptians] would sell their own daughters to the highest bidder - and throw in a wife as backsheesh. <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,52</i>)

>  The munificent nature of the Major's lavish distribution of "backsheesh" seemed to fairly take way the breath of the Committee. <i>(Feb 28, 1934,NZ Truth,10</i>)

>  Bucksheesh. The first word taught to all Egyptian babies. <i>(Jan 24, 1944,NZEF Times,III/135 : 11</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Each day the buckshees are issued quite fair, / Each man drawing his proportionate share. <i>(1987,P.O.W.,60</i>) [NOTES: Quoting Burfield-Mills, 1942]

>  <b>MILLER</b> ... nearly got a buckshee. <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,164</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Oct 14 in 1939 Miller Camps Tracks and Trenches 164]

>  Many men would have given five years of their life to have got my `buckshee'. <i>(Dec 7, 1917,Great Adventure,109,1988 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Gray)]

>  So-and-so got a bakesheesh [sic] (slight wound). <i>(Jun 22, 1917,NZ Free Lance,11</i>)

>  "Py corry, the baksheesh!" Interpreted, this meant that Hori was going to have a free trip to Blighty. <i>(Sep 7, 1917,NZ Free Lance,11</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> A `buckshee' was all very well, a neat little hole through the arm or leg, with a trip to `Blighty' at the end of it ; but some frightful smash ... was another thing. <i>(1927,Auckland Regiment,155</i>)

>  He may have even been lucky enough to get an N.Z. Buckshee. <i>(Aug 2, 1918,Christchurch Girls' High School,V/49 : 8</i>)

>  They missed the "buckshee" which means Blighty for a change. <i>(Aug 16, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/50 : 31</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (something) free of charge; in NZ <i>baksheesh</i> rare: in a context of gifts of food, etc. at Maori hui recorded in

>  That's why you get it buckshee. <i>(Jan 25, 1943,NZEF Times,II/83 : 5</i>)

>  I'll subscribe to your buckshee paper.. Johnny Enzed and what he has said... <i>(Oct 16, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/173 : 5</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> The quantity ... of the `backsheesh' varies according to your rank. buckshee, usu. as aj, popularised in New Zealand by WW1 <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,70</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> ... buckshee cocoa at the Y.M.C.A. <i>(1933,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,138</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Sep 18 in 1933 Miller Camps, Tracks and Trenches 138]

>  ... she comes nosin' round for bukshee bully-beef. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,56</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Many tins of our meat and jam have gone out [to the villagers] as `buckshee.' <i>(1922,Happy Wanderer,32</i>)

>  There is a species of male known locally as a `Queenie' who is talking to a Y.M.C.A. girl and making me sick so I must close. A [250] queenie wears its hair brushed straight and kept in position with Anzora Cream. It talks with a vile affected accent like the Diocesan girls and crawls round the women for buckshee trips. <i>(Jan 22, 1919,Great Adventure,249</i>) [NOTES: 1919 Jan 22 Great Adventure (1988) 249-250 (Hutton)]

>  From Arabic came `buntock,' a rifle, and `moira,' a drink; from Egyptian, `buckshee' and `disaster' for piastre. <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>LEE</b> `I'm broke. Can't afford necessities, let alone luxuries. Unless you want to stand treat for a pauper? Buckshee?' She hurried away. ...Another New Zealander overstayed of [sic ?on] leave came to cadge a few bob as I walked down the street. <i>(1976,Soldier,6</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> ... nearly got a buckshee. <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,164</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Oct 14 in 1939 Miller Camps Tracks and Trenches 164]

>  A man on his own could just scrape along.. if he had the use of a buckshee bach. <i>(1960,Mate,5/39</i>)

>  [Song sung in WW1 about an unpopular sergeant : ?orig. from Tommies, but changed] Coolness under fire! C--- u--- f!/ We've got a `Buckshee' sergeant/ And he's never seen a gun./ He's mentioned in dispatches for drinking drivers' rum,/ And when the Huns are strafing you should see the --- run./ C--- u--- fire! <i>(Feb 1919,Quick March,19</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Yes. Buckshee. No Payment. Not a cracker. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,144</i>)

>  It'd probably be "buckshee". <i>(Dec 2, 1953,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  One did not ride on his train "de baldo" - or as we should say, "buckshee". <i>(May 1936,NZ Railways Magazine,29</i>)

>  He counted out the handful of copper coin by coin, "and a d buckshee," he said. <i>(Nov 9, 1920,Otago Witness,56</i>)

>  Not a bad description for a "baksheesh" journalist, eh? <i>(Sep 15, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/2 : 48</i>)

>  The N.Z.Y.M.C.A. provide backshee cocoa, biscuits and coffee for the wounded, and a "counter lunch" for the staff. <i>(Apr 24, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/42 : 130</i>)

>  I had the distinction of having one of the first "buckshee" cups of tea and cakes from the modern kitchen. <i>(Jul 21, 1941,NZEF Times,I/4 : 8</i>)

### buckshot
 
 <i>n.</i> a settlers' term for granulated lava (always imbedded in sandy alluvium). [1851 (Morris), then coll.; Morris (459) quotes <i>Australasian Quarterly</i> (?not spec NZ) nf W3.]]

>  `Buckshot' was the early settlers [sic] word for granulated lava; ... <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. shrapnel](sho-spa.md#shrapnel))

In case you were wondering what your adolescent daughter was really trying to tell you: Gs means girlfriends, budget means of less than acceptable quality and extras refers to people who butt in on conversations uninvited. <i>(Dec 5, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### buff
 
 <i>v.</i> to paint over (graffiti)

>  In some cases restorative justice processes are used, whereby offenders meet the offended against and agree on how to remedy the offence - usually by painting over ("buffing" in hip-hop talk) the offending signature... Angry residents, neighbourhood groups, church groups, councils and community service agencies throughout the country are buffing away daily in the belief that, if you paint over a tag within 24 hours, the tagger will eventually give up on that particular wall. <i>(Jan 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  "Yeah. I was really, really buff," he says. "It is such a great way to see the country." <i>(Nov 13, 2002,Capital Times,16</i>)

### buff weka
 
 <i>n.</i> a yellowish-buff subspecies of weka, Gallirallus australis hectori , taken to the Chatham Islands in the early 20th century to avoid extinction. (See [weka](../W#weka),[buff](bri-byo.md#buff))

>  Lake Wanaka's buff weka have thrived so successfully since their transfer to Te Peka Karara Island (Stevenson's Island) in Lake Wanaka from the Chatham Islands in 2001 ... <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,21</i>)

>  The buff weka is a mischievous bird, inquisitive, noisy and with a sense of humour. It interacts well with humans. The buff weka roamed the east coast of the South Island and Central Otago but was a victim of predators and became extinct on the mainland in 1920. <i>(Dec 13, 2001,Southland Times,A11</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The weka extended both into alpine and lowland tussock country, especially the sub-species (the `buff weka') occupying the eastern South Island. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/207</i>)

>  <i>134.c. Gallirallus australis hectori</i> (Hutton, 1873) <i>Buff Weka</i> South Island ... now probably extinct on mainland. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,42,1980 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### buffalo chips
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>VEITCH</b> Waggoners used wood substitutes for fuel for cooking during the journey from Central to Dunedin. `Buffalo chips' (dried dung), `yellow pine' (straw), or `kaladdies' (the flower and stem of flax) were burned in a can which was hung behind the waggon. <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,17</i>)

>  <b>GOWANS</b> Her dirty fingers poked into the embers her flax flowers, dead grass and dung, which she called her `yellow pine' or `buffalo chips' or the melodic-sounding `Kaladdies'. <i>(1985,Heart of the High Country,35</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sl.] dried dung, used as fuel in the South Island where no wood was available.

>  <b>ANGUS</b> On the treeless terraces of the Upper Clutha, the wagoners' campfires burned with such unusual fuels as 'buffalo chips' or dried dung, 'yellow pine' or straw and 'kaladdies' or flax stalks. <i>(1981,Aspiring Settlers,61</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>adj.</i> (informal) (of weather) bad [ca1930 (but probably considerably before this date: Rey Byrne) Scots shepherd: North Otago station `It's coming up buffy-guffy']

### bug
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a crayfish

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> No other explanation would satisfactorily provide the reason why the man, with a wild cry of `Grab me bloody bug!', ignored the man ... After all, ... the big red crustaceans are never desperate enough to perform vertical leaps of three feet ... <i>Ibid.</i> 78 `Eight pounds of bug down his [sc. the cat's] furry cakehole! Shell and bloody all. ...' <i>(1981,Fish Factory,77</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a name for authorities, ?teacher, ?prefect [1953 14 M A9 Thames DHS 24 BUG]



 
 <i>n.</i> a plastic raft

>  The bugs, developed and made in New Zealand, are basically inflatable armchairs - a hard plastic seat surrounded on three sides by flotation chambers. They're manoeuvrable, easy to carry, and can go down streams impassable to big rafts. <i>(Nov 29, 1999,Evening Post,19</i>)

### bug-nest
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The count [of prisoners of war] is hardly over before the boys are back in the `bug-nest', where so many hours can be passed - if you're a good sleeper. <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,4</i>)

### bugabuga
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pukaha](pop-pyo.md#pukaha))

### bugger
 
 <i>n.</i> [person, vernacular]

>  A "Good Bugger" gone too soon. Thanks Mate for the wonderful times and friendship; you'll be sadly missed. <i>(Sep 21, 2010,NZ Herald,B8</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> In various forms and expressions it is as widely used in NZ as elsewhere, and not popularly regarded as an obscene, nor even by many a coarse, word. Often used familiarly or friendly-like, but not as often in this sense as `(old) bastard'; cf OED: and OEDS In low language: A coarse term of abuse or insult: simply `chap; customer; fellow' (cf Baggage) Leic Gloss 1881 `this little bugger'; see esp. P: for various forms, and an interesting note; some expressions which are not (or not well) attested elsewhere follow. There is no claim that they are exclusively, in even predominantly, New Zealand English. [Used as a catchphrase in 1999 following a TV advertisement in which it was the only word used in the sound track.]

>  Paul Schwabe complained to TVNZ that the use of the word "bugger" breached broadcasting standards which require the obesrvance of good taste and decency... The Authority declines to uphold the complaint. <i>(Jul 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  <i>The Ultimate Encyclopedia of Rugby, The Rise and Rise of Australian Rugby, Rugby Facts and Fun for Kids,.. Bugger Rugger: Love in the Scrum</i> ... nah, that last one's not real. <i>(Aug 20, 2003,Capital Times,13</i>)

>  I've recently been heard to laugh at and praise the originality of a TV ad. Fittingly, its catchphrase could well be adopted by the government as its campaign theme : bugger. <i>(Mar 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  The bugger bandwagon rolls on, with everyone from T shirt makers to local advertisers capitalising on acceptance for swearing in the Saatchi and Saatchi Toyota ad. This week, Masterpart Brake & Clutch joined in with a blackboard sign commiserating the start of the week. "Bugger, it's Monday", was accompanied by a drawing of a depressed non-smiley face. <i>(Apr 28, 1999,Independent (Auckland),20</i>)

>  Helen's still leader. Bugger. <i>(Apr 1999,Double Standard</i>) [NOTES: Undated billboard]

>  The "bugger" advertisement is a phoney, and those supporting it are pseuds and posers, anxious to be seen as liberated. People don't say "bugger", they say "bugger it." <i>(Mar 30, 1999,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Cheeky, of course, is what his rounded face is; little is what his height is; and bugger, as we know, is a word coined by an advertising agency meaning "a serendipitous accident". <i>(May 2, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  The bugger disease spreads... A pest control owner's new Toyota ute mysteriously lost its new signwriting this week. The replacement word? You've got it: Bugger. <i>(Mar 20, 1999,Evening Post,60</i>)

>  Buggeur! All Blacks gutted by semi-final shocker. <i>(Nov 1, 1999,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  It was all a bit much, coming back after a couple of refreshing weeks out of the country just in time to watch the All Blacks lose to France. Zut alors. Or, as those among us who can no longer bear to speak French prefer, bugger. <i>(Nov 20, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

>  "Kia Ora, good evening and bugger!" Carol Hirschfeld. <i>3 News</i>, after the French beat the All Blacks. <i>(Jan 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

>  ACT NZ leader Richard Prebble said that.. "bugger" was surely in general enough use that it could be quoted [sc. in Parliament]. It was even used to sell motor cars - a reference to the beloved "Bugger!" ads for Toyota, which had made a television star out of a huntaway-cross, now known as "The Bugger Dog." <i>(May 21, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  ...it's the ultimate Toyota "bugger!" moment. <i>(Aug 9, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  We have been honoured to have been a part of your life. You taught us all right to the end. The last lesson was a reminder not to lay down for any bugger. <i>(May 4, 2010,Dominion Post,C11</i>)

>  Last time I spotted [Sergeant] Dan he was still grinning, just, but he had a tear rolling from his eye. Buggers. <i>(Apr 20, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  He was one of the funniest yarn-tellers I've encountered, It's a bugger. He's gone. <i>(Dec 21, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  I only subscribe to one magazine a year, but I think I might have chosen the wrong one. Bugger - I've just renewed my subscription. <i>(Sep 15, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  The company swiftly admitted that the hackers had exploited " a known security issue for which a patch exists" in the company's web-server software.. As we say in Kiwiland: "bugger". <i>(Feb 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),39</i>)

>  "We're looked on as hicks, us farmers. Look at the 'bugger' ad - it was a good laugh, but it was at the expense of farmers doing stunts they wouldn't dream of. <i>(Mar 14, 2003,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

>  "We're looked on as hicks, us farmers. Look at the 'bugger' ad - it was a good laugh, but it was at the expense of farmers doing stunts they wouldn't dream of. <i>(Mar 14, 2003,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

>  "Bugger", 2000: because it was funny. The ad for Toyota's 4WD wasn't the first time the word was said on TV. On election night 1996, PM Jim Bolger clearly said, "Bugger the pollsters." <i>(Sep 1, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  There was bound to be the odd "oh bugger" moment. <i>(Mar 1, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

>  The Society for the Promotion of Community Standards got grumpy and sought a re-classification, but the new R18 handed down by the Board of Review had no additional audience restrictions at all. Bugger. <i>(Jun 1, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  [Caption] Hercules, the "bugger" dog shows off his skills for his new ad campaign... The bugger dog is back - and helping the <i>Sunday Star-Times</i> mow down the opposition. <i>(Aug 17, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

>  Another Bledisloe Bugger was in the offing with the Wallabies only a try from winning when George Smith scored in the 74th minute. <i>(Aug 17, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A1</i>)

>  Yet the canning of the Vector float is still one for the "bugger!" file. <i>(Aug 24, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> (coarse slang) =?

### buggerise
 
 <i>v.</i> a quasi - euphemism for <i>bugger</i> in senses: v.t. `ruin'; v.i. `mess about, procrastinate'. [known to me [DEH] from the late 1930s; ca1935 (HWO)]
