>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>buggerise about</i> aimless messing about; eg `Stop buggerising about and get on with the game, will ya.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,22</i>)

>  So while they buggerise round with their building blocks,he gets set to leave. <i>(Jul 9, 2003,Contact (Wgtn),19</i>)

### buggerlugs
 
 <i>n.</i> a term of slightly irritable endearment to children, not felt as offensive or impolite; [ca1920 (probably well before)]

>  Advertisement for Smith and Brown A woman says (excitedly): `These old buggerlugs at Smith and Brown are offering ...' <i>(Apr 5, 1986,TV1</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> The Brigadier was Brigadier, sir. Alas.. in the smoky privacy of the bunkhouse, these two grand people became Bugalugs and Ma Bugalugs. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,142</i>)

### buggers afloat
 
 <i>n.</i> dumplings, often containing raisins and cooked in a camp oven [also known as doughboys and in the US, hush puppies]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I make what my family calls "B-----s Afloat", but we'd better call them: <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,122</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Doughboys, that's what they're called, but we called them buggers afloat. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,89</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> We'd make dumplings, too, fried up in the camp oven. Buggers afloat we called them [c1916] ... And what did we call the poached eggs? A pair of bastards on a raft, and we'd have tits on toast - belly pickled pork on toast. I still use those old names; everyone laughs at me. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,29</i>) [NOTES: (Bill Henderson)]

>  <b>Patricia Grace</b> While my cousins and I kept the fires going, brewed tea, cooked beggars-that-float and made sandwiches, the games went on. <i>(1998,Baby No-Eyes,254</i>)

### buggers-on-the-coals
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>buggers-on-the-coals</i> damper, with currants preferably. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,22</i>)

### buggy
 
 <i>n.</i> Orig. a US borrowing for a usually four-wheeled vehicle; thence any light passenger vehicle, as distinct from the main, light freight vehicle, the express wagon: applied (often humorously) to motor vehicles, before the term was specifically adopted in the 1960s for e.g., beach-buggy. loosely and humorously applied to any wheeled vehicle, whether horse-drawn or not

>  The old American buggy that used to be in N.Z. was the best carriage for rough ground I ever knew. You cannot get one now. <i>(Oct 16, 1907,Chudleigh Diary,443</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> And oh, the glad sense of relief when she heard the noise of the buggy growing fainter and fainter down the long road. <i>Ibid.</i> 223 There was not an inch of room for Lottie and Kezia in the buggy. When Pat swung them on top of the luggage they wobbled; ... <i>(1912,Stories,120,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> They climbed into a four-wheeled buggy drawn by two horses, which a bright-faced cadet (who had failed his army examinations and had been shipped oversea) had driven down the day before. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,310</i>)

>  <b>VAILE</b> <i>Buggy</i>. An antiquated vehicle for light transport - usually to carry four persons and drawn by two light horses. <i>(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,299</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> My father always drove an Abbott buggy, and would go right across country in this conveyance, ... [fn] American four-wheeled buggy built of hickory wood by a man named Abbott. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,164,1954 ed.</i>)

>  We had what I think was [290] called a `station wagon' [on our Otago station], or four-wheeled buggy; the <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,289</i>)

>  <b>HOGAN</b> Days of Billy-can and Pikau,/ Horse and Buggy, sleet and snow,/ Railway camp and crowded `Boozer',/ <i>(1962,Billy-Can Ballads,1</i>)

>  <b>PRESTON</b> `Mother and father, we three children ... travelled in convoy in two buggies, a light one with small, fast horses and a large `express' pulled by half-draughts. <i>(1978,Woolgatherers,104</i>) [NOTES: [Reference 1901]]

>  <b>BOYD</b> A buggy became the status symbol of a rangatira. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,10</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> ... a buggy <i>(1861,Reminiscences</i>)

>  Thomson came in buggy <i>(Jun 1, 1862,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [Hocken TS]]

>  In January, 1862, and again in February and March, buggies are mentioned, and this adjunct of advanced civilisation (for those times) is referred to every now and again in the remainder of the diary. <i>(1862,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: c1862 Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 113]

>  We started in the buggy at 3 and had a pleasant drive up ... <i>(Mar 18, 1867,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/236</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> A horse and buggy. <i>(1869,Comic Guide to Dunedin,5</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> There was Mr. Allen with a very nice pair-horse buggy built on the American principle. <i>(1874,By Sea and Land,172</i>)

>  <b>INNES</b> [The bullock-dray] put me in mind of old times, when buggies, carriages and dogcarts were unknown, and we were content to ride in a bullock-dray. <i>(1879,Canterbury Sketches,42</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> It was before the days of buggies. At any rate, buggies or vehicles of any [118] sort were a rarity. The journey had to be accomplished in a bullock-dray. The station bullock-driver was, like all bullock-drivers, addicted to swearing. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,117</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW</b> Our `buggies', a generic name in the colony for every description of vehicle on springs short of the `express' wagon, were carefully overhauled. <i>(1888,New Zealand Of Today,81</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> ... they had hired for the journey a four-wheeled trap, called a buggy ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,39</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> <i>Buggy</i>, originally an American expression for a specially-constructed four-wheeled vehicle, is in the colonies often applied to every sort of light open trap; it almost answers to the Irish <i>`car',</i> which is by no means confined to the jaunting-car of the sister-island. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,170</i>)

>  ... the buggy, a peculiarly Colonial contrivance, most convenient for going through rivers, ... <i>(Jan 28, 1890,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1890 Feb 28 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 76]

>  <b>WILSON</b> The buggy, a peculiarly Colonial contrivance, most convenient for going through rivers. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,76</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> BUGGY: Light type of four-wheeled vehicle, horse-drawn. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,185</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 185]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> You can then find Dr Hamilton, get a buggy or trap of some kind, ... [p.216, Smithyman fn 2] Buggy. American buggies had four wheels, English or Indian had two. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,56,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Barren Reefs, or Buck Reefs.</i> ... In Otago they consist of very wide, massive, bold outcrops of `hungry' glassy quartz ... <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/81</i>)

### building consent
 
 <i>n.</i> an approval required from the territorial local authority to carry out building work, including construction, alteration and demolition (Building Act 1991) [Jim Cameron writes: This term replaced the <i>building permit</i> issued under the Local Government Act 1974 and its predecessors.]

>  The building consent, formerlv building permit, is familiar to anyone who erects or alters a house. It does not appear in dictionaries. In Britain it is subsumed under the term planning permission. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a section specifically for building (usually a house) on. [ca1945]

>  VALUABLE BUSH AND BUILDING SECTIONS. <i>(Dec 15, 1852,Taranaki Herald,1</i>)

### Buildings, the
 
 <i>n.</i> the wooden (former) government buildings in Wellington.

>  I ... found the Premier wanted a private secretary. When I got to the Buildings, I was told Sir Cornelius was busy. <i>(Sep 11, 1911,Triad, The,45</i>)

>  A prominent feature ... is the massive pile of buildings which forms the central location of the Civil Service of the Colony. `The Buildings', as they are locally designated, have the appearance of being thoroughly substantial and permanent in character, and it is not a little surprising to a stranger, and destructive of his appreciation, to find that the ponderous edifice is constructed of wood. It is certainly claimed for the structure that it is the largest wooden one of a permanent character in the world, but that is a distinction which, even if it is deserved, is one perhaps of questionable honor. <i>(1883,Bishop's Guide to Wellington,22</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

>  Duncan's flock buisted and ear marked. <i>(Apr 30, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  Naturally Scoticisms are prevalent, but only one or two will be noted here. On April 30, 1859, we read: `Duncan's flock buisted and earmarked,' and thereafter the word `buisted' is often employed. I understand it is an old Scots term for raddle or for marking with tar, etc., or for any marking which was not burnt in by the process invariably called branding. <i>(Apr 30, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1859 Apr 30 Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 97]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In connection with stock the following terms were used: - `Let the sheep draw down to the Taieri'; `stags,' `crawlers,' `stragglers,' `a horse with couples in his mouth,' `the bull baked on the road,' `cut and tailed,' `lagoon washing,' `spaying cattle,' `killed a pleuro,' `herding lambs,' `buisting weaners,' `a camp for the cattle,' ... <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,85</i>) [NOTES: [1858-1869] Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 85]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to brand sheep or cattle. [[hist. OED]]

>  <b>MURISON</b> Drafting and buisting weaners. <i>(Mar 1866,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: Puketoi Station Diary March 22-23]

### Bulgarian bug
 
 <i>n.</i> a yoghurt starter

>  <b>SARGESON</b> And over in the kitchen they ... were all set to go; but Mrs Anderson was trying to remember if there was anything she had forgotten. Yes, to put some fresh milk on the Bulgarian bug. Though it could wait. [note 215] ... - bacillus bulgaricus, the special yoghurt culture. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,215,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> When the great Bulgarian Bug craze hit the town I connected it with Mr Kostoff; this was something secret too. Kept in a covered jar in the darkest corner of the kitchen safe. smelling of sour milk, the Bug seethed with a life of its own - that sinister mould held mysteries, of that I was more than sure... Now I wonder if it wasn't some kind of fertility cult the women built around the Bulgarian Bug; what they did with it I never knew. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,89</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>adj.</i> many, a lot [From <i>N.Z. English to U.S. English Dictionary</i> at http://nz.com/NZ/Culture/NZDic.html]

As soon as the requisite condition is obtained the leaf should be stripped from the stalks, graded, tied into hands and bulked down .. a room for bulking down should be thoroughly dry, clean, warm and not subject to sudden changes of temperature. <i>(Feb 20, 1930,NZ Journal of Agriculture,135</i>)

### bulk funding
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes : This term is in DNZE in relation to the education sector, with a 1991 first citation... The pre-history of <i>bulk funding</i> may be worth examining.]

>  According to an independent legal opinion of the bulk-funding contract, bulk-funded schools which had incurred outstanding debt, or hired more staff than they were entitled to, or had surplus staff requiring redeployment, would not be allowed to return to the centrally resourced system. <i>(Feb 1999,North & South,16</i>)

>  The report stated that bulk funding was implemented in New Zealand primary schools from 1878 until 1901, and in secondary schools from 1903 to 1920, but both systems were rejected because of the inequities they created between schools and their staffs and between teachers in particular schools. <i>(Nov 1992,PPTA News,5</i>)

>  The input of the Government's decision to cut the budget for day relief teachers and distribute it to schools by bulk funding is now being felt in the schools. <i>(Apr 1992,PPTA News,1</i>) [NOTES: There is a discrepancy in volumes numbers and dats between the two PPTA quotes which needs checking]

>  A long term bulk funding to research by government. <i>(Aug 1993,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,93</i>)

>  Bulk funding is taking its toll. <i>(Mar 28, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),13</i>)

### bulkie
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of "bulk tonnage operator" [King (24/3/1999) says bulk tonnage operators were firms which developed from the 1960 in reaction to the then regulations limiting road competition with rail. These firms consolidated small lots of goods into wagon lot, thus creating "bulk" goods (from the Railways perspective) out of much smaller units. Their workers did not in fact "load bulk goods" - it was the result of their working that the goods became "bulk". Such firms still exist; their current formal name is "freight forwarders" although one hears bulk tonnage operatore or "BTO" occasionally. Examples include Mainfreight and Daily Freight... Given that the expanded name of "bulk tonnage operator" is some thirty years older than [your] example, I would be surprised if its contraction is not also somewhere recorded earlier than 1991.]

### bulky piece
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a class of wool that is sold in short pieces, unattached to a fleece, similar to crutchings.

 <i>n.</i> a New Zealand Railways tanker

 <i>v.</i> to brag; to deceive with exaggeration or boasting. [ca1935]

 <i>n.</i> (historical) a five-shilling piece; a crown [[OED slang: crown piece (cf BULLS-EYE 11) 1812; P: late 18-19C.; W3.]] (See [half-bull](h-hea.md#half-bull),[half a bull](h-hea.md#half-a-bull))

>  [?wharf-loafers'] -->five shilling piece <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,153</i>)

>  Following are other local money-names ... 5s. piece - <i>caser, K </i>or <i>bull</i> ... <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,(Red Page)</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from Loafer, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> Crown [:] dollar, bull, `oxford scholar' <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,151</i>)

>  The crown was called a<i> bull</i>, a<i> cartwheel,</i> a <i>caser, five bob</i>, and most familiar of all a <i>dollar,</i> the latter dating from the time when the rate of exchange was four US dollars to the pound. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a big old male whale

>  <b>GRADY</b> <i>Bull</i> - A big old male whale. A juvenile bull is a young whale that has not reached maturity. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,226</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> short or euphemistic for bullshit [incl bulldust, similar euphemism]

>  A lad rang up and said he detected a distinct odour of "bull". <i>(Nov 24, 1938,NZ Observer,9</i>)

>  The farmer just said, 'Bulldust. Southlanders always do well in the north.' <i>(Jul 24, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  No bull, and very little moolah. .. No freebies, no gimmicks, no bull. Take a test drive at your local Holden dealer today. <i>(May 12, 2010,Horowhenua Chronicle,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> product of water that is put into an empty spirit cask, to draw the spirit from the wood. [OED online: 1824 J. D. COCHRANE Journ. Russia & Tartary 225, I could do nothing but bull the barrel, that is, put a little water into it, and so preserve at least the appearance of vodkey.]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> I found the fellow, dead drunk, with the bung hole of the cask down, and a can half full of `bull', a term used when water is put into an empty spirit cask, to draw the spirit from the wood. <i>(1871,Autobiography,ch v/16</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  In the first place they do not use enough dynamite to `bull' the holes which are from 18ft to 20ft. ... At Sydney Street one permanent hand who draws 3 [pounds] a week wet or dry, `bulls' the hole then puts on his coat and inspects the scenery; <i>(Jun 22, 1907,NZ Truth,105/5/5</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The contractors ... knew their gelignite inside out, ... to know to what extent it was wise to `bull' the end of a deep bore-hole with one or more comparatively light untamped charges so as to form a chamber deep in the rock into which could be loaded the heavy, concentrated charge that would break out the living rock in jagged blocks ... <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,191,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> ... hand-drilling continued to be used in the stopes to avoid wetting the fill and it was customary to bull these holes, that is to enlarge the bottom of the hole by firing a small charge in it to make room for a larger [192] charge, ... <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,191</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a regular 'lumper' in the shipping industry, as it was (aka royal)

>  Each foreman had his regulars, known as "bulls" or "royals" ... <i>(1993,Wharfie,p12</i>)

### bull artist
 
 <i>n.</i> [also bullshit artist, bulldust artist]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He's got no bloody culture. ... The real bull artist. ... Walking into [204] the shops with his blah blah salesmanship. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,203</i>)

>  bull artist n. braggart, blowhard, empty chatterer. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  <b>NORGROVE</b> Now I know you're a bull artist. <i>(1987,Shoestring Sailors,125</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bull artist </i>boaster and/or charlatan; eg `Don't believe a word he says. That joker's a bull artist from way back.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,22</i>)

>  `Professor' William Boyd, as he once styled himself, has thus reached the high point of his career in the ranks of the ... professional bulldust artists. <i>(Apr 4, 1961,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Whoever coined that was an unmitigated fuckwit, a bullshit artist supreme. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,170</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> artist person with notable aspect defined by preceding adjective; eg booze artist, bull artist. NZA originally, now general. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,11</i>)

### bull bar
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a strong protective steel frame on the front of a utility vehicle. (See [also bullbar](bri-byo.md#bullbar))

>  Whoever forms the government, MMP needs to go up on the hoist for a serious tune and lube, and that flashy five per cent bull-bar needs to come off. <i>(Oct 8, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>) [NOTES: figurative]

>  <b>KERR</b> My truck has strong bull bars and if you don't shift I'll go straight through your yards. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,164</i>)

### bull beef
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I helped at cows ... etc etc while all my men in Waitangi dressed to the eyes, swilling and swagering like bull beef. <i>(Feb 11, 1889,Chudleigh Diary,366,1950 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an uncircumcised male cattle beast bred esp. for marbled meat.

>  ..despite the forbidding contour they've established a thriving bull beef operation. <i>(1988,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Feb/Mar 21</i>)

>  Taking bull beef production from flat land up into hill country can be done successfully but requires additional farming skills. <i>(1988,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Feb/Mar 24</i>)

>  Farm manager wanted for an intensive bull beef farm. <i>(Mar 16, 2002,Dominion,47</i>)

>  Part of the cost of running bull beef has been an elaborate electric fencing system with outriggers on all fences and gates. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 7</i>)

>  From vegetables to bull beef, Palmerston North farmer Paul Argyle runs a smooth operation on his Kairanga/Bunnythorpe property. He's farming a 190 hectare mixture of private, family-owned land and leased land with a mixed enterprise of potatoes and squash with bull beef as the backstop. <i>(Aug 31, 2001,Dominion,Supp 27</i>)

### bull birch
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i> [(Central Otago)]

>  <i>bull birch n, NewZeal</i> : BEECH 4 <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,293</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i> <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen)]

>  <i> [Notho]fagus fusca</i>,Hook. f. ... Tooth-leaved Beech. Black Birch of Auckland and in part of Otago and Southland. Black or Bull Birch of Lake Wakatipu. Red Birch of Wellington, Nelson, and in part of Otago and Southland. It is not so easy to see why any difficulty should have occured in the identification of this fine timber-tree apart from the misleading tendency of the common names generally applied. The thin yet firm texture of the leaf, the prominent veins, the sharply-toothed margins, are characters that can only be confused with those afforded by a careless observer. Yet merely owing to the use of common names based upon colour, and applied or rather misapplied to the leaves, bark, or wood at the fancy of the bushman, no species has been more misunderstood. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/300</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

>  Bull-birch, of southern lakes district. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,125</i>)

>  <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/307</i>) [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner) [Otago] see cit. 1 b. above.]

### bull break
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence across a gully or other natural boundary.

>  <b>STACEY</b> I was left at Tarndale to keep an eye on a lot of young cattle we had put above a bull break in the Wairau. I was guided by fences out to the Wairau River from where the bull break was easy to locate. 1996. ibid. 92 We then mustered the head of Rainbow back-boxing the two mobs and putting them above the bull break before riding to Tarndale. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,91</i>)

### bull harvester
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person or apparatus used for the collection of bull semen for artificial insemination.

>  More sensational. perhaps, in the AI field is the mysterious work of the 'bull harvester' .. I'm not sure I'd recognise a bull harvester doing the same, and that's worrying. <i>(Apr 13, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

### Bull in the Ring
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  Bull in the Ring required strength; King Caesar, a joyous cunning game. <i>(1978,History of Drummond & Gladfield,42</i>)

### bull kelp
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Durvillaea antarctica</i> Rimurapa; <i>Durvillaea utilis</i>, rimurapu; a large brown seaweed found on exposed rocky coasts.

>  Now a heavy mass of bull kelp was wrapped around the line, dangerously adding more strain. <i>(Jan 4, 1933,NZ Tablet,12</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> Bull kelp.. was cut into two-foot lengths, more or less, punched open down the middle with the hand to form a pouch, into which the whole fish was slid and a stone placed in the opening to keep it in, then placed on hot stones and embers to cook. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,143</i>)

>  [species name] kelp, bull .. [scientific name] <i>Durvillea antarctica</i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  As a child I found the idea of falling off rocks into surf transfixingly awful. It was something to do with the idea of being pulled below by the writhing tangle of bull-kelp octopus tentacles. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/37</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> And then there's rimurapa, bullkelp, Durvillea antarctica, a huge, magnificent plant that makes undersea forests round most of the southern coasts. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,61</i>)

>  <i>Durvillaea utilis</i> <i>(1909,AJHR,C-12</i>) [NOTES: (And)Cockayne Botanical Survey Stewart Island]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Durvillaea utilis</i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The Giant *Bull Kelp (<i>Durvillaea utilis</i>) is a truly Antarctic sea-weed. <i>(1929,NZ Nature Book,II.i [OED2]</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> The [mutton-bird] containers are made of bull kelp (<i>Durvillaea Utilis</i>), which grows in profusion on the rocky fringes of the islets. <i>(1940,Rakiura,208</i>)

>  <i> bull-kelp</i> A very large kind if seaweed on the New Zealand coasts. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),53</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Seaweeds of the intertidal belt, Stewart Island, here consisting mainly of Bull-Kelp (<i>Durvillea</i>), Strap-weed (<i>Xiphophora</i>), Moss-weed (<i>Bostrychia</i>), Neptune's Necklace (<i>Hormosira</i>), and <i>Sea Bombs</i> [sic](<i>Adenocystis</i>). <i>(1961,Flora NZ,Flora 4 Fig.1.,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Durvillea antarctica</i> (Cham.) Har. Common name: <i>Bull kelp </i> Maori names: <i>Rimurapa, rimuroa</i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,21</i>)

>  Other edible varieties of seaweed commomly used by the Maori include rimurapu or bull kelp (Durvillea antarctica), which was also dried and then boiled with tutu juice to form a jelly, and the beaded Neptune's necklace (<i>Hormosira banksii</i>), which is palatable raw. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,9</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> She threw off her clothes, got in through the heaving bull-kelp, swam out and brought the boat ... back to safety. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,151</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Durvillea antarctica</i> BULL KELP ... Rimurapa ... Native to: New Zealand. Also right round southern hemisphere except Tasmania and Australia. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,307</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Out on line by door two bull-kelp bags blown up and drying. Under the table finished pohas stacked. Birds in kelp bags with totara bark wrapping ... <i>(Apr 20, 1952,In the Grip of an Island,153</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Alternatively, it [tutu juice] was boiled with the pith of the pitau fern or pieces of bull kelp seaweed (<i>rimurapu</i>). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,6</i>)

>  Thick leathery bull kelp (Durvillea antarctica) grows from saucer-sized holdfasts at the water's edge. Its brown whip-like forms frame the rocks: a floating, writhing fringe extending seven metres from shore. <i>(Feb 14, 2004,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

>  Pre-European Maori harvested these penguins for food, along with flightless birds and seals, and transported them back to camp by slitting open giant bull kelp, stuffing it with the booty and sewing it up again. <i>(Jun 2003,Directions,No. 48/52</i>)

>  A huge wave rolls in from the Southern Ocean, splaying the giant bull kelp that floats on the surface like spaghetti, and explodes against the cliffs. <i>(Mar 6, 2006,Press, The,B7</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> He took part in the landing through the bull-kelp of Auckland Island. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,113</i>)

>  The meat was packed in bags made of a sea-kelp, known.. to the pakeha sea-farer as "bull kelp". <i>(Sep 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> The accepted tale that he [sc the Moriori] caulked the seams of his crate-like affair ... with `rim-rap',' bull-kelp, and used inflated bladers if this material ... is, to put it mildly, tosh! <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 363</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 363]

>  Our other big common seaweed, the bull kelp <i>(Durvillea antarctica)</i> is very low in potash salts ... <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/150B</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> The bull-kelp lay stranded on the rocks ... <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,21</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> All that bull-kelp is a death trap. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,84</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [cf. bullhead, bully bull-trout, bulltrout] (See [cf. bullhead](bri-byo.md#bullhead),[bully](bri-byo.md#bully))

>  <b>TANCRED</b> There is also a bull-trout [in fresh water] and shoals of smelts. <i>(1856,Natural History of Canterbury,16</i>)

>  A spotted fish - called `bull-trout' - seldom weighing more than half-a-pound, and averaging much less, is found in clear streams ... <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,32</i>)

>  [Kingfishers] descend ... to the ... sunny flats on the Grey River, subsisting on insects and small bull-trout `or bullys' until the arrival of the whitebait. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/215</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> We also obtained a number of the large New Zealand bull-trout (Galaxius alepidotus), which provided us with a good meal. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,162</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> In fresh-water rivers and streams, a fish resembling the bull-trout is to be met with at time, and which rises readily at a bait. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,49</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### Bull's country
 
 <i>n.</i> John Bull's country: Britain, esp. England

>  Some of the people who come from Bull's country to this place were born tired, they cumber the earth suffering with that tired feeling, and are too tired to die. <i>(Jul 30, 1910,NZ Truth,266/6/1</i>)

>  This sort of thing is a bad investment for New Zealand, and as we are threatened with an invasion of fifty girls, and another 200 boys, it is up to the Executive to close the door to the refuse of Bull's country. [re: importation of British boys for farm labourers]. <i>(Feb 4, 1911,NZ Truth,293/5/8</i>)

### bull's eye
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of marble

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies ... <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a type of cake

>  <i>BULLS' EYES</i> ... butter ... icing sugar ... flour ... cornflower [sic] ... B.P. ... 2 eggs, roll into balls, press out flat, make hole in middle, fill with jam ... bake ... <i>(Sep 1934,Marigold Book of Recipes, The,27</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> butter (sts ? `milk'). [ca1910-1912 R.C. Nelson College Boys' slang]

### bull-a-bull
 
 <i>n.</i> [Anglicisation of Maori poroporo.] <i>Solanum aviculare </i> (occas. <i>S. Nigrum</i> black nightshade, a common weed); poroporo (`a branched leafy shrub') or its berries, [also poroporo -->[1919 Jul 29] Ver. (WB).= Vernacular names used by settlers bulabul Solanum aviculare supplied by W. Best, Otaki (And) -->[a1927] Ver.= Vernacular names heard by J.C. Andersen from sawyers, settlers, etc. (And) bullybul <i>Solanum aviculare </i>] (See [also poroporo](pop-pyo.md#poroporo))

>  We came across many bushes of buli-buli, which is a plant something like the cape gooseberry, but its fruit is twice its size. <i>(Sep 14, 1927,NZ Tablet,37</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> Even in the North Island the average bushman will speak of the pukatea tree as "bucketeer", and not to call the poroporo shrub a "bull-a-bull" would be considered affectation. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,404</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> `And as for fruit, the place is full/ Of the delicious bull-a-bull.' <i>(1845,New Zealand (various authors/titles),217</i>) [NOTES: 1845. `New Plymouth's National Song,' in Hursthouse's `New Zealand,' p. 217:]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> [bulli-bulli] <i>Solanum aviculare </i> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> bullibulli] <i>Solanum aviculare </i> <i>(1906,Introductory Classbook of Botany</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> [reference Wellington 1850s] ... we could often get a good feed of ripe pura pura (mostly called bulla bull) berries. <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,18</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in Outlook in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 18]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Solanum aviculare </i> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Solanum aviculare </i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The bul-buls shone in their brown sheaths <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,321</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bull-A-Bull; Bullybul. Poroporo</i> (a flowering shrub): NZ: 1845 (Morris). <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,104</i>)

>  <b>PATRICK</b> Alongside it ran a clear stream, on the banks of which wild fuchsia and bulbuls grew luxuriously. <i>(1990,From Bush to Jubilee,12</i>) [NOTES: Quote from TAINE 1840]

>  The inconspicuous scented tufts of the green clematis mingled with the purple blooms and big yellow berries of the bulibul. <i>(Dec 16, 1897,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  How many readers know the native shrub, with its dainty blue flower, nasty-smelling foliage and bright orange berries - purupuru by name but commonly called bullabull? <i>(Mar 11, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,40</i>)

>  <b>HIGGINS in LE PROU</b> We ate bull-a-bulls flour, fuchsia, red pines, tetoke. <i>(1989,Waters Back,17</i>) [NOTES: Citation from 1842]

>  <b>TANCRED</b> [Near the sea coast] also grows a shrub, called by the natives bulbul (Solanum aviculare) with handsome, deeply-cut leaves, and bearing a yellow fruit which is eaten. <i>(1856,Natural History of Canterbury,21</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Hapuka in every tide;/ And as for fruit, the place [Taranaki] is full/ Of that delicious bull-a-bull. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/217</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> bull-a-bull. The common way of pronouncing Poro-poro, a solanum producing an edible berry. <i>(1861,New Zealand (various authors/titles),209 (Taranaki) [fn]</i>)

>  [bulli-bulli] <i>Solanum aviculare </i> <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Solanum aviculare </i> <i>(1890,Descriptive & Priced List</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Solanum aviculare </i> <i>(1896,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bull-abull,</i> or <i>Bullybul, n. </i>a child's corruption of the Maori word <i>Poroporo</i> (q.v.). a flowering shrub of New Zealand. It is allied to the <i>Kangaroo-Apple</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,62</i>)

>  Hapuka in every tide;/ And as for fruit, the place [Taranaki] is full/ Of that delicious bull-a-bull. <i>(Oct 14, 1846,Wellington Independent</i>) [NOTES: 1846 John Hursthouse's Taranaki Song printed (so H.Fildes) in the `New Zealand Spectator' and the `Wellington Independent' 14 October 1846]

>  ... borra borra. <i>(1844,Report of Select Committee on NZ</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> [Song in honour of New Plymouth] And as for fruit, the place is full/ Of that delicious bull-a-bull. <i>(1853,Emigration to NZ,111</i>) [NOTES: 1844 July 15 in 1853 Hursthouse Emigration 111]

>  bul-bul <i>Solanum aviculare </i> <i>(Jan 1856,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal</i>) [NOTES: Jan Tancred Notes on Natural History Canterbury in Edinburgh New Philos. Journ.(NS) (And)]

>  Slang words of Maori origin were once common, but most of them are dead and dying [and include] ... `cockabully' (kokopu) `bullabull,' a shrub (poroporo), `biddybiddy' (piripiri), ... <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PORO PORO (<i>Solanum aviculare </i>), also called bullibull (a corruption of the Maori name), and potato berry. It is found commonly on the bush edge in lowland districts throughout both islands. It is a perennial leafy spreading bush 4 to 8 ft. high, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,152,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Solanum aviculare (The Poro-poro) </i>... The plant is often called by the settlers, the Bulli-bull. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,389</i>)

>  <i>poroporo</i>, a plant (Solanum aviculare) with beautiful large blue flowers and large orange-coloured berry eaten by boys and birds: often corrupted to bullybul. S. nigrum is raupeti. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>bullibul, bullibulli [=] poroporo</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,9</i>)

>  The raw material for the drugs will be extracted from the shrub <i>Solanum aviculare </i>, known locally as poroporo or bulli-bulli. <i>(Oct 13, 1977,Dominion,1/1</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ~ poroporo<i> Solanum </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>SOLANUM LACINIATUM.</i> Poroporo (bullibul) is a soft-wooded shrub ... Two species are called by one Maori name. Two Latin names, <i>S. laciniatum</i> and <i>S. aviculare,</i> are both more than a hundred years old but it was only in 1954 that Baylis defined clearly the difference between the two species and then the two names came into general use. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,168</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Solanum aviculare </i> Forst.f. Common name: <i>Bullibulli </i>Maori name: <i>Poroporo, kohoho </i>... Some authors have identified `poroporo' as S. nigrum [Black Nightshade], a smaller, weedy plant, and it is possible that the Maori did not distinguish the species. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,93</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i> Solanum laciniatum</i> POROPORO ... Bullabulla <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,77</i>)

>  <i> bull-a bull n. NZ</i> = POROPORO. [Anglicized form] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>v.</i> bulrush, raupo

### bullbar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  'Bull Bars' should be renamed 'Pedestrian Bars'. <i>(Nov 1997,AA Directions,38</i>)

### bulldog
 
 <i>n.</i> kingaseeny

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> It was [at Blockhouse Bay school c1950s] Kingaseeny (or Bulldog, King Dick or Bar-the-Door for those outside Auckland) at lunchtime, rugby on Wednesday, the gang after school, ... <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,25</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  But may I suggest that in a future edition [of the <i>Collins English Dictionary </i>two more New Zealand words which are associated with deer hunting be included, namely net -gun', and the verb `to bulldog'. <i>(Apr 5, 1987,Dunedin Star Weekender,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MONKFISH </i>(a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i> (ii) <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (both) Whitefish. (c) Other common names: (both) Monkfish, Monks, Stargazer, Flathead (also applied at times to export gurnard); Churchills, Toebiters, Bulldogs, Spotted Stargazer, Maori Chief (Dunedin); Muddies. (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier, Cook Strait, Nelson, Banks Peninsula, Bluff-Stewart Island. ... Monkfish is a general term for species of the Families Uranoscopidae and Leptoscopidiae. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,37</i>)

>  [species name] stargazer .. [scientific name] <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> .. [other name(s)] monkfish, boofs, bulldogs, churchills, flathead monk, giant stargazer, monks, muddies, spotted stargazer, toebiters, whitefish, sandcod <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Monkfish Kathetotoma giganteum </i>... Also occurs in southern Australia. Known by a number of local names: giant stargazer, flathead, bulldog, boof, etc. The name monkfish is well established for this species in New Zealand, whereas overseas it is used for similarly shaped but unrelated anglerfishes. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [helicopter deer capturing] to leap from a helicopter on to a deer and capture it with a rope..

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> Jeff Carter of Te Anau bulldogged sixty-seven live deer in two days when jumping for Jim Kane. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,57</i>)

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> Mau Bradley has just bulldogged a live red deer on this steep ridge in the Kaimanawas. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,46</i>)

>  <b>JANES</b> Stitch was the only one awake, so he climbed out on the skid, bulldogging the first, roped it and hefted it aboard, then rode the skid till I caught up with the second. He bulldogged that one too. <i>(1994,Great New Zealand Hunting Stories,264</i>)

### bulldogger
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [helicopter hunting] a person who leaps on to a deer from a helicopter.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> On another trip, capturing fallow, Joe had two bulldoggers, Mau and Stitch, and three deer handlers. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,129</i>)

### bulldogging
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the jumping from helicopters to capture live deer.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> While all this bulldogging was going on, and thousands of deer being captured ... <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,59</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> bulldogging: Catching wild deer by dropping on them from helicopters. <i>(1998,David McGill's Complete Kiwi Slang Dictionary,23</i>)

### bulldozer
 
 <i>n.</i> bull nose holder

>  <b>I. Davey (Whangarei)</b> BULLDOZER is an implement still used to hold a bull by its nose. It probably has a more genteel name, but I knew (and used) them 40 years ago by this name before the term for a tractor-powered grader came in. <i>(Jul 29, 1966,Letter</i>)

### bulldozer dribble
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) small stumps and fragments of timber that pass under a bulldozer blade or roll off the blade end.

>  <b>FERRIS</b> Bulldozer dribble - small stumps and fragments of timber that would pass through under the bulldozer blade or roll off the blade end. <i>(1940,Cavalcade of Farming 1840-1940,71</i>)

### bulldust
 
 <i>n.</i> euphemism for <i>bullshit</i> lies, rot, nonsense; boasting - as a noun or as an exclam. (<i>Bullswool </i> is another variant; the politest is the innocuous Bovril; cf. also bull, heifer dust).

>  <b>BENNETT</b> bullswool <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91</i>)

>  `Professor' William Boyd, as he once styled himself, has thus reached the high ... point of his career in the ranks of the ... professional bulldust artists. [?enter as se ARTIST 2] <i>(Apr 4, 1961,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  <i>bull/bulls wool/bull dust:</i> rude term for disbelief in a statement; e.g., `He spoke a lot of bulls wool.' <i>(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> bulldust nonsense <i>(1984,Blackball 08,242</i>)

>  <i>bulldust</i> nonsense or reaction to the serving up of such; polite version of `bullshit'; eg `I'm sorry, but you're just talking absolute bulldust.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,22</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> Ah, bulldust, father, bulldust <i>(1984,Blackball 08,99</i>)

### buller
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a lowcut lace-up gumboot [usually worn by down land farmers.]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bullers</i> gumboots, a brandname for a low-top, lace-up variety popular in bush country but often scarcely recognisable by the makers after have been cut down further to lighten them up, had the laces replaced by wire and had holes made in them to let the water drain out. [<i>G. Johnston</i>] <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,17</i>)

### Buller's albatross
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) .

>  <b>Buller's albatross</b> <i>Thalassarche bulleri</i> This species breeds on The Snares and Solander Id at the western end of Foveaux Strait. Although many have been killed in fishing operations, the population is increasing. <i>(2007,NZ Geographic Poster,No 84 Mar-Apr</i>)

>  The lives of three Bullers albatrosses hang in the balance after they were found covered in oil near Solander Island, in Foveaux Strait .. Solander Island is one of only two breeding sites for Bullers albatross in the world. <i>(May 21, 2002,Dominion,3</i>)

>  .. the annual incidental catch in recent years has been dominated by 5 taxa: white-capped albatross, sooty shearwater, white-chinned petrel, Buller's albatross, and Salvin's albatross. <i>(Feb 2007,Niwa Update,23/1</i>)

>  .. it may, for example, have contributed to an increase in numbers of Bullers albatross. <i>(Nov 28, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

### bullet
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>FRIGATE TUNA</i> (Frigate mackerel)<i> Auxis thazard</i> ... The frigate tuna is a slender fish and is the smallest of the tunas found in the New Zealand region ... They are called bullets by tuna fishermen and can cause a great deal of trouble to purse seiners working skipjack schools [by gilling themselves in the net and taking time to remove]. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,293,1984 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [1980 [what is the reference for this] In a washing machine in the laundry, detectives discovered two `bullets' - a term given to cannabis in a transparent wrapping. Twenty-seven more `bullets' were in a preserving pan hanging from the kitchen ceiling.]
