### bulley/bullie
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bully](bri-byo.md#bully))

### bullhead
 
 <i>n.</i> a freshwater fish (?Morris <i>Eleotris gobioides</i>); ?bull-trout; ?bully @n; ?cockabully.

>  There are only three fish in the River Avon, sufficiently common to justify the supposition that either of them may be the adult form of the Whitebait; these are the Bull-head, the Silver-fish, and the Smelt. The name of Smelt is applied indiscriminately to the two latter fish, we will ... retain the trivial name of Silver-fish as a distinction. We may first dismiss the Bull-head ... It is an Acanthopterygious fish ... Head, large, one third of the length of the body ... [86] The Silver-fish again belongs to the <i>Malacopterygii abdominales</i>, or fishes having soft fin rays, with the ventral fins placed below the abdomen. ... We now come to the ... fish ...called, by boys, the Smelt; but why a Smelt, I know not. ... the Smelt is a Galaxia ... <i>(1875,TrNZI,2/85</i>) [NOTES: (Powell)-]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The very common and widely distributed broad-headed or upland bully is <i>Philypnodon breviceps</i> ..., which probably inspired the original name of `bull-head', later shortened to `bully' and applied to the whole lot. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,228</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BULLEY Probably our most common freshwater fish the Bulley is also known as the Bullehead [sic] and is a basic staple diet of our trout. ... Several varieties of Bulley exist in New Zealand including the Blue-gilled, Common, Hutton's, Large and Upland varieties. <i>Ibid.</i> 28 BULLHEAD: <i>see Bulley </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,24</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 45. ELEOTRIS GOBIOIDES. C. and V. Bull-head. <i>(1872,Fishes,29</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> BULLHEAD (<i>Eleotris gobioides</i> ). <i>Ibid.</i> 302 <i>Eleotris gobioides</i> C. and V. Bull-head, or Hawai <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,129</i>)

>  Several varieties of smelt (<i>Retropinna</i>) called by the Natives `inanga', ...; bullheads of different sorts, and a slimy fish the `kokopu' (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>) the young of which are supposed to be the New Zealand `whitebait' are widely distributed ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,431</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bull-head </i>... (2) a fresh water fish of New Zealand, <i>Eleotris gobioides</i> , Cuv. and Val., family <i>Gobiidae.</i> see <i>Bighead. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,63</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The bully, or bull-head, and the kokopu, or native trout, live entirely in fresh water. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,74</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Hawai ... 4. Eliotris gabioides</i>, bull-head; <i>a fish. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,50</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BULLEY (TOITOI) <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> (Cuv. and Val.) The bulley is one of the most abundant of our native freshwater fish. ...The Bulley, or Bullhead as it is known in some parts, forms an important food for the introduced Trout throughout New Zealand. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,318</i>)

>  <i>bull-head n</i>. small Tasmanian and S. Australian shark; New Zealand freshwater fish. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <i>bull-head n.</i> small Tasmanian and S. Australian shark; N.Z. freshwater fish. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  `One of the backbone', as they love to call themselves - especially when they are bulldozing Governments for a helping hand - named R.J. Taylor, of near Sefton, was charged at the Rangiora Court with sooling dogs on to an insurance agent, using indecent language, and assault. <i>Ibid.</i> 56/4/3 But this must not be permitted by the people, who have been too long bulldozed and bamboozled, humbugged and hocussed by the plutocratic penny prints of this city. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/1/7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the introduced catfish

>  Further research had found the catfish - brown bullhead - were not likely to colonise open water, where trout food was produced. <i>(Jan 26, 2004,Dominion,A4</i>)

### bullhead shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [while term is universal for the family of heterodontiformes, in NZ it refers to the NZ heterodontiforme species Port Jackson shark; one ref]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, bullhead 136-7 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### bullock
 
 <i>v.</i> tackle a job (like a bullock); go bull-at-a-gate at [cf. bullocking 1950 at least used of energetic forwards in rugby union: e.g. Three forwards bullocked their way past the twenty-five and on for a try.] (See [cf. bullocking](bri-byo.md#bullocking))

>  <i>bullock ... 2. v.i. NZ & Austral. colloq.</i> work strenuously. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,93</i>) [NOTES: [Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 144]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>- to bullock</i> is still regarded as acting belligerently, and rugby forwards, in commentator's parlance, <i>bullock over </i>for tries. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,22</i>)

>  We should make it a condition that [assisted immigrants] be strong and willing to go up-country and bullock hard. <i>(Sep 9, 1905,NZ Truth,12/8/3</i>)

>  The first Rubgy test [was] one in which the heavier New Zealand forwards ... `bullocked' their way to victory. ... A penalty try ... was awarded for obstruction to Stanley who was racing ... for the ball, which had been kicked over the Blacks' line. <i>(Sep 11, 1922,Quick March,20</i>)

>  Just keeps bullocking, like the rest of us. <i>(1950,Landfall,14/125</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton)]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> In mine and threshing-mill he had his day/ Bullocking, a strong back and a weak head/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,80</i>) [NOTES: (1949)]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Webfoot Charlie bullocks the dead Cornishman's body to the side of the sleeping Dane, ... <i>(1982,Collected Plays,15</i>) [NOTES: 1956, (Jack Winter)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The Ruamahanga forwards bullock into the ruck and Keith Maxwell, given the ball, takes a snap pot at goal ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,217</i>)

>  When they got back to the farm Chris had arrived. He heard them ... and saw, bullocking in through the gate, two 650s, red, and orange, on one a Maori ... riding out of any bikie saga ... <i>(1973,Islands,5/272</i>) [NOTES: Gifkins (Winter Riders)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Bullock</i>.- Never called oxen in New Zealand, whether working bullocks or otherwise. <i>Bullock-dray</i> was a rough cart with two wheels and a pole, to which the bullocks were attached by yokes over their necks, and a chain. It was usually drawn by a team of six or eight. <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The bull-head, or bully, which is a sluggish fish, resting mostly on the bottom, or lurking beside or under stones, or among the weeds if present. It can be easily caught in cupped hands ... speckled gray or blackish - brown, sometimes washed with yellow, sometimes almost entirely black. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,301</i>)

>  Transport, by packhorses or bullock, was the chief problem... After recovering the GBP50 paid for the "bull," the brothers made about GBP50 a month. <i>(Jul 27, 1939,NZ Centennial News,27</i>) [NOTES: Quote from WALKER 1862/63]



 
 <i>v.</i> to shift, haul etc. with bullocks (See [cf. bullocking](bri-byo.md#bullocking))

>  <b>REED</b> Here Caldow and Malone worked 6 million feet, which was bullocked, and then trammed, to the mouth of the Kaimarama River near the old booms. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,255</i>) [NOTES: [?ca1900]]

>  <b>WHEELER</b> I ask Bert about transporting [kauri] logs. `The [Kauri Timber] Company bullocked the kauri [79] logs to the streams, and the logs were floated out from trip dams on the Pungapunga.' <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,78</i>)

>  It seemed as though the horses would be unable to "bullock" the heavy vehicle through the mud. <i>(Aug 20, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  The seed has to be packaged on horses and carried even by men to tracks, where a sledge will take it to the main road, to be bullocked to the different bays for transport by steamer to Lyttelton or Dunedin. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> Here comes Yankee George, a-bullocking his horses along, - he's a card, an' no mistake. <i>(1877,Colonial Travel,307</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a rugby forward

>  <b>FANNING</b> Forwards may be roughly classified into bullocks, sheep, goats, and men. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,8</i>)

### bullock bench
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MALFROY</b> For small operations where the expense of a Pacific carriage or travelling bench is too ambitious, a good ... breakdown bench can be built locally on the principle of the `Bullock' bench which has been used in some parts of New Zealand for the last fifty years. <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,19</i>)

### bullock cart
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACKIE</b> D[illon] at one time farmed his own land in the Waimea and was in the habit of driving his own bullock cart into Nelson. The bullock is the general and almost only vehicle in this district. It is a two wheeled cart with a pair of bullocks; occasionally we see 4-6 or 8 bullocks yolked [sic] according to circumstances. <i>(Apr 25, 1853,Traveller under Concern,102,1973 ed.</i>)

>  Went to Merton with young Sartin & his bullock cart & brought back a load of board for shutters to keep Maories out. <i>(May 29, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/590,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> Along the banks of the Waikato we met now the endless trains of bullock carts, pack-horses and horse drays going to the front for transport service during the grand move of the General. <i>(1864,Memoranda,83</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Every able-bodied man was speedily at work ... and soon a hundred bullock-cart loads of timber were on thespot selected for the post. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/166</i>)

### bullock dray
 
 <i>n.</i> a usu. four-wheeled vehicle, with a pole rather than shafts, drawn by a team of bullocks; a rough two-wheeled cart to which bullocks were attached by neck-yokes and chains (see Kennaway <i>Crusts</i> illustration), (See [cf. bullock wagon](bri-byo.md#bullock-wagon))

>  The river was very high ..., having got over that you had to walk some way and then get into a bulock dray and cross a branch that people at home [sc. England] would look at a long time ..., the bullocks sticking up in the middle each time. <i>(Nov 4, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,203,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>J G WILSON</b> Linton over in the township and ordered a Bullock Dray seven feet by five feet, for Body, three inch axles and four feet eight wheels, four inch tires with an Iron Bark pole running right through the Dray. <i>(May 4, 1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 May 4 J.G.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 188]

>  The Government Bullock Dray left Maketu. <i>(1870,McLean Papers,33/197</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> On the road we overtook one of our bullock-drays taking wool to the coast: the driver had taken down behind the dray a strong ... stock-horse, ... to be exchanged ... for one of ours. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,168</i>)

>  <b>INNES</b> [The bullock-dray] put me in mind of old times, when buggies, carriages and dogcarts were unknown, and we were content to ride in a bullock-dray. <i>(1879,Canterbury Sketches,42</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> By a pleasant fiction these [early Dunedin roads] were called dray roads; ... they reverted ... to ... hopeless bogs. This was attested by the embedding of many a confiding bullock dray, which ... remained for weeks in its new resting place. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,111</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> The traffic of winter-time, when horses and bullock drays moved along a track apparently metalled with cream cheeses, ... <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,1,1971 ed.</i>)

>  Besides using boats on the rivers some of the settlers soon acquired bullock drays. <i>(1943,St Michael's Angl.Ch.,8</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... Gilbert decided to send Mike ... , as he had the bullock dray already loaded ... to take over to the out-station, and both ... thought that Mike could be trusted to keep the boundary in the mean time; ... <i>(1881,Waitaruna,144</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> In this high country he met some of the original selectors - men who had pushed out into the unknown in bullock drays and had seen tent give way to mud or stone hut, and that replaced by the long, low wooden homestead, with its deep verandah, its big, comfortably furnished rooms ... <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,261</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> ... John had taken himself, his Herefords, and his family off into the bush twelve miles away. Nemesis herself might have pitied Caroline (who had come in by bullock-dray to have a tooth out), ... <i>(1938,Promenade,86</i>) [NOTES: [hist]]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> He has made a rough track to this rough home by smoothing one or two extra rough bits and sloping off a few creek banks to allow bullock drays or sledges to get through. Sledges were the [34] common vehicle of the earliest times, followed by bullock drays, and then, after the diggings broke out, by the long-bodied, four-wheeled bullock wagon and also by horse-drawn drays and wagons with greatly increased accommodation. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,33</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The bullock-dray, used instead of a waggon, has a pole, to the end of which the yoke is fastened. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,447</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> At 9 Blanche and I met the Bullocks Dray at the College. <i>(Oct 15, 1850,Auckland Journals,25,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Bullock drays are in general use, horses are not yet plentiful in this settlement. <i>(Mar 29, 1853,Traveller under Concern,94,1973 ed.</i>)

>  There are other carriages very interesting to me - bullock-drays with their teams of ten oxen and loads of groceries waiting to finish cargo ... <i>(Mar 31, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/146,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Richmond)]

>  <b>PAUL</b> [Station Terminology] ... a bullock-dray with a team of six bullocks. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,88</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,viii/128</i>)

>  Sent Bullock dray to Stotburn with fowls <i>(Sep 12, 1865,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> By means of a bullock-dray families could sometimes be brought to church, ... <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,15</i>)

>  <i> ... Bullock-dray</i> was a rough cart with two wheels and a pole, to which the bullocks were attached by yokes over their necks, and a chain. It was usually drawn by a team of six or eight. <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Andrew drove a bullock-dray for Chapman. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,54</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Before the French settlers arrived at Akaroa in August, 1840, the creak of a bullock dray and the laughter of men and women had been heard on the Canterbury Plain. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,43</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> They [the horses] had to be good, for the road over the plains was nothing more than the wheel marks left by early bullock drays. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,15</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> [hist] When I first came out I was taken in a bullock dray ... to a place out of Wellington. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,38</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> Many sheep, and later packhorses and bullock drays, were taken through this track to the Wairau until it became almost impassible. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,52</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... we'd all have to go to school on the bullock dray. It was a great big square, open thing; they'd put seats across it and away we'd go. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,34</i>)

>  The space around the grain stores in Tyne street presents a busy appearance from day to day. On Wednesday we counted no less then 26 bullock and horse drays, and 6 or 8 horse wagons waiting their turn for unloading. <i>(May 1874,Oamaru Times</i>) [NOTES: 1874 May Oamaru Times in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 155]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> There is at last some attention being paid to the roads about here. Up to now, there have only been bullock-dray tracks. A contract was recently taken ... to do some side-cutting on a road through uncle's run. <i>Ibid.</i> 62 ... they travel thither by road - which is a bullock-dray track from the Waihora boundary - past the homestead, ... <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,38</i>) [NOTES: c1870s (post-1872)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On one of my bullock-dray trips ... I was accompanied by another dray driven by Bill Familton ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,139</i>)

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> For miles and miles now there was an unbroken stream of soldiers, bullock-drays, artillery, packhorses and orderlies meandering over the plains and fern ridges of the sacred Maori delta. <i>(1864,Memoranda,88</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... it is commonly reported that a heavily-laden bullock-dray could cross it in perfect safety. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,74,1870 ed.</i>)

### bullock horse
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The bullockies invariably took their hack, known as the `bullock horse' with them, ... These horses were tied on behind the drays by a neck-rope, and dragged along if they were not inclined to go willingly. Sometimes, when the dray was being driven through low scrub, the bullock horses would get some nasty knocks from the stems flying up and hitting them. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,156,1954 ed.</i>)

### bullock janker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> I can even remember way back to seeing the bullock jankers go by here, taking the logs to the mill. <i>Ibid</i> 34 If it was fine but muddy he'd send a man to take us in the old bullock janker; it was just too [sic] big wheels with a long pole and two bullocks, one each side, hooked on with great big yokes around their necks. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,10</i>)

### bullock jarker
 
 <i>n.</i> [[F. Jim Robb : also used somewhere later in the book: ?=jerker.] [May be janker: check tat omitted at janker.]]

>  <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,34</i>) [NOTES: 1981 Christine Hunt Speaking a Silence 34]

### bullock language
 
 <i>n.</i> the practical language of command used by bullock-drivers to bullocks.

>  A tall young man ... but he takes to a blue serge shirt and `Way! Woap!' with bullocks in a manner not to be enough commended. <i>(Dec 1, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/105</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> Nearly all the drivers used the same bullock language, giving the command always in the same words ... [180] <i>wo</i> `halt'; <i>back</i>; come here; [183] <i>gee</i>; hoit. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,188</i>) [NOTES: [D.J. McLeod]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [1889 (but still referred to in a non-historical, non-obsolescent way in Truth]

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> If the English sailors have acquired a not undeserved reputation for their curses, surely the New Zealand bullock-drivers must be reckoned superior to them in this accomplishment, for such an assortment of fantastic oaths from all kingdoms of Nature, Heaven and Hell I have never heard before from mortal lips. Astounded I listened to this blue-eyed son of Albion and was at last impelled to ask him why he cursed so horribly; it could serve no purpose and only made the beast more stubborn. `You don't understand, Sir,' the young man answered. `I'm only speaking the bullock language. The animal certainly wouldn't understand or obey me if I spoke to him as I would to my horse. The bullock has been brought up to carry in this way, and if I want to get on fast, I've got to talk to him like this.' To this argument advanced by the driver with a good-natured smile I naturally could find no reply. I refer it to the linguists to enrol this new language in the appropriate family of dialects. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,36</i>) [NOTES: [c1860 <i>Weiner Zeitung</i> (Vienna) Nos 57-65 in German] in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 36]

>  <b>HART</b> Bullock drivers are seldom choice in their language under the most favourable circumstances, but to drive a team with a load of firewood ..., from Papanui to Christchurch, was a work of so much difficulty, that the flow of bullock language was more than ordinarily profuse [March 1852]. <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,27</i>)

### bullock team
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>IRELAND</b> During this time he worked for his tucker on a bullock team, as a goad-boy. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,26</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The first day was devoted to a ploughing match, for bullock-ploughs and for horse-ploughs. ... only eleven ploughs were on the ground, seven of which were bullock-teams ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,247</i>) [NOTES: [quoting 1847 Mar]]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Next day we yoked up two of our bullock-team, and hooking them on to a rough sledge, started early to cll at our two depo[circumflex]ts of fresh pork. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,155</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Mr. Hamlin ... was ... engaged with a bullock-team, in removing goods from the farm house ... <i>(1879,Waikato War,55</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> A bullock-team at this moment stopped at the inn-door, the driver entered, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,1/3/41</i>)

>  Willie Dix and bulock team arrived in the morning and Martin with horse team in the afternoon. <i>(Feb 14, 1884,Chudleigh Diary,326,1950 ed.</i>)

>  In the south, rough tramways are laid down in the bush, and the logs hauled on low carriages to the mills by horse- or bullock-teams. here the great round baulks of timber are `broken down' being cut lengthwise (by saws moving up and down vertically) into `flitches', which are passed over to the circular-saws to be ripped into boards and scantling. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,158</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> They [grasshoppers] actually stopped a bullock-team this week. The big-winged fellows kept flying into the faces of the leaders ...; and the `bullocky' had to take his team back to the homestead and unyoke them. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,34,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1872 in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 34]

### bullock teams
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Another play unknown at a later date was the widespread Bullock Teams: It was easy to make a team of working bullocks from pieces of supplejack, with their opposite branches doing duty as horns. The manufacture of yokes, substitutes for chains and model sledges gave a boy the pleasant feeling that accompanies successful achievement. Leading the team by a piece of string was the next step. (1870; Waipu) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,64</i>)

### bullock tie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WHEELER</b> [caption] Logging pan for dragging logs, bullock tie (or wishbone) used on the yoke, and in front a logging dog that grips the log to prevent it from falling off the logging pan. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,34</i>)

### bullock track
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rough pack track suitable for pack-laden bullocks.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The homesteads of the stations thus created were connected with the port of Napier either by sea or by rough bullock-tracks following the lines of old river-beds ... <i>(1921,Tutira,121</i>)

>  The bullock track was via the old Lake road and some of the swampy creeks were a terror to cross. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,3</i>)

>  <b>MCRAE</b> .. for the coming-and-going to some of the outlying blocks there would of necessity have to be a bullock track. <i>(1963,By the Braes of Balquether,31</i>)

### bullock wagon
 
 <i>n.</i> a four-wheeled vehicle drawn by bullocks (See [cf. bullock dray](bri-byo.md#bullock-dray))

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... bullock-waggon <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,18</i>)

>  ... A <i>bullock wagon</i> was much the same as a bullock dray, but had four wheels and was drawn by 10 or 12 bullocks. Bullocks were also driven in sledges (two or four bullocks). <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>HYDE</b> `A man has to settle down some time. How else did all this grow? Our mother came here by bullock-wagon, when she was eighteen.' <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,144,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> He has made a rough track to this rough home by smoothing one or two extra rough bits and sloping off a few creek banks to allow bullock drays or sledges to get through. Sledges were the [34] common vehicle of the earliest times, followed by bullock drays, and then, after the diggings broke out, by the long-bodied, four-wheeled bullock wagon and also by horse-drawn drays and wagons with greatly increased accommodation. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,34</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> Bullock-wagons were still in use when I was a boy, but the noises I heard the drivers make were certainly not music. <i>(Jan 20, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,13,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> It was customary, as at Waipiro Bay ..., to have a wool store and a dump press near the beach. The dumped wool was taken by bullock wagon into the surf, transferred to surfboats, and rowed out to the ... steamer lying offshore. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,10</i>)

### bullock whip
 
 <i>n.</i> [also bullocky whip (1960 below)]

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> The whip was rawhide plaited sinnet, about six feet long, thick at the upper end, where it was fastened to a handle as long as itself, but tapering nearly to a point, to which was attached a narrow strip of rawhide, called `the fall,' finishing off with a twisted `cracker' made of `weetau' or dressed flax in [43] place of whipcord, and was a powerful flagellator in the hands of an experienced `bullock puncher.' <i>(Sep 19, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Sep 19 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 42-43]

>  <i>Bullock whip</i>.- Whip used by bullock driver. It was made with a very long handle (perhaps eight feet) and a long lash. `Come here' and `gee back' were the words the driver used to turn the bullocks left and right, accompanied by a crack of the whip on the opposite side. (C.) <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In driving bullocks the best bullock-whips were made from the skins of plump wild sows - these skins pleated [sic: ?plaited] well and lasted well. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,154</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The art of cracking a `bullock-whip.' <i>(1940,Te Waimate,139</i>) [NOTES: [Reference 1870s-80s]]

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> `Bullocky' whips were not so long in the leather as stockwhips, but had very long, flexible handles, ... <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,142</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> This necessitated much expert use of the bullock whips, and not a little sulphurous language. <i>(1966,In the Lee of the Hokonuis,295</i>)

### bullock yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sturdily-built yard on large stations where wild bullocks and horses are mustered and broken.

>  <b>CHINN</b> With every rider now concentrating on his duties, we drove the wild horses towards the station bullock yards where several decoy horses were quietly feeding. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,74</i>)

### bullock yoke
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> I yoked up the bullocks. The bullock yoke was a square piece of white pine smoothed and hollowed out to fit the necks of the two bullocks, with holes for the iron bows which went round the neck and through the yoke, being fastened above with a key tied with a bit of greenhide; in the centre a bolt with a ring called `the start' passed through the yoke, to which the chain was fastened from the sledge. <i>(Sep 19, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Sep 19 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 42]

### bullock's kai
 
 <i>n.</i> raurekau, <i>Coprosma australis</i>

>  <b>KELLY</b> [The eels] are then covered with the broad leaves of the raurikau (or bullock's kai as it was known by the early settlers). <i>(1892,Journey Through Waitara,3</i>)

### bullock-chain
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> There was a rattle of the yokes and bullock-chain as the sledge tried to follow, ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,155</i>)

### bullock-drawn
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> ... now a generation has arisen to whom a bullock-drawn vehicle would be as strange as a Roman chariot. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,41</i>)

### bullock-driver
 
 <i>n.</i> one who drives a bullock-team; bullocky, bullock-puncher; also swear like a bullock driver.

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> It was before the days of buggies. At any rate, buggies or vehicles of any [118] sort were a rarity. The journey had to be accomplished in a bullock-dray. The station bullock-driver was, like all bullock-drivers, addicted to swearing. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,117</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Bullock drivers who handle from 12 to 24 animals are also employed [in the sawmills]. ... A good `Bullocky' is like a good shepherd, his voice is seldom heard. ... The saying ... to `swear like a bullock driver', is common. <i>(1913,Country Work,40</i>)

>  ... she heard Bloomfield say that Price was a dirty bullock-driver. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8/4/14</i>)

>  ... bullock-drivers, stockmen and carpenters. <i>(1846,McLean Papers,3/226</i>)

>  Thursday week we met to the number of ninety-five visitors, besides bullock drivers and some Maoris ... <i>(Dec 1, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/104,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  ... you have the Canterbury bullock-driver before you. <i>(Aug 22, 1857,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> As soon as maybe after his arrival, [the new settler] engages himself as a shepherd, or bullock-driver, or hut-keeper at a station, ... makes a hut of sod or wattle and dab, ... encloses [his land] with ... a post and rail or ditch and bank fence. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,27</i>)

>  McGill bullock driver ... All hands washing Humes ewes. <i>(Nov 29, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  Shennan's and Black's drays came with four bales wool. Bullock drivers taking a spell.' <i>(Oct 23, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1859 Oct 23 Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 102]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> I am informed that the having faithfully learned the ingenuous arts, has so far mollified his morals that he is an exceedingly humane and judicious bullock-driver. <i>(Mar 24, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,4/50</i>)

>  Built grate in fireplace; put up bunk for bullock drivers. <i>(Jul 1, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1861 July 1 Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 78]

>  <b>WALKER</b> (Central Otago) I am bullock driver, although it is hard work I prefer it to selling [in a store]. <i>(Jan 4, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  We are a good party here, two shepherds and one bullock driver, gentlemen's sons, Tennant a cadet, he used to be at Harman's. <i>(Jun 20, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,93,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Thousands of sheep now depasture on that well-remembered corner of `the plains,' on those gently-swelling downs; ... we have the bleating of flocks, the bark of the colley[sic] as it rounds up its charge, the loud crack of the stockwhip, the hearty curse of the bullock driver delivered `ore rotundo'; ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/49</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <i>April</i>, 1861 - It is Sunday. We rose later than usual. There are five men sleeping in the hut. I sleep in a bunk on one side of the fire, Mr. Haast ... sleeps upon the opposite one; my bullock-driver and hut-keeper have two bunks at the far end of the hut, along the wall; ... The usual mutton and bread for breakfast with a pudding made of flour and water baked in the camp-oven after a joint of meat - Yorkshire pudding, but without eggs. While we were at breakfast, a robin perched on the table and sat there a good while pecking at the sugar. <i>(Apr 1861,Samuel Butler</i>) [NOTES: 1861 April Butler's Note in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler - A Memoir 1/87]

>  <i>Bullock-driver.</i> - Man employed to drive bullocks. The old nickname for him was <i>bullocky, bullock-puncher</i> being a much later word. Bullocks on the road have to feed until about 10 a.m., so that bullockies were generally lazy, slow starters in the morning. They were famous for swearing, and the old story of the parson who said, `Even a bullock driver has a soul to be saved' is still told up country. (C.; 1898 Morris Austral-English) [c1962 One of the last bullock teams was driven (ceremonially) in Canterbury in 1962.] <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>HEYWOOD</b> ... bullock-driver ... <i>(1863,Vacation Tour at the Antipodes, A,167</i>)

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> On the Wairoa Road, Hewitt, a commissariat man, and a bullock driver by the name of Hamlyn were shot; ... <i>(1864,Memoranda,67</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> If nice expressions you would learn,/ Colonial and new,/ Some bullock driver who is bogged,/ Is just the man for you: <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No.5/64</i>) [NOTES: The Leary]

>  <b>COTTLE</b> As long as a man earns what he wants on the square, ... we don't care a rap whether he is a Member of Parliament or So-and-so's bullock-driver. <i>(1891,Frank Melton's Luck,2</i>)

### bullock-driving
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Every now and then he leaves his up country avocations. He then returns to his shepherding, cooking, bullock-driving ... <i>(Mar 24, 1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,49</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Mar 24 in 1863 Butler First Year iv/50]

>  <b>FARJEON</b> ... bullock-driving is scarcely a gentlemanly occupation... <i>(1866,Grif: a Story of Colonial Life,200</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> I took the job of hedge-trimming at first, and, after having finished this all round the station land, which consisted of some 1500 acres parcelled off into paddocks and fields, I began to pick up bullock-driving; ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,146</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Talking of bullock-driving reminds me of an incident that occurred the other day, and which rather amused me at the time. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,67</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In 1860 he was bullock-driving for Low of Galloway .. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,186</i>)

### bullock-fence
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I was once called upon to endeavour to obtain compensation from a native who had destroyed ... several hundred yards of stout bullock-fence. ... [29] ... rushing at the posts and rails, [he] vented his rage in chopping them to pieces with his axe. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,28</i>)

### bullock-plough
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The first day was devoted to a ploughing match, for bullock-ploughs and for horse-ploughs. ... only eleven ploughs were on the ground, seven of which were bullock-teams ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,247</i>) [NOTES: [quoting 1847 Mar]]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> A season or two later bullock ploughs were used ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,95</i>)

### bullock-puncher
 
 <i>n.</i> bullock-driver

>  <b>McMORRAN</b> Sometimes [the cattle] took charge of the town, and almost defied the efforts of the experience "bullock punchers" in charge to pen them in the yards provided for them. <i>(1900,Some Schools & Schoolmasters of Early Wellington,4</i>)

>  <b>W H S ROBERTS</b> The whip was rawhide plaited sinnet, about six feet long, thick at the upper end, where it was fastened to a handle as long as itself, but tapering nearly to a point, to which was attached a narrow strip of rawhide, called `the fall,' finishing off with a twisted `cracker' made of `weetau' or dressed flax in [43] place of whipcord, and was a powerful flagellator in the hands of an experienced `bullock puncher.' <i>(Sep 19, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Sep 19 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 42-43]

>  <b>INGLIS</b> The whare with its idle group of Crimean-shirted stockmen ... bullock-punchers, horse-breakers, fencers and general rouseabouts ... came back to us. <i>(1887,Our NZ Cousins,163</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> A <i>bullock-puncher </i> is a man engaged to drive bullocks to market or port. The expression is painfully true, as these men, who receive but very poor wages, and are recruited from the worst class, ill treat the poor animals by <i>punching</i> their sides with a pointed stick. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,170</i>)

>  <b>BROOKES</b> One of the bullock-punchers did his duty for him. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,27</i>)

>  A stream of adjectives one would hardly expect from a bullock-puncher. <i>(Jul 1, 1905,NZ Truth,0/2/3</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> [In the accommodation house were] the villainous-looking 'lag' from 't'otherside', the bullock puncher, whose every alternate word was a profane oath, the stockrider, in his guernsey shirt and knee boots ... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,39</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> <i>(1920,Tutira</i>)

>  ~ Man employed to drive bullocks. The old nickname for him was <i>bullocky, bullock-puncher</i> being a much later word. Bullocks on the road have to feed until about 10 a.m., so that bullockies were generally lazy, slow starters in the morning. They were famous for swearing, and the old story of the parson who said, `Even a bullock driver has a soul to be saved' is still told up country. (C.; 1898 Morris Austral-English) <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The next day the snow had almost ceased so the `bullock-puncher' pushed his mob on as fast as conditions allowed. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,136</i>)

>  He said he received the earliest part of his training in public speaking as a "bullock puncher" in the district where the new pastor was born. <i>(Jun 12, 1890,Otago Witness,19</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bullocky, bullock teamster or driver.

>  The town of Tapanui had now started, and the sawyers, bush-fellers and 'bullock-punchers' put up their temporary huts. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,9</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Owners and employees had worked shoulder to shoulder as .. bullock-punchers. <i>(1921,Tutira,382</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bullocky, bullock puncher: by tradition the bullockies were considered the most fluent and imaginative swearers of all. [bullock puncher] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,12</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> The whip..was a powerful flagellator in the hands of an experienced 'bullock-puncher.' <i>(Sep 19, 1856,Roberts Diary,43</i>) [NOTES: in Beattie (1947) Early Runholding in Otago]

### bullock-punching
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. cow-banging etc](../C#cow-banging-etc))

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> Heavy bullock punching. <i>(Nov 10, 1868,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,168</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> After a spell of `quill-driving' it takes one a week or so to get into one's stride of splitting, fencing, bullock-punching again; ... Meanwhile, one can make good money at hard graft. <i>(1875,Adventures in Maoriland,61,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> I have returned to my old occupation of jackaroo, and am at present at Fernyhurst [Station]. ... Only a shepherd is employed here besides myself; so I have to do the milking and bullock-punching and make myself generally useful. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,87,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>cham</b> He soon got charge of a team and was loud in expatiating upon the art of bullock-punching. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,411</i>)

>  <b>ARTHUR</b> <i>(1894,Kangaroo and Kauri,90</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> T.A.P. & J.P. bullock punching - carting stones into creek &c. I.I. home in morning and weeding - killed 3 sheep. Tuesday 10th/ very fine & hot/ N.E/ Heavy bullock punching - stone carting &c. I.I. weeding G.S.P. for mail - Seal up from Xchurch by mailman <i>(Nov 9, 1868,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point</i>)

### bullock-sledge
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CLIFFORD</b> It would be easy to bring a bullock-sledge thus far. <i>(Apr 3, 1844,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Apr 3 Clifford to Wakefield in 1854 NZ Company Reports XVII 62]

>  <b>BARKER</b> Bullock sledges. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,3</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> The wedding was at Half-Way Bush, and the day before she drove there in her bullock sledge in charge of the bridecake, which ... was wrapped in her macintosh. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,176</i>)

### bullocker
 
 <i>n.</i> a bullock-driver (See [bullocky](bri-byo.md#bullocky))

>  <b>ARTHUR</b> I once saw ... a bullocker who was working with his team. <i>(1894,Kangaroo and Kauri,91</i>)

### bullocking
 
 <i>n.</i> pertaining to the hauling etc., (of logs) by bullocks; [[ca1900] 1953 see vbl n]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Sam hadn't long been here before making his way up to Hawke's Bay and becoming a professional bullock driver. Early in his bullocking career Sam and three other bullockies, ... were engaged in carting stores from Napier to Te Aute . <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,115</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> vigorous, aggressive (esp. used of rugby union forwards); (of work generally) strenuous, arduous

>  <b>COWAN</b> In his crippled old age he was cheered now and again by a visit from some old friend of his bush-scouting, bush-clearing, bullocking days when one could march all day with a seventy-pound swag ... <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,223</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> Big bullocking bastards always mauling each other about. <i>(1947,Big Game,8</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Now the bullocking forward is king of the field and the game is to work close down the sideline and get into penalty range of the goalposts. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,80</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Describes manual labour demanding mainly brute strength;`It's uphill and in gravel soil down there. To dig a trench would be hard, bullocking work; I wouldn't tackle it, anyway.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],129</i>)

>  <i>bullocking adj</i>. hard arduous, <i> i.e. bullocking work. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> barging through

>  Mansfield did some good work for Ponsonby, though still inclined to do a little 'bullocking'. <i>(Aug 30, 1890,NZ Observer,11</i>)

>  <b>FROBISHER</b> To use his own emphatic expression, he set his face against all such senseless "bullocking" [sc. in dancing] <i>(1893,Sketches of Gossipton,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> hauling, drawing, carting by a bullock team

>  <b>REED</b> While bullocking on a skidded road ... During bullocking operations in a portion of Puhipuhi, a nineteen foot log was being taken out on a catamaran. <i>(1853,Story of the Kauri, The</i>) [NOTES: [?1900] 1953 Reed Story of Kauri 186 (R. Ross)]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> hard manual work; hard slogging; hard heavy work (See [cf. bullock v](bri-byo.md#bullock))

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> `There's no bullocking attached to the job; all that'll be done for you.' <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,37,1985 ed.</i>)

### bullocky
 
 <i>adj.</i> Like a bullock, violent or physically strong

>  <b>FANNING</b> Those were the times [20 years ago] of packs of [rugby] Goliaths, brawny but `bullocky'. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,61</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Then the swine made a bullocky rush for home, though too late, the gate withstanding the impact. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,179,1954 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for the driver of a bullock team; also called bullock-puncher; bullock-driver

>  <b>COX</b> Clear the road, make room for the bullocky from Australy. <i>(1884,Recollections (various authors/titles),67,1854 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [Ref.: Shagroons in Cby] [1854] 1884 Cox Recollection 67]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> They [grasshoppers] actually stopped a bullock-team this week. The big-winged fellows kept flying into the faces of the leaders ...; and the `bullocky' had to take his team back to the homestead and unyoke them. <i>Ibid.</i> 72 To my astonishment, the bullocky headed his team straight for the river where it was quite unfordable. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,34,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> It was interesting to watch the leaders. The pair in front [sc. of the bullock team] were the leaders, they answered commands immediately, and the bullocky could get them to do all sorts of things. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,159</i>)

>  I've been told that I'm the only bullocky left. <i>(Jul 9, 1973,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  I said to Chris,"<i>Let me help you into the dray, you are exhausted.</i>" I was told in no uncertain terms that "<i>bullockies do not ride in the dray!</i>" <i>(Feb 2001,NZ Memories,30</i>)

>  <b>VANCE</b> It became the stopover places for bullockies, wagoners, drovers and wayfarers. <i>(1976,Bush, Bullocks & Boulders,86</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> No bullocky ever loved a tui, for the simple reason that these birds are great imitators, and they would often lead these bullockies astray. The bullocky would follow what he thought was the sound of one of his bells, and instead, find a tui up a tree. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,161</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> `And as for them bullockies' said she, ... <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,335</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> When the bullockies came with their load, boys,/ The days of the first of the gold. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,44</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ slang for bullock-driver. According to Barrere and Leland's `Slang Dictionary,' the word has a somewhat different meaning in America, where it means a drover. See<i> Punch.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,63</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> When the bullockies came with their load, boys,/ The days of the first of the gold. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,44</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ slang for bullock-driver. According to Barrere and Leland's `Slang Dictionary,' the word has a somewhat different meaning in America, where it means a drover. See<i> Punch.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,63</i>)

>  <b>EDEN</b> <i>(1872,My Wife and I in Queensland,49</i>)

>  <i>Bul&#769;.lock-y </i>(bul&#769;.l&ubreve.k-&ybreve.), <i>n</i>. The driver of a bullock wagon. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2019</i>)

>  The storekeeper gives [the gum] to his packman or bullocky to carry to the store. <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/206</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Bullock drivers who handle from 12 to 24 animals are also employed [in the sawmills]. ... A od `Bullocky' is like a good shepherd, his voice is seldom heard. ... The saying ... to `swear like a bullock driver', is common. ... Log-haulers, worked from an engine, are used in some mills. Hooking the winding rope on to suitable trees ... demands ... quickness of judgment. Wages. ... bullocky 10s to 12s a day, hooker-on 9s to 12s a day. <i>(1913,Country Work,40</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> Here in 1860 I came into the world ... About this time it was necessary to go the Christchurch for stores and the Dad and bullochy [h-spelling] went. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,58</i>) [NOTES: 1925 William Andrew's Recollections in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 58]

>  <i>bull n</i>. ...bullocky n. sl. a driver of a bullock team. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  ~ Man employed to drive bullocks. The old nickname for him was <i>bullocky, bullock-puncher</i> being a much later word. Bullocks on the road have to feed until about 10 a.m., so that bullockies were generally lazy, slow starters in the morning. They were famous for swearing, and the old story of the parson who said, `Even a bullock driver has a soul to be saved' is still told up country. (C.; 1898 Morris Austral-English) <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The Waimea run belonged to ... McKellar and the station consisted of two sod huts, one for the two bosses and the other for the men, the latter being a `bullocky', a shepherd and a boy. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,110</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> A pioneeer told me that a bullock team (driven by a bullock driver, usually called the `bullocky') might cart in `provisions' ... [ca1860-1870] <i>Ibid.</i> 43 The `bullocky' and his whip (and language) have a fairly conspicuous place in the early colonial literature, but there were different schools of thought concerning effective methods, ... <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bullocky</i>. Also a New Zealand usage. Both in New Zealand and in Australia, prob. since ca1840 or 1850. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1003</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> It was occupied by Dick Lamb, Mr. G.P. Donnelly's bullock driver. ... Dick Lamb, the bullocky was a big bearded man, an inveterate pipe smoker and born story teller. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,85</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> I asked Jack Hawley, the `bullocky' at Latta's Mill in Owaka Valley, to come over on Saturday afternoon. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,67</i>) [NOTES: [c1900]]

>  <i>bullocky ... n.</i> a bullock driver. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> On my property a bullocky was bringing out a heavy kauri log on a bullock waggon .... <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,42</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> ... he smiled as the bullocky screamed again, firing the whip with one horny hand while the other attacked a boil on his neck with a pruning knife. <i>(1970,Mackenzie,17</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> The bullocky worked on his own account, doing cartage work for the local stations. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,26</i>)

>  Working team is pride and joy of bullocky [caption] ... Mr Falconer owns one of the country's last [bullock] teams. <i>(Feb 6, 1981,Dominion,7</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> In its early days, the Gretna was the haven for bushmen and bullockies. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,96</i>)

>  Those days ... were the good old `bullocky' years; at one time the Ketemarae miller ... had seventy bullocks in the teams carting sawn timber about the district. <i>(Sep 11, 1922,Quick March,13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a driver of a bullock team.

>  <b>ANGUS</b> Swearing was elevated to an art form by the most fluent bullockies and shepherds <i>(1981,Aspiring Settlers,70</i>)

>  Clear the road, make room for the bullocky from Australy. <i>(1854,BLANK ENTRY,67</i>) [NOTES: in COX (1884) Recollection 67]

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> When the bullockies came with their load, boys <i>(1897,Station Ballads,64</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> bullock language; hard swearing

>  <b>BAKER</b> The noun <i>bullocky</i> has even been evolved to describe the language used by such men. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,47</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>Bullocky</i> has been used to describe the language supposed to be characteristic of bullock-drivers. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

### bullocky's joy
 
 <i>n.</i> treacle or golden syrup (See [cf. cocky's joy](chi-con.md#cocky's-joy))

>  <b>DUDER in GIFKIN</b> Grandfather Bill spread 'bullocky's joy' (golden syrup) on my toast. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,29</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> He might be given [by the station cook] a fresh bun with treacle, `Bullocky's joy.' Certainly not butter or jam, which were not very plentiful <i>(1954,Te Raka,11</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `Yeh. I'll bet a 7 pound tin of bullockies joy to a box of matches that he's still alive alright.' <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,157</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Doug still remembers the hungry stations where they ate old ewe, potatoes, rice pudding and, at the weekend, one pound of `brownie' per man. ... The boss said `brownie' was cake. We knew it only as bread dyed with bullocky's joy (treacle), and sparingly dotted with currants. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,97</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> For when dad was a boy,/Bullocky's joy,/ The only jam they knew,/ Was treacle - black/As an old coal sack/ And as thick as rubber glue./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,27</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> Out in camp he lived on hard bread, meat and bullockeys' joy. <i>(1946,Amuri,169</i>) [NOTES: (?OEDS2 has 109)]

>  <i>Bullocky's joy</i>.- Golden syrup or treacle. (C.) <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> [Bullockies' Joy] : Golden syrup or treacle. <i>(1933,Sheep-station Glossary</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The bread was no good because it had to be made in bullocky's joy tins. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,166</i>)

### bullocky's mate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> The sledge would slide over [the logs]. Usually they were helped by the bullocky's mate., or offsider, who would go along with the most evil-smelling tin of fat.. and spread the fat on the skids where the runners would slide, to assist the bullocks and move their load along more easily. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,160</i>)

### Bullring
 
 <i>n.</i> Rugby Park, New Plymouth - a happy coincidence of allusions - to the Taranaki Mascot, Ferdinand the Bull, and to Taranaki's most popular player and captain, Mark "Bull" Allen

>  This week they did it with huge dollops of character in front of 25,000 fans in a frenzied cauldron dubbed the Bull Ring. <i>(Sep 1, 1996,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  Allen was reluctant to comment on whether he expected to resume the captaincy of the Taranaki side in "the Bullring", as Rugby Park has become known. <i>(Sep 3, 1996,Dominion,24</i>)

>  O'Brien, who masterminded the.. thrilling defence in a packed Bullring, said he would like to see players in his own squad.. gain some financial benefit. <i>(Sep 8, 1996,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  What's hurting at the moment is the fact the provincial team has lost two NPC games on the trot at the Bullring in New Plymouth. <i>(Aug 19, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

### bullrope-man
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Before preference to unionists was passed the pay [for coal workers in the Wellington Branch of the Waterside Workers' Federation] was per hour for shovellers 1s 6d and for top hands (i.e. winchmen, bullrope-men, trimmers, and tippers), 1s 3d. <i>(Jun 9, 1911,Maoriland Worker,14/12/3</i>)

### bullrush
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  The kids formed a slampit - a wider, more open and more vicious form of a moshpit - and started bullrushing the other fans from the opposite sides of the arena. <i>(Apr 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game. [1984 16-17 M E77 Pakuranga Coll. 21 BULLRUSH [games out of school] M2] (See [Kingy-seeny](kee-kiw.md#kingy-seeny); [Charlie across the water](c-chi.md#charlie-across-the-water))

>  <b>McGILL</b> Bullrush involved as many of the school as possible, all at one end of the paddock, one person in the middle. At the cry of 'bullrush' we all ran and he had to catch one of us. It was also called Charlie across the water and kingaseeny <i>(1989,Kiwi Baby Boomers,41</i>)

>  In its simplest form bullrush involved one person standing in the middle of the field, then everyone else lined up on one side. The middle person would call someone's name (often in a rhyming way) into the middle. The object being of course to get past the middle to the other end of the field. If the runner was caught he or she joined the middle to call and catch more runners. Should a single runner get through there would be a bullrush, where everyone ran through the middle... These games had a thousand different names in earlier times: broken barley, king dick, punch king (where you had to punch the runner down!), goosey and so on... An excellent variation was.. cockarusha, in which everyone, instead of running, tries to hop through the middle. <i>(Apr 15, 1999,City Voice,19</i>)

>  Life itself is like a game of bullrush. Maybe that's why it is now virtually banned in every school in NZ, its [sic] too rough... Hayley has fond memories of bullrush, "That's where you got your boyfriends..." <i>(May 13, 1999,City Voice,19</i>)

>  It's like a game of bullrush, in which the single-objective SOE rushes to the finish line, and everyone else gets a quick yank of the jersey if they're quick. <i>(Aug 28, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  "There are lots of games children don't play any more such as bullrush - now octopus - stuck in the mud, and french skipping. ..." <i>(Jul 7, 2005,Waiheke Weekly, The,10</i>)

>  "I drank a lot and watched AUSA [Auckland University Students' Association] try to play 'Bullrush', a sleazy hitting-n-chicks game." <i>(May 1, 2000,Salient,7 : 5</i>)

>  It's like a game of bullrush, in which the single-objective SOE rushes to the finish line, and everyone else gets a quick yank of its jersey if they're quick. <i>(Sep 28, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Their frenzied games - tickling, playing bullrush, giving one another Chinese burns - had an element of schoolyard sadism that linked with a generalised sense of the grotesque. <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> `Do you play Bull Rush at your school?' John asked. `Bull Rush?' said Ewan. ... `You get out on the field and draw a line at the ends. You pick someone to be He to stand in the middle. He calls out someone's name, and he has to get across to the other end. The guard has to try to tackle the runner and hit him three times. But if the runner gets across he calls out Bull Rush and everyone runs ...[sic]' `That's King-a-Sene!' said Ewan. `But we're only allowed to tag the player, we're not allowed to tackle him ...'[sic] ... `Do you play with the girls at all?' `Sometimes we play Drop the Handkerchief. The girls can join in. But most of the time they play Eely Ily Oh, or Queenie, or some cissy game like that...'[sic] `Do you play shinty or football?' `Yeah. Football. And we play scrag in the winter and tippeny runs in the summer...'[sic] <i>(1978,Elver, The,61</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> Some other children have come over from the neighbouring [58] farm and they're all playing <i>bullrush. Bullrush! </i>they shout, and Ida runs across the lawn with flying hair and Selby, the youngest, trails the others as they dive after her. <i>(1986,Address to a King,57</i>)

>  But taking them back to our house for pizza and cake was a mistake, and the game of bullrush was a serious miscalculation... <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  The street.. was still as good for a game of cricket or bullrush as it had been a generation before. <i>(Feb 1992,North & South,37</i>)

>  Is there one single New Zealamnder who has not played bullrush in some form or other?... There are variations. The main one is the classic 'bar the door' (which for some phonetically evolved into 'barbador'), where the main difference is that runners line up at both ends of the field. These games had a thousand names in earlier days: broken barley, king dick, punch king (where you had to punch the runner down), goosey and so on... Kerikeri school banned it (they called it king asini) three years ago. <i>(Nov 5, 1999,NZ Education Review,00</i>) [NOTES: check date and page]

>  In the foreground, the under-10 Dragons finish with bullrush. They have already dodged puddles and clay clods to get to the gym, but there is energy left for threading round team-mates to reach the other side. <i>(Sep 18, 2010,Your Weekend,10</i>)

### bulls
 
 <i>exclam.</i> ?abbrev. of bullsh(it)! or alteration of balls! combined expressing disbelief. [1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 24D BULLS]

### bullseye
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>COOP</b> With August shearing it is customary and generally advisable to `ring-crutch' or `bullseye' the ewes before putting the rams out at tupping. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,28</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to ring-crutch. (See [ring crutch](red-roa.md#ring-crutch); [buttonhole](bri-byo.md#buttonhole))

>  <b>COOP</b> With August shearing it is customary and generally advisable to 'ring-crutch' or 'bullseye' the ewes before putting the rams out at tupping. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,28</i>)

### bullsh
 
 <i>n.</i> abbrev. and euphemism of bullshit, also as an ejaculation [ca1920 fairly frequent W.H.B. Orsman e.g. Talk bullsh ; plenty of bullsh about him etc.] (See [contrast shouse](sho-spa.md#shouse))

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>kidney pie</i>, insincere praise or what the Americans call <i>bull</i>, and Australians <i>bullsh</i>, and both, in less hurried moments, <i>bull-sh-t</i> <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 286]

### bullshipper
 
 <i>n.</i> euphemistic for bullshitter

>  <b>SARGE</b> [Taranaki boasting] ... Taranaki was a wonderful dairying centre and produced many great cattle, particularly of the bull-variety. In fact, the people of Taranaki were regarded as the greatest bull-shippers in New Zealand, if not in the world. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,8</i>)

### bullshit and jellybeans
 
 <i>phr.</i> [phrase originated with Tim Shadbolt and his subsequent book of that title]

>  If you go to your accountant or lawyer, he will say it's all bullshit and jellybeans, but it's not. <i>(Apr 4, 2001,Dominion,12</i>)

>  Dame Catherine.. joked that an alternative title for Ms Evans's thesis could be <i>Bullshit and Jellybeans Revisited"</i>, in reference to the book published by then student radical Tim Shadbolt who went on to become mayor of Waitemata and Invercargill respectively. <i>(Mar 10, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  <b>35</b> `Tell me about your work [in advertising] ...' Peter pulled a face ... `You know what it's like,' he said. `It hasn't changed.' After a moment he added, `Bullshit and jellybeans.' <i>(1984,Einstein,COX</i>)

>  Before I met her I would make $2000 in a day parasailing, take it to the pub that night and bank what was left. That's all bullshit and jellybeans. <i>(Dec 28, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

### bullshit and kauri gum
 
 <i>phr.</i> usu. as an exclam., an extended variant of preceding phs influenced by one version of the story quoted under bovril : Englishman, New Zealander and Maori swapping tall stories. The Englishman rubs two sticks together: result, fire; the New Zealander rubs two kauri trees together: result, kauri-gum; the Maori, two bulls: result, bullshit. [ca1935 at least: popular among schoolchildren]

### bullshit castle
 
 <i>phr.</i> used of an administrative HQ; e.g. Victoria University, the Vice-Chancellor's or Registry office suite (ca1960s)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bullshit Castle.</i> Airforce HQ: NZ airmen's: WW2. (Slatter.) <i>(1984,Dictionary of Slang,152</i>)

### bullshit tower
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) the Auckland police headquarters building

>  I also learnt from Auckland police their frustration over the inaction of the police hierarchy. The boys in "bullshit tower" couldn't comprehend a multinational, multi-million-dollar crime group, detectives said ... <i>(Oct 23, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

### bullswool
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) polite var. of bullshit [1960 16C F B4 Wanganui Girls C. 5 BULL'S WOOL (=RUBBISH)]

>  We live in a land of beautiful bullswool. <i>(Dec 16, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,8</i>)

>  "Bullswool," say those about the Scuttlebutt, et al. <i>(Oct 30, 1998,Independent (Auckland),11</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the [slang] expressions listed by children in two schools are: `Up the shoot, going eyes out, bullswool, go jump in the lake, ... shivery dick, shiver me timbers, ... you're telling I, got it on the brain, like smoke in a wheelbarrow' (Kaitaia), `Sheila, crow, bud, frig it, grub, holy moses, blast it, mole, balls to you, shut your face, ... nerts to you, go bite your back, have a roll, fluff off, scram, ants in your pants, rag bag, ... you're bats, dronk, drongo, dill, dip, dippy, ya big lug, greetings gruesome, who dented your beak. (South Wellington). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/677</i>)

>  Suppose we had to discuss the much-advertised `Bulls wool', and I wanted to say in Italian : `Do not hesitate to use `Bulls wool' on any washable fabric ...' <i>(Apr 11, 1921,Quick March,46</i>)

>  [Cartoon of G.B. Shaw] In an interview after bathing at Mt. Maunganui in his red bullswool costume G.B.S. said `The Beach was the finest in the world.' <i>(1934,Otago University Capping Book,44</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> The <i>Bedlam News</i> reported his colleague's response: `Bullswool.' <i>(1936,Politician, The,51</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Don't think that it's all bullswool. <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,153</i>) [NOTES: 1940 Geo Meek `Wool Commander' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 153]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Bullswool</i>, the Australian name for the fibre of the stringy-bark tree, expresses scorn or contempt (cf US <i>bulldust</i>; indeed, <i>bullswool</i> itself may have originated independently in America) <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Bull's wool, I said. <i>(1946,That Summer,149</i>)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> ... s'all bullswool. <i>(1948,Maleesh George,35</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bull's wool</i>. 3. Nonsense, meaningless talk, ballyhoo: NZ and Aust: since ca1920. (Niall Alexander, letter or Oct 22, 1939; B1942) Prob. ex sense 1. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1003</i>)

>  <b>CROSS</b> [OED2]That last bit was bulls-wool of course, but I had to be careful. <i>(1957,God Boy, The,67</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>bullswool</i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> bull's wool: nonsense. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,298</i>)

>  <i>bullswool n.(Coll.)</i> inner covering of the stringy-bark tree; <i>bullswool (Sl.)</i> nonsense, humbug. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  [c1920-1950] there are vigorous <i>Slanging Contests</i> in which the contestants try to outdo each other by using phrases like the following: `You're up the shoot.' `Oh, bullswool.' `Go jump in the lake'. .... <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,138</i>)

>  <i>bullswool</i> : rubbish <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>bull/bulls wool/bull dust:</i> rude term for disbelief in a statement; e.g., `He spoke a lot of bulls wool.' <i>(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bullswool</i> nonsense, or exclamation at serving up of; tenuous link to tenuous fibre of strigy-bark gum tree; c.1920. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,23</i>)

>  [letter ending] - I am etc., `BOOLZ WOOL.' Palmerston North, November 2nd, 1909 <i>(Nov 6, 1909,NZ Truth,228/7/5</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> I've unlearned a lot of Bullswool, that's all, and it allows me to think straight now.<i>Ibid.</i> 242 bullswool nonsense <i>(1984,Blackball 08,98</i>)

### bullu-bul
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bull-a-bull (poroporo)](bri-byo.md#bull-a-bull)))

### bully
 
 <i>n.</i> Cockabully; pl. occas. <i>bullys.</i> usu. of ?<i>Galaxias</i> sp(p) or <i>Gobiomorphus</i> spp., cockabully, toitoi [ca1935 Havelock (children)]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> BULLIES: Sub-order GOBIOIDEA Bullies are not the same as cockabullies. The family these rather severe-faced fishes belong to is widely distributed over moderately warm and even tropical countries, and reaches its southern most distribution in New Zealand. The bully family is called Gobiidae by some writers, Eleotridae by others. Bullies are spiny-rayed fishes with the paired ventral fins placed well foward; they have a genital papilla for passing out eggs or sperms; and the males look different from the females. Two genera are recognised at present, <i>Gobiomorphus</i> and <i>Philypnodon. </i> <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,226</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Food was brought from home, including herrings we had caught, cooked, and put into vinegar. But `bullies,' mushrooms, and whitebait were among the things cooked in the fort in old fruit tins on wire tripods over two or three ends of candles stuck on a board. ... (1900; Waitara) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,112</i>)

>  A notice in the local Press brought me further examples from other eastern rivers - namely, the Ashley and Hurunui; and I was told that they [sc. torrentfish] were known as `shark bulllies', in allusion to the inferior position of the mouth, coupled with some resemblance to the gobies, commonly called `bullies'. <i>(1910,TrNZI,42/389</i>)

>  [Kingfishers] descend ... to the ... sunny flats on the Grey River, subsisting on insects and small bull-trout `or bullys' until the arrival of the whitebait. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/215</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The bully, or bull-head, and the kokopu, or native trout, live entirely in fresh water. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,74</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> The beloved creek where bullies wait the hook. <i>(1912,Brown Bread,2</i>)

>  The bulley (<i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> Cuv. & Val.) is unknown outside New Zealand. <i>Ibid.</i> 8/296 The bulley, or toitoi, is known from the Bay of Islands to the Bluff ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/294</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> (Cuv. & Val). BULLY; TOITOI or KOKOPU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,1971,49 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Cockabully : Often shortened to bully, especially by fishermen. Gobiomorphus gobioides, a small freshwater fish. The word is probably derived from kokopu, one of the native names for it. The other native name is toitoi. <i>(1933,Sheep Station</i>) [NOTES: Glossary]

>  <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> : [European Name] Bully [Maori Name] Toitoi. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> : [European Name] Bully [Maori Name] Toitoi. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> (Cuv. and Val.). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/415</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> Here [Taupo] they [kokopu] associate with the common bulley, Gobiomorphus gobiodes. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,23</i>)

>  <b>MANNERING</b> The principal native fish upon which the trout prey are bullies (eleotris gobiodes) [sic], smelts (Retropinna), whitebait (galaxias attenuatus) ... <i>(1943,Eighty Years,158</i>)

>  Kokopu ... name corrupted by children to cock-a-bully and abbreviated bully. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Cockabully. Often shortened to <i>bully</i>, especially by fishermen; <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> , a small freshwater fish. The word is probably derived from kokopu, one of the Maori names for it. Another Maori name is toitoi. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,362</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The seats [of the boys' fort at Waitara, c1880-1890] had a covering of cut-down sacks ... But `bullies' mushrooms and whitebait were among the things cooked ... <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/606</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BULLEY (TOITOI) <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> (Cuv. and Val.) The bulley is one of the most abundant of our native freshwater fish. It is often referred to as a close relative of the Kokopu (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>) ... This is not correct ... The Bulley, or Bullhead as it is known in some parts, forms an important food for the introduced Trout throughout New Zealand. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,318</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> [cf. also BULLHEAD, a fish] <i>Kopu ... 3. Gobiomorphus gobioides, bully</i>, a freshwater fish. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,138</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ~ pakoko, titarakura, toitoi <i>Gobiomorphus Philynodon </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BULLEY Probably our most common freshwater fish the Bulley is also known as the Bullehead [sic] and is a basic staple diet of our trout. ...Several varieties of Bulley exist in New Zealand including the Blue-gilled, Common, Hutton's, Large and Upland varieties. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,24</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Tiny, largely transparent bullies are also amongst the migrants ... common bully <i>(Gobiomorphus cotidianus)</i>, redfinned bully (G. huttoni),</i> and bluegilled bully (<i>G. hubbsi). These are sometimes caught in large quantities by whitebaiters. The fishermen call them `Dan Doolin Spawn' or `Whalefeed'. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,189</i>)

>  <i>bully</i> ... 2. a small New Zealand freshwater fish. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,144</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 93 & 137; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 63]]

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Gobies and bullies</i> Families Gobiidae and Eleotridae. ... Bullies are mainly freshwater and estuarine ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,135</i>)

>  <b>spei</b> The whitebait runs begin definitely in August and continue through the two following months; but in November the shoals begin to include young smelts and bullies (<i>Gobiomorphus</i>) which prey actively upon the whitebait, the bullies being particularly voracious. ... <i>Ibid.</i> Of the two gobies, more often called `bullies,' are by far the better known. <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides </i> is small, but active and voracious, and can be seen in all slender streams and watercourses darting across the bottom, for `bullies' keep low down. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,196</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 196]

>  <b>spei</b> The whitebait runs begin definitely in August and continue through the two following months; but in November the shoals begin to include young smelts and bullies (<i>Gobiomorphus</i>) which prey actively upon the whitebait, the bullies being particularly voracious. ... <i>Ibid.</i> Of the two gobies, more often called `bullies,' are by far the better known. <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides </i> is small, but active and voracious, and can be seen in all slender streams and watercourses darting across the bottom, for `bullies' keep low down. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,196</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 196]

>  [Trout] are taken freely at night with "bullies" a fish rather like our Miller's Thumb. <i>(Mar 20, 1918,Blackwood's Magazine,377</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bulldozer [gummy ca1949; ca1955 Marlborough Woman (R. Mason)] (See [gummy](gra-gyp.md#gummy))

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Anchor the bully over there and winch her across. <i>(1981,Fish Factory,156/453</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> meat

>  Schooner Auckland.. 49 tins bouilli. <i>(Dec 23, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

>  There were five of us squatting on bully cases round an improvised table in a dug-out. <i>(Dec 22, 1941,NZEF Times,I/26 : 22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a certain tip; the dinkum oil (on a horse) (prob f. <i>bully</i> aj `first rate', i.e. <i>the bully gen</i>, with an ironical play on <i>bull-shit</i>). [ca1950 (prob earlier, even 1930s) Auckland (V. O'Sullivan) e.g. I've got the bully on X for Epsom on Saturday. I got the bully straight from the horse's mouth.]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bully.</i> adj. First-rate, `champion', splendid: Canada, Aust and NZ, from ca1860, ex US ... .us; <i>Ex the late 17-18C. S.E.</i> bully, <i>worthy, admirable, applied only to persons. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,105</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANNERING</b> In the North Island, the bully run follows the whitebait. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,158</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WHITE</b> The young one looks as if he had be [sic] fed on cabbage and water, and imprisoned in a scrap and bully pot all the days of his life. <i>(1846,Private Journal Written At Mata,21</i>) [NOTES: 1846 J. White MS Journal 21]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The talk ranges from the value of the `bullie' (collie-bulldog cross) in pig-hunting, the Hokonui coalmine and Minnie Dean's baby farming. <i>(Jul 23, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bulldozer or track tractor.

>  tractor with tracks: bully. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  'Without Graeme, everything would have been so much harder,' he says. 'He can take the bully anywhere and gets the job done well.' <i>(Oct 10, 1974,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> bully: 2. a bulldozer. <i>(1998,David McGill's Complete Kiwi Slang Dictionary,23</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> Although whitebaiters know them by the name "whalefeed" they are in fact the young of several species of freshwater bully: the redfinned bully (<i>Gobiomorphus huttoni</i>), the bluegilled bully (<i>Gobiomorphus hubbsi</i>) and the common bully (<i>Gobiomorphus cotidianus</i>)... The third species referred to as "whalefeed" is the common bully. This rather drab fish is the most commonly seen bully. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,21</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Common Bully <i>Gobiomorphus basalis </i>(Gray) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,411</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 411]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The common bully (<i>Gobiomorphus</i> sp(p)) is plentiful at river mouths, and occurs also in some upland lakes like Te Anau and Alexandria. The giant bully,<i> G. gobioides,</i> grows up to nine inches long - three inches longer than the biggest common bully - and is most often met with in lowland lake, swamps and spring-fed creeks. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,226</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Tiny, largely transparent bullies are also amongst the migrants ... common bully (<i>Gobiomorphus cotidianus</i>), redfinned bully (<i>G. huttoni</i>), and bluegilled bully (<i>G. hubbsi</i>). These are sometimes caught in large quantities by whitebaiters. The fishermen call them `Dan Doolin Spawn' or `Whalefeed'. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,189</i>)

>  Both the common bully, up to 15 cm, and Cran's bully, up to 10 cm, may occur within the park ... <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,73</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Gobiomorphus breviceps</i>

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> The other species is the upland bully <i>Gobiomorphus breviceps</i>. This is found well inland... The upland bully is by far the stockiest of the species, and is distinguished by the orange to grey-brown spots on its cheeks. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,22</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Upland <i>Philypnodon breviceps </i> Stokell [error for UPLAND BULLY check] <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,411</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 411]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The very common and widely distributed broad-headed or upland bully is <i>Philypnodon breviceps </i>... This is a high country species, found in tarns, alpine streams and lakes at over 1000 feet. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,228</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> Although whitebaiters know them by the name "whalefeed" they are in fact the young of several species of freshwater bully: the redfinned bully (<i>Gobiomorphus huttoni</i>), the bluegilled bully (<i>Gobiomorphus hubbsi</i>) and the common bully (<i>Gobiomorphus cotidianus</i>)... The bluegilled bully is dinstinguished by a steely-blue stripe on the gill membranes and its distinctly dark-spotted, almost leopard-like pattern on the head. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,21</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> torrent-fish; <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i>

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> A very distinctive and unusual looking fish occasionally caught by whitebaiters is the torrentfish <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i>. It is [23] sometimes known as shark bully because the mouth is receding, opening below the head. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,22</i>)

>  A notice in the local Press brought me further examples from other eastern rivers - namely, the Ashley and Hurunui; and I was told that they [sc. torrentfish] were known as `shark bulllies', in allusion to the inferior position of the mouth, coupled with some resemblance to the gobies, commonly called `bullies'. <i>(1910,TrNZI,42/389</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> upland bully

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The very common and widely distributed broad-headed or upland bully is <i>Philypnodon breviceps </i>... This is a high country species, found in tarns, alpine streams and lakes at over 1000 feet. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,228</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> large bully, <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i>
