>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The common bully (<i>Gobiomorphus</i> sp(p)) is plentiful at river mouths, and occurs also in some upland lakes like Te Anau and Alexandria. The giant bully, G. gobioides, grows up to nine inches long - three inches longer than the biggest common bully - and is most often met with in lowland lake, swamps and spring-fed creeks. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,226</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Tiny, largely transparent bullies are also amongst the migrants ... common bully (<i>Gobiomorphus cotidianus</i>), redfinned bully (<i>G. huttoni</i>), and bluegilled bully (<i>G. hubbsi</i>). These are sometimes caught in large quantities by whitebaiters. The fishermen call them `Dan Doolin Spawn' or `Whalefeed'. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,189</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Blue-Gilled Bully <i>Philypodon</i> sp. nov. Stokell, ex Hubbs MS. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,411</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 411]

### bully taw
 
 <i>n.</i> a marble

>  <b>FRAME</b> `Treasure' I played ringie and holie. I played funs and keeps. I had pee-wees and bully taws and changers that weren't made of glass mind you. And I won nearly every game because the kids were so dazzled by the look of my stinkers. ... They came for miles to play marbles with me ... <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,116,1961 ed.</i>)

### bully-bull
 
 <i>n.</i> raupo head: ?f. bully-bull + bulrush portmanteaued; ?children's use c1880-1890. (See [bull-a-bull](bri-byo.md#bull-a-bull))

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The seats [of the boys' fort at Waitara, c1880-1890] had a covering of cut-down sacks and the beds had ... a sugar-bag pillow holding the soft towsled heads of the `bully-bulls', the raupo heads. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/606</i>)

### bully-dam
 
 <i>v.</i> to abuse; curse (f. ? bully + damn).

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> [A tough mill-manager speaks] The bush won't have weaklings. I bully-dam them from the jump, and if they play up they know it. <i>(1907,Tracks  We Tread,72</i>)

### bulrush
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Typha angustifolia</i>, any of a group of large marsh plants found in swampy areas; in NZ; raupo.

>  He had got the stump of a tree placed where he intended I should sit, and had made a cushion of bulrushes which was placed upon it. <i>(Aug 1820,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,289,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> [New Zealanders' houses] are built of bulrushes and lined with the leaves of the palm-tree, neatly platted [sic] together. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),153</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... bulrushes (raupo) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/82</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Bulrush. <i>Typha angustifolia. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>)

>  The pollen also of the flowers of the large Bulrush (<i>Typha angustifolia</i>) was ... collected ... by the Southern tribes, and made into large gingerbread like cakes, called Pungapunga. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kopupungawha</i>, n. <i>bulrush, scirpus lacustris. Raupo ...typha angustifolia;</i> bulrush; <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,70</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ See Wonga--> and Raupo--> <i>(1898,Austral English,64</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>T</i>[ypha] angustifolia, Linn. ... <i>Bulrush; Raupo</i>. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,743</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> What we used to call flags then [in the 1840s], but now known as bulrushes or by the Maoris called rapo (or raupo), were used by the coopers then. They were inserted between the joints and kept the oil casks as tight as a drum. .ho;1966 McLintock (ed) Encyclopedia NZ 3/705-712 bulrush .. .. raupo <i>Typha muelleri </i> <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,29</i>)

>  <i>bulrush ...n</i>. see <i>wonga</i> and <i>raupo</i>. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  <i>bulrush [=] jointed cladium: juncus pallidus: purua grass: raupo: scirpus lacustris: tall spike-rush</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,9</i>)

>  <i>(1785,Cook's Third Voyage</i>) [NOTES: see raupo]



 
 <i>n.</i> [(Andersen 14 entries), 1845 EJW Ad NZ, 1919 Cockayne NZ Plants.]]

### bulrush flour
 
 <i>n.</i> raupo pollen used as flour (See [cf. pungapunga](pop-pyo.md#pungapunga))

### bulrush hut
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. raupo hut](ra-red.md#raupo-hut))

### bulrush-caterpillar
 
 <i>n.</i> the fungus growth resembles a bulrush head [f. appearance, like a bulrush head].

>  <b>WARD</b> Bulrush-caterpillar ... [aweto] <i>(1872,Life Among the Maories of NZ,54</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> [I will] describe the most curious of the New Zealand native insects ... called the bulrush [184] caterpillar <i>(Sphaeria Robertsia) </i>- native name, Aweto. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,184</i>)

### bulrush-pollen cake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FULLER</b> BULRUSH-POLLEN CAKES (PUNGAPUNGA OR KONEHU RAUPO) ... Next the flowerheads of the raupo were placed in finely-woven flax baskets and shaken over the mouths of the bags so that only the fine seed was collected. This pollen was put into smaller baskets and covered with rangiora leaves and baked in the umu until steam burst from the top, when the cakes would be cooked. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,28</i>)

### bum
 
 <i>v.</i> to weep; used occas. since 1867 as a borrowing from Scots dialect.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Tom Marlingspike actually <i>bummed</i> when he bid the old man goodbye; and bless your hearts, the old man bummed too. <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a toady to authority, cf bumboy, bummer [(St Patrick's Colleges, Wellington; St Bede's College.) 1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 1 BUM [M2]]

### bum buzzard
 
 <i>n.</i> one who sits out the war on his backside [-] (See [cf. base walloper](b-bla.md#base-walloper))

>  <b>DAVIN</b> As Tony displayed his identity card to the Cypriots at the gate he savoured.. the palpable back-from-the-desert air with which he passed these base wallahs and bum buzzards, deflating the pale pigs' bladders of their importance as they flitted from office to office. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,156</i>)

### bum nuts
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural, informal) dags, wool encrusted with faeces.

### bum tucker

### bum-boiling
 
 <i>adj.</i>

### bum-rattler

>  dag: a bum-rattler. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large raisin bun made by station cooks.

### bum-sliding
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This is bum-sliding at its best... What better way to celebrate a climb than to indulge in a 100-metre, bum slide on the way down? <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Dominion Post,F12</i>)

### bum-squasher
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. bomb-squasher](bla-bri.md#bomb-squasher))

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies ... <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### bumbarber
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Perhaps the nearest to [profanity] is the "bumbarber" - the man who dags the sheep for the shearer. <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person who dags sheep with handshears prior to shearing.

>  Perhaps the nearest to this is the "bumbarber" - the man who dags the sheep for the shearer. <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bumbarber: dagger. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,13</i>)

>  Bumbarber: a worker who dags sheep. [bum-barber] <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### Bumble Puppy
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. <i>West Coast</i>: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; <i>Wellington</i>: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. ### bumblebee
 
 <i>n.</i> [cuisine]

### bumboy
 
 <i>n.</i> (boarding schools spec.), a screw. [ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> `Erina darling,' she smiled, oozing charm and sweetness, be a dear and see whether your mother and father are tucked up safely away from our gossip.' `When did your last bumboy [?a cringing servant] die,' Erina grumbled as she got up ... <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,374</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> ~ (n) Assistant superintendent. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,246</i>)

### bummer
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) ?crawler, toady; (?bludger). [Not originally a NZ term. OED gives the first reference in this sense to USA 1967; 1960 18C M B11 Nelson Boys C. 3 BUMMER (WHO SUCKS UP TO MASTER)]

>  Come up, while I fill your ear you bummer and bully's backer. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,78</i>)

### bump
 
 <i>n.</i> rugby move

>  <b>ELLISON</b> I never saw a Britisher who knew anything about the "bump". <i>(1902,Art of Rugby Football, The,71</i>)

>  A favourite mode of attack in New Zealand is the bump, that is the player with the leather crouches himself while going at full speed, and butts with all his force (using hip or shoulder) at the opponent who is essaying to tackle. <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/237</i>)

>  We read.. of.. "Jack" Taiaroa with his wonderful hip and shoulder bump. and in a way we ask. ourselves if our glory has not departed. <i>(Jun 25, 1910,Dominion,12</i>)

>  <b>STONE</b> Opai on attack was a dangerous man to handle - with his speed, his own particular fend, bump, wriggle and other tricks. <i>(1938,Rugby Players Who Have Made NZ Famous,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

Some of the `temporary' clerks were drafted into useful spheres, while the majority got what is commonly called `the bump.' <i>(Sep 14, 1912,NZ Truth,377/1/2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a hill or mountain

 <i>v.</i> to chuck out

### bun
 
 <i>n.</i> a bowler hat; short for bun hat q.v.

>  In the 90's the younger generation discarded their beards ... The flat-topped hat gave place to the `bun' or `hard-hitter' ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,131,1978 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [c1890s]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> A bowler hat has become<i> bun</i> in colloquial speech, taking its place beside the Australian <i>boxer</i> or <i>peadodger</i> and the American <i>derby. </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bun</i>. 6. A bowler hat: NZ: 20C., B1941. Ex the shape. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1003</i>)

>  <i>bun ... NZ sl</i>. (in full <i>bun hat</i>) bowler hat. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,94</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Our victim removed an antiquated bowler hat (referred to by the vulgar as a "bun"). <i>(Jul 15, 1924,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  His hat, an old "bun", went bowling up the street. <i>(Apr 17, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> sugar [? reference : I have not heard this; is he mishearing sugar bun][HWO.

>  <b>WINKS</b> Three phrases which Americans can never understand are `shout' for `treat', `had it' for `fed up' , and `bun' for `sugar'. <i>(1954,These New Zealanders,54,1956 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a form in which ensilage is stacked [[?bin/bun].]

>  <i>(Jan 1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,86(1)/37</i>) [NOTES: (R. Mason)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a silage or fertiliser stack, shaped like an inverted saucer or bun.

>  ..enclosing fences can be placed right at the edges of the bun and the only pasture lost is that covered by the bun itself. <i>(Jan 15, 1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,38</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> The bun clamp is simply a heap of grass shaped like an inverted saucer. <i>(1961,Agriculture,368</i>)

>  Wedges have much more surface exposed than stacks and buns, but they are adapted admirably for stacks and buns. Elsewhere they should be replaced by buns. <i>(Jan 15, 1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,37</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Silage has been constructed here [in NZ] in stacks, 'clamps' and 'buns' which require no constructional work. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,400</i>)

>  I am sticking to the 'bun' technique for silage-making this year. The main reason that I am sticking with buns for silage is pasture damage. I have a feeling that I'll end up by having two pits, concrete and all and still make buns to be fed out before the ground conditions become soft. [bun clamp] <i>(Nov 1975,NZ Dairy Exporter,49</i>)

### bun hat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He was thin... and wearing an old bun hat. <i>(1950,Landfall,4(21) : 14</i>) [NOTES: Check page and cit - 4:21 or as above]

>  <b>INGRAM</b> What a motley looking mob we were! ... Some wearing `bun' hats, some straws and others felts. Some in navy serge suits, some in grey tweeds, some even in knickerbockers ... <i>(Jan 4, 1917,Anzac Diary,9,1987 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> A bowler hat has become a <i>bun</i> in colloquial speech. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  He was thin ... and wearing an old bun hat. <i>(1950,Landfall,4/21</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> `What do you think of the bun hats?' Ng. asked, as the saddle horses were passing. <i>(Nov 11, 1955,Shepherd's Calendar,247,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <i>bun n. ... NZ sl.</i> (in full<i> bun hat</i>) bowler hat ... do one's bun NZ sl. lose one's temper <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,94</i>)

### bun head
 
 <i>n.</i> a hair style favoured by ballet dancers, with bun on the back of the head; (by metonymy) a (would-be) ballet dancer

>  'I was what you called a bun head. All that you think about is ballet.' <i>(Apr 28, 2005,Wellingtonian,20</i>)

>  You can always tell the budding ballerinas in the audience... They are the ones with the bun heads... The slicked back hair style screams "ballet" as loudly as a tutu. <i>(Feb 19, 2003,Capital Times,11</i>)

### bun loaf
 
 <i>n.</i> = scone loaf?

>  <b>BYCROFT LTD</b> Bun loaves ... <i>(1934,Bread Baking in NZ,174</i>)

### bun-scuffler
 
 <i>n.</i> ??play on scoffer

>  Beware, you bun-scufflers! Down at Kaitanga the other day Thos. Stephens ate one. The jury's verdict was suffocation caused by eating a bun. <i>(Sep 8, 1906,NZ Truth,64/1/4</i>)

### bunch
 
 <i>n.</i> an irregular lump of ore

>  BUNCH.- An irregular lump of ore. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,53</i>)

>  ... the news that a new `bunch' (vein or mass) of copper has been struck ... fills the workers with professional joy. <i>Ibid.</i> 32/315... the workmen were within 2 in. or 3 in. of a `bunch' of copper-ore, which in twelve months yielded a profit of [pounds]24,000. <i>(1900,TrNZI,32/312</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Cornish Tin Mines)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to gather all stringing sheep in a muster together into one mob.

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> Stage two involves musterers moving out from Jack Mack's hut each morning and hunting up the sheep along the gullies and ridges, pushing them down Jack Mack's valley, stringing them over the hills heading back towards Bush Hut, then bunching them and crossing them over Martin's Creek. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,12</i>)

### bunch of fluffy ducks
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) variant of BOX OF FLUFFY DUCKS

>  "It's certainly not a bunch of fluffy ducks in Romania, but I'm, there to work and I work hard. <i>(Mar 2006,North & South,66</i>)

>  What a bunch of grizzling old sods you years-past are. Look at me. A bunch of fluffy ducks. <i>(Jan 2, 1993,Evening Post,6</i>)

### bunchy
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) of lodes, opening out and contracting suddenly; ore distributed irregularly

>  The lodes [are] bunchy (opening out and contracting suddenly) <i>(1906,Mining Handbook,49</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a boyfriend/girlfriend. [1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 28 BUNCHY [boy/girl friend]]

### bunfight
 
 <i>n.</i> a contest (rather than general English a tea party) (more literal)

>  The expected bunfight between Coutts and arch rival Oracle skipper Chris Dickson failed to materialise. <i>(Jan 5, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B6</i>)

### bung
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a blind-ended part of a sheep's large intestine.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bung: a blind-ended part of the large intestine of a sheep. Cattle bungs were once used as casings for belgian sausage. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) cheddar cheese, as produced in small factories around New Zealand.

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> bung: cheddar cheese. Like thousands of their fellows up and down the country, they together made 'bung' for export, and had a hell of a good time doing it. <i>(1985,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,12</i>)

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> As long as I'm able to produce bung with the bulk of it going first grade, and keep the yield up to about 2.5, they seem to be satisfied ... <i>(1985,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,33</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i> in this collocation, often a mixture of senses of <i>bung(ed)</i>. It can mean a `blind' (i.e., useless, ruined) eye, or and eye <i>bunged-up</i> (sc by a blow, or (a bung in the eye) or by infection). It is not clear to which of these (or both) the 1963 citation below refers. cf P: <i>bung-eye</i> Aust coll for and ocular inflammation caused by flies : late 19-20C.

>  <b>ANDERSON & AITKEN</b> [Maori child speaks] He's got a bung eye. <i>(1965,Speech and Idiom of Maori Children,37,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A part of the human body is injured, or in an unhealthy state. .`Bob would be a good man to call on for help, but he's got a bung arm. He's got it in plaster.' Thus, a `bung' eye may be either caused by a blow, or swollen or discoloured, etc. through sickness. .in 4;`He's got a bung eye.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],137</i>)

>  <i>bung adj. Austral. sl. 1.</i> dead. <i>2</i>. destroyed.<i> 3. </i> useless. <i>4. go bung. a</i>. to fail or collapse.<i> b.</i> to die. [C19: < Abor.] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,145</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 94; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 63]]

>  [The toilet] provides an opportunity for adult time out from tired, excruciating conversations with the desperate and bung. <i>(Jun 3, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D9</i>)

>  It wouldn't be inappropriate to say he looks a bit <i>bung</i>. <i>(May 26, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  <b>TOOGOOD</b> I was worried that my bung eye would get me rejected as unfit. <i>(1979,Out of the Bag,38</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> We were bung, completely down and out. <i>(1948,This New Zealand,137</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i> esp. of parts of the body, injured, gammy, failed in use (a bung eye is a common collocation and may be partly

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Another noticeable thing about Cunamulla, was the large number of men with bung eyes, caused, apparently, by the little soldier flies missing their objective, when trying to sting their quarry, the field flies. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,249</i>)

>  <b>ADSETT</b> Pet's two front teats were what his father called `bung' but the back ones made up for them. <i>(1963,Magpie Sings, A,96</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I played one game too many and got a bung knee for life out of it; I was saying to Tommy Morrison the other day. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,189</i>)

>  <b>PORTER</b> ... I developed a very sore ankle which I must have twisted ... I didn't fancy living in a tent with a bung leg. <i>(1970,Nor'west Arch,61</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> The mechanism is faulty and will not function. .in 4;`You can't use that crane over there - it's bung. That new chap was trying it out - I think he's put it out of action.' See also p. 138 <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],132</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `... Why aren't you playing?' ... `Got a bung ankle. Don't want to hurt it again when thers's a big match next week.' <i>(1952,Julian Ware,8/68</i>)

### bung in
 
 <i>v.</i> to barge in, to rush in.

>  `What you've got to do is to keep up agin the tote and when I signal, bung in and get the div. quick,' a youth was explaining to his `cobber'. <i>(Jan 10, 1916,Triad, The,43</i>)

### bung it on
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bung it on</i> skite, exaggerate, behave temperamentally; common among antipodean shearers. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,23</i>)

### bung juice
 
 <i>n.</i> strong alcoholic liquor

>  <b>PICARD</b> ... got too full of bung juice, dropped dead ... <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A</i>)

### bung out
 
 <i>v.</i> to die

>  It's good policy to keep the premiums paid up. Rather-r-r-r, especially if it's the wife's, and she bungs out first. <i>(May 9, 1908,NZ Truth,151/1/6</i>)

### bung-a-bung
 
 <i>v.</i> to kill [Philip Temple e-mailed on 2/7/2000 that "On 12 March 1843 in Nelson, Te Rangihaeata told Arthur Wakefield that he would 'bung-a-bung' him if he went to the Wairau... In the DNZE p. 103 you deal with the word 'bung' which can mean become useless, broken, ruined and 'bung out' meaning to die. But your earliest reference for these words is 1887 and the origin is given as Australian pidgin. I wonder what the earliest Australian reference is and whether Rangihaeata would have picked it up from this source so early. It seems that if he was using it, then it already had some currency in NZ by then." Harry Orsman replied that "I think (and I can recall this from the limits of an imperfect memory) that this use in early Maori-Pakeha New Zealand relations was a pidgin word from 'bang(a)bang' meaning to shoot. It wasn't related to the Aboriginal word usually spelled 'bung' 'dead', etc."]

### bung-bung
 
 <i>n.</i> ? a killing (See [cf. bung-a-bung v](bri-byo.md#bung-a-bung))

>  [They] said that the Natta-row-cowas ... were collecting to make a bung-bung of them. <i>(Aug 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,235,1966 ed.</i>)

### bunga
 
 <i>n.</i> an Islander (See [bonga](bla-bri.md#bonga),[boonga](bla-bri.md#boonga))

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> [Skull'd] sunk piss with a bishop and mouthed more damns/ when the world didn't shape up than a bunga eats yams,/ <i>(1982,Rose Ballroom, The,28</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> choked, crammed, crowded

### bunger
 
 <i>n.</i> Anglicisation of Maori ponga (phs M. dialectal [p a], and now pronounced usually [ n ], [ ], but often in non-Maori districts [ ] etc: possibly the 19C. pronunciation was more commonly [ ] etc.)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bunga</i>, or <i>Bungy</i> ... settlers corruption of <i>punga.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,65</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> Bunger (Vern) Bunga <i>(1900,Our Stolen Summer</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen)]

>  <b>DOBBIE</b> <i>(1921,NZ Ferns</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen)]

>  <b>PARTR</b> <i>Bunga, bungy</i>. Punga (the stem of the black fern): NZ coll: mid 19-20C. Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,109</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,45</i>) [NOTES: [quotes Andersen Popular Names]]

>  <b>G TURNER</b> <i>(1932,Diary</i>) [NOTES: Dannevirke]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bunger</i>. Ponga (a New Zealand plant): NZ: since ca1860; slightly obs by 1926. B1941. By `Hobson-Jobson'. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1004</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Other Maori words in changed form are .. bunger (Maori ponga), a common name for the treefern <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,168</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sick farm animal.

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> A dead scrag of a ewe on flat; a bunger lamb that will not get over it. <i>(1926,Brabazon Diaries,7</i>)

### bungi(e)/bungy
 
 <i>n.</i> variants of ponga

>  <b>MORRIS</b> [cit above] <i>(1898,Austral English,65</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Bungy</i>. Tree fern. I have only heard this word used on the West Coast; it is just as often pronounced <i>pungy</i>. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>)

>  <i>Bungy</i>.-I understand from both West Coasters and North Islanders that this term is used throughout New Zealand (but so far as I know not in Canterbury) and that it is a corruption of a Maori word, <i>punga. </i> <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss. additions]

>  <b>PEARSON</b> bungy : West Coast corruption of Maori<i> ponga</i>, a kind of tree-fern. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,420</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> The principal building material used [on the Southland bush farms c1900] was fern-tree, known locally as bungie, upright on some walls, horizontal on others ... <i>(1953,Chaslands,11</i>) [NOTES: [Southland]]

>  <b>MAY</b> the ramshackle shanty-town of calico tents and `bungy' stores on the Hokitika sandspit had a temporary air about it, <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,147,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> We rode through the bush to our bungi house [of tree-fern logs]. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,55</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### bungy
 
 <i>v.</i> (figurative) to drop

>  The elimination of title confusion had the most spectacular effect on the NZ Woman's Weekly which bungied from 36% coverage to 14.5% coverage of all adults. <i>(Feb 11, 1994,Independent (Auckland),9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a curved metal support to relieve the strain on a shearer's back

>  Some shearers set up a "bungy," a curved metal support on springs which takes some of the weight off the upper body and greatly relieves the back. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/84</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an elasticated cord or rope, used for securing baggage and in bungy-jumping; also short for bungy-jumping

>  We need cultural equivalents of the jet-boat and bungy. <i>(Jan 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  The man killed in a bungy-jumping accident in Auckland on Saturday was correctly attached to the bungy when he fell, police said yesterday. Thomas Wayne Hemi, 19, storeman, of Papakura, was killed after falling from a platform set up for bungy jumps. ... Mr Hemi's feet were correctly attached to the bungy when he fell, Mr Wood said. He would not comment on whether the bungy was faulty ... <i>(Feb 12, 1990,Dominion,1</i>)

>  A J Hackett's bungy enterprise began in Europe in 1967 when he threw himself 147 metres off the Pont de la Calle. <i>(Nov 30, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  The company didn't just pioneer commercial bungy; it pioneered safety standards in the industry, too, independently enforced by Standards NZ. <i>(Nov 30, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  I felt sorry for those taking up bungee jumping ...if there were differences of opinion on something as basic as spelling `bungee'. I've opted for `bungee'; but he said it could just as well be bungie, bungy, bunje, bunjie, bunjy and not be too far out. Also seems we lead the world in this newest of extraordinary sports. <i>(May 5, 1989,Contact (Wgtn),4</i>)

>  Then came the first death; last month a man was killed when something went wrong at Rainbows End, Manakau, and, in bungy-talk, he ate it. <i>(Mar 26, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

### bunk
 
 <i>n.</i> a shelf-bed built against a wall (orig. nautical as far as NZ use goes, taken over by immigrants from sailing-ship experience (cf. berth n.), and used now esp. by trampers);nsee also Maori bunk (trampers') [1951 <i>Bunk</i> still very much in use among trampers (cf. double-bunking) where it is the usual term for rough bedstead (usually attached to wall) in a tramping hut.] (See [cf. double-bunking](dou-dyn.md#double-bunking))

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... a `bunk', or wooden shelf, supported Colonel Wakefield's bed. 1845 <i>Ibid.</i> 1/330 For the information of those who do not know what a <i>bunk</i> is, I must explain that it is a bed place built against the wall of a house or ship. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/205</i>)

>  <b>H S CHAPMAN</b> The squatters' huts, [...] are native built whare's improved in their form and size by imitating our cottages. The sleeping place is a `bunk' or standing bed place as on board ship ... The whare consists of one large room with a bunk in each corner - a standing table between each pair of bunks and one large clay chimney. <i>(Nov 24, 1847,Letters to His Father, 1843-1851</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Nov 24 H.S. Chapman Letters to His Father [TS,ATB] in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 140]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> On our arrival, the only quarters we could procure were two rooms of a weather-boarded house, ... The architect had probably been a ship's carpenter, for he had fitted up one of the rooms with tiers of [9] sleeping places of the form called by sailors bunks. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,8</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> John jobbing - Harry putting up Bunk in back room - <i>(Oct 30, 1855,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,18</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... Laying ourselves down for the night on the beaches or bunks [in an accommodation-house]. <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,32</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> It [the run manager's residence] consisted at this time of only one small slab hut, with four bunks and a chimney; a couple of hundred yards away was an old wool shed, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,24</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Station <i>bunks</i> - that is wooden bed-frames of the simplest and rudest construction, with a sacking bottom. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,256</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> The bunks - rude structures of bush poles, with flour bag sacking - had been torn down; and clothing ... heaped pell-mell on the floor. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/iv/85,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> As he strode into the kitchen now, men near his bunk made way for him. <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,57,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FOSTON</b> [New chum speaks] `What is a bunk, sir?' `Oh, a rough bedstead.' <i>(1921,At The Front,54</i>)

>  <i>Bunk</i>. A bed, whether made of wood in a hut, like a berth in a ship, or of fern and tussock in a tent. ([1874 Lady Barker Station Life], p.34) <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> It is Sunday. We rose later than usual. There are five men sleeping in the hut. I sleep in a bunk on one side of the fire, Mr. Haast ... sleeps upon the opposite one; my bullock-driver and hut-keeper have two bunks at the far end of the hut, along the wall; ... The usual mutton and bread for breakfast with a pudding made of flour and water baked in the camp-oven after a joint of meat - Yorkshire pudding, but without eggs. While we were at breakfast, a robin perched on the table and sat there a good while pecking at the sugar. <i>(Apr 1861,Note</i>) [NOTES: 1861 April Butler's Note in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler - A Memoir 1/87 April, 1861 -]

>  Built grate in fireplace; put up bunk for bullock drivers. <i>(Jul 1, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1861 July 1 Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 78]

>  <b>WALKER</b> ... a tolerable shakedown in a woodshed fitted with bunks. <i>(1863,Journal,11</i>)

>  <b>LADY CAMPBELL</b> The beds [of whalers' huts], or, as they are called, `bunks', are arranged after the fashion of the berths in a ship, two deep, and curtained. <i>(1864,Martin Tobin,1/189</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> We peeped in at the men's huts ... two rows of `bunks' (berths, I should call them). <i>Ibid.</i> 90 [The bushman] began to make plans for F- and me to stop [at back-country station hut] all night, offering to give up his `bunk' (some slabs of wood made into a shelf, with a tussock mattress and a blanket). <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,34,1874 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> There were some jokes going round that night in the two-roomed shack full of bunks. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,75</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The ship term `bunk' was applied to the beds built against the walls in the men's huts. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  <i>Bunk</i>, probably American in origin, was early in use in New Zealand. Edward Jerningham Wakefield felt it necessary to describe it as a `wooden shelf' for his readers, when he found it in use here. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua</i>) [NOTES: [see also MAORI BUNK (trampers')] Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> In the [mustering] hut were two bunks, a tucker box, an old frying pan, various billies and a teapot. <i>(1969,Wild Irishman,107</i>)

>  <i>bunk</i>: The name for a shearer's bed. Years ago shearer's bunks were often in three tiers and made up with straw filled palliasses for mattresses and borrowed woolpacks for warmth. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/22</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  <b>G.B. Lancaster (contributor)</b> Think I'll go to bunk. <i>(Dec 1903,Huia, The,12</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to sleep, go to bed (with) (constructions: bunk with, in with, down with (a person); bunk down, `go to bunk'); earlier literal use on sheep-stations and in men's camps: but modern (ca?1945) widened.

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> He was bunking in with Jimmy Burns in the big hut. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,226</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The single men ate in the cookhouse and bunked singly or in groups, in huts. <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,15</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to carry (a person) on a bicycle (bar)

>  <b>ADSETT</b> I'm not fussy so long as she keeps her skirts out of the bloody spokes. Last [girl] I bunked home took me hours to unwind her. <i>(1963,Magpie Sings, A,175</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <i>bunk</i>: to miss school <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>WILKINSON</b> `Truancy' is by no means an objective word ... `Why are you bunking?' I said to a Maori pupil I met in the Square an hour before school officially finished. `I'm waiting for a bus, sir.' `Well, you're still bunking' ... `Sir, I'm on my way home. Bunking is when you hang around the square instead of going to school or home'. <i>(1981,NZ Way, The,24</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> any bed, esp if makeshift; `the sack'. [ca1945 at least, among university undergraduates and others e.g. I'll make up a bunk for you on the floor.]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Hunting pignuts on all fours/ In the great funfair none do well./ Tom likes boys and Bob likes whores/ And both will share a bunk in Hell. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,237,1961 ed.</i>)

### bunnies are hovering
 
 <i>n.</i> a drinking game

>  Other well-known [drinking] games are Hokonui Swindle, Bottles, Fluffy Ducks, Bunnies are Hovering, Cardinal Huff, All Blacks, Matches, and The Amazing Grimaldi Brothers. All these games involve either a complicated intellectual exercise, tongue twisters, mathematical calculations or aerobatics in a confined space; High Candelabras for example, involves intoxicated individuals attempting to traverse the four walls of a room without touching the floor. <i>(Mar 13, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,16</i>)

### bunny
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) (female, widgie); Cuba Street Yank, (milkbar cowboy). ca1949-1950 for long-haired, curiously loudly dressed street-corner louts (with their female counterparts). [The Australian terms became suddenly popular (e.g. at Wellington Training College) after Feb-March 1951, after an article in the `Southern Cross' (and `Pix`). In 1952, although the Australian terms were known they were not used much. bunny (i.e. a long-eared one; descriptive of two long swish backs on either side of head) was perhaps by 1953 not so popular as milkbar cowboy or Cuba Street Yank.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a hard-case; character



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BUNNY (n) Stupid incompetent person. British slang since early 20th century. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,246</i>)

>  Then, on the other side of the coin from the `apathetic students', there is a group which decries the administration I have talked about as `bourgeois, capitalist and politically unsound'. They want to advance Students Associations as bases for radical political movements rather than `conservative' servicing of the membership; students. There are a number of descriptive terms - `loonies', `bozos', `bunnies' and `touchie-feelies' - in general, the `looney left'. <i>(Sep 29, 1986,Salient,2</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [1984 14 F E139Wgton Girls C. 25 BUNNIES [new kid] -->1953 14 M A4 Thames DHS 28 BUNNY [girl as a sex object]]

>  <i>bunny</i>: young person <i>disco bunny</i>: young teenager <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>MANTELL</b> So you're the bunny coming up from Wellington. Thought you were driving up. [policeman to another policeman] <i>(1983,Murder to Burn,155</i>)

### burden
 
 <i>n.</i> the tops or heads of stream-work which lie over the stream of tin.: check with EDD: ?is overburden used in this way???]

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Burden</i>: The amount of ground to be broken by a charged blasthole. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),313</i>) [NOTES: Glossary of Waihi Mining Terms]

### bureau manager
 
 <i>n.</i> the wharf labour system manager

>  The Lyttelton waterfront has been working under the bureau system of engaging labour for nine years now. ... `And,' the bureau manager said, `the men wouldn't like to go back to the old way again.' Under the old system, foremen of the various shipping companies stood on a small platform or block with the men awaiting employment before them and nominated those they required to work their own ships. The trouble with this `auction block' system, as it was called, was that each company tended to give preference to a certain group of men. It engaged the same men first all the time and employed others only when the preferred group were all at work. This meant very often the available work on the waterfront was not evenly shared; and that's what the bureau system tries to avoid. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,91</i>)

### bureau system
 
 <i>n.</i> a system for hiring wharf labour which replaced the auction block system.

>  The Lyttelton waterfront has been working under the bureau system of engaging labour for nine years now. ... `And,' the bureau manager said, `the men wouldn't like to go back to the old way again.' Under the old system, foremen of the various shipping companies stood on a small platform or block with the men awaiting employment before them and nominated those they required to work their own ships. The trouble with this `auction block' system, as it was called, was that each company tended to give preference to a certain group of men. It engaged the same men first all the time and employed others only when the preferred group were all at work. This meant very often the available work on the waterfront was not evenly shared; and that's what the bureau system tries to avoid. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,91</i>)

### burg
 
 <i>n.</i> a place, locality; town; in 1976 cit `house' [an Americanism, consolidated for a time in active NZ usage by the influence of the `talkies'. I can remember it as a very popular slang word at Havelock School, ca1935-1940, but I have since heard it only occasionally used (and then only humorously) since.]

>  I am in Westport, a small burg about 650 km south of Auckland. <i>(Jul 26, 1998,Sunday Star Times,F9</i>)

>  <b>EDDIE ATTWOOD</b> After a long day's railway travelling broken by a stop at Taihape and again at Marton, which allowed us time to become acquainted with those rising burgs, we arrived safely back in the bosom of the family. <i>(Apr 23, 1932,Diary,35</i>) [NOTES: Eddie Attwood's Diary in NZ Memories No. 36, June-July, 2002, p. 35]

>  <b>CLYDE</b> In smaller towns there is sometimes indifference to anything outside `the murmur of their burg.' <i>(1925,New Zealand: Country and People,47</i>) [NOTES: [with reference to films]]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The men and women in this little burg ... <i>(1927,Old Savage,167</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> They don't need cops in this town. Girl guides could control this burg. <i>(1974,Predicament,60,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> `What say we go down to my burg and scoff some home-brew ...' <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,29</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Coupla years. Must be. When I first came to this burg.' <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,131</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> TINY: ... not as if this [prison camp] is a submarine or something. JACKO: Might as well bloody be. Locked up in this burg. <i>(1985,Shuriken,21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a burglary [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  Did some more burgs and went back inside. <i>(2007,Best New Zealand Fiction, Vol 4, The,41</i>) [NOTES: 'The Feijoa is Brother to the Guava', David Eggleton, 2007]

>  "Lock me up" he cried. "I've done a burg." <i>(Dec 24, 2004,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  He got in with the wrong crowd, and went on "burgs" with them... He quit the mob, but kept on with the burgs. <i>(Jan 14, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  I got in with mob kids and started crime... I wasn't going to do it again after my first burg but it gets to you, it's exciting,so I did it again. <i>(Sep 16, 1997,Dominion</i>)

>  He was had up for burgs (burglaries) and assault on a girl (not me!) <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  The other thing that makes you exasperated with the Sunday Theatre you were so looking forward to is the Kathy solution to everything - go do a burg and really big one that all your friends and lovers are involved in and which as sure as night follows day goes all terribly wrong and everybody dies except for you. <i>(Jul 31, 2001,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  "You can do heaps of burgs without being sent to jail," a 15-year-old boy said. <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Evening Post,5</i>)

 <i>n.</i> also as a nickname ?from Burglar Bill.

>  <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23./208</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

### Burgon and Ball
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>MEEK</b> BURGON AND BALL: Hand shears with a single bow. Present-day shears have a double bow. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <i>Burgon and Ball</i>: Brand of sheep-shears with a single bow. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/22</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### burgoo
 
 <i>n.</i> coarse porridge (esp. oatmeal porridge), often in later use, humorously, for `porridge'. [ca1941-1945 R.C. Prison use for `coarse porridge' ca1950s Les Cleveland and N.C.C. Small <i>burgoo</i> for `porridge' common among trampers and mountaineers]

>  A waiter is usually supposed to be familiar with such democratic breakfasts as "burgoo" and ham and eggs, such dinners as corned beef and carrots, and plum duff. <i>(Jun 2, 1920,NZ Free Lance,29</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> It knocks burgoo on the head / When you've got no blooming bread. <i>(1987,P.O.W.,52</i>) [NOTES: Quoting Burfield-Mills, 1942]

>  <b>HODDER</b> Breakfast ... generally consisted of a pot of bergou each - a farinaceous dish, known by the Scotch as `porridge' and by the English as `chicken's food.' <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,7</i>)

>  The [prisoners] partake of ... a pewter plateful of porridge, or bergoo. <i>(1864,Saturday Review,4/15</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> Though rationed off for a short time to a pannikin of burgoo morning and evening ... we ran short ... <i>Ibid.</i> 112 [Goldfields] Boiling our billy of burgoo for tea. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,111</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> ... `burgoo' [is] oatmeal porridge. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,197</i>)

>  <b>McHUTCHESON</b> [<i>Burgoo</i> very frequent] <i>(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland,(passim)</i>)

>  With the long list of Labor [sic] members in mind since the time of Ballance, when McLaren was eating burgoo on his native heath, people looked for an explanation of this statement. <i>(Mar 26, 1910,NZ Truth,248/4/5</i>)

>  Burgoo : porridge (used by seamen). <i>(1941,Troopship Tattoo H.M.T. 32,9</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> [The Scot's] home menu of burgoo and remnants. <i>(1946,Amuri,112</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Ya couldn't get work shovellin burgoo to a Scotchman. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,43</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> <i>burgoo</i> porridge <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,95</i>)

>  <i>burgoo</i>: An old term for porridge. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/22</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  I want 'em for the "bergou" in the morning. <i>(Jun 7, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/45 : 204</i>)

>  See-saw, Johnny McGraw Ate Burgoo till he strained his jaw. <i>(Aug 16, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/50 : 37</i>)

>  A propos of the Scot and his "burgoo." <i>(Jan 16, 1904,Free Lance,13</i>)

>  Oscar, a dark horse, surprised everyone by a cooking demonstration of a "burgoo." <i>(1937,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,VI/68</i>)

>  <b>BLAIR</b> Or like to hungry Scots' burgoo - / Off to the Diggings. <i>(1889,Lay of the Old Identities, The,34</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a refreshing drink made of oatmeal.

>  <b>COULSON</b> Lime juice went out of fashion owing to complaints of heartburn, and oatmeal and water, or 'burgoo' as it was called, became the universal drink. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,37</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LYON</b> Some of the old country farmers supplied beer with the food [for mowers], but as a general rule, excesses led to tea or water from soaked oatmeal, called burgoo, as being more suitable. <i>(1952,Faring South,122</i>)

>  Sailors [sic] word for porridge. Here the word is applied to the oat-meal and water taken to the woolshed or harvest field to be drunk between smoke-ohs. I have also heard it called <i>beer</i> and <i>brose</i>. (C.) <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (See [puriri](pop-pyo.md#puriri))

### burke
 
 <i>v.</i> to choke, strangle [see SOED]

>  <b>BURGESS</b> Sullivan said: `It was a pity; he was such a nice young fellow. I then made him sit down and there we strangled him. ...' [105] Sullivan in his revelation of the tragedy said, `We burked him.' <i>(1866,Confessions,104,1983 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to avoid (some difficulty or unpleasantness)

>  If every man with a wife could burke his deservings there wouldn't be a bachelor in the country. <i>(1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,IX/302</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> ... came rain in ruled lines that caused Clint to burk the soaked fern [-bed] and crouch over the fire. <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,179</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> He had saved his craven life at the undercut bluff when it parted the mob wayback in the dry creek, and had so burked all the work that came after. <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,30</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> I'll exchange [my job] ... But I won't burk. <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,45</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>burk</i> to avoid work, c.1880-1920; G.B. Lancaster in <i>Sons o' Men: `I'll exchange ... but I won't burk, see?' [plagiarised from P8] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,23</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to jib (at), baulk (at) (possibly a var. of <i>baulk</i> in this sense). [ca1940, and not infrequent in modern usage. e.g. He burked at paying the ten shillings]

>  The average New Zealander, still endowed with the sturdy independence and the strong individuality of his pioneer forebears, is inclined to birk at restrictions as what he may do - particularly with his property. <i>(Feb 1951,NZ Inst. Arch. Journal,18/23</i>)

### Burke's bin
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also skip](sho-spa.md#skip))

>  He needs to prove the party is a collection of complementary talents, not a motley Burkes [sic] Bin. <i>(Apr 13, 1997,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  `GET YOURSELF a Burke,' he says. `I've already got a boss,' I say. `But does he help you shift rubbish?' he asks ... And then I realise we are at cross purposes. In my time I've met plenty of Right Burkes (they invariably wear walkshorts) and even one Left Burke (who's the Member for West Coast), but the Burke's bin is a newy on me. <i>(Dec 4, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> And if they hadn't made up their quarrel there'd be no Ida to have in turn, ... and no Selby to rummage in the Burkes bins around the city streets. <i>(1986,Address to a King,57</i>)

>  <i>Worst Case Scenario</i>.. gave one that distinct feeling that helpful handy hints such as how to jump off a burning building and into a dumpster, or what we'd call a Burke's bin, and survive a 35-foot leap, was a telly show response to September 11. <i>(Mar 18, 2003,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  It started over a Burke's bin in the wrong place, the violence the more terrifying for its apparent unpredictability. <i>(Oct 26, 1991,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  The other side of the 'Burke's bin case' was pretty awful, too. <i>(Oct 26, 1991,Evening Post,25</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [berko](b-bla.md#berko))

### burl
 
 <i>n.</i> a spin; a go; a try [ca1940 2. give it a burl]

>  Something's got to be done to encourage the right class of hommes to give it a burl. <i>(Jan 22, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/187 : 4</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> And so while [my wounded leg] was still ugly and they weren't worrying I'd give it a burl. And I made it, got clean away. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,227</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Come round, she said, it'll be open slather. Might [181] as well give it a birl. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,180</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> MRS JOHNSON ...: I don't know how I'll sing without a piano. JOHNSON: Give it a burl, old girl. <i>(1963,Pohutukawa Tree,44</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He'll give it a burl, that boy, I'll give him that in. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,162</i>)

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> He's all right. We'll give it a burl for a while. <i>(1976,Travelling Man,21</i>)

>  <i>burl </i>or <i>birl n. Austral. & N.Z. inf. 1.</i> a ride in a car. <i>2</i>. an attempt; try (esp. in <i>give it a burl</i>). [C20: ? < BIRL in Scots sense: to spin or turn] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,146</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 95; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 64]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>give it a birl/burl</i> make an attempt; from causing the coin to spin in the game of two-up; also <i>give it a go</i> and <i>give it a pop</i>; eg `If nobody else will ride the damn horse, I'll give it a birl/go/pop.' Mostly NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The fleeces should not be tied up with strings; the lint mixes with the wool, and makes what are called `burls;' no tying is requisite. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/393</i>) [NOTES: 1857 H.F.Tiffin in Hursthouse NZ 2/393]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [1980 Goldie Brown (Auck) burl: Common in NZ pine timber.]

>  BURL. A tumour-like woody swelling on the trunk or branch of a tree. Syn. E. Burr. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### Burma Road
 
 <i>n.</i> used humorously and figuratively for various local, ill-formed rough roads or tracks; a hilly back-country road [[W. Ramson cf road from Wangaehu Valley to Katikati, Karioi locally known as `the Burma'; occas. fig. in Aust; nf W3.]]

>  ... the far more valuable bituminous field opened up at Stockton is served by a road whose description is the ominous half-truth, the `Burma Road'. <i>(1948,NZ Geographer,164</i>) [NOTES: (Pascoe `Coal from Buller')]

### burn
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A Foundation for Alcohol and Drug Education workshop.. will look at the growing problem of methamphetamine, otherwise known as P, pure, burn, crystal or ice. <i>(Aug 6, 2003,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  Methamphetamine (called Pure, P, Crystal Meth, Burn or Crank) is chemically only slightly different to amphetamine - generally the effects are the same, but it is more potent. <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to smoke (a cigarette) [1984 17 M E117Pakuranga Coll. 33 BURNS [illegal smoking] -->2. A fall. -->1984 16 F E26 Pakuranga Coll. 16 BURN: OR A BURN [have a fall]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a stream; creek used esp. in 19C.; common in place-names of Otago and Southland: now obs. if not obs. NZ in speech.

>  <b>WILSON</b> [Central Otago - Wakatipu : (cf <i>creek</i>) NB not <i>wee</i>] Below us to the East the creek, a small tiny burn, rushes and tears away over crags and stones making a great noise evidently trying hard to make itself important, ... and so down to Mataura. <i>(Jan 26, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 26 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 152]

>  ... the burns coming in from the hills ... in searching the banks of several burns on the other side of the Taieri. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/133</i>) [NOTES: (Booth)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> We have crossed several burns of respectable size and many minor creeks during our journey, but we have now to cross a river ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,132</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> There are now several bonnie burns coming down the mountain sides to the river, the largest of which are the famous Roaring Meg and Gentle Annie; and a visitor ... may wash his colours of gold from almost anywhere while his wee wife boils the billy. <i>Ibid.</i> 69 I was invited ... to stop the weekend [near Cromwell], and as Donald was going out ... to try the wash in a nearby burn, I thought it a great idea. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,20</i>) [NOTES: [local]]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On the other side of the [Taieri] river the flow of `animal' burns [Ewe, Gimmer, Sow, etc.] is interrupted by the intrusion of a mere `creek', even if it is named Eden after the place where Adam named all the animals. <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,36</i>)

>  This river is fed by the Swine and other burns. <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/271</i>) [NOTES: (Beal)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (an instance of) clearing bush, scrub, unpalatable grass, etc. from the land by means of fire to improve pasture or to bring into cultivation by clearing logs or bush. (There seems a distict cleavage between early South Island and early NI usage, cf. burn v1. In the South Island `a burn' was a periodical burning off of old tussock to prepare the way for good crop of new grass. In 20th century usage, however, has been applied (e.g. in the Pelorus Sounds) to burning off noxious weeds, fern, scrub etc. to get a good growth of green feed (cf. match-farming). In the North Island, `a burn' was chiefly applied to clearing away (felled) bush by burning, a burning off (or over) of unwanted bush: that is, to clearing or bringing land into cultivation, not merely to improving pasture. burn off seems chiefly used in this North Island sense (cf bush-burning)) (See [also bush burn](bri-byo.md#bush-burn))

>  <b>BARKER</b> [The wind] must not be <i>too</i> violent, or the flames will fly over the grass, just scorching it instead of making `a clean burn.' <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,194,1870 ed.</i>)

>  The time for felling the bush in order to ensure a good burn in at [prob. typo.:<i> in</i> not deleted] the proper burning season - which is generally most favourable about the beginning of February - is from August to October. <i>(Oct 1877,Country Life</i>) [NOTES: 1877 Oct NZ Country Journal (Kelly) quoted in 1958 NZJ Ag 96 (4): 375]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> ... an amount of small wood is thus secured to guarantee a `good burn', ... <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,231</i>)

>  <b>KELLY</b> The bush is felled carefully so as to give a good burn. <i>(1892,Journey Through Waitara,20</i>)

>  Everything depends on a successful burn. <i>(1850,Otago Journal,6/108</i>)

>  The fire if it is a good burn destroys all the small stuff, leaves, and burns the surface of the ground well. <i>(Nov 10, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/158,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H.A. Atkinson)]

>  We got the burns over ...[sic] James & Arthur fired ours after nine o'clock on the night of the 15th. <i>(Feb 25, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1960,1/356 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Jane M. Atkinson)]

>  Arthur is going to make a clearing on our land this year, and ...[sic] after the burn would put up a slab house of two rooms which might serve afterwards for cowshed or kitchen or workshop ... `Bird's heart is in the bushland, his heart is not here.' <i>(Aug 8, 1855,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/172,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Jane M. Atkinson)]

>  <b>FULLER</b> Grass springing up after a burn. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,168</i>)

>  ... while the Australian bush recovers more or less from the burn that of New Zealand perishes ... <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/492</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

>  <b>BELL</b> The season promised to be a good one for bush-burning; and those who had bush-felled were congratulating themselves on the prospect of getting excellent `burns,' while many, who, ... had been unfortunate in getting bad `burns,' were now hopeful of clearing off much of the timber by means of a second fire. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,198</i>)

>  There is very little logging-up done now-a-days, and with a good burn it is quite unnecessary. ... two or three chains around the site should be logged up and burnt. <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,21</i>)

>  The swampy nature of the soil ... and the too abundant rains make a successful burn impracticable. <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/352</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

>  ... the same good quality of sheep pasture that in the past had given this district an excellent name as a fertile country immediately following the `burn.' <i>(1929,TrNZI,59/406</i>) [NOTES: (Aston)]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The `burn' of 1902 ... was not what is technically known as a `clean' fire. ... this fire left unburnt ridge-caps, the tops, sometimes even the upper slopes. <i>(1926,Tutira,170</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> The big burn was the spectacular event for which their visit was timed. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,55</i>)

>  These [sown pastures] may be divided into two groups - (a) sown on cultivated land, and (b) surface sown after forest, scrub or fern burn. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,359</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> A bad burn [of cleared bush] may spell ruin to a man with limited capital. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,25</i>)

>  Burning hard fern should not be a regular practice, but often good burns can be made in spring and sometimes in autumn and if the burns [n2] can be oversown and the pasture well-managed afterward, it is a help in the control of hard fern. <i>(May 1958,NZ Journal of Agriculture,96 (5): 457</i>) [NOTES: (Burgess)]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The `burn' of this bit of bush has not been a good one. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,31</i>)

>  <b>McLAUCHLAN</b> If the summer rains came and spoilt the burn, it meant a great loss for the farmer; once the leafage of fallen timber drops and becomes a soggy mess on the ground, it is almost impossible to get a successful burn. <i>(1981,Farming of NZ,177</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>burn</i> drive a vehicle fast, phr. <i>go for a burn, originally a bikie's burn-up; a burn off</i> to drive so fast you leave the competition behind; eg 'Let's go for a burn, eh?' `Dunno, don't think we could burn off old Scotty, now he's got that rebore.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fast car-ride; (from <i>burn the road up, burn the herbs</i>, etc.).

>  <i>Took car to go for `burn'.</i> an 18-year-old [Palmerston North] youth who converted a $1,300 Jaguar car and drove it ... [at] speeds of 110 miles an hour, told the Police he had wanted to go for a `burn' with his mates ... <i>(Aug 7, 1963,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> TAKE A SPIN in a motor vehicle. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,47</i>)

>  <i>to go for a burn</i>: to go for a drive in a car <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]



 
 <i>v.</i> (mainly NI) to clear or improve (land) by burning the standing or felled cover.

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Commenced burning on the flat on this side the creek up the Valley. <i>(1846,Journal,27</i>) [NOTES: [?to 2. S.I.??]]

>  As there was a nice breeze we lighted our clearings. Bill and the Ronalds also burnt - in all about 46 acres. I had not time to see how mine had burnt. <i>(Mar 10, 1856,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/201,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>BELL</b> A few, fearful of a change in the weather, had already burned with fairly satisfactory results; and the general topic of conversation ... was of bush-burning. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,198</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  They wanted to burn the large clearing that day. <i>(Feb 26, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/143,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> You will have burnt a large patch of feed at the outset. [152] Burn it in early spring, on a day when rain appears to be at hand. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,x/151-152</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> If the weather has been very dry ... we attempt to burn a great flax swamp. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,195,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> [sc. land] In the afternoon T.A.P. & W.P.P. burnt the faces round the Point. <i>(Sep 17, 1866,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,81</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to hit, beat up [1984 17 M E105Pakuranga Coll. 30A BURN (to hit, smash]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock or area where bush has been felled, undergrowth burned and grass sown.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Miller & Witherow over to new burn to get old cull dairy cows... <i>(Jan 30, 1929,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Drafting sheep from New Burn and Wade Creek etc. <i>(Mar 12, 1930,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  The new grass on the burns is always stocked with hoggets ... <i>(Dec 31, 1964,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> At first the homestead was just sitting in a bare paddock or burn. <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,25</i>)

>  Saw large mob of sheep on the new burn outside Hughie's fence. <i>(Feb 5, 1879,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> The new burn was looking well; the turnips, rape and mustard would feed a lot of sheep in a few month's time, and everywhere the grass was showing up well. <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,184</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Bidi-bidi came very thick on the new burns and it was a race to get the shearing done before it started to stick. <i>(2000,Mountain Farm, Bush Farm,22</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to fire (tussock grassland or scrub or fern country), to bring on new growth; to create feed by burning; to periodically fire pasture; burn off (the effect of the verb seems applied to the land or country rather than to the vegetation to be burnt); often in collocations to burn country, burn a run; (mainly or orig. SI)

>  Went to sheep and then tried to burn the run over Stoney creek but the grass was not close enough. <i>(Sep 22, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [Hocken TS]]

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> T.A.P. & W.P. burning the Rockwood run ... <i>(Aug 31, 1866,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,79</i>)

>  We are going to burn the country to prepare it for sheep. <i>(Sep 22, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,145,1950 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [sc. land]]

>  <b>BARKER</b> I am quite sorry that the season for setting fire to the long grass, or, as it is technically called, `burning the run', is fairly over at last. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,194,1874 ed.</i>)

>  ... they disappear as the country is burnt by the runholder ... <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/199</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> There are many things to be taken into consideration when burning. Climate - is your country early or late? Are you carrying ewes or wethers on the block that you propose to burn? In what state is your neighbour's country? If your country is warm and early, with a small rainfall, it is a good rule to burn early in the season ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,96,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When they got on to the Waimea Plains a dense smoke assailed them from fires set alight by newly-arrived runholders `burning the country' around the Five Rivers vicinity. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,49</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 49]

>  <b>HARPER</b> One day we went on an expedition [on Mt. Peel station] into the mountain valleys to `burn country', a process adopted here for the improvement of pasture. ... The fire spread up the hill-sides ... and soon covered a large extent of country, burning for several days, ... <i>(Sep 1, 1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,21,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> ... to burn a run. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,168</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an area of land cleared by fire.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Saw large mob of sheep on the new burn outside Hughie's fence <i>(Feb 5, 1879,Tutira,125</i>) [NOTES: source uncertain; seems a bit early for Tutira(DEH);[try also?Rockford or other Jnl; not Chudleigh]]

>  Perhaps to their position on the edge of a previous burn and of the later fire [the moa-bones] may in a measure owe their preservation. <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/504</i>) [NOTES: (White)]

>  This fern springs up ... on neglected burns. <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/79</i>)

>  On the `burn', under the `Judge's Chair', at 725 feet the <i>Dracophyllum scoparium</i> and <i>D. longifolium</i> are being replaced by<i> Poa litterosa</i> and <i>Bulbinella Rossii. </i> <i>(1912,TrNZI,44/23</i>) [NOTES: (Aston)]

>  This fern springs up in countless thousands on neglected burns. <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/79</i>) [NOTES: (Carse)]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Suddenly, like a slap in the face from an unseen hand, where one had looked for fresh wonders of forest beauty, spread a settler's recent burn! Gaunt and charred, prone on their mother's bosom, lay the mighty monarchs of the woods. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,147</i>)

>  The shearing muster should be made over the cleanest routes, contamination from Sources such as road-droving, dirty country, seed, heavy thistle, burns or blowing sand being avoided where possible. <i>(1957,NZ Journal of Agriculture,95 (4): 323</i>)

>  ... Pete crawled through the Wild Irishmen and forded the creek beside the shingle slip ... Then he boulderhopped along towards the burn. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>) [NOTES: [trampers' ? burnt part of country; ? creek]]

>  <b>McKENZIE</b> Settlers of a later generation discontinued the practice of clearing a new `burn' of all logs. <i>(1935,Gael Fares Forth, The,80</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Sowing the Burn [heading] <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,243,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> There is a good cheque you can earn, seeding a burn,/ Or cutting some cow-cocky's scrub./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,74</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> At first the homestead was just sitting in a bare paddock or `burn'. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,25</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  Went on the hills burning and Greenly lit a fire on a spur that had grass and flax six feet high on it. <i>(Oct 3, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,201,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> T.A.P. ... went towards Rockwood [run] in the afternoon to burn - ... <i>(Sep 7, 1866,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,80</i>)

### burn and graze
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [of land development] where fern and scrub is burned and stocked quickly to inhibit regrowth.

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> The burn-and-graze technique most commonly used in farms such as those in the Mohaka region during the nineteenth century was developed by Archdeacon Samuel Williams and J.H.Coleman, who discovered that fern could be burnt in January when the sap was up, and that regrowth would mature in about a month. <i>(1985,Farming in the Mohaka State Forest,7</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Because this expansion into marginal land was coupled with farming techniques - such as the 'burn and graze' method of running sheep - that led to the rapid degradation of the land, it was not long before farming became impossible in some areas... <i>(1985,Farming in the Mohaka State Forest,50</i>)

### burn back
 
 <i>v.</i> to burn material towards a bushline or grassfire or back into the path of an outdoor fire to control it. NZF Service 1954. (See [cf. back-burn v](b-bla.md#back-burn))

### burn off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to burn remains of bush, scrub and fern from land once timber or scrub has been cleared or to burn tussock on winter country, particularly in high country South Island.

>  Most of the settlers are "burning off" now, and the valleys are full of smoke. <i>(Jan 22, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,23</i>)

>  <b>DUPPA</b> Burn off portions of the run for winter feed to destroy tuft grass. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,45</i>) [NOTES: in Crawford (1949) Sheep and Sheepmen of Canterbury]

>  All bush ready for burning should be burnt off this month. <i>(Feb 26, 1870,Weekly News,20</i>)

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> Burning off. <i>(Mar 20, 1893,Brabazon Diaries</i>)

>  One or two attempts to burn off the rushes and sow down grass seed have proved unavailing. <i>(Jun 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

>  Should the fern be burnt off in mid-summer or in the autumn, the ensuing growth will be much less vigorous than the previous growth. <i>(1916,NZ Farmer,312</i>)

>  The fern is not burnt off. Mr Thacker considers burning unnecessary and harmful to the grass. <i>(Jul 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,10</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> After cutting the tea-tree and burning it off <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,18</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> After 1905, 600 acres were milled from heavy bush and sown down in English grasses after burning off. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol III,151</i>)

>  <b>FEARON</b> He burnt off the scrub at the base of the hill. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,212</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> He employed Maori to help him clear and then burn off the land. <i>(2001,Riding Country,54</i>)

>  <b>DILLON</b> The fern was all burnt off by the surveyors. <i>(Jan 16, 1843,Dillon Letters,17</i>) [NOTES: Letters Jan 16 (1954)]

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> Burning off. <i>(Mar 21, 1893,Brabazon Diaries</i>)

>  Following the intitial drainage work, the earlier farmers, after cutting down the remaining bush and scrub, burned off and surface sowed the land. <i>(Jan 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,11</i>)

>  Development land is usually covered with a preburn spray by aircraft, burnt off then root-raked with a bulldozer. <i>(May 12, 1993,NZ Farmer,25</i>)

### burn over
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to burn large areas of tussock land.

>  They [young tussocks] stop growing much earlier in the year than if they had not been burnt over. <i>(1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,9</i>)

>  One the winter country, in the spring, as soon as the ground is sufficiently dry, as much as possible is burnt over. <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> burn off v2.

>  The surface vegetation ... had been systematically burnt over, year after year, in order to encourage fresh growth for the use of stock. <i>(1881,TrNZI,14/77</i>) [NOTES: (Travers)]

### burn stain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a charcoal fleecewool stain of sheep grazed on a bush burn.

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> The years went by, the sheep ceased to have their fleeces classed as 'burn stain' ... <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,118</i>)

### burn the country
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [South Island tussock country] to burn tussock in late spring or early summer to promote new growth.

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> We are going to burn the country to prepare it for sheep. <i>(Sep 22, 1864,Chudleigh Diary</i>)

### burn the run
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [S.I. tussock country] to burn the long tussock in late spring or early summer to promote new growth.

>  <b>BARKER</b> I am quite sorry that the season for setting fire to the long grass, or, as it is technically called, "burning the run", is fairly over at last. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,194</i>)

### burn-off
 
 <i>n.</i> a burning off

>  The impact commenced centruries ago, with Maori burn-offs incinerating at least half of the island's primordial forest cover. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/20</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> In the afternoon burnt(?) off. Not an over excellent burn off - there having been of late a good deal of wet. <i>(Feb 27, 1861,Journal,28,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> In due time we fired [the bush] and had a clean burn off. <i>(1869,Guide to Farming in NZ,18</i>)

>  <b>MORTON</b> ... smoke of fresh burn-offs ... <i>(1928,Along the Road,64</i>)

>  So I ask my Dad about a burn-off, and we take the kerosine and matches and set the whole hillside alight. <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories III,141</i>) [NOTES: Mitcalfe `Black Cat']

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> We passed ... three Maori women ... stooping to cut puha from a manuka burn-off. `A readymade Van Gogh, ... Or rather The Puha Gatherers.' <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,18</i>)

>  ... the completion of the burn off. <i>(1850,Otago Journal,6/103</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Not so the farmers who roundly cursed the settler who had started them with an imprudent burn-off, the swagman boiling his billy or children playing with matches. <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,75</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> We sometimes read today of a large controlled "burn off". <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,49</i>)

>  <b>McLAUCHLAN</b> [Caption] After the burn-off, a settler in the Pohangina Valley sows grass among the tangle of stumps, roots and charred trunks of the dead bush. <i>(1981,Farming of NZ,173</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the practice of seasonal burning of high-country tussock and other grasses

>  Throughout September, burn-offs were carried out. <i>(Dec 1871,Longlands Station Diary</i>)

>  Only a fringe of river flat eases the strain of creating dairy pastures where the annual burn-off was once the recognised way to get a brief flush of green grass. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,25</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) stubble burning.

>  Farmers planning a burn-off can take advantage of the new computerised system. [burnoff; burn off] <i>(Nov 17, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,10</i>)

>  It warns would-be seekers of the good life that it can come with noise .. smell .. smoke (stubble burn-off) dust and spray. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,April/May 18</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a burning of scrub and bush prior to sowing in pasture

>  <b>MAY</b> In due time we fired it and had the satisfaction of having a clean burn-off. <i>(1869,May's Guide to Farming in NZ,18</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> Every farmer in our area seemed to require a wind from a different direction for his burn off. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,117</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Much of it remained in farmable condition, although there were areas where repeated burnoffs had taken their toll ... <i>(1985,Farming in the Mohaka State Forest,38</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> In the case of timber, that was often the direct result of the fires of the early Maori moa-hunters, and later, the extensive burn-offs of short-sighted European settlers. <i>(1993,Station Country I,24</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> .. in spite of some fairly good burn-offs, the scrub kept coming up again. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,60</i>)

### burn-out
 
 <i>n.</i> From the 1930s at least, according to W. Martin and Bob Adams of NZ Seamen's Union, a bad discharge from a ship, a transferred and figurative usage from stoking the furnace of a coal-burning ship. An inefficient or tyro stoker who built a bad fire had a `burn-out' in the furnace - i.e. the fire went partially out. He was allowed to get away with one of these, but on doing a second, was eased off the ship. Hence it came to mean a bad discharge in general.



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> Whether he prefers the burn-out to the wash-out I have not as yet been able to ascertain. <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,17</i>)

### burner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The truth or otherwise of the premises laid down by both burners and anti-burners has never been accurately determined. <i>(1916,TrNZI,48/163</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> graffiti

>  Graffiti Speak... Burner: A great piece. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

### burnet
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  burnet<i>: Kinds of brown-flowered plants. One sort called `Australian sheep's burnet', grows in Australia and is naturalized in New Zealand. </i> <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),55</i>)

>  <i>burnet, sheep's</i> [s] <i>Poterium polygamum</i> Waldst. and Kit. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,9</i>)

### Burnham bot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  An influenza epidemic, colloquially known as the 'Burnham bot', knocked some of the exuberance out of the troops in November [1939]. <i>(1955,Supply Company,4</i>)

### Burnham boy
 
 <i>n.</i> an inmate of Burnham Industrial School.

>  Burnham Boys - A Bad Training <i>(Sep 2, 1905,NZ Truth,11/8/3</i>)

>  Three more Burnham boys escaped last week. <i>(Jan 27, 1906,NZ Truth,32/1/5</i>)

>  <i>(Oct 5, 1907,NZ Truth,120/6/4</i>)

>  Go and ask some of those Burnham boys that you carry on with, and get out of the cell, and don't come near me again. [prisoner in Lyttelton jail to another (homosexual) prisoner]. <i>(Oct 5, 1907,NZ Truth,120/6/5</i>)

### burning off
 
 <i>n.</i> the burning of bush or felled trees

>  Lois began talking of the "burning-off." "It was a wonderful sight," she said. <i>(Nov 25, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  <b>C. CHAPMAN</b> As Xmas is the time for burning off, and the clearing must go on without his superintendence ... <i>(Nov 30, 1844,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Nov 30 C. Chapman in 1960 Drummond Married and Gone to NZ 69]

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> ... employed remainder of day in burning off. <i>(1847,Journal,87</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> From their skill [...] in using the American axe, clever management of fire, and knowledge of what is termed `burning off' they are found to be capital hands in the clearing for cultivation of bush land. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: 1853 C.F.Hursthouse NZ[??], The Emigration Field of 1851 14 in 1963 Apr NZ Geog 56 (Hargreaves)]

>  <b>FITTON</b> Considering the facility of breaking in land here, ... the clearing and burning off for the entry of the plough varying from ten to twenty shillings per acre ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),99</i>)

>  <b>RIDGWAY</b> The burning off should be driven as late in the season as it is safe to do. <i>(1860,Voices from Auckland,73</i>)

>  ... the soil being mostly alluvial, the `burnings-off' and grass fostering influence of stock will rapidly improve it. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,134,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> FALLING [bold] is done in winter, and burning off in the middle or end of summer ... <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,232</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> When they've done the burning off, the aeroplanes are going to come in and plant the grass. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,154</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `Those concrete posts must be good for burning-off.' <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories III,212</i>) [NOTES: 1973 Henderson `The Dead Bush' In 1975 NZ Short Stories III 212]

>  The burning-off of the alpine scrub and the bush in the gullies ... would deprive the bird of its normal food ... <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/82</i>)

>  <i>*Burnt chops</i>.-An expression somewhat akin to the expression <i>*Burnt Spur</i>. which denoted the business of high country mustering: `So and so has taken to the <i> b.s.'</i>. meaning he has taken on mustering. <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss additions]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the burning of tussock.

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> Unfortunately, too, burning off became part of the tradition of hill country sheep farming in New Zealand. <i>(1952,History of Northern Southland,38</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Burning off has for many years past been a tool of management, used by runholders and graziers ... <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,192</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the burning of fallen scrub and bush.

>  <b>PAUL</b> Fern land requires no previous labour (except burning off) to prepare it for the plough. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,97</i>)

>  The good seasons we have experienced for burning off has given bushfarmers confidence and there will be some large contracts let by private individuals. <i>(Jun 28, 1898,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  When grass was sown in the ashes after the burning off of the scrub, the strike usually was excellent and the first season a good bite of feed was thrown. <i>(Jul 15, 1947,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

### burning season
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dry summer that allows felled bush to be burned thoroughly.

>  We have experienced good burning seasons for the past three seasons and it is to be hoped that next summer's burns will be as good as last year's. <i>(Nov 3, 1888,Egmont Star,15</i>)
