>  The time for felling the bush in order to ensure a good burn in at the proper burning season - which is generally most favourable about the beginning of February - is from August to October. <i>(Oct 1877,NZ Country Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1877 Oct NZ Country Journal (Kelly) quoted in 1958 NZJ Ag 96 (4): 375]

>  I once discovered a pigeon's nest near a bush-felling camp, ... and before the burning season had arrived they had successfully hatched ... three young ones ... <i>(Nov 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,112</i>)

### burnt feed
 
 <i>n.</i> juicy, tender grass that springs up from the ashes of a burn or burn-off (cf. burn off v.)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Burnt feed means contented and well-conditioned sheep. ... Your sheep will not ramble, for if they have plenty of burnt pasture they are contented where they are. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,x/152</i>)

### burnt quartz
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COOPER</b> We commenced operations, and discovered ... several fine leaders containing burnt quartz. Our neighbours as they passed all congratulated us on our prospects, but we shall see. <i>(Nov 7, 1867,Digger's Diary,10,1978 ed.</i>)

### burnt up
 
 <i>adj.</i> burned dry, scorched 1828 to c1960

### burnt-off
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> This was burnt-off country, black-and-green on top, blue papa underneath, too poor for the grass to be more than a bristling crop. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,128,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> Ploughing burnt-off land proved `a sorry business for horses', until he put a heavy piece of leather next to the hoof before he nailed on the shoe. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,27</i>)

### burnt-over
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Sc. bush.)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There were the scrublands of blackened second growth, ... the reproachful columns of burnt-over bush ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,20</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] burned but not cleared of bush or scrub.

>  Burnt-over areas with logs and bracken adjacent to pasture are especially favourable for hares. <i>(Dec 1964,NZ Journal of Agriculture,483</i>)

>  <b>LANDS & SURVEY</b> This situation persisted until 1950 when the possibilities of using aircraft for sowing seed and manure on burnt-over hill country became apparent. <i>(1975,Prosperity from Pumice Land,13</i>)

### burr
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also burl](bri-byo.md#burl))

>  <b>MASSEY</b> <i>Burr</i> Contorted growth on the tree trunk closest to the stump. <i>(1987,Woodturning in New Zealand,93</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a burr-bearing plant; in New Zealand, especially biddy-bid. 1. Native <i>Acaena</i> spp. esp <i>Acaena sanguisorbae</i> biddy-bid, hutiwai; an almost generic term in popular usage in early 1840s-1860s usage; replaced by biddy-bid, but still a usual general term in ref. to wool. a. Simplex : common bur(r); also native, New Zealand burr.

>  <b>WHITE</b> <i>Acaena Sanguisorbae </i> <i>(1896,Ancient History of the Maori</i>) [NOTES: (And) -->[c1833]]

>  Another advantage to be found is the absence of <i>burrs</i> in the New Zealand runs. <i>(1846,NZ Journal,IV/173/195</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [quoting from Wellington Almanack 1846, 1848] Another advantage is to be found in the absence of <i>burrs</i> in the New Zealand runs; [fn] in New South Wales these are found to tear the wool, ... [1848 fn] In some parts of New Zealand there does exist a small <i>burr</i> , called <i>otiwai;</i> ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,123</i>)

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> ... burn[ed] off a lot of burr on top of ridge. <i>(1849,Journal,234</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> Another advantage is to be found in the absence of <i>burrs</i> in the New Zealand runs; in New South Wales these are found to tear the wool ... <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Our camping place was on a bed of burrs which covered [our] blankets all over. <i>(1854,Journal,8/165</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Burr. <i>Acaena</i> . <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>)

>  Acaena Sanguisorbae Burr <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/124</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> Hutiwai [Maori name] .. Burr [Settlers' name] .. Acoena sanguisorbae <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Piripiri [Maori name] .. Burr [Settlers' name] .. Acoena sanguisorbae ..Herb.. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,177</i>)

>  <i>Acaena Sanguisorbae </i> <i>(1907,AJHR,C-8</i>) [NOTES: (And)1907 Cockayne Report on Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>ACAENA ... sanguisorbae ... var. A.</i> ... Burr, Biddi-biddi. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,31</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FEATON</b> ACAENA SANGUISORBAE (<i>Vahl</i> .) The Native Burr. ... The `HUTIWAI', or `PIRIPIRI'.- ... an infusion of the leaves is said to afford a substitute for tea, but it is the opinion of the writer that the results are not commensurate with the trouble of brewing. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,125</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Hence the plant [<i>Acaena sanguisorbae</i> ] is only too familiar, as the `Native Burr' to the sheep farmers alike of New Zealand, Australia, and Tasmania. ... To the Otago settler the plant is also known as the `Bidi-bidi,' or `Beta-beta;' to the North Island Maori as `Hutiwai,' `Piri-piri,' (Colenso), or `Piri-kahu,' (Colenso). <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,59</i>)

>  [NZ BURR] <i>Acaena Sanguisorbae</i> <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: Turner Botanical Examination of the Higher Waimarino District]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>new zealand bur n, usu cap N & Z</i> : a creeping mat-forming New Zealand plant (<i>Acaena microphylla</i> ) that is sometimes cultivated for its grayish to rosy bronze foliage and showy crimson spines <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1524</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Acaena novae-zelandiae</i>

>  <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: 1909 Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island (And)]

>  <i>(1911,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Cockayne Report on the Dune-areas of NZ in AJHR C-13 (And)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Acaena Sanguisorbae</i> var. minor.

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... you will see [on Disappointment Island in the Auckland group] ... patches of vivid green. This last arises from the presence of the antarctic burr (<i>Acaena Sanguisorbae</i> , var antarctica</i> ). <i>(1910,NZ Plants,118</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>bur [=] Australian sheep's bur: Bathurst bur: beggars' ticks: bur daisy: burdock: bur medick: cobblers' pegs: spotted bur medick: piripiri: woolly bur medick</i> [s]] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,9</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> false bidi bid,piri piri

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SHEEP'S BURR (<i>Acaena ovina</i> ), called also Australian burr, false bidi bid, and very loosely piri piri or bidi bid, is not uncommon in fields and waste places in certain localities of both islands, but is commoner in the north. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,99,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> ... in the wool of sheep, as Australian burr (<i>Acaena ovina</i> ). <i>(1926,Tutira,245</i>)

>  <i>Australian sheep's bur</i> [s] <i>Acaena ovina</i> A. Cunn. <i>Ibid.</i> 69 <i> sheep's bur [=] Australian sheep's bur</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,3</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> AUSTRALIAN SHEEP'S BUR (<i>Acaena ovina</i> ) ... is widespread in New Zealand, frequently seen in poor pasture and on roadsides. <i>(1981,Weeds of Lawns,9</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>bur, buffalo </i> [s] <i>Solanum rostratum</i> Dunal. ... <i> buffalo, bur</i> [s] <i>Solanum rostratum</i> Dunal <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,9</i>)

### burr-reed
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Sparganium subglobosum.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Bur-reed. <i>Sparganium simplex</i>. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>)

>  (And) <i>Sparganium subglobosum</i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

### burra burra
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bull-a-bull (poroporo)](bri-byo.md#bull-a-bull)))

### burrow
 
 <i>n.</i> of a muttonbird

>  <b>MONRO</b> But, as the mutton-bird seldom makes a new burrow, but returns to the old ones year after year, and as these burrows are nearly horizontal, the natives have made vertical openings into them, through which they extract the young one. The hole thus made is closed up with a plug of grass, and serves for many years. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 254]

>  It is thought that water may have entered some of the burrows and that some of the younger birds may have been drowned. <i>(May 4, 1939,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  During the full moon the birds often stayed at sea until early in the corning, returning in their strange blundering way just long enough to feed their anxious young cheeping in the burrows. <i>(Apr 20, 1993,NewsVUW (Wellington),9</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Many of the Kuaka burrows were really alive with revelry... Often a Kuaka would sit for long periods just outside a burrow mouth, gazing, and I believe at intervals, singing into its cavernous depths. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,28</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> From twos and threes, from dozens, from scores, from hundreds, from thousands, from tens of tousands of burrows, rose an intensifying babel of sound. <i>(1925,Bird Life on Island & Shore,128</i>)

>  When a stick is thrust into the burrow the sitting bird gives a loud double hiss, and then often "Ti-ti-ti." <i>(1932,Life Histories of NZ Birds,78</i>)

### burry
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fleecewool] containing burrs from Bathurst burr or piripiri.

>  .. keeping burry fleeces, cotts & c., from clean wool could be considered with advantage. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,39</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Burry Wool: Wool containing burrs such as clover burr and Bathurst burr. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,53</i>)

### Bursary
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) usually a University Entrance Bursary, gained by public examination, to pay fees and a subsistence allowance for full-time university study (19?30s); more recently (1960s, 1970s) various kinds of allowance for university fees and/or subsistence grants (Fees Bursary, Tertiary Bursary etc.) for tertiary educational institutions [replaced by Level 3 of NCEA in 2004]

>  <b>LELAND</b> Bursary. 7th formers (or bright sixth formers in the `right' schools) (17-18 years old) take this exam to earn some financial support (above tuition, for University). <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,19</i>)

>  <b>Faith Oxenbridge</b> Natasha beats Rima by three marks in the Bursary English exam, but Rima doesn't care. <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,39</i>)

>  Even with the debacle of the calculus Bursary exam, Morris believes the NZQA does a good job in setting and organising exams. <i>(Feb 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I finished school, bummed around for a year or so in Australia and then returned to apply for a bursary and whatever else they would give me to study medicine. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,10</i>)

### burst
 
 <i>v.</i> (historical)

>  I have no wish to see the Palmerston (Manawatu) Club bursted. <i>(Apr 16, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a spree (esp. a drinking spree, often a spending spree), a bust usu. constr. on the burst, on the spree, occas. early to have a burst; now mainly replaced by bust; an old word for a spree; burster, an old word for southerly buster. 1. a. A (protracted) drinking spree.

>  <b>HARPER</b> [The station hands] gravely plead that to `have a burst' is necessary for health after the long monotony of station life and fare. <i>Ibid.</i> 65 You may argue with them [sc. station hands], and they gravely plead that to `have a burst' is necessary for health after the long monotony of station life and fare, ... <i>(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,64,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Once or twice a year, he went ... to Christchurch, - or to <i>town</i>, as it was more emphatically called, - and, like hundreds of his class, he went down with the set resolution<i> `to have a burst'.</i> ... While there [at the inn] he would spend every farthing of his year's wages, ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,167</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> `He is a groom at the pub ..., and is a remittance man. Every now and again he gets a remittance from Home, and has a tremendous burst for a week or two. One not infrequently came across remittance men, generally on the goldfields, at some public house, employed as groom and general rouseabout, or perhaps cook. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,36</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> It's the first burst we have had. <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,125</i>)

>  Our port of shipment in the early "fifties" was the "boulder bank".. to whence also came all the bullock drivers and shearers' cheques, to be "knocked down" on their periodical "bursts". <i>(Oct 30, 1890,Otago Witness,32</i>)

>  (Torlesse's Report on Canterbury) The north-west wind ... something like the ... `hot winds' of Australia ... is almost invariably succeeded by one from the south-west, which in the summer time is a cool and pleasant guest, but in the winter season what is aptly termed `a burst', and sometimes lasts for two or three days, being generally accompanied by rain if not by snow and sleet. This wind is similar to the well-known `south-easter' of New Zealand, which, here, tends up with the land, and so has a south-westerly direction. <i>(Jul 5, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  Going off for his yearly `burst.' <i>(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,64,1914 ed.</i>)

>  Gum-diggers seldom make much money. ... Now and then they may do better, but it only results in a `burst'. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/27</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> Like a fish on land I thirst,/ .../ Oh, give me the drunken burst,/ The song, and the thick voice loud. <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,200</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> coach a bagman `on the burst', with a candlestick, to bed. <i>(1869,Poems (various authors),3 [I]</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> `Hisabella' had gone on the `burst', having bought, for some fabulous sum, a bottle of rum from a passing swagger. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,221</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Though he could take his whisky regularly and freely, and did so, he had ... his periodically recurring seasons when he would get on `the burst,' and soak himself in it. ... O'Byrne took little notice ...; and Davie ... had thought it best on the one occasion in which Dan had `broken out,' to give him a wide berth. <i>Ibid.</i> 129 He then trudged off homewards, and ... went on the `burst' for a week. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,101</i>)

>  He would purchase half a dozen bottles of rum ... and go on a regular burst ... <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,179</i>)

>  <i>Burst, bust.</i> Drinking bout. e.g. `He has been on the burst for a week,' `He has had an awful bust.' ([1874 Kennaway Crusts], p.167) <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Douglas [Finance Minister] and pack are on the burst [headline] <i>(Jun 9, 1987,National Business Review,6</i>)

>  Joe Ropati on the burst - top performances on return to Warrington [caption] <i>(Oct 13, 1989,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: (sports)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a (southerly) burster, buster.

>  <b>TORLESSE</b> The north-west wind ... somewhat resembling the `hot winds' of Australia ... is almost invariably successded by one from the south-west, which in the summer time is a cool and pleasant guest, but in the winter season what is aptly denominated `a burst', and sometimes lasts for two or three days, being generally acompanied by rain, if not by snow and sleet. <i>(1851,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,9</i>)

>  The north-west wind ... something like the ... `hot winds' of Australia ... is almost invariably succeeded by one from the south-west, which in the summer time is a cool and pleasant guest, but in the winter season what is aptly termed `a burst', and sometimes lasts for two or three days, being generally accompanied by rain if not by snow and sleet. This wind is similar to the well-known `south-easter' of New Zealand, which, here, tends up with the land, and so has a south-westerly direction. <i>(Jul 5, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>) [NOTES: (Torlesse's Report on Canterbury)]



 
 <i>n.</i> ? a crash (cf. go bust)

>  <b>BAILEY & ROTH</b> Land of legal managers who scoop the pool before the burst,/ (The survival of the fittest equals the survival of the worst). <i>(1967,Shanties,82,1892 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. break](bla-bri.md#break))

>  <b>WALLACE MURDOCH</b> ... [sic] the cattle broke away ... [sic] and this burst put any chance of my seeing and describing to you Moa Cottage that is to be at an [end]. <i>(Feb 1860,Diary</i>) [NOTES: c1860 Feb Wallace Murdock Diary quoted in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 116]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> [sc. of sheep] [Sheep] will race off helter-skelter to feed ... Therefore have some one stationed a good way off to check [156] their first burst, and stay them from going too far and leaving their lambs; ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,x/155-156</i>)

### burst oneself
 
 <i>v.</i> to over-exert (oneself) (at heavy work); to work with a furious burst of pace.

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> You don't want to burst yourself one week and be useless for two. <i>(1938,Guest of Life, A,326</i>)

### burst up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  The policy of "bursting up" large estates by rating on unimproved value.. is scarcely consistent with the non-taxation of native lands. <i>(Jul 30, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  We have heard a great deal about the desire to "burst up" the large estates. <i>(Oct 4, 1913,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  Setting aside the absurdity of buying land for settlement while there are millions of acres of Crown lands to burst up, I'll tell vou what that means. <i>(Nov 27, 1890,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  [Hdg] "Bursting Up" Big Estates <i>(Jul 27, 1914,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

>  <b>TRAMP</b> &#65279;There are no natural grass lands in the North, consequently no squatter, and no big estates to burst up. <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,47</i>)

### burst-up
 
 <i>n.</i> ?a hyper-correction of bust-up [nf dicts].

>  The Hutt, which is Tom Wilford's stronghold, is pointed out to Doc. Newman, but the little retired medico won't budge, and things generally promise to develop into a huge burst-up. <i>(Jul 22, 1911,NZ Truth,317/1/8</i>)

### burst/bust one's boiler
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `I guess and calculate, stranger,' drawled Ben, `that you'll burst your boiler if you let the angry passions burn.' <i>(1948,Taina,4</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Uncle Angus never let himself become excited ... The old girl will bust her boiler if she's not careful, he thought. <i>(1978,Elver, The,58</i>)

### burster
 
 <i>n.</i> southerly burster, buster; see also southern burster.

>  <b>SHAW</b> We had a favourable wind in the straits when all of a sudden.. an opposite burster came on in a manner so furious that the captain.. declared it was blowing a hurricane. <i>(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,183</i>)

>  The weather ... was exceedingly cold, but has now changed to fine again, proving that the sum of a few `bursters' which blow from time to time, at long intervals, is our real winter; that as a season, we have scarcely one to be called. <i>(Jul 29, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> When within a few miles of Wellington Harbour, a wind came on in the opposite direction, or, as they term it, a `burster but-end first.' <i>(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,128</i>)

>  <b>STACK</b> Suddenly, with the violence of a whirlwind, the south-west gale [54] burst upon us, enveloping us in a blinding cloud of dust, ... which it had gathered up as it rushed along the plain. <i>(1906,Through Canterbury,53,1972 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [an example of use of <i>burst</i> with a <i>southerly</i>] [c1860] a1906 Stack Through Canterbury (1972) 53-54]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The southerly `burster', as it is called, gradually creeps up and at last drives the other [NW wind] off the field. A few chilly puffs, then a great one, and in a minute or two the air becomes cold, even in the height of summer. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,110</i>)

### bursting up
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [burst up v](bri-byo.md#burst-up))

>  That was the real way to provide a remedy, and not to say we will impose a "bursting up" tax. <i>(Oct 30, 1890,Otago Witness,27</i>)

### burton
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DAVIS</b> After I come home at night the jargon runs round and round my head ... On meat - bit of a jockey here, boys; give us legs, legs; bring out your burton; blind her off; we'll need rollers and staging; turn off the blowers, mister mate, or we don't go on the freezer. <i>(1964,Watersiders,105</i>)

### bury
 
 <i>v.</i> to put in segregation

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Bury: (v) Put in segregation. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,246</i>)

### burying-ground
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> It's one of their [sc. Maoris'] old burying-grounds. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,53</i>)

### bush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Bushes that would have been looked upon as worthless in the past are now being worked for anything that will cut up into a plank. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/446</i>) [NOTES: CHECKED DEC 1989 AGAINST AND/OED2: nf references on bush checkfile]



 
 <i>n.</i> [(almost nothing s.v. bush n. and adj. in the Orsman dataset, hence little here; see instead DNZE) (TD)]

>  Even at the moment when Crump appeared on the scene, says Sturm, feelings about "the bush" and the men who roamed it were already close to nostalgia. <i>(Feb 20, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  <b>Lady Barker</b> 'Louis did not know the term "bush" for "forest".' <i>(1874,Boys,p18</i>)

>  He scudded away.. into "the bush" without saying goodbye. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  This ... was due to the fact that for want of percussion caps the Maori had used match-heads, cut off and inserted in eyelets, as was the common native fashion when caps ran short, and this bush contrivance failed to detonate. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/508,1983 ed.</i>)

>  We'd pretend we were so "bush" that we didn't understand a word of English. <i>(Jun 1998,Mana,No. 22: 37</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... in another year or two `bush hostelries' (the nuclei of little villages) will probably spring up by ferry, plain, and pass ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/251</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Nevertheless with the customary bush hospitality, he invited the man in for a drink of tea. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,150</i>)

>  <b>WEKEY</b> The different varieties of indigenous timber known to exist in the province are the black birch or pine, the goa, the totara, and manuka. Most of these ... serve to supply the limited demand created by what is called `bush architecture'. <i>(1862,Otago As It Is,45</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> [The Maori scouts] marched barefoot. Steve's bush uniform consisted of a blue jumper and a pair of trousers cut short at the knees. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/342,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> There's no water like bush water. <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown,67</i>)

>  The aftermath saw the wounded Heretaunga subjected to a bush court-martial, then executed that night. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a patch or area of native bush [now used as a collective noun.]

>  <b>PAUL</b> You should try to have a bush on or near your section. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,94</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Most stations have a bush near the homestead. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,62</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. the dark mass of a splendid 'bush' extending over many hundred acres. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,69</i>)

### bush and bog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large disc-like plough.

>  <b>TAIT</b> At 24 he left home, beginning by buying a Fordson tractor and bush and bog discs, with which, after burning off the scrub, he double-disced and then fenced the first 50 acres. <i>(1961,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol III,515</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bush and bog: an enormous disc-type plough used for breaking in rough, and usually scrub-covered, country. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,14</i>)

>  For sale: set bush and bog scallop disc blades. [bog discs] <i>(Dec 5, 2001,Press, The,5</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> .. you're getting up the nerve to ask if you may borrow the bog discs or the grass roller. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,81</i>)

### bush angora
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild goat.

>  ..he captured some of the Waipu bush angoras near his home farm. The Waipu bush angoras had, by the time of their capture, developed some distinctive characteristics. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,21</i>)

### bush apiary
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a set of hives situated in native bush areas.

>  The importance of bush apiaries in beekeeping.. Gradually through the years beekeepers have been discovering the value of bush apiaries financially and for the upkeep of their apiaries on clover lands. Today many commercial beekeepers have one or more bush apiraies established..Most beekeepers prefer to work hives of Italian bees and have used them when they have established their bush apiaries...Some beekeepers, however, prefer to keep a hybrid strain in their bush apiaries. <i>(May 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,425</i>)

### bush area
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that is either in standing or newly-felled bush.

>  This wretched blackberry causes enormous expense to struggling farmers on newly burned bush areas which cannot be ploughed for some years. <i>(Dec 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,478</i>)

### bush ball
 
 <i>n.</i> a ball (dance) held in the bush

>  <b>HAY</b> Bright and cheerful, neat and comely, pleasant partners at a bush-ball are these half-Anglicized daughters of the Ngatewhatua. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,268</i>)

### bush bank
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sidling or riverbank clad in native bush.

>  <b>PASCOE</b> In the evening they all descended to the valley of the Raukokore, with huge bush banks ... <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,32</i>)

### bush baptist
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who has strong fundamentalist views but is not a church member; a person usually with no formal theological training, who is fond of publicly expressing strong, individualistic, and often evangelical, religious beliefs.

>  [A correspondent writes] I am a solicitor and likewise a bush baptist. <i>(Feb 15, 1908,NZ Truth,?/6/0</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Good job I'm a bush baptist; I can please myself as to what rank I parade with. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>bush-baptist,</i> a person of uncertain religion; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 286]

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... derivatives ... commonly used both in New Zealand and Australia [include] ... <i>bush baptist. </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,15</i>)

>  <i>bush baptist n.</i> person of doubtful religious persuasion. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <i>bush baptist n.</i> person of doubtful religious persuasion. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  <i>bush baptist NZ & Austral.</i> person of strong religious belief but not conforming to any established denomination. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bush baptist</i> religious fundamentalist or religious poseur; first used in Boer War. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)

### bush basil
 
 <i>n.</i> the pepper tree or kawakawa. (See [pepper tree](p-per.md#pepper-tree),[kawakawa](k-kea.md#kawakawa))

>  [heading] ON OUR PLATE. Wild foods on the world stage: *Pikopiko heads (fern frond) *Piripiri (New Zealand chili [sic]) *Kawakawa (bush basil) <i>(May 21, 2005,Dominion Post,A16</i>)

>  Horopito, which has a chilli pepper bite and kawakawa, also known as the pepper tree or bush basil, are dried and used as rubs and seasonings. <i>(Jul 27, 2005,NZ Herald,3,VIVA ed.</i>)

### bush bedspread
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cured skin, used as a bed cover.

>  Sheep skins cured in this way make excellent bush bed spreads, hearth rugs or door-mats ... <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,927</i>)

### bush bee
 
 <i>n.</i> a wild bee (found often in the bush A1). [[1971 Smithyman's fn 5, p.220] Bush-bee. A native bee, of which Tillyard (1926) names twenty as covered by this comprehensive label.]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Gum was also won by tapping, the exuded or `bush-bled' gum being chipped off at regular intervals, but this method was sometimes inimical to the trees. ... invented and used with success, and thousands of tons of gum which could not have been worked profitably by other means was won in this way. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,70,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BRACKEN</b> [Bush children] hunting for the hidden sweets where the bush-bees are humming. <i>(1871,Behind the Tomb,63</i>)

>  <i>(May 2, 1896,Bulletin, The (Sydney)</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> And the earth is bare and barren where the bush-bee used to hum, ... <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,156,1971 ed.</i>)

### bush beer
 
 <i>n.</i> (illicitly) home-brewed beer.

>  [Cook Islands Ordinance] And whereas the manufacture of bush beer has been productive of much crime ... <i>(Oct 10, 1905,NZ Truth,17/5/7</i>)

>  I would here point out that it is a mistake to imagine that bush-beer drinking is an old Maori custom. <i>(1911,Poo-Bah of the Pacific, The,9</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> There was a tavern ashore [in Suva] where `bush beer' and locally made rum were sold. <i>(1948,Taina,3</i>) [NOTES: [Fiji]]

### bush belt
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area or band of land covered in native bush.

>  The great trans-island bush belt already referred to covers a variety of soils. <i>(Jun 1, 1922,NZ Farmer,710</i>)

### bush bird
 
 <i>n.</i> a bird which inhabits the bush; a forest bird.

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... the first awakening of the myriads of Bush-birds ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,100,1870 ed.</i>)

>  bush-birds <i>(1869,TrNZI,/64</i>)

>  ... the little bush-birds flew in or out amongst them with unerring accuracy ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/602</i>)

>  Many of our bush-birds play an important part in the fertilization of our native trees and shrubs. <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/127</i>) [NOTES: (Popplewell)]

>  <b>BUICK</b> The Moa was not a bush bird: it was too large and clumsy for that ... <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,132</i>)

>  <i>piopio</i>, a bush-bird (Turnagra tanagara), the North Island thrush and (T. capensis) the South Island form: both extinct soon after 1900. A riverbed bird, the dotterel, and a narrow-leaved plant, dianella, also bear this name, but the thrush is usually associated with the name: in any case the dianella has a name tu[u]rutu. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>HOGAN</b> Where the bush birds all sing in the springtime/ As the Kowhai pours gold in his lap./ <i>(1962,Billy-Can Ballads,6</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Eventually the engine-sound ceased and, in sudden queer silence among the sounds of cicadas and bush birds, we glided up to a fern-fringed jetty ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) one of a native bird species that dwells in native bush.

>  <b>BARKER</b> The first awakening of the myriads of Bush-birds. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,100</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> The kakapo, the kokako, saddleback and stitchbird must be reckoned among those bush birds least likely to be seen by a tramper. <i>(1988,Hills, The,31</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> .. when he had got over his shyness he was interesting to listen to and told the children about the bush birds. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,145</i>)

### bush blanket
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a coarse, rough dark grey woollen blanket.

>  <b>KEENE</b> The frame of his bed was made of rough unplaned timber which supported a split sack over which a generous supply of dry fern covered with sacks served as a mattress. Over this were several dark grey 'bush' blankets that kept him warm in the cold weather. <i>(1978,Mimiwhangata Station,56</i>)

### bush block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area of land covered in native bush [often allocated in hunting contracts.]

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> .. an opossum hunter requesting access through our farm to his allotted bush block ... <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,77</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Harold pegged out bush blocks and amassed material for bush felling to begin. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,64</i>)

>  .. the couple began to build up their assets, buying a bush block in 1993, selling it three years later ... <i>(Aug 18, 2005,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  The bush block owes $60,000 hard core debt .. is it worth jeopardising my home farm by using it as security for borrowing to prop up the bush block ... <i>(Sep 26, 1985,NZ Farmer,20</i>)

>  After that, there was more land to clear and grass, and a bush block to attend to. <i>(Feb 1990,NZ Dairy Exporter,25</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> He ranged the bush blocks and employed the predominantly Ngapuhi bush gangs ... <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,79</i>)

>  In 1972, fresh to the board from the backblocks of Taranaki, Wiggins was amazed to find it harder to convince the wool industry of the merits of objective measurement than it was to develop a bush block in the tiger country around Whangamomona. <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Winter 3</i>)

>  There are 13 properties fronting the beach, 14 pastoral and 16 bush blocks. <i>(2002,Sunday Star Times,Home 2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bushland property intended for development into farmland.

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> Washing-day must have presented to the bush-block woman difficulties which would seem to us in these earlier days all but insuperable. <i>(1940,Women of NZ, The,109</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> .. the home block was then only partly cleared and sown, and the 500-acre bush-block was barely touched. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,495</i>)

### bush boar
 
 <i>n.</i> -

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Spearing bush boars on horseback occasionally equalled the same gallant sport in the Deccan. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/62,1974 ed.</i>)

### bush boss
 
 <i>n.</i> the man in charge of the logging operations in the bush as distinct from those at the sawmill.

>  <b>GIBSON</b> The bush bosses gave the Saturday as a whole holiday <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot,72</i>)

>  <b>R GILBERD</b> Next in importance to the bush-boss is the `breaker- out', where steam haulers are still in use. <i>(1950,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (Okaihau)]

>  A Forest Ranger speaks of a sawmiller `having a meeting with his bush-boss'. <i>(May 5, 1953,NZ Forest Services Report</i>) [NOTES: ca1953 May 5 NZ Forest Service memorandum]

>  BUSH BOSS A logging foreman. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) ganger or ranger in charge of a bush gang.

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> .. in 1922 he went as bush boss for Parker Lamb Timber Co, working Pickens' bush at Paramata Mokau. In 1923 Jim broke his leg and Fred relieved him as bush boss for the six weeks he was away ... <i>(1982,Where it all Began,85</i>)

>  <b>REA</b> Jimmy Jones used to be the bush boss when Kenny and the others worked there. <i>(1991,Honey and Cheese,26</i>)

### bush boundary
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a boundary between native bush and farm land.

>  The fence will be on a bush boundary adjoining Pakihiroa Station. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,21</i>)

>  <b>NGAWHIKA</b> Bushmen who worked in the forests or cockies with bush boundaries went a long way to keep the dogs among the action when pigs were hard to find over the summer months. <i>(1996,Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting,143</i>)

### bush boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a boy born in and bred to the bush (See [cf. bushman](bri-byo.md#bushman))

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Hugh was truly a boy and a bush-boy. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,11</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Very frequently, as bush boys and girls well know, their [Muehlenbeckia] rope-like stems hang swaying from the forest roof .... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,33</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> = bushman

>  [Taihape in those early days] carried a big floating population of bushmen ... Those bush boys were game. Most of them had never been on a horse, but upwards of 30 of them came forward to try their luck [at bareback riding]. <i>(Aug 31, 1963,Dominion,7</i>) [NOTES: (Ian McKay) [ca1910]]

### bush bread
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a damper cooked in ashes of an open fire or a basic loaf cooked in a camp oven.

>  Dampers, that is flour and water mixed together ... and thrown on the ashes ...; this is bush bread and I had rather have it than any bread I ever tasted. <i>(1849,Otago Journal,v/71</i>) [NOTES: (`A Settler')]

>  We three act as cook week about, which allows our man more time to work and make dampers, that is, flour and water mixed together till it becomes a paste, and then thrown on the ashes where it bakes well; this is bush bread, and I had rather have it than any bread I ever tasted. <i>(Apr 14, 1849,Letters from Otago,28,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> brownie: originally cocoa damper or bush bread. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,21</i>)

### bush breakfast
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a basic early morning meal.

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> Next morning, after the usual bush-breakfast of sharp-edged coffee, mealy bread, and thick steak underlying a deposit of onions ... <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,246</i>)

### bush broom
 
 <i>n.</i> [noun.]

>  It was only needful to sling our tent and fly over the poles ... and give it a good sweep out with a bush broom, to render it fit for occupation. <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,165</i>)

### bush bud
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I've tried once, I bought a tinny (bush bud) from a high school kid, cos i don't know much dealers around. <i>(Apr 6, 2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.norml.org.nz/postt136.html]

### bush bull
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild bull, usually found in bush country.

>  <b>ALLEN</b> No lambs brown with utuwai seed, no signs of wild pigs rooting in the pastures, no bush bulls bellowing.. <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,14</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> I think that had gone too, along with the utuwai, the pig rooting and the wild bush bulls. <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,15</i>)

>  On that trip Tom shot eight bush bulls. <i>(May 27, 1982,NZ Farmer,80</i>)

### bush bun
 
 <i>n.</i> [[?ca1900]]

>  <b>REED</b> ... some bush buns. These were made from pieces of left-over dough, kneaded and made into handy sizes, and containing a sprinkling of currants and raisins. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,138</i>)

### bush bunk
 
 <i>n.</i> ?made out of bush materials - manuka rails etc

>  The construction ... was that of most bush bunks; the main supports consisting of a section of `bungi' laid on the ground at each end. These supported wine-berry poles ... <i>(Jan 10, 1920,Quick March,41</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I hardly expected he would stop with me any time, but I accepted hid offer, and he knocked up an extra bush bunk in the bach, and came to live on the job. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,46,1975 ed.</i>)

### bush burn
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>W MORGAN</b> Last week sowed some grass seed on bush burn. <i>(Apr 27, 1861,Journal,29,1963 ed.</i>)

>  [Cocksfoot] is especially valuable on certain classes of `bush-burns'. <i>(1936,DSIR Bulletin,No.49/64</i>) [NOTES: (H.H. Allan)]

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... the temporary fertility of the bush burn declined. <i>Ibid</i> 240 [caption] Fig. 164. Seed mixture for bush burn being mixed on floor in seed-merchants store. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,239,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> In the old days, there were a lot of bush burns, with the result that bare bellies and bare points were much more frequent than today. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,9</i>)

>  We also have<i> bush burns</i>, areas where the bush has been recently burnt off, and hills are<i> bush-clad</i>. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,24</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <b>MASTERS</b> About half a mile in from the Inland road, the track passes through an old bush burn. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,34</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Bush-line </i> or sometimes [161] <i>forest-line </i> (for American <i>timber-line,</i> English <i> tree-line </i> and Australian <i>tree-line </i> or <i>forest-line), bush-clad, bush burns </i> (areas where the bush has recently been burnt off) are tramping as well as general terms, as is the use of <i> shingle </i> for angular as well as rounded stones in <i>shingle slide </i> , or (earlier) <i>shingle slip </i> , also recently called <i>scree </i> as in Scotland and northern England. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,160</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Aristotelia serrata </i> (J.R. & G. Forst.) Oliv. Common name: <i>Wineberry </i> Maori name: Makomako ... This handsome shrub is common on bush burns and the outskirts of forests. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,50</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... much undressed seed and even seed [244] cleanings were used in the so-called cheap mixtures for rough bush-burn country. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,243,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The settlers ... soon learned the art of the `bush burn'. <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,39</i>)

>  <i>bush burn NZ</i> burning of bush on cultivable land, land so cleared. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... the weak, weedy swards that are often found, particularly where poor seed mixtures were used in the original bush-burn sowings. <i>Ibid.</i> 259 ... so many original bush-burn sowings lacked the quality seed available today. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,251,1970 ed.</i>)

>  The "bush burn" farmer at least replaced the forest with grass. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(4) : 10</i>)

>  Bushburn techniques had not been perfected; but the younger men, unable to break into the `squattocratic' holdings of the Middle Island, were moving north. <i>(Oct 1955,NZ Geographer,xi/102</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The settlers ... soon learned the art of the `bush burn'. ... In Taranaki and the Manawatu ... good pastures were established in the `bush burn' country, but on much of the hill country throughout the North Island there was constant danger of counter attack by bracken ... <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,39</i>)

>  Harry Barrow, although then over 70, ... nipping in and around the logs, flicking to and fro handfuls of grass seed, the grand old bushburn mixture, sown with local variations mostly from one end of New Zealand to the other, another green face for New Zealand: ... <i>(Feb 3, 1985,NZ Times,12</i>) [NOTES: (Jim Henderson)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the process of clearing land of forest and scrub by burning.

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> From Marlborough to Southland, short steep slopes ploughed out of tussock or ploughed after bush burns, exhibited dimpling in various stages. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,101</i>)

>  <b>O'CONNOR</b> ... the fate of most of the forest was to yield to a method of clearing called 'bush burn.' <i>(1970,Growth of NZ Farming 1890-1918, The,2</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> In 1848 G B Earp described in detail the process of forest clearance by bush-burn. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,14</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> One danger of the bush-burn was that, through a wind change, it might veer round and destroy homes and other developed properties. The Richmond-Atkinson family correspondence contains numerous references which clearly show that control of bush burn was not the only inherent problem in clearing the land. [bush-burn] <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,15</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> For the first few years after the bush burns, the fertility of that country was high ... <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) area of newly burned bush and scrub land, ready for pasturing.

>  .. the bush burns absorb each year anything from 2,500 to 3,800 tons of grass and clover seed. <i>(Oct 20, 1914,NZ Journal of Agriculture,231</i>)

>  Finish the burning-off of any bush, scrub or manuka where clearing is being carried out, and sow bush-burns as soon as possible. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,219</i>)

>  These paddocks have never seen a plough, having been sown on the original bush burn and oversown since with white clover and short rotation ryegrass. <i>(Sep 1, 1951,NZ Dairy Exporter,14</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> For many years, the bushburn, as we called the newly won grass, was stocked with sheep and a few cattle. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,118</i>)

>  I am sowing a bush-burn this coming year on hill country. <i>(Oct 20, 1926,NZ Journal of Agriculture,286</i>)

>  Mr Raine has been developing the property, establishing pasture on land that was once bush burn, scrub and fern. <i>(Mar 30, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an instance of bush burning

>  As may easily be seen after a bush-burn ... <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/436</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

>  ... now that the effect of the early bush-burns is disappearing sheep are showing signs of malnutrition over considerable areas. <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,12/4</i>)

>  After the bush-burns, the sheep did well for a time, except that there were always some `wasters.' the number of which kept increasing. <i>(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/57</i>)

>  Bush sickness is always found in the volcanic district on coarse rhyolitic pumice soils and `dopiness' on soils derived from fine volcanic dust or mud showers. A soil on which `dopiness' develops is a leached loam and is found on deteriorated lands, i.e., lands which originally were highly fertile due to the ashes from the `bush burn,' the land subsequently becoming gradually less productive ... <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,81</i>)

>  To burn everything cleanly but the heavy trunks is so important to the success of the bush burn. <i>(Jan 22, 1964,Weekly News,39/2</i>)

>  <i>bush burn NZ </i>burning of bush on cultivable land, land so cleared. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: -->1986 NZPOD 96 [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### bush burn country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that has been felled of bush then burned.

>  <b>FERRIS</b> The firestick became the real emblem of farming progress in New Zealand for many years, rather than the plough. Much of this 'bush-burn' country has since been cultivated. <i>(1940,Cavalcade of Farming 1840-1940,34</i>)

>  Considerable numbers of Lincolns and English Leicesters were run on the new 'bush-burn' country ( that is country which has been cleared of forest by felling and burning, leaving many blackened stumps and logs) , as their wool stood up well to the rough conditions. [bush-burn country] <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,47</i>)

>  An important topical matter is the sowing to grass of bush-burn country. <i>(Feb 20, 1930,NZ Journal of Agriculture,125</i>)

### bush burn farming
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the cultivating of pasture from forest and scrub.

>  In other parts of the North Island 'bush burn' farming was progressing on a grand scale. <i>(Jul 15, 1947,NZ Journal of Agriculture,22</i>)

### bush burn mixture
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mix of grass-seeds most appropriate for sowing in newly-cleared and burned land.

>  The cut scrub was burnt and sown from the air with 20lb of bush-burn mixture to the acre ... [bush-burn mixture] <i>(Dec 31, 1964,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

### bush burn seed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mix of grass seeds most appropriate for sowing in newly-cleared and burned land.

>  4 The 250 acres cut in the winter before last was burnt and sown with 20 lbs of bush-burn seed. [bush-burn seed] <i>(Dec 31, 1964,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

### bush burner
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person employed to fire fallen bush before pasture is laid.

>  The weather is very dry all along the coast and bush burners and harvesters of grain and grass seed are rejoicing. <i>(Feb 18, 1888,Egmont Star,16</i>)

### bush burnt
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land or pasture] following a bush burn.

>  .. in the first place, cocksfoot was sown, broadcast on bush burnt country some 70 years ago in this district ... <i>(Aug 1, 1927,NZ Farmer,955</i>)

### bush camp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> But all the little troubles of the day's tramp were forgotten when the night came down upon us in our snug little bush camp. <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,11 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Early in March of 1869 Titokowaru had established himself in a bush camp at Otautu ... -->sc a temporary erection in the bush for living in <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/294,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> The the camp had to be near running water ... It was usually made of the trunks of sapling trees ... and the walls were mde from leaves of the nikau palm. ... I have slept many times in those bush camps, and I was always conscious of being very comfortable indeed. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,151</i>) [NOTES: (Joe Gasparich, Northland kauri country)]

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> In the early decades of the 20th century some longer bush tram operations included a bush camp located close to the felling areas... A typical bush camp comprised men's huts, a store shed, and a cookhouse. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,58</i>)

>  I remember one man telling me how interested he'd become in Charles Dickens. He'd been introduced to it in a bush camp, and he said, 'My word, he's a good writer., isn't he!' <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,154</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PFAFF</b> [advt] For 33 years `K' Jam has been known and appreciated in all parts of the coast - in bush camps and mining townships, ... as well as in the thriving towns. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,166</i>)

>  <b>MORTON</b> ... a real bush-camp ... <i>(1928,Along the Road,24</i>)

>  <b>M</b> The best thing that could happen to him would be six months in a bush camp getting down to things. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> His father had been called out to a remote bush camp to perform a marriage ceremony. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,150,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> For the first time I realised the importance of teamwork and the bond that exists in times of danger. The bond that grew and existed in that bush camp was never broken. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,62</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> But I can remember that I was glad to get back to the bush camp, ... to familiar surroundings and people. <i>(1964,Stamper Battery,11</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a camp, usually temporary, where bushfellers live and work.

>  <b>WILSON</b> Sixty years ago some country homes were still using colonial ovens, which had superseded the camp ovens, except in bush camps. <i>(1962,Road to Porangahau, The,41</i>)

>  <b>CLEVELAND</b> Is this the way to enact the fabled tradition of energy and trust in each other you have inherited from goldfields and bush camps? <i>(1966,Silent Land, The,72</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> Cats, dogs and rats wandered away from the bush camps and farms, and stayed in the bush, living on birds and their eggs. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,13</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Initially Guy Reed, younger brother of the two partners, was installed in a bush camp in 1915 to begin development of the steep and awkwardly-shaped property. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,132</i>)

>  <b>DAHM</b> The galley was the living room of the bush camp - dining room, kitchen and social room - it was all three. <i>(2000,People of the Axe, The,35</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> For many years the large bush camp remained at Pukekaroro. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,431</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> Big bush camps had their own cook and a good cook helped keep a camp happy. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,12</i>)

>  <b>DAHM</b> Before we moved into the new house Mum had done her cooking exactly the same as the bush cooks did in the bush camps. <i>(2000,People of the Axe, The,24</i>)

### bush canary
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) Mohoua ochrocephala (S.I. bush canary) and Mohoua albicilla (of the North Island, also called upokotea). [see also canary]

>  <b>MCCONOCHIE</b> When here in great profusion, the bush canary, our favourite songster, together with the rifleman .. moved from tree to tree. <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,12</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> There were bush canaries with a yellow head and breast and brown wings. <i>(1984,Tales of the Coast,57</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> .. or maybe the din of the bush canaries as they gather into flocks before rain ... <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,105</i>)

### bush candlestick
 
 <i>n.</i> a makeshift candlestick

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> I found ... tied to a stick, (a bush candlestick), about two inches of candle. <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,48</i>)

### bush cane
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... we caught every day shoals of white-bait; a piece of gauze fastened to a circle of supple-jack or bush cane, much resembling an ordinary butterfly-net, was all that was required for their capture. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,115</i>)

### bush carpenter
 
 <i>n.</i> a self-trained rough carpenter; a carpenter without formal training; a rough carpenter (See [cf. bush lawyer](bri-byo.md#bush-lawyer),[bush philosopher](bri-byo.md#bush-philosopher))

>  Our local "bush"-carpenters did their best. <i>(Sep 17, 1947,NZ Observer,21</i>)

>  Registration of builders now being carried out by the New Zealand Master Builders' Federation would eventually provide a safeguard, both for the public and for the builders themselves, against the activities of `bush carpenters,' according to a statement issued by the federation today. <i>(Aug 26, 1953,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  (PA) - The Auckland Master Builders' Association is preparing to wage war against what it calls `bush carpenters' - jobbing carpenters who `are bringing the whole industry into disrepute,' in the words of Mr.F.G.Rabone, president of the association. <i>(Aug 26, 1953,Dominion,25</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Frank and his bush carpenter set to work by erecting the frame of a lean-to of scantlings. <i>Ibid.</i> 65 That bush-carpenter's economy in doing without piles had been a disastrous mistake ... <i>(1955,Land of My Children,29</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> bush carpenter a man, self taught, usually limited to simple carpentry. .in 4;`Well, they were only bush carpenters they gave it a go because there was no one else, or they couldn't afford a tradesman.' `That's about Ted's form - he's just a bush carpenter.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],139</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> [They] were out in the alley, leaning on each other ... and vowing eternal friendship in the face of all drongos, no-hopers, wowsers and bush carpenters and all those poufs who had promised to the big game and then backed out ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,171</i>)

>  Leaving three carpenters (real, apprentice and bush), an architect and cook to look after building operations, the rest ... went down the road ... <i>(Apr 1954,Tararua Tramper,4</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> I found that Fletcher ... had secured an iron building, which was being converted by a bush carpenter into suitable premises for a bank. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,27</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> ~: Handy, rough carpenter. <i>(1933,Sheep-station Glossary</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> As it was too far to get a tradesman up for any repairs, we were all, what was called, `Bush Carpenters'. <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,52</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bush</i>. In New Zealand also; esp., <i>bush carpenter </i>(or <i>carpentry</i>): since ca. 1910. (Niall Alexander, letter of Oct. 22, 1939.) <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1005</i>) [NOTES: 1939 Alexander in 1949 Partridge Dict. Slang Addenda 1005]

>  <b>WILSON</b> The structure built [?c1900] by the bush carpenter was about 12 feet by twelve feet. <i>(1950,My First 80 Years,105</i>)

>  `Like most farmers I'm a good bush carpenter,' he smiled when asked about his qualifications as a builder. <i>(Oct 1972,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> At one end the seats had been cut away by some bush-carpenter allow living quarters. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,122</i>)

>  <i>bush carpenter n. Austral. & N.Z.</i> a rough-and-ready unskilled workman. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,147</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 96; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 64]]

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> Taddi was a good bush carpenter; he soon knocked up a rough cottage for the men to live in, better than the flax whare they agreed. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,33</i>)

>  Barbara Baynton ... began life as the daughter of a bush carpenter in New South Wales ... <i>(1987,Happy Endings,238</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bush carpenter </i>self-taught or rough and ready carpenter. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> He was what is called a bush-carpenter: that is, [OEDS i.e.] a wandering carpenter, who travels from station to station, doing any little odd rough [OEDS omits] jobs wanted. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,101</i>)

>  [?transitional use] A large proportion of the country settlers of New Zealand are compelled ... to become their own architects, or to appeal to some bush carpenter, who has no skill in the art of designing, though, perhaps, a good enough mechanic. <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,1131</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a carpenter with little training.

>  <b>JONES</b> As it was too far to get a tradesman up for any repairs, we were all, what was called, 'Bush Carpenters', and most stations had small forges, and people could shoe their own horses ... <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,52</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an itinerant station rouseabout, usually specialising in fencing or carpentry.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Herb was the bush carpenter. <i>(1984,Tales of the Coast,64</i>)

>  <b>NEWPORT</b> With the help of Tommy Whilten, sometimes referred to as a 'bush carpenter', a substantial swingbridge was erected. <i>(1989,Footprints Farewell,87</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> He was what is called a bush-carpenter ie a wandering carpenter, who travels from station to station doing any little odd jobs wanted. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,101</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a rough or unskilled doctor; `sawbones'.

>  <b>LEE</b> Too many bush-carpenters and vets. in the Medical Corps. <i>(1937,Civilian into Soldier,147</i>)

### bush cattle
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) wild cattle.

>  <b>GROVER</b> In no time I would have a horse, and who would they ask when they needed a drover for a herd of bush cattle that had to be driven a hundred miles? <i>(1967,Another Man's Role,5</i>)

>  It is now some eight years since I first saw several small herds of wild bush cattle. <i>(Feb 1894,NZ Farmer,55</i>)

>  Yes, bush cattle certainly had a good life, with plenty to eat and drink and no fear of drought. <i>(May 27, 1982,NZ Farmer,80</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> This area abounded with bush cattle which we would see from time to time. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,96</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> Bush cattle are very nervous at sight of a man on foot ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,75</i>)

### bush chalet
 
 <i>n.</i> a shelter hut

>  <b>COWAN</b> There are Government shelter huts, iron-roofed little bush chalets ... 10 and 19 miles from Glade House [Te Anau] ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 134</i>)

### bush children
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. bush boy](bri-byo.md#bush-boy),[bush girl](bri-byo.md#bush-girl))

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> The Katui school ! ... How I adored the succession of teachers who came to impart at least the rudiments of knowledge to us `bush children'. <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,78,1956 ed.</i>)

### bush chimney
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a basic wattle and daub or mud-lined chimney.

>  <b>ELLISON</b> The old bush chimney was never quite the same again. <i>(1956,Whirinaki Valley, The,51</i>)

>  <b>Masters</b> Among his other accomplishments, he was adept at erecting bush chimneys. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,158</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Among his other accomplishments, he was an adept at erecting bush chimneys. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,158</i>)

>  Blue Knee went to the bush chimney and stirred embers below the water tin suspended from the cooking rail, the usual girder spanning the full width of the chimney. <i>(Aug 1986,Islands,37-41</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> But sitting on her hard chair warming herself by the fire in the bush chimney [in the tent], my indomitable grandma pursed her lips. <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,149</i>)

>  <b>ELLISON</b> .. the huge fire that was burning in the corrugated iron 'bush chimney.' <i>(1956,Whirinaki Valley, The,41</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> It was welcome fuel for his small stove set in a bush chimney on an evening when he was 'dining on a soup bone.' <i>(1993,Colonial Outcasts: A Search for the Remittance Man,151</i>)

### bush chop
 
 <i>n.</i> [woodchopping]

>  [1910 photograph of competitors with a block on which is written] MOKAI BUSH CHOP 1910 <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,188</i>) [NOTES: 1910 in 1980 McGill Ghost Towns of NZ 188]

### bush chopping
 
 <i>n.</i> a spell of chopping bush or logs

>  <b>JOHN HURNDELL</b> many burnings off and bush choppings. <i>(1881,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1881 John Hurndall's Diary in 1988 Warr From Bush-burn to Butter 52 1881 February 24:]

### bush clearing
 
 <i>n.</i> the act or process clearing of bush by felling (and burning); (a loose comb.) from verbal phrs <i>to clear bush</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> In his crippled old age he was cheered now and again by a visit from some old friend of his bush-scouting, bush-clearing, bullocking days when one could march all day with a seventy-pound swag ... <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,223</i>)

>  <b>SOUTHEY</b> As workmen they are sought after for bush-clearing. <i>(1852,Colonial Sheep and Wool,63</i>)

>  Bush-clearing has destroyed many of the habitats of this moss ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/423</i>) [NOTES: (Brown)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a clearing, open space, an area cleared in the bush

>  Self-sown wheat that I have seen stood taller than my head ... but that is in a bush-clearing, and probably stronger than on the plains. <i>(1851,Canterbury Papers,11/308</i>)

>  <b>NEAL</b> Last year I used to look at [the peach trees] from our bush clearing and your house looked as if it was on fire. <i>(1969,From London to Lonely Rai</i>) [NOTES: 1867 July 30 Charley Turner in 1969 Neal From London to Lonely Rai 47]

>  I and 5 men cut down groundsel all the afternoon. ... It has all come on a bush clearing from a few branches. <i>(Dec 12, 1892,Chudleigh Diary,382,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Every evening at dusk scores could be seen flitting across the bush-clearings ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/220</i>)

>  ... a couple of Australian Tree-swallows hunting flies about the head of an old rata in a bush-clearing ... <i>(1899,TrNZI,31/23</i>) [NOTES: (Townson, Buller)]

>  <b>BELL</b> To the artistic eye, the bush clearings ... may appear as a sore blemish on the face of the landscape. Thickly strewn with blackened logs and branches, ... these clearings stand out in ugly contrast with the virgin native bush. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,2</i>)

>  <b>HAWDON</b> I'll be back in time for shearing,/ And to burn the new bush clearing. <i>(1907,New Zealanders & the Boer War,100</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... on unused bush-clearings where the Californian thistle alone flourishes. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,134</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The bridle-track led past little bush-clearings with fenced-in Maori plantations; ... <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,169,1933 ed.</i>)

>  No more fearful than was the pa Te Huripapa/ In the bush clearing, where the river/ Opened its mouth, ... <i>(1960,Penguin Book of NZ Verse,257</i>) [NOTES: (K. Sinclair `Waitora')]

>  <b>MARSH</b> She lived in a deserted lumberman's shack in a bush-clearing. <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,108</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> In the settlers' bush-clearings at Eketahuna, in the Forty-mile Bush, I found them [sc. ducks] long after dark ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/259</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> The bushclearing when chopped, burnt, and sown, was as a rule open to the wide ... <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,70</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the cutting down and burning of forest and scrub for pasture.

>  <b>WASWO</b> As organised settlement increased bush clearing gathered momentum, a peak being reached in the 1870s and 1880s. Initially the [bush] clearing was concentrated on the lowland forested areas ... <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bush farm or land area cleared of bush for farming.

>  I would like to know whether it [canary grass] is suitable for bush clearings and if stock are fond of it. <i>(Jul 15, 1911,NZ Journal of Agriculture,81</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> It was a great day for the youngsters on the bush farms when Chew Chong, the old Chinese storekeeper, found out the value of fungus which grew on the stumps in the bush clearings. <i>(1945,Pioneer Looks Back, A,25</i>)

>  .. the inland bush clearings also added a considerable production of first-class cocksfoot seed, which was harvested by hand, as at Akaroa. <i>(Apr 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,346</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> The traditional [dairying] practice was to hand milk cows in a nearby bush clearing, out in the fields or in a yard close by ... [bush-clearing] <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,63</i>)

>  <b>NEWPORT</b> The large, long-wooled Lincoln sheep were popular at one time and thrived extremely well on the lush grass of the new bush clearings. <i>(1989,Footprints Farewell,75</i>)

### bush clematis
 
 <i>n.</i> (a native species)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Around one twist in the creek we came to a fantastic display of bush-clematis. The white flowers, all shimmering with sunlight, dripped from every tree in sight. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,33</i>)

>  <i>Bush Clematis, Clematis paniculata.</i> Large strong woody vine which climbs trees. Produces lovely white flowers ... <i>(1971,New Zealand's Heritage,1/267</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> From early in spring the ... kowhai and occasional treetop white of the climbing bush clematis (<i>C. paniculata</i>) are common sights. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,158</i>)

### bush clerk
 
 <i>n.</i> a clerk who deals with kauri-milling operations in the bush (See [cf. bush boss](bri-byo.md#bush-boss))

>  <b>GIBSON</b> [A bush-boss c1890 speaks:] You're now a bush-clerk, and that's the Kauri analogy to a midshipman's job in the Navy. <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot</i>)

### bush clothes
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) plain, unfashionable working attire.

>  <b>MARRIOTT</b> I was confronted by a thin old man in rough but clean bush clothes and with an axe tucked under his arm. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,21</i>)

### bush cocky
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bush settler or bush farmer with a relatively small holding.

>  <b>BATES</b> You know, it must be better to be a copper around here than a bush cocky. <i>(1982,Bridge to Nowhere, The,117</i>)

>  <b>BATES</b> The boat crew unloaded some stores for a group of bush cockies working in the area. <i>(1982,Bridge to Nowhere, The,21</i>)

### bush concert
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... bush concert ... <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,4/854</i>)

### bush contractor
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... it is the utter recklessness displayed by saw-mill proprietors, bush contractors, and splitters which is destroying our forests at a rate constantly increasing. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/183</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  <b>MOORE</b> The bush contractor was the man who ... usually contracted with a sawmill owner to fell the trees, crosscut them into logs, and deliver the logs to some agreed point. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,39</i>)

>  <b>STALLWORTHY</b> Mr.H.T.Smith, an enterprising bush contractor, purchased and improved land at Arapohue; ... <i>(1916,Early Northern Wairoa,39</i>)

>  His first job, though, was in a bank - until a bush contractor lured him out to prune pine trees. <i>(1995,Mana,10 : 5</i>)

### bush cook
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ST JOHN</b> Good, nay delicious, as the <i>ko[u]ra</i> is when boiled, ... or even when knocked up in a curry by the untutored paws of a bush cook. <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands</i>) [NOTES: 1873 St John Pakeha Rambles in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 554]

>  ... when the bush cook is not using heavy damper as a slow poison. <i>(Jan 20, 1906,NZ Truth,31/1/7</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> He had known that Allen was not a bush cook, but he had said it because a cook was the most urgent need of the moment. In fact, there is seldom a moment in a bush camp when a good cook ... is not the most pressing need of the place. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,43,1971 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [sawmilling]]

>  <b>REED</b> .. someone for a craving for more dainty fare than that provided by the bush cook. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,124 (137, 150)</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> The bush gang ... would include first of all a good bush cook, used to cooking over an open fire in the open fireplace of a hut, and able to bake and roast in the `camp ovens' generally used. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,40</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> NNow, bush cooks that I knew were tremendous fellows at preparing sweets of various kinds .. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves</i>) [NOTES: (Joe Gasparich, Northland kauri country)]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He.. was replaced by a rough Kiwi bush-cook who in a collarless grey shirt shouldered his way through the officers, pointed a hairy arm at Colonel Campbell and shouted, "She's cooked." <i>(1998,From Top to Bottom : Reinga to Bluff,53</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Bush cooks that I knew were tremendous fellows at preparing sweets of various kinds - cakes and so on. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person employed to cook in a bush felling camp.

>  <b>MOORE</b> The bush gang .. would include first of all a good bush cook ... <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,40</i>)

>  <b>DAHM</b> Before we moved into the new house Mum had done her cooking exactly the same as the bush cooks did in the bush camps. <i>(2000,People of the Axe, The,24</i>)

### bush corridor
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a native bush land area, often a gully, that connects two or more land areas.

>  That said, it is worth mentioning that without the bush corridors that link the sanctuary with other parts of the city, we would not be delighting in the native bird life that we now do. And these bush corridors are not inviolate. <i>(Aug 16, 2006,Capital Times,4</i>)

>  I would add, however, that we only enjoy the sanctuary's success thanks to the bush corridors that link the sanctuary to other parts of the city. <i>(Aug 17, 2006,Wellingtonian,10</i>)

>  In the long-term [sic] restoring the streams' [sic] riparian zone will create a bush corridor for bird species and habitat for the plants and animals living in them. <i>(Jul 24, 2006,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: www.ew.govt.nz/news and events/mediareleases]

### bush costume
 
 <i>n.</i> early costume or outfit suitable for wear in the bush A1 or C4. [The following describe a bushman's or bush-farmer's outfit.] (See [cf. bush hat](bri-byo.md#bush-hat))

>  In a belted, blue bush man's frock, hatchet in hand ... he looked in truth the scholar disguised as a wood chopper. <i>(1846,NZ Journal,6/171/166</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Clad in our `bush' costume, but without weapons, and each with a <i>toko toko</i> or long walking-staff in our hands, my fellow traveller and myself set off in excellent spirits, accompanied by five Maori lads, who carried our baggage ... in their <i>pikau</i> or knapsacks, strapped over their shoulders with the leaves of flax. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/3</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> The bush costume of the [Artillery] officers in New Zealand [c.1846] consists of a blue serge shirt, coarse trowsers, hobnail boots, and a cabbage-leaf hat or cap; as like the dress of the street orderlies as anything can be. It is a pity that those who identify a soldier with his red coat, gold lace, feathers and trappings, cannot see the reverse of the picture. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,38</i>)

>  The colonial practice of standing idly smoking at the shop doors in broad daylight, and of wearing a bush costume ... <i>(1852,Australian & NZ Gazette,60/503</i>) [NOTES: (Auckland)]

>  <b>EARP</b> The colonial practice of standing idly smoking at the shop doors in broad daylight, and of wearing a bush costume, is more honoured here in the breach than the observance. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),63</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> A little more attention to dress, and somewhat more formality of manner, are observable in Auckland than in the other settlements. The colonial practice of standing idly smoking at the shop doors in broad daylight, and of wearing bush costume is more honoured in the breach than the observance. <i>(1853,Auckland,67</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> My costume consisted pair of white duck trousers, checked shirt, stout boots, large cabbage-tree hat and a sort of blue flannel frock confined at the waist by a leather belt. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDSON</b> ... blue woollen shirt girthed around the waist by a brown leather belt. <i>(1854,Summer's Excursion,119</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> Proprietor of a farm ... seen in the village in a blue smock-frock ...] <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,54</i>)

>  <b>YOUNG</b> He was accompanied by some of the Missionaries in their `bush' costume, consisting of a white `wide-awake' and other articles to correspond with it. <i>(1858,Southern World,24</i>)

### bush country
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The back bush country is being rapidly cleared for pastoral purposes, chiefly sheep-grazing. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,259</i>)

>  The best fern-root grew on freshly burnt bush country .. <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/71</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> To his abilities as a tracker and scout, and his capacity for hard marching in rough bush country with a heavy swag of ... provisions. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,223</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Theirs was to be a bush farm, for the Government was opening up some bush country said to be the finest land in all New Zealand. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,17</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> The dawn chorus of the birdlife in bush country during the late spring ... is an awe-inspiring sound. <i>(1980,Stag</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> In looking across this lake you perceive a flat bush-country. <i>(1859,Journal,297</i>) [NOTES: 1859 Rochfort Journal Two Expeditions 297]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `Having followed it [the road] up for a few miles to the bend in the Turangarere Valley, we returned to the Maori track over Te Horo-o-moeku, from whence a splendid view of the [47] bush country extending far south towards Marton, was obtained.' <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,46</i>) [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 46-47]

>  <b>BELL</b> `... you would be the right man for the [12] bush country,' McKeown said, `... Flash Harry, who has a contract ... was saying only this morning that he would take on a couple of wages men at bushfalling - so there's a job at hand ...' <i>Ibid.</i> 223 One gets some lovely views in this bush country of yours. <i>(1889,In the Shadow of the Bush,11</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that carries or has carried bush and is newly-broken in for grazing.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Matai Moana block, which is strong bush country, normally carries about 5,000 ewes and 300 Black Poll cows. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,126</i>)

>  4 and 6 tooth woolly wethers off bush country. <i>(Nov 5, 1903,Hawera Star,3 250</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The balance, about twenty-five per cent, was good bush country. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,39</i>)

>  <b>DONEY</b> On bush country, after burning, the rye grass and clovers are almost certain to do well. <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,6</i>)

>  5,000 acres - men's whare, carrying capacity 2 sheep per acre, Hawke's Bay district - first class land for cattle; all bush country. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,234</i>)

>  I am felling and burning this year 25 acres of bush country. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,342</i>)

>  The settler on bush country did not require a great deal of capital to start his farming operations. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,13</i>)

>  We have always farmed in steep bush country... <i>(Aug 1, 1949,NZ Dairy Exporter,45</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> My father was keen for me to hold on to my bush country. I was in favour of having it fallen out and converted into pasture. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,52</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMSON</b> Whether bush country will make good pastoral country can usually be ascertained by the sort of trees which are most predominant. Tawa bush country will make good grass country, whereas birch bush country will usually turn out to be poor. <i>(1981,Sheep Farming in the NZ Bush,1</i>)

### bush cow
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bush cattle](bri-byo.md#bush-cattle))

>  <b>GRACE</b> Now they are making every effort to reclaim their bush cows, and great numbers are milked regularly <i>(1928,Pioneer Missionary Among the Maoris, A,19</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 31 T.S.Grace Report of Turanga District in 1928 Grace A Pioneer Missionary 19]

### bush creamery
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an early dairy factory located in a bush district.

>  But between the prices given at a bush creamery and for milk sold for town supply there is a wide margin. <i>(Jul 1899,NZ Farmer,238</i>)

### bush creek
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Here the edge of a bush-creek is fringed with <i>Muehlenbeckia. </i> <i>(1912,TrNZI,44/64</i>) [NOTES: Checked dec 1989 against AND/OED2: nf references on bush checkfile]

### bush creeper
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a native climbing plant, suitable for mattress material.

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> The actual beds were made of long straight saplings about an inch thick, on top of which was spread soft fern and feathery bush creeper <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,66</i>)

### bush cricket
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an informal district cricket match.

>  bush cricket - an annual district picnic and cricket match, often between neighbouring districts. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### bush crow
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... I always had a couple at least of wood-hens and ducks, or bush-crows, suspended from the ridge-pole of my tent. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,43</i>) [NOTES: [usu. here simplex crow]]

### bush custom
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a behaviour typical of life in a bush settlement.

>  <b>BOOTH</b> According to bush custom it was usual to dub all fresh arrivals 'new chums' until they had satisfactorily passed certain ordeals in bush life. They should be able to ride a buckjumper... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,15</i>)

>  We find no-one "at home" but there is a billy handy, in which there is some good lukewarm tea, which following the bush custom, we proceed to commandeer. Refreshed by the national draught, we make onwards ... <i>(Aug 14, 1900,Hawera Star,4</i>)

### bush dairying
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the basic dairy farming activities in newly cleared bush districts.
