>  <b>WASWO</b> In the 1850s bush dairying in the Wairarapa was closely associated with the establishment and later expansion of smallholder farm settlements. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,17</i>)

### bush dak
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of marijuana

>  The demand for Northland's "bush dak" has dropped, he believes, because of a shift towards new genetically engineered strains of cannabis, known as "skunk". <i>(Jun 27, 1998,Dominion,13</i>)

>  Northland as the "bush dak" capital has been mortally wounded. <i>(Jun 27, 1998,Dominion,13</i>) [NOTES: Check date 27 or 29 June]

>  City drug users.. are turning away from the unsophisticated "bush dak" of the Far North to trendier designer drugs. <i>(Aug 27, 1998,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Many growers have left town and everyone is getting a bargain in the over-supplied cannabis market, as dealers struggle to get rid of their "bush dak". <i>(Jun 27, 1998,Dominion,13</i>)

### bush devil
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> a weta (See [taipo](t-ten.md#taipo))



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHARPE</b> In the middle of that cable there was a hell of a sag, and we could see no way to evade the tightening job. As equipment we had a `bush devil', a sort of wire-straining outfit on which two men are expected to apply a pressure up to twenty tons, and a massive wrench. Hooking the strainer on to the base of a handy tree we got its claws, or whatever you call them, gripped on the long cable, above the turn buckle, and worked the lever until the joined sections were slack. <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,32</i>)

### bush diamond
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a worthwhile person with a rough and ready manner.

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> I think he is one of these bush diamonds, rough on the outside. <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,43</i>)

### bush disease
 
 <i>n.</i> a potentially fatal disease of cattle and sheep, caused by cobalt deficiency; bush sickness (See [also Tauranga disease](t-ten.md#tauranga-disease))

>  Mr Gilruth visited the Rotorua bush lands re `bush disease' ... <i>(1899,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,11</i>)

>  27. Aston, B.C.: Bush Disease. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,808</i>)

>  <i>(1911,NZ Journal of Agriculture,3/394-399</i>)

>  "Bush" Disease. This affliction appears to be due rather to innutrition [sic] than to any disease, and is apparently confined to the Rotorua district. The animals seem to pine away, but if removed to other and older pastures they usually recover. <i>(1897,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,V/68</i>)

>  Bush-disease in Cattle. Beyond a few cases belonging to a settler of Arahiwi, I have not seen anything. <i>(1898,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,VI/88</i>)

>  [Hdg] "Tauranga" or "Bush Disease". This peculiar wasting disease, which is confined to the tract of land lying in the vicinity of Tauranga, and extending from there to Rotorua and Lichfield, was made the subject of an investigation by Mr. Park three years ago... It appears that both sheep and cattle will do well and put on condition for the first few months after being placed on this diseased country, but as time goes on a gradual wasting sets in, which ends in death if they are not removed to another district. <i>(1899,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,VII/87</i>)

>  All the evidence gathered points to the condition being one of malnutrition rather than of disease, properly speaking, and I have therefore adopted the term 'bush sickness' in connection with it, instead of 'bush disease' as heretofore. <i>(Aug 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,118</i>)

### bush district
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area where bush settlers felled and farmed bush land.

>  <b>BELL</b> In one of the lately settled bush districts of the Wellington Province, where the clearings were as yet comparatively few and of no very great extent - ... this story opens. <i>Ibid.</i> 10 [He had] emigrated to the Colony some years previously, and, at first in the South Island and afterwards in the Bush districts of the North, had ... found employment ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,1</i>)

>  <b>DONEY</b> Essays on Bushfarming 16 In a bush district this [run-out] is a very serious matter, because when the grass leaves the ground, what is the farmer to do? <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,16</i>)

>  Lambing in the bush districts this season has been very successful. <i>(Jan 1893,NZ Farmer,18</i>)

>  As thistles in the bush district thrive so well (too well for most farmers) here is a means of transferring a crop that has no value and only chokes up the ground, into a valuable article.[silage] <i>(Jun 21, 1898,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  It is a curious fact that it is almost impossible to raise this tuber anywhere east of the Whareroa road (in the bush districts, at all events.) <i>(Aug 15, 1900,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  In bush districts it is not easy - in some cases practically impossible - to save hay or grow roots ... <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,6</i>)

>  In the bush district north-west of Masterton and Eketahuna there is abundance of feed on account of the showery weather. The common trouble in the bush district is the scarcity of winter feed. <i>(Apr 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,224</i>)

>  .. districts which were either firstly infested with blackberry or Californian thistle; or secondly, bush-districts where the surface-sowing of grass-seed was the customary method of laying down grass. <i>(May 1914,Hawke's Bay Herald,22</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> In the former bush districts of the North Island, and in the South Island scrub and fern areas. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,5</i>)

### bush doctor
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a local resident in an isolated area with some knowledge of first aid.

>  <b>BARKER</b> A bush doctor, name of Tomkins, who was likely to be round by Simmons' 'cos [OEDS omits '] o' his missus. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,150</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The Rosses, who were our neighbours, often relied upon my father who was the local 'bush doctor' as he had a good knowledge of medicine. <i>(1997,Pigs and Me: Tales of the Bygone Bush.,63</i>)

### bush dog
 
 <i>n.</i> a wild dog (living in the bush); occas. a Maori dog (See [cf. bush-bee](bri-byo.md#bush-bee),[bush pig](bri-byo.md#bush-pig))

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> ... heard the barking of a bush dog. <i>(1847,Journal,85</i>)

>  The dogs belonging to the native who was our fellow-traveller to Omurua, put up a wild bush-dog, which was exceedingly fierce, and attacked them furiously; ... It was covered with long bushy hair, and in appearance greatly resembled a jackall. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/130</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> `I was tired of you and Hamlin,' he [Manaena] told Tanner at the Commission hearing: `you were like bush-dogs chasing me'. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Canis familiaris</i>, the dog introduced by European settlers run wild or as semi-domesticated, esp. a mongrel dog associated with Maori settlements, usu. with epithets, bush(-)dog (bush usu. in sense [no.?] `wild, undomesticated'), Maori dog (Maori often in derog. sense [?no.] `mongrel', or in sense [no.?] `un-domesticated'), native dog, wild dog.

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> ... heard the barking of a bush dog. <i>(1847,Journal,85</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The dogs belonging to the native who was our fellow-traveller to Omurua, put up a wild bush-dog, which was exceedingly fierce, and attacked them furiously; ... It was covered with long bushy hair, and in appearance greatly resembled a jackall. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/130</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> `I was tired of you and Hamlin,' he [Manaena] told Tanner at the Commission hearing: `you were like bush-dogs chasing me'. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PASCOE</b> I have seen [wekas] by sheer cheek and audacity rout an experienced bush dog .. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,231</i>) [NOTES: c1899 Douglas in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 231]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a dog trained to work in the bush

>  <b>PASCOE</b> I have seen [wekas] by sheer cheek and audacity rout an experienced bush dog ... <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,231</i>) [NOTES: c1899 Douglas in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 231]

### bush drain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ditch or drain lined with manuka fascines, or those from other native trees.

>  <b>FISK</b> Bush drain: the next cheapest alternative. Dig the trench at least 900 mm deep. Put in a 600mm layer of branches with fine leaves and twigs at the top to stop soil filtering down into the bigger wood. If you have a cheap metal Source, use about 450mm of scrub then 150 mm of coarse shingle. <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,27</i>)

### bush dress
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Self-consciously clad in their bush dress, `essential attire', they were hanging-on in there for what had been billed as the most incredible book launch of the year ... <i>(Nov 16, 1986,Sunday News,5</i>)

### bush drive
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a method of treefelling where trees are part-scarfed in front of one or more that are fully felled, taking the part-scarfed trees down with them. (See [Taranaki drive](t-ten.md#taranaki-drive))

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> A 'bush drive' was contrived by cutting the trees up the slopes only partially through, then the ones at the top on the ridges were felled completely and these would carry those below down the whole hillside in a spectacular fashion. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,162</i>)

>  Those great crashing bush-drives, when one tree was aimed to hit another partly-cut tree, which in turn smashed into another half-severed tree, and so on smashing and roaring downhill to the gully's bottom, ... the bush snails dying, the woodhens confounded, the brown pebbled creek and all its reflections from the beginning shrinking back in dismay. <i>(Feb 3, 1985,NZ Times,12</i>) [NOTES: (Jim Henderson)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> It was a wonderful sight to watch a bush drive. It was a work of art and those bushmen were experts. <i>(1998,Goodness Gracious Me,116</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> A "bush-drive": that's a really terrific thing if you haven't seen one. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,71</i>)

### bush edge
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> Now ... there was some hard fighting ... in the clearing at the bush-edge. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,84</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... winged gossamer of alien bush-edge weeds - wall lettuce, Canadian groundsell and hawkweed. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,65</i>)

>  We moved along the bush edge, encountering another juvenile <i>P. turneri ...</i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,13</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Shooting had been confined mainly to the bluffs facing the Wilberforce River, along the bush-edge of the flats and in the matagouri scrub on the river flats. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,86</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... winged gossamer of alien bush-edge weeds - wall lettuce, Canadian groundsell and hawkweed. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,65</i>)

>  Behind the bush-edge fringe of mountain beech, silver-beech extended some hundreds of feet up the mountainside, then mountain came in again to the grassline. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,48</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> They rode along the bush-edge to where long tussock clearings fingered up the gullies. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,53</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a margin between dense bush and open farmland.

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> I used binoculars to watch the bush edges .. First a white face would appear at the bush edge ... <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,180</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> .. we snared quite an assortment of dodgers - even half a dozen wild cattle caught napping well out from the bush edge. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,97</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> An old hind feeding just beyond the bush edge ... <i>(1964,Deer Shooting Days,39</i>)

>  <b>HIST AFFAIRS</b> Apart from the bush-edges sketched upon early maps there are few records of the vegetative cover of Hawke's Bay in early days. <i>(1958,Picture of a Province,8</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> At the bush edge he piled the debris in a long row like a blockade fence. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,119</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> The shepherd doing the 'beat' by the bush edge would often carry a rifle. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,93</i>)

### bush edition
 
 <i>n.</i> of an up-country newspaper, the `rural' or backblocks edition.

>  The bush-edition howls out innumerable orders. <i>(1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,8/128</i>)

### bush engine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> The Hauhaus at this place made use of a curious bush-engine against their enemies. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/150,1983 ed.</i>)

### bush estate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(Jun 24, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/147</i>) [NOTES: [get quot] (J.M. Richmond)]

### bush ewe
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe grazed on undersown bush or rough country.

>  86-tooth ewes, off hills; 700 bush ewes ... <i>(Mar 8, 1907,Hawera Star,8 375</i>)

### bush excursion
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> Riding, boating, cricket, and bush excursions, are the favourite out-door amusements. <i>(1853,Auckland,68</i>)

### bush express
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The "bush express", which consists of one carriage known as the "pie-cart" and a light locomotive, leaves the cookshop daily at 7.15 a.m. <i>(Jul 25, 1928,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

### bush face
 
 <i>n.</i> (bush, attrib. in sense of bushed `covered with bush') (See [cf. bush country](bri-byo.md#bush-country),[bush range(s)](bri-byo.md#bush-range(s)))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... high bush faces. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,110</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> All the bush faces were spotted with the dead branches of trees. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,32</i>)

### bush falcon
 
 <i>n.</i> an endangered indigenous bird of prey, the smallest of the three forms of karearea.

>  The Bush Falcon can be found in forest (including exotic pine plantations) and bush patches of the North Island and northwestern South Island. <i>(2007,Internet - see Notes,2</i>) [NOTES: www.ranz.org.nz/raptor-information.]

>  <i>New Zealand Falcon Falco novaeseelandiae</i> GMELIN, 1788 ...[155] OTHER NAMES: <i>Karearea, sparrowhawk, bush hawk, quail hawk, eastern falcon, southern falcon, bush falcon. ... Bush falcon: In the North Island confined to the main ranges, ... Eastern falcon: The main ranges of the South Island ... Southern Falcon: Fiordland, the Auckland Islands, and Stewart Island. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,154</i>)

>  Bush falcons - the most widespread of the three races of New Zealand falcon or karearea - are highly territorial. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Geographic,No. 39/59</i>)

>  [Caption] A rare New Zealand bush falcon perches on the hand of the Matthew Wong who nursed it back to health after it was wounded by an air gun pellet. <i>(Feb 3, 2000,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> As I came back I saw a bush falcon in the beech before the door, dusky-black when he spread his wings, but speckled like a thrush below. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,163</i>)

### bush falling
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the felling of native forest and undergrowth.

>  <b>DONEY</b> A considerable saving is effected in the cost of bushfalling ... <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,2</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> We worked steadily at bush-falling. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,184</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Bush-falling had barely been started. <i>(1921,Tutira,203</i>)

### bush fare
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The mountain ... added to the<i> bush </i>appetite we had brought with us. Instead of the old proverb, `Good wine needs no <i>bush</i>', the Bishop ... always reads it, `Good bush needs no wine'; ... I feel as if I have a lee-way of a fortnight's bush-fare to make up. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,111</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Aug 16-18 Abraham Walk from Auckland to Taranaki in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 111]

### bush farm
 
 <i>n.</i> a farm cleared from the bush

>  <b>DAVIN</b> ... someone from a bush farm had taken it from a wild pig's litter and shoved it in the sale ... <i>(1959,No Remittance,138</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Several miles beyond this block, out across the rolling gumland hills, thickly covered with short manuka scrub, lay a back-block section, a bush farm in the early stages of development ... <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,38,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Theirs was to be a bush farm, for the Government was opening up some bush country said to be the finest land in all New Zealand. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,17</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> He married her and took her off to his first bush-farm. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,115</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> Rover surprised me by arriving at my bush farm three miles from the Bonnet. ... After that he often appeared where I was cutting bush. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,65</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Any man who chooses to spend three years breaking in a bush farm in the North Island . <i>(1889,New Zealand After 50 Years,144</i>)

>  My experiences in settling here on our bush farm. <i>(1916,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1916 letter in 1939 Winter King Country 13]

>  <b>MULGAN</b> [He] married, and invested in a bush farm at Normanby, in Taranaki. This was in 1881, ... <i>(1958,Making of a New Zealander, The,81</i>)

>  `The Melon' comments: `Diggers, Go for the bush farm If you like, but tale a 'orrible example of me, and beware of widders all yer life!' <i>(Nov 1, 1918,Quick March,15</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> A settler ... and his young son had been found tomahawked on their bush farm ... about four miles from Drury; ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/253</i>)

>  [Advt] 146 Acres - Workingman's Bush-farm, about 16 miles from Auckland... The property consists of about 90 acres of Virgin Bush. <i>(Jun 4, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm property developed from standing bush.

>  Remembered 30th July defendant was on the bush farm. <i>(Jun 30, 1888,Egmont Star,9</i>)

>  Among those valleys the courageous women helping their soldier husbands to make a 'go' of the new bush farms. <i>(Oct 30, 1926,NZ Dairy Exporter,41</i>)

>  No two bush farmers, even of experience, are of same opinion regarding the best method of working a bush farm. <i>(Jul 1887,NZ Farmer,196</i>)

>  <b>DONEY</b> There is a matter which is rarely ever attended to on bush farms that should command some attention .. the planting of shelter. <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,10</i>)

>  For sale - Improved bush farm, situated ... <i>(Apr 17, 1896,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  I am dairying on a bush farm. <i>(Jul 20, 1923,NZ Journal of Agriculture,63</i>)

>  When I was first married, my husband took me to a lonely bush farm ... <i>(Oct 29, 1927,NZ Dairy Exporter,40</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> It was a great day for the youngsters on the bush farms when Chew Chong, the old Chinese storekeeper, found out the value of fungus which grew on the stumps in the bush clearings. <i>(1945,Pioneer Looks Back, A,25</i>)

>  <b>SPENCER</b> We were going back into the bush country to make our home. It was a bush farm in the Waioeka Gorge. <i>(1992,Waioeka Pioneering Saga,203</i>)

>  He took up a fair-sized bush farm at Onuku, and married Miss Grandi. <i>(1945,BLANK ENTRY,20</i>) [NOTES: in KEEGAN (1983) The First Generation]

### bush farmer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who develops and farms a property from bush settlement.

>  <b>WARR</b> A bush farmer, with about 200 acres, would be felling the last 20-30 acres of his holding after about ten years ... <i>(1988,From Bush-Burn to Butter,42</i>)

>  No two bush farmers, even of experience, are of same opinion regarding the best method of working a bush farm. <i>(Jul 1887,NZ Farmer,196</i>)

>  All the talk in the district now is butter, and it is scarcely to be wondered at when it is considered how closely the market price of that commodity is bound up with the bush-farmer's prosperity. <i>(Dec 1, 1888,Egmont Star,9</i>)

>  <b>DONEY</b> All classes of sheep are not suitable for the bush farmers. <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,3</i>)

>  Were it not for the rather low reading of the financial barometer, the bush farmers of the Dominion would have little ground for complaint. <i>(Oct 20, 1914,NZ Journal of Agriculture,232</i>)

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> I also found that I was a bush farmer with 60 acres of clearing for the most part badly burned ... <i>(Jan 17, 1858,Letter,338</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>WASWO</b> ..most of the bush farmers chose sheep because they were more economical and they provided the quickest return. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,8</i>)

### bush farming
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WARR</b> Walter Riddell was an early bush-farming pioneer on the Otago Peninsula. <i>(1988,From Bush-Burn to Butter,57</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the clearing of bush in order to lay down pasture.

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> My own future is as uncertain as ever. I do not mean as regards my occupation .. bush farming ... <i>(Dec 2, 1855,Letter,184</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>DONEY</b> [title] <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming</i>)

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> He had seen fortunes lost at bush farming as a result of bad burns. <i>(1977,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,96</i>)

### bush farmstead
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cleared area where land can be lived on and farmed.

>  <b>COMBS</b> The clearing of a bush farmstead was a full-time job in itself. <i>(1951,Growing Up in the Forty-Mile Bush,37</i>)

### bush fastness
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bush section for development into a farm.

>  .. the success or otherwise of the pioneer grazier depends to a very great extent upon the security of his fences, especially in the bush fastnesses of the King Country. <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,927</i>)

### bush feed
 
 <i>n.</i> green-feed growing in the bush; leaves of forest trees and shrubs as feed for stock (see, for example, burnt feed).

>  We have 36 head of cattle running this winter on the back bush land.... The bush feed keeps the cattle in fine order in winter time. <i>(Aug 8, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/154,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W.Richmond)]

>  <b>HAY</b> He [the open-land farmer] has no bush-feed for cattle as we have, ... It is eight or twelve years before the bush-farmer gets a [208] chance of ploughing; ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,207</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) strippings of foliage from native bush for stock feed.

>  <b>REA</b> .. in the winter-times [they] would drive their cows over, pitch a tent and spend weeks at a time cutting bush feed for them. <i>(1991,Honey and Cheese,73</i>)

>  Farm management has also changed from wintering the herd for three months on bush feed and roughage, to winter cropping <i>(Jan 1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,27</i>)

### bush fence
 
 <i>n.</i> in the only recorded use the sense and force of bush are obscure, and if not a slip of the pen for `brush', prob. means `made of material from the bush A1' (cf. 1855 Journal St Leonard's Station, Canterbury - `brush fence' for stock yard'; (See [cf. bush harrow](bri-byo.md#bush-harrow); [brush harrow](bri-byo.md#brush-harrow))

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> In my time we would make an early potato garden by felling the trees in a sheltered hollow where there was no growth underneath, making a bush fence of the trunks and branches round the plot which made it both sheltered and stock proof. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,8</i>)

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Making a bush fence. <i>(1849,Journal,293</i>)

>  <b>PERHAM</b> ... a kind of yard fenced with logs piled one on top of the other. In New Zealand it would be called a `bush fence'. <i>(1958,Kimberley Flying Column,32</i>)

### bush fencing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  All hands at bush fencing and all very tired. Karawas [supplejacks] as thick as a cloud, every tree hung together like wax. <i>(Jun 25, 1888,Chudleigh Diary,362,1950 ed.</i>)

### bush fever
 
 <i>n.</i> a fever of cattle caused by the ingestion of certain bush plants

>  <b>KEENE</b> Those [cattle] accustomed to hina-hina could eat it with impunity, but to strangers it was poison. We lost four milking cows ... We had no further trouble with bush fever unless strange cattle were brought in. <i>(1967,Tale of a Timber Town,61</i>)

### bush fight
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> Those who escaped Ngatapa ... includ[ed] a number of women who survived the terrors and hardships of the bush fight. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/281,1983 ed.</i>)

### bush fighter
 
 <i>n.</i> an irregular during the Land Wars, skilled at fighting in the bush (See [cf. bushranger](bri-byo.md#bushranger))

>  <b>ALEXANDER</b> Active and wiry men, of sound constitutions, are best adapted for bush-fighters. <i>(1873,Bush Fighting,1</i>)

>  Four further AC patrols - cavalry and bush-fighters - were dispatched from inland localities to scout and endeavour to intercept Te Kooti. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### bush fighting
 
 <i>n.</i> irregular or guerilla fighting in the bush (esp. during the Land Wars)

>  <b>F MATHEW</b> [The natives] beat us in skirmishing and bush fighting. <i>(1847,Report,4/15</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> They [officers] can fancy them [bush-rats] to be Maories, by a little stretch of imagination, and practise bush-fighting from behind the bedposts. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,26</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER</b> Bush fighting is a comprehensive term for warfare conducted in forests, in broken ground and on the hill-side. Whereever cover can be got, in attack and defence, under the canopy of heaven, there bush fighting can be practised, and it is highly useful to practise it as a part of army manoeuvres. <i>(1873,Bush Fighting,1</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Yet there were not only settler-soldiers but many of the veterans of the 65th which could have been formed into an excellent forest-ranging corps, competent to follow the Maori into the roughest country ... But it was not until 1863 that the value of such bush-fighting companies was recognized. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/211</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Finally the British Government withdrew the bulk of the Imperial forces and Frederick Weld, head of the new `self-reliant' colonial Administration, proceeded to employ the New Zealand militia in unconventional bush fighting [43] better suited to cope with the elusive Maori. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),42</i>)

>  <b>BARTLETT</b> `Von' [Tempsky] was dead, famed through New Zealand for his reckless bravery and his skill in bush fighting. <i>(1974,Emigrants, The,78</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Had the Maori combined, they would have driven the Government troops and settlers into the sea, and the English nation would then have learned what the Caffres are now teaching them, that the arms, dress and equipment of the English soldier are the worst possible for bush fighting. <i>(1852,Saunders' Monthly Magazine,Vol. 1/</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Saunders' Monthly Magazine Vol I in 1976 Canterbury Colony 19]

### bush fire
 
 <i>n.</i> a fire in uncleared or forest land; also called a forest-fire

>  <b>WELD</b> If provisions run short [the sheepfarmer] should not think it a degradation, gentleman born though he might be, to heap fresh logs on the bush fire, to put on the soup pot, or to bake a damper in the ashes. <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,11</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> As the night darkened we saw far in the distance the red glow of a huge bush-fire. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,14</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> That night I enjoyed - what had often pleased me to read of in books - a bush-fire. Six or seven large old dry trees had been chopped down and cast onto the already large fire , which threw out a capital heat and lit up the whole of the bush surrounding us. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,72</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> By the side of the creek, the Grey Party ... pitched their tent. Of course they lit a bush fire. They rolled huge logs into it, and piled branches of odiferous gum-trees on it, until it blazed high up into the brilliant atmosphere. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,2/vi/64,1889,1974 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [?Aust ref]]

>  <b>MONE</b> ... I smoked my evening pipe. This I did by the light of a good bush-fire, that danced and flickered on the trees and scrub around me, and showed the only living animals that formed my society - grey bush rats. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,31</i>) [NOTES: <i>Ibid.</i> 45 Another hour saw the whole party under a temporary break-wind, with a roaring bush-fire hissing under the pouring rain, ...]



 
 <i>n.</i> [[Aust. usage applies to any large fire in the open; NZ mainly to a forest fire - other fires would be termed scrub fires, etc.]]

>  A great bush fire on either side of Merton ... <i>(1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/345</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Man, bushfires and cats have destroyed [tuataras] on the mainland. <i>(1855,Journal,9/138</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> We were then just opposite Akaroa Bay, ... Last night we saw a light which we thought to be the Southern light, but which turned out to be a bush fire. <i>(Oct 17, 1862,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> For the prevention of bush and other fires, and the restraint of persons firing bush, scrub, ... to the danger of life or property. <i>(1949,Selected Documents,112b</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Nov 2 Sir George Grey Memorandum in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 112b]

>  ... the climate is so humid as to be unfavorable[sic] to the spreading of bush fires. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/8</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  ... the constantly recurring bush fires have cleared off the stately Ti palms (so fragrant in early spring); ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/49</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  ... the once silent woods now resound with the blows of the felling-axe ... the Wattle-bird is no longer to be found in its old haunts; it seeks shelter amongst the higher parts of the bushy gullies ... the condition of the forest is changing under the effects of ... constantly-recurring bush fires. ... The Kokako loving a moist temperature will probably soon entirely forsake its ancient places of resort. <i>(1874,TrNZI,6/146</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  Very bright Aurora australis due south at 9.30, 10 etc. Abner has seen this light two or three times lately ... They thought it was a bush fire. <i>(Nov 3, 1882,Chudleigh Diary,314,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> Far away up this vista, some thin blue smoke went up from a bush fire. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,94</i>)

>  <b>HOPEFUL</b> ... there were fearful bush-fires on the west coast of the North Island at Taranaki, whence many of the settlers had to flee for their lives. <i>(1887,Taken In,186</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> When the base of the mountains is veiled ... by the blue smoke of the too frequent bush-fires, the sharp peaks seem to rise to empyrean heights ... <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,151</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Bush fires were now more frequent. Decaying timber, the remnants of former fires, ... and stumps were now like tinder. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,199</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bush-fire</i>, ... forests and grass on fire in hot summers. 1868. C. Dilke, `Greater Britian, `vol. ii. part iii. c. iii. p. 32: <i>(1898,Austral English,70</i>)

>  ... we find extensive tracts of forest composed chiefly of beech, unfortunately fast disappearing in the saw-mills and in the bush-fires which so frequently devastate the hillsides. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,186</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The New Zealand Quail, which was very plentiful, is nearly extinct now: Bush-fires have burnt it, and dogs, cats, and rats have eaten it, but the Australian and Californian varieties have filled its place. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,69</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Jessie was used to bush fires. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,62</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> This was in the big bush fire that destroyed so much property at Waimate in November, 1878. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,142</i>)

>  And from the engine-room there was enough smoke to make you think there were not two kerosene engines below, but a bush fire. But even an Australian bush fire couldn't smell as bad. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,15</i>)

>  It was like reading those school essays we used to write, before Professor Gordon, in which every noun had to have at least one `expressive adjective', in which a bush fire was a fierce holocaust raging down stately corridors of ancient rimus. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23/213</i>) [NOTES: Pearson `Fretful Sleepers']

>  We may speak of kauri forest or beech forest, but we get lost in the bush, fight bush fires, and climb above the bushline. The kauri forest, like the mixed podocarp forest, the sub-tropical rain forest, ... and such-like are terms that come from the botanist and forester, not from the layman - or from the bushman. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,23</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']



 
 <i>n.</i> [is this a separate use?]

>  <b>BULLER</b> But, partly owing to the introduction of dogs, ... and partly to the prevalence of the so-called `bush-fires' or burning of the runs (a necessary incident of sheep-farming in a new country), the Quail has rapidly disappeared ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/226</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to burn bush in the process of clearing land for pasture.

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> Bush fired. Went to Norsewood evening. Home early. <i>(Feb 1893,Brabazon Diaries,11</i>)

### bush fireplace
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As the casualty was getting worse while the final touches were being put on to the building, the party had to leave without having a chance to try the bush fireplace, always a touchy thing to build. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,266</i>)

### bush firing
 
 <i>n.</i> the act or work of burning (falled) bush to clear land; the setting fire to forest (cf. bush- burning).

>  The houses in this neighbourhood have been in some danger from bush firings ... then just as several people had lighted their clearings a strong gale from the south-east, what is called here `the mountain wind', sprang up. In a few hours all the dead trees and stumps in the neighbourhood were on fire. <i>(Feb 24, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/141</i>) [NOTES: (J. Maria Richmond)]

>  Bush-firing and logging up is a job which is often given to a new chum. <i>(1889,Collinson's Colonial Magazine,26</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the burning of native bush to develop pasture.

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> The houses in the neighbourhood have been in some danger from the bush firings. <i>(Feb 24, 1854,Letter,141</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I 141]

### bush flat(s)
 
 <i>n.</i> a bushed or forested flat or flats (See [cf. bush country](bri-byo.md#bush-country))

>  Another very considerable stream running through fine bush flats. <i>(1847,NZ Journal,7/190/90</i>)

>  ... secluded lagoons on the bush-flats in the vicinity of Lake Brunner. <i>(1888,TrNZI,21/223</i>) [NOTES: (Smith)]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Four deer and three hours later we came to a long bush-flat where the trees were enormous. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,136</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area of stocked flat land, at least partially covered in bush.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The mustering of this block involved combing the bush flats with the dogs. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,222</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> We worked our way up the valley, seeing the old cow and young bull as we went, until finally we located a good camping spot on a bush-flat beside the river. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,65</i>)

### bush flax
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Astelia nervosa</i> (prob. from `growing in the bush A1, rather than from gen. Eng. `growing as a bush; bush-sized'); a tussocky, flax-like plant (astelia) with berries, red when ripe, found usually in bush; also called kakaha.

>  <b>MURPHY</b> <i>(1885,Handbook of NZ Gardening</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen) [bush flax]]

>  <i>(1907,AJHR,C-8</i>) [NOTES: (And) [bush flax] Cockayne Report on Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]

>  <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And) [b-flax] Cockayne Botanical Survey Tongariro National Park]

>  <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: (And) Turner Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  <i>(1915,AJHR,C-6</i>) [NOTES: (And). [b-flax]Cockayne Plant Covering Kennedy's Bush (Canterbury)]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i> ASTELIA ... nervosa</i> ... Bush Flax. KAKAHA (strong). <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,21</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Astelia fragrans </i>BUSH FLAX ... Kakaha ... Big fresh green tussocky clumps up to about 1 m tall. ... (= <i>A. nervosa </i> var. <i>sylvestris </i>). <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,300</i>)

>  Kaiwharawhara was the food of the bush flax, the edible young leaves and stems of a plant found growing in the forked branches of the puketea tree. <i>(Dec 17, 1983,Evening Post,8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Astelia montana</i>

>  Cockayne Botanical Survey Stewart Island <i>(1909,AJHR,C-12</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### bush flow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [beekeeping] the nectar flow from native flowers.

>  In the southern part of the Hawera district, early bush flows of nectar can be expected from five finger, Maori privet, and kowhai from September onward. <i>(May 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,472</i>)

### bush flower
 
 <i>n.</i> generic term for any flowers growing in the bush, apparently occas. used by (?literary minded) botanists (See [cf. bush bee](bri-byo.md#bush-bee),[bush bird](bri-byo.md#bush-bird))

>  The Parroquet may be frequently observed in confinement, and the Tui ... has to exchange for the dewy nectar of bush flowers, a monotonous diet of soaked bread ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/44</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> [The Bell-bird's] food consists of ... small berries, such as those of the karamu (<i>Coprosma lucida</i>) and other shrubs, and the honey of various kinds of bush-flowers. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,94,{1888 2nd edn 1/90} ed.</i>)

### bush fly
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> Bush flies, mosquitoes and sandflies were numbered among the lesser scourges. Food, blankets and clothes were quickly `blown'; ... <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,277,1962 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEAME</b> He dried his whitebait on coconut matting and preserved it that way to the delight of many blowflies referred to by native West Coasters as bush flies. <i>(1985,Gold, Coins, Greenstone, & Bottles,30</i>)

### bush flying
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [dee-hunting] the piloting of fixed-wing aircraft at low altitudes in bush-covered terrain.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> Goodwin was a pioneer in bush flying in New Zealand. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,49</i>)

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> .. It's the sort of thing a pilot is not supposed to do, but I had to get out that day and I made it. That's what bush flying is all about. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,136</i>)

### bush folk
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) inhabitants of bush districts.

>  <b>DONEY</b> I am glad to say that most of the bush folks know how to work. <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,24</i>)

### bush food
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> Rannock fed his prison labour on honest bush food: fresh meat, billy tea, enormous flat damper scones cooked on the three-legged camp oven. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,70</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> A rather good bush vegetable is the <i>pikopiko</i>, the curled shoots of the <i>mauku</i> fern. The natives have long given up the use of these bush foods; ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/428,1983 ed.</i>)

### bush fright
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> Jake hesitates. There's a loneliness about the bush and something that pulls taut the nerves. ... No wonder the pioneers burnt the bush. Jake remembers old settlers speaking about such experiences. Bush fright, they reckoned. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,80,1979 ed.</i>)

### bush fruit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The sweetest of the bush fruits was the tawhara (the fruit of the kiekie) but unfortunately it only bore fruit and ripened once every seven years. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,28</i>)

### bush fungus
 
 <i>n.</i> hakeke, Taranaki wool (See [also fungus](fly-fuz.md#fungus))

>  <b>COWAN</b> They unloaded a packhorse and brought in a dozen bags of bush fungus, the hakeke of the Maori, which grows on old stumps and logs so plentifully. Now fungus was always a marketable commodity, for the China market; it brought sixpence a pound or more from the storekeepers, who sold it to the town dealers; it went to make a choice soup for rich Chinese. <i>(1937,Pakeha & Maori,1/4</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Our chief source of revenue was to gather in the bush fungus, which usually grew upon the dead and partly decayed stems of karaka and mahoe trees, dry it in the open air, and sell it to the Chinese merchants <i>(1951,Confessions,20</i>)

### bush gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of forestry bushfellers or pruners.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A bush gang must always work as a team and work a block accordingly, ... A bushman has two meals during the day, one at 10 a.m. and the next at 2 p.m. They are always referred to as `smoko'. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,59</i>)

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> The camps were company-owned and provided bush gangs with accomodation and meals close to the job. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,58</i>)

>  <b>CLEVELAND</b> The bush gang drag out the logs they have cut. <i>(1966,Silent Land, The,81</i>)

>  <b>NGAWHIKA</b> In November of '84 the opportunity of employment with the freezing company came up so left the bush gang to start work at their Mamaku plant. <i>(1996,Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting,88</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> He ranged the bush blocks and employed the predominantly Ngapuhi bush gangs to cut 1665 acres in the winter of 1911. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,79</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> For some years she cooked for bush gangs, her home some bush shanty away up a hidden gully. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,193</i>)

### bush garden
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOLMES</b> Initially ... they [Maoris] set about felling and clearing areas of bush in readiness for cultivation ... This forming of `bush gardens' was the traditional means of claiming invaded territory. <i>(1984,Chatham Islands,25</i>)

### bush girl
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bush boy](bri-byo.md#bush-boy))

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Very frequently, as bush boys and girls well know, their [Muehlenbeckia] rope-like stems hang swaying from the forest roof .... <i>(1910,NZ Plants</i>)

### bush grass
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) rank grass.

>  <b>CARR</b> .. there was a grove of kahikatea trees with a small patch of bush-grass just in front. <i>(1997,Wild Boars of Oruaiti, The,10</i>)

### bush ground
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land with standing bush, to be developed into farm land.

>  Anyone with a large capital can take up a block of bush ground. <i>(Jul 1887,NZ Farmer,196</i>)

>  The venture [sheep-grazing in bush] will be watched with interest, as there are many persons who argue that bush ground is not suited to sheep farming. <i>(Jul 1887,NZ Farmer,215</i>)

>  <b>DONEY</b> Anyone can, with a large capital, take up a block of bush ground and have it felled, burned, grassed and stocked. <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,13</i>)

>  A piece of bush ground is in general preferred for the purpose ... <i>(Sep 13, 1849,Letter,141</i>) [NOTES: in NZ Journal (1850)]

### bush gully
 
 <i>n.</i> (Almost a literary cliche with Lancaster and others.)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> ... sneaking down to the bush-gully to conduct her orisons among the birds. <i>Ibid.</i> 175 Great bronze pigeons cooed ... down in the bush-gully ... <i>(1938,Promenade,133</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a steep-sided valley covered in native vegetation.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> ... across the horse paddock to the mouth of the bush gully. <i>(1954,On the Hill,17</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Pigs were very plentiful in the bush gullies of Mt Misery, only a few hundred yards away from his camp <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,218</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> The terrain here was gorsey faces, and native bush gullies which ran from the flat to the main ridge. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,26</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> His window looked out towards the bush gully. <i>(1954,On the Hill,10</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> .. it was about 300 acres in area and consisted of bush gullies, native grass and on the far side, manuka and tauhinu. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,16</i>)

### bush gum
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of kauri gum

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Bush gum was obtained by climbing the trees.. and one man would pull the other up into the tree where he used a hatchet to harvest the gum. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,27</i>)

>  The [kauri-gum] samples analysed were - (a) Resin recently secreted by the tree; (b) resin which had been a long time exposed to air or lying in store (bush gum); (c) fossil resin (range gum); (d) resin obtained from peat-bogs (swamp-gum). <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/303</i>)

>  The kauri Gum Commission of 1921 recommended a standard grading comprising some thirt different grades of fossil gum and four grades of bush gum. <i>(1936,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,17/370</i>)

### bush gun
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a firearm suitable for stalking noxious animals in native forest areas.

>  <b>AXBEY</b> .. there are plenty of .450-577 and .577 lying around, rusting - not much good for open country, but ideal bush guns. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,130</i>)

### bush hand
 
 <i>n.</i> (spec. combin.: early usage.) bushman; one experienced in bushcraft, or in rural or backblocks life (See [cf. hand](h-hea.md#hand),[old hand](o-on.md#old-hand))

>  <b>but</b> [?station hand] How many hands shall you want? We will say a couple of good bush hands, who will put up your hut and yards and wool-shed. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,10/147</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> First came two of the most experienced `bush-hands,', who carried a tomahawk or light axe .. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,183,1870 ed.</i>)

>  I have to thank the men who accompanied me, who proved themselves first-rate bush hands, and well earned the extra wages paid. <i>(1897,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Wilmot `Report to Parliament' in 1950 Reed and Reed Farthest West 182]

>  ... a bush-hand, living the life of a hermit in his little whare of tree-fern stems, up in the Waio river-bed. Notes (Potts) <i>(1871,TrNZI,5/182</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a man employed in the bush (e.g. by a sawmill), at bush-work; a forestry cutter

>  In the kauri distrct, bush-hands are commonly paid 20s., or rarely 18s. per week, with board and lodgings ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Handbook,333</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>MANDER</b> He had the gumfield trade in full, and most of that now done by the bush hands. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,90,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> When he heard that bush-hands were wanted, he and Mum moved into the Kanaeranga bush. <i>(1964,Stamper Battery,7</i>)

>  ... a bush-hand, living the life of a hermit in his little whare of tree-fern stems, up in the Waio river-bed. <i>(1871,TrNZI,5/182</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  Amongst the sporting events of John McKellar's time were the wild pig hunts he organised for townspeople, bush hands etc and many great days were held on the hills ... <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,15</i>)

### bush harrow
 
 <i>n.</i> prob., `a rough harrow made from brush or bushes' (= shrubs) but with overtones of bush A1 (i.e., poss. `made of material from the bush': cf. bush fence, -pen) phs a borrowing of American or Eng. provincial bush-harrow for the more common `brush-harrow', or perhaps merely brush replaced by the more freq. NZ bush.

>  <b>BEETHAM</b> Oct.9th. Commenced hauling off logs with bullocks when the fellows came with their assistance made a bush harrow and saved all the land I had grubbed; ... <i>(Oct 9, 1858,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Oct 9 William Beetham Diary in 1967 Rickard Strangers in the Wilderness 98]

>  <b>WAITE</b> The seed was merely thrown down, and a large bush harrow drawn by four or five Maoris, with two women riding on the brush to keep it down, was all the cultivation thought of. <i>(1869,West Coast Goldfields,21</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a primitive harrow made from forest and scrub materials.

>  <b>BEETHAM</b> Made a bush harrow and sowed all the lands I had grubbed. <i>(1858,Diary,98</i>) [NOTES: in Rickard (1967) Strangers in the Wilderness]

### bush harrowing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the using of a primitive bush harrow to prepare land for pasture.

>  <b>BAGNALL</b> The early trials of fending off wild dogs, pit-sawing, grubbing and bush harrowing had given way to massive improvements, grassing and stocking. <i>(1976,Wairarapa : An Historical Excursion,359</i>)

### bush hat
 
 <i>n.</i> a hat suited for wear in the bush; a ?wide-a-wake hat; ?cabbage-tree hat (cf. bush-costume).

>  This usually consisted of my brother's ... boots ... my cherished bush hat (a cross between an Australian outback hat and a Daniel Boone cap) and my ... parka. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,63</i>) [NOTES: (Andrew Fagan)]

>  <b>MARRIOT-WATSON</b> Throwing himself again on the bracken, he pushed his bush-hat from his head. <i>(1890,Web of Spider,14</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [forestry and bush work]] a hard hat.

>  <b>WATERSON</b> An hour later, after a lot of bailing using Wart's bush hat, they had managed to make the rocky shoreline. <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,139</i>)

>  bush hat - forestry hard hat. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### bush hawk
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a falcon Falco Novaseelandiae, that frequents high, usually forested country. [known as sparrow hawk in the South Island, a predator of both air and ground prey.]

>  <b>HUTTON & DRUMMOND</b> The Bush-hawk or Sparrow-hawk, lives in the mountains, where the forest is low and dense. <i>(1904,Animals of NZ, The,153</i>)

>  <b>JARDINE</b> The kea shares the mastery .. with another .. rejoicing in the name of Falco novaseelandiae, commonly called the sparrowhawk in the south, bush-hawk elsewhere. <i>(1978,Shadows on the Hill,80</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Bush hawk: a North Island term for the bird known as sparrow hawk. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,9</i>)

### bush hearse
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country and forestry] a makeshift stretcher of branches for carrying injured persons.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The accident took place out at Andersons Camp, twenty miles in from Mt White and the wounded man was sledged out on a 'bush hearse.' A bush hearse, I should explain, is a forked branch dragged V end first with a litter rigged up on the fork of the branches. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,144</i>)

### bush hen
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Gallirallus australis</i> (and other spp.), Maori hen, weka, woodhen.

>  <b>HAY</b> The Weka ... is found plentifully in the woods. Settlers call it the `bush-hen'. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/337 We wish it had been a weka, or bush-hen, as that is more succulent eating; ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/219</i>)

>  <i>bush hen NZ</i> = WEKA. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1336]]

>  The sea-gulls would come down by the shore and bush-hens would call as it grew dark and then only our sad native owl would cry upon the night. <i>(2003,Long Journey to the Border,83</i>)

### bush hill
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hill covered in native bush or forest.

>  As far as we can see roll the bush hills, fold on fold. <i>(Oct 30, 1926,NZ Dairy Exporter,41</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> .. the creeks began in the bush hills and flowed out as clean pebbly rivulets ... <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,67</i>)

### bush hive
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild hive or a hive placed by apiarists in the vicinity of native bush.

>  In taking a bush hive, honey wax, pollen, propolis, brood and dirt generally get mixed a good deal..which accounts for people being upset after eating bush honey. ..Maoris have been upset after eating honey taken from a bush hive. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,765</i>)

>  They [beekeepers] trust that the bush hives will build up again in time to deal with the manuka flow. ibid. 427 Usually there is a light flow from Maori privet (Geniostoma ligustrifolium) in this area, and this surplus is taken from the bush hives in early November. <i>(May 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,425</i>)

### bush holding
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a privately-owned bushland area to be developed for farming.

>  From the description you give of your bush holding, you have fenced the front and sides but not the back. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,778</i>)

>  .. I strongly advocated the 'community' sheep-dip, and yards, for bands of new settlers, starting upon new bush holdings in the hinterlands of our forest country. <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,927</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> .. my father found himself at the age of 23 the owner of a bush holding in Taranaki. <i>(1945,Pioneer Looks Back, A,4</i>)

### bush honey
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) honey from wild bees in native bush or hives set close to native bush.

>  <b>LYON</b> ... I never knew the pakeha to suffer any after-effects from the use of bush honey, and I have felled many bee trees. <i>(1952,Faring South,105</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> We usually had a good supply of bush honey [ca1900]. The most likely place to find it was in a hollow tree. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,61</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> A strongly flavoured New Zealand bush honey is best for this dish. [Canterbury lamb with honey] <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,102</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> It meant shorter hours at the mill, less milk ..., less bush honey from their hives. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,147</i>)

>  It seems to be the opinion of some that the New Zealand bush honey is not much good. ..But it is only a description of the bush honey got in January... <i>(Sep 1889,NZ Farmer,371</i>)

>  In taking a bush hive, honey wax, pollen, propolis, brood and dirt generally get mixed a good deal..which accounts for people being upset after eating bush honey. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,765</i>)

>  Most important in the production of bush honey is the location of the apiary. If manuka honey is the aim, care should be taken to see that the site chosen is suitable, not only for that particular honey, but also for native bush honey if possible. <i>(May 15, 1956,NZ Farmer,425</i>)

### bush horse
 
 <i>n.</i> one trained to find its way in the bush

>  <b>LUSH</b> The Bishop has often told me she is a first-rate bush horse and so she proved herself to be - she went on and on - in and out - struggling through this and plunging through that and at last, ... emerged on the road and near the spot where Jessie had parted from me. <i>(1867,Waikato Journals,109,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> The trained bush horse will stand quietly where his rider leaves him ... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,42</i>)

### bush hotel
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rudimentary bar set in an isolated or forest area.

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In his reminiscences to me Mr James Pollock said, `Old White at Pyramid had a cloot hotel,' so it was presumably a canvas edifice. The `old hands' used to tell some queer stories about this `bush hotel' and did so with an amusing mixture of condemnation for its worst features and pride in its eccentricities. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,38,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> Went over the 'crow' at the bush hotel for a riotous non-stop jag. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,75</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> It took Harry some time to locate Ned and not unexpectedly found him drinking in the bar of a bush hotel. <i>(1993,Colonial Outcasts: A Search for the Remittance Man,169</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> It is a dish fit for kings and if ever you pitch your camp at a bush 'hotel' ask for kaka stew as a favour. <i>(1922,Reminiscences of a New Chum in Otago,34</i>)

### bush house
 
 <i>n.</i> (old term for) a garden summerhouse

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Having constructed our framework, the thatch, or covering, was the difficulty ... We would ... brave the storm for another handful of grass, toitoi,or any other accessible material - so that about midnight, we could call our covering water-tight for a bush house. <i>(Mar 21, 1848,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Mar 21 Brunner Exped. Middle Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 310]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> `Come around to the bush-house' ... They seated themselves together on a rustic seat among the ferns. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,164</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> Glass structures can be too hot here in summer, so my plants are migratory, and go out under the shade of trees, or into a skeleton [23] shed with bushes round the sides and forming the roof, commonly called a `bush-house,' and for summer these places afford much pleasure and occupation; ... <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,22</i>)

>  Common in damp bush; also cultivated in bush houses on the island ... <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/20</i>)

>  The main objects of a bush-house is [sic] to subdue the intense sunlight, keep a cool moist atmosphere around the plants, and to protect them from hot or dry winds. <i>(1920,Yates' Garden Guide,93</i>) [NOTES: 14th edition]

### bush hunter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] a hunter for deer, wild pigs, goats and possums in native bush or conservation country.

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Most bush hunters at one time or other have encountered stags trapped in this grim fashion. <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,39</i>)

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> .. the moment a pilot starts to hunt, it becomes evident whether he has been a good foot-slogging bush hunter. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,91</i>)

### bush hut
 
 <i>n.</i> a hut erected in the bush from materials taken from the bush (See [cf. bush house](bri-byo.md#bush-house))

>  <b>McLEAN</b> We slept at the Heu Heu posts, where Pohe erected a good bush-hut. <i>(1850,Journal,3/72</i>)

>  <b>MURDOCH</b> The floor of [Moa Hut] is of clay, and the roof without any ceiling. Still, it is warm and comfortable enough, and is a very good example of a bush hut. <i>(Feb 1860,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Feb Wallace Murdock Journal quoted in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 116]

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... the mistress of this charming `bush-hut' [previously `a picturesque gable-ended little cottage surrounded by a rustic fence] insisted on our having some hot coffee and scones and wild honey ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,181</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> The little hut was quite comfy for a bush hut: just two rooms and no luxuries. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,87</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> I remember Jim ... telling me in his early years on Pitt Island he was riding past a bush hut which Ned Moore and his wife were occupying while Ned was felling bush. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,63</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> It was not long before the Rongo family instructed the brothers in building a ponga and nikau whare (bush hut) alongside their tent, in which they could cook and eat their meals. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,14</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small whare, made of ponga and nikau or similar native materials.

>  <b>HUNT</b> The little hut was quite comfy for a bush hut ... <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,87</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> It was not long before the Rongo family instructed the brothers in building a ponga and nikau whare (bush hut) alongside their tent, in which they could cook and eat their meals. <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,14</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> To add to the spice was the bush hut that was being built upriver in the Wanganui opposite Plover Flat. <i>(1998,Sea of Gold,  A,35</i>)

### bush inn
 
 <i>n.</i> a remote country inn or accommodation house

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> At sunset we reached Rangitikei. The `house of call' was here situated on the opposite side of the river, ... At least Scott, the landlord, came over and ferried us across to his house, which is the best bush inn in New Zealand - containing six or seven good-sized rooms with glass windows. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,23</i>)

>  <b>GAMBIER</b> We ... marched [c1860] on foot through the wonderful forest to a small bush inn kept by a man called Selby, ... <i>(1907,Links in My Life,116</i>)

### bush inspector
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Merv is one of 13 bush inspectors employed throughout New Zealand by the Department of Labour. Each is a man of many years of experience in his field. Their principal concern is the safety and welfare of all persons in `bush undertakings and sawmills' <i>(Aug 27, 1982,Dominion,8</i>)

### bush it
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>ADAMS</b> A little more experience in `bushing it' would have taught us to have left the bush at once to its fate ... <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,40</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to make a camp in heavy bush.

>  <b>ADAMS</b> A little more experience in 'bushing it' would have taught us to have left the bush at once to its own fate. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,40</i>)

>  <b>GOLDIE</b> I resolved to scramble along the side of the lake .. even though we had to 'bush it' for a night or two. <i>(May 5, 1862,BLANK ENTRY,98</i>) [NOTES: in Beattie (1947) Pioneers Explore Otago]

### bush itch
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILMOT</b> Homer unable to work today because of the `bush itch' and the men got their blankets flyblown. <i>(1882,Diary</i>) [NOTES: c1882-1883 Wilmot's Diary in 1968 Hall-Jones Early Fiordland 159]

### bush jacket
 
 <i>n.</i> a bush shirt or swanndri [An item of army tropical kit similar in cut to the 1970's 'safari' jacket or suit. "I appropriated my father's after the war with its capacious pleated breast pockets, "bellows" hip pockets - in front; belt and epaulets, if that is the name for the shoulder bands which carried rank badges, either by means of eyelet holes or by sliding on a fabric sleeve embroidered with crowns, stars, batons or whatever. It was generally understood that the garment, and its name originated in Africa - but it could have been some other "colonial territory" such as India.]

>  Mr Johnstone [a Kaikohe fisherman] was wearing knee-length gumboots and a <i>bush-jacket</i>. `I kicked my gumboots off and had to get down under the water to pull my <i>swanny</i> over my head,' he said last night. <i>(Jun 4, 1985,NZ Herald,2</i>)

>  A bush jacket containing personal possessions belonging to the missing Rastafarian was found not far from the body, police said. <i>(Aug 19, 1985,Dominion,1</i>)

### bush jigger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> Bush jiggers were the motor-driven creations used to transport humans along bush trams... Small hand-worked jiggers were used extensively on the NZR but they were not common on trams... The first bush jiggers were constructed early in the 20th century... The earliest confirmed jigger appeared in April 1908 when it was reported that a trolley had been built by Gamman at Te Ohu to convey 10-12 men to and from work... The principal use of bush jiggers was to get the bushmen to and from work. Compared to other options they were faster, safer,warmer, and drier. <i>Ibid.</i> 58 The development of the bush jigger spelled the end for most bush camps because they made it possible to travel to work over long distances in comparative speed and comfort. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,46</i>)

### bush job
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bush clearing contract.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The first operation when starting a bush job is to do the scrubbing ... <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,58</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> This [drinking] would go on for weeks and weeks, especially when they'd all cut out a big bush job. <i>(1997,Pigs and Me: Tales of the Bygone Bush.,76</i>)

>  <b>DAHM</b> .. it was a long way to that bush job. The bush job was in the Mangaoporo Valley, near Mount Hikurangi on the East Cape. <i>(2000,People of the Axe, The,88</i>)

### bush journal
 
 <i>n.</i> a journal written in the bush

>  <b>COLENSO</b> My bush journal ... <i>(1848,Journal,2/49</i>)

### bush journey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> For such [who declare all travel barren], a bush-journey in New Zealand will have neither pleasure nor profit; but not so to an intelligent and observant man. <i>(1853,Auckland,128</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> As they arrived there, after their rough bush journey of ten days they were received with loud cries ... from the women. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,54</i>)

### bush justice
 
 <i>n.</i> rough justice

>  He had good reason to believe the gang wanted to extract retribution from him. The penalty he had already suffered was substantial, a penalty by way of `bush justice.' <i>(Nov 13, 1979,Evening Post,32</i>) [NOTES: [AND 1959]]

### bush karaka
 
 <i>n.</i> [(?bush =?).]

>  <b>BROOKES</b> .. whitewood, fern trees, bush karaka ... puketea. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,20</i>)

### bush kennel
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a makeshift shelter for a sheepdog at a mustering hut or camp.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We were building bush kennels for our dogs, a few chains from the hut. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,42</i>)

### bush knife
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> The Rangers' arms were ... in Von Tempsky's company, a bowie knife with a blade 10 inches or 12 inches long. ... `We find them long enough to rach our enemies with,' so the Rangers could have said of their bush-knives that were quite long enough for close quarters. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/266</i>)

### bush lagoon
 
 <i>n.</i> a lagoon surrounded by, or in the, bush.

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> [The birds] of the plains, swamps and bush-lagoons. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,124</i>)

### bush lamp
 
 <i>n.</i> a slush lamp or a bush lantern (a candle stuck into the neck of an upturned bottle)

>  <b>cham</b> The bush-lamp had burned low. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,36</i>)

### bush land
 
 <i>n.</i> land covered by bush, as distinct from open country (contrast open land, open country; and in a different semantic area fern-land). [the form develops from an orig. loose combination of <i>bush</i> A1, `forest' and <i>land</i> with a sense (or implication) `land covered with bush', earlier forest-land; 1. land covered with native bush. 2. farmland developed from cleared bushland. [Cf 1835 Yate New Zealand 17-18 The forest-land is peculiarly rich; indeed, were it not so, it would be utterly impossible that the [18] immense vegetation constantly going on should be supported.] to a closer combination or compound with a sense `cleared bush-land, farmland once covered with bush', with the senses often hard to distinguish. (cf fernland]]

>  <b>CARRINGTON</b> Fern or Bush land. <i>(1841,Journal,12</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> There is also less general undergrowth [in the Nelson forest], and the bush land will be cleared with less attendant expense than at Wellington. ... [125] ... The expense attendant on clearing bush-land ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),103</i>)

>  Immediately behind it [the fern land] is a belt of bush land, as it is here called, composed of the most beautiful shrubs from five to twenty feet high ... This land is considered the best; behind this commences the timber district, which I have not seen. <i>(1843,Letters from New Plymouth,26</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Mar 2) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 26]

>  Bush land is best : it is all humbug what is said about fern land being so good. <i>(1843,Letters from New Plymouth,28</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Mar 12) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 28]

>  Fern and bush land. <i>(1842,Letters from New Plymouth,144,1843 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1842 W. Bayly in 1843 Letters from Settlers 144]

>  A belt of bush land as it is called here. <i>(1842,Letters from New Plymouth,154</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Stephen Gillingham in 1843 Letters fropm Settlers 154]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,103</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> Beyond the bush land, that which is called grass land chiefly produces a very cutting wiry species, very similar to that which in swamps we call tree grass, but without the stem or trunk on which the former is elevated above the water surface; these plumes of grass on this dry land in burning diffuse a fragrant and aromatic odour, ... <i>(May 13, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 13 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 220]

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> Some provision might easily and justly be made [for the European residents] ...; about ten acres of <i>good</i>, bush land for each child would not be too much, ... <i>(Apr 19, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Apr 19 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 209]

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> Frederick Hunt gave quite a nice piece of bushland to Isabella, just as he had given to his other two daughters, Elizabeth Gregory and Mary Anne Langdale, I presume as dowries when they married. <i>(1958,Last Entail Male,f42</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> ... cleared bushland ... <i>(May 23, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 23 Tuckett's Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 223]

>  <b>MONRO</b> The natives, as elsewhere in New Zealand, have cultivated the bush-land, ... <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 242]

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> ... taking one crop off the land, and that bush-land. <i>(1846,Journal,19</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Fresh fern land is at first infertile through what we call `sourness'. ... Grass land is less subject to `sourness'; and Bush land not at all. ... Bush land, is the common forest land. <i>Ibid.</i> 331 Bush land is richer than either Fern or Grass land; <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/330</i>)

>  The Korari is the name of the flower stalk, often, but wrongly, used as the name of the plant. The edible root is the Aruhe. The word `Muka' is commonly applied to the dressed fibre; it is the correct name for the inner leaves. Cleared bush-land is suitable for the cultivation of the flax plant, and even swamp-land is improved by drainage. In the various flax swamps near nelson ...the finest plants grow upon the soil that has been thrown out of the ditch ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/117</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy NZ Flax)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Harakeke or Korati [sic](<i>Phormium tenax</i>) is the justly celebrated New Zealand flax. It is plentiful everywhere, on bush-land and open-land, rich soil and poor soil, hill and dale, from the Reinga to the Bluff. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/204</i>)

>  My meadow fescue sown on the 15 acre of bushland felled by Manuel is a perfect failure. <i>(Oct 6, 1886,Chudleigh Diary,348,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ... it takes from ten to fifteen years before the plough can be used in bush land, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,13</i>)

>  Where bush land has not been sown with cocksfoot ... <i>(1919,TrNZI,51/366</i>) [NOTES: (Laing)]

>  <b>KEENE</b> b. A rural farming area (overtones of bushland 1. with bush) (does this however belong to 1b.). <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,100</i>)

>  Arthur is going to make a clearing on our land this year, and ... [sic] after the burn would put up a slab house of two rooms which might serve afterwards for cowshed or kitchen or workshop ... `Bird's heart is in the bushland, his heart is not here.' <i>(Aug 8, 1855,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/172,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Jane M. Atkinson)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> [country district] ... they lighted a fire, and `slung the billy', - (Anglice[e-acute]: a tin vessel used for preparing tea) - the indispensable concomitant of all repasts, and the chief luxury of dwellers in bush land. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,Bk III ch v 88</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> ... I recall his [the botanist-Inspector's] chuckle when we showed him ... the huge white caterpillar known throughout bushland [in North Auckland] as the `rata grub', so-called because it was always found at the foot of a rata and was supposed to be the origin [101] of the tree itself. It is a curious creature - a great caterpillar with a strange tree-like fungoid growth, growing sometimes to a height of several inches, from its body. I think everybody believed in the legend of the rata-tree caterpillar; ... <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,101</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> BUSH LAND AS COMPARED WITH FERN [heading] `Almost all the crops ... have been taken from what is called fern land ... But ... I know that when I left New Plymouth district ... the bush land crops of wheat were deemed inferior ... to those of open fern lands; ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),101</i>)

>  ... <i>Pterostylis graminea</i> is in flower on the ridges of steep bush-land hills. <i>(1884,TrNZI,16/389</i>) [NOTES: (Adams)]

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> It [sc. wheat] was in very good condition and heavy, grown on cleared bushland, a nice sandy loam, ... <i>(May 23, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 23 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 223]

>  <b>WILLCOX</b> The `Bush' or forest land, although most difficult to reclaim, becomes most speedily productive; but `Fern' land, that is land covered with fern and shrubby plants is ... ultimately the most profitable. <i>(1860,Handbook of New Zealand,80</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> We prefer bush-land to open-land for pioneer farming. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,207</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>Bush</i>, or wooded land, is <i>cleared </i>at about 8l. or 110l. per acre; ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,187</i>)

>  Bush land can be cleared for about [pounds]6, that is, the bush burnt off; of course the stumps are left to rot out. <i>(Apr 16, 1849,Letters from Otago,31,1978 ed.</i>)

>  Some ...[sic] who wished to stop, intend proceeding to Auckland as they are frightened of bush land, and the block containing fern, which will be next opened, is hardly likely to be in the market for 6 months to come. <i>(Sep 24, 1853,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/131,1960 ed.</i>)

>  The land is cleared bush land full of stumps, only fit for grazing. -->1852 Jan 18 Nairn and Stephen Journal in 1947 Beattie Pioneers Explore Otago 27 Our day's travelling was over some high undulating land with flax tutu high grass and several blocks of bush land with very good timber. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/81,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richmond)]

>  <b>EARP</b> All reserves for townships or agricultural establishments shall have reserves marked off of one-twentieth of all bush-land within the limit of such block, or if there be no bush within such block, then a sufficient reserve of bush-land outside the block, ... shall be made. <i>(1858,Handbook Wellington,13</i>) [NOTES: [quoting Land Regulations]]

>  <b>FITTON</b> But we cannot say we have had [187] a fair trial of bush land. <i>Ibid.</i> 261 ... I have heard that in forest or `bush' land, where there were large trees to fell, ... the land has cost about [pounds]40.[sic.]an acre before the crop was in the ground. ... I have been told by settlers on small allotments of the cleared `bush' land ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),186</i>)

>  <b>WELCH</b> Bush land is common forest land. <i>(1863,NZ Guide,22</i>)

>  <b>BATEMAN</b> The forest, or what is colonially termed `bush-land' takes some time to clear. <i>(1881,Colonist, The,183</i>)

>  ... large areas of bush land being annually cleared. <i>(1899,TrNZI,31/7</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  HOMESTEAD SYSTEM. Under this system the settler makes no payment for the land, the only cost to him being the expenses of survey. On the fulfilment of conditions, which are five years' residence, the erection of a house, and the cultivation of one-third of the selection if open land, and one-fifth if bush-land, the Crown grant is issued. `Do you own any land?' `Yes, three hundred acres of bush land.' `Have you any cleared?' `Sixty acres'. [ca1900] <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,284</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> If I had a couple of hundred acres of good bush land, and a wife like that girl down there would make, I wouldn't call the Queen my aunt; and, by Jove! I mean to have a buck at some of those sections in the Whakatangi Block that'll be thrown open shortly, ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,84</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Soon came bushland with here and there a small clearing where a few houses and perhaps a store, huddled beside the railway. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,19</i>) [NOTES: [cf bush country]]

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> ... their way still led through bush-land, and ... tiny birds began to appear: perky tomtits in immaculate suits of black and white, ... grey warblers trilling their lilting arias. <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,69</i>)

>  The bush-land is the best land. <i>(1843,Letters from Settlers,153</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Paul Inch in 1843 Letters from Settlers 153]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) country that grows native bush or forest, or is undeveloped for farming.

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Bush land can be cleared for about GBP6, that is, the bush burnt off. <i>(Nov 1849,Letter,72</i>) [NOTES: in Otago Journal]

>  When a person takes up bush land without having experience or giving the undertaking great study, he runs a great risk of falling into grave errors. Bush land is not like the open. <i>(Jul 1887,NZ Farmer,196</i>)

>  For sale - 4470 acres bush land, rata, tawa, pukatea, and rimu timber ... <i>(Nov 28, 1903,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  Near Tauranga, 5800 acres good bush land, ring-fenced. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Weekly News,2</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> .. they literally hacked out their farms from cut-over bushland. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,464</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> .. a huge white caterpillar known throughout bushland as the 'rata grub'. <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,101</i>)

>  <b>ROBSON</b> Bush land is a dangerous instrument for poor colonists. [bushland; bush-land] <i>(1967,Bits and Pieces,53</i>)

>  <b>PRATT</b> The piece of land I was preparing for cropping with potatoes was bush-land that had been cleared and planted by the Maories [sic]. <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,93</i>)

>  Its [cocksfoot's] chief value lies in it being a strong, hardy grower, and especially adapted for surface sowing upon bushland, hillsides and other places ... <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,338</i>)

### bush language
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) basic, colourful language, possibly involving swear words.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> [To our British teacher] a paddock was now a field, our tucker or crib bags (made out of sugar bags) became satchels or lunch bags and our oilskin coats became macs. We preferred to retain our bush language. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,38</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> With the bush language there comes a certain attitude to the hills, a conviction that the bush is a great place to be ... <i>(1988,Hills, The</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> To use an amusing phrase of that inimitable Bush language, it was country that was 'without a word of a lie, as steep as a hen's face.' <i>(1947,Wayleggo,59</i>)

>  <b>MARRIOTT</b> Dan told them in real bush language just what he thought of stupid townies and refused to give them the consignment notes. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,84</i>)

### bush lantern
 
 <i>n.</i> a makeshift lantern (cf. bush lamp, bush candlestick; joey, manrope lantern, West Coast lantern)

>  <b>PYKE</b> A bush lantern is constructed by placing a candle within the neck of a clear glass bottle, the bottom of which has either been knocked off by a sharp scientific tap from a practised hand, or more artistically removed by pouring in a little cold water, and then thrusting the bottle end-wise in a fire <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/xii/42,(1889, 1974) ed.</i>)

### bush larrikin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  So long as the farmer persists in cutting down the native growth ..., or so long as the gum-digger and bush-larrikin are allowed to put a match into anything that will burn, there is not much hope for either prevention or restoration. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/496</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

### bush law
 
 <i>n.</i>
