>  <b>MAY</b> ... sham, humbug, bush-law, vanity, and vexation of the spirit ... a gathering of quacks, where he who has the loudest voice and the greatest stock of impudent assurance will be listened to with most respect <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,252/3/1,1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [May quoting a correspondent]]

### bush lawyer
 
 <i>n.</i> a self-trained legal "expert"

>  I smirk every time I see the president-in-waiting's legal team described as "Bush lawyers." <i>(Dec 5, 2000,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> A man you could learn from. Clever, they said, a regular bush lawyer. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,16</i>)

>  [Hdg] A Bush Lawyer <i>(Feb 15, 1890,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  Mr Justice O'Regan remarked that bush lawyers were everywhere... "In fact, you will still find the typical bush lawyer right in the bush." <i>(Oct 18, 1943,NZEF Times,III/121</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> We have to have all our i's dotted and our t's crossed in case they put up a bush lawyer against us. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,129</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> A good boss, he paid well. A man you could learn from. Clever, they said, a regular bush lawyer. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a thorny bush creeper



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) <i>Rubus cissoides</i>, a tenacious vine of the spp. Rosaceae notorious for trapping and starving sheep. [a tenacious indigenous bramble, ,<i>Rubus australis</i>, taramoa, or tataramoa, with small white flowers, renowned for forming barriers on bush paths. It was also well-known by the jocular sobriquet of taihoa, wait-a-while, or wait-a-bit.]

>  Bush lawyer berries, tree fuchsia berries and supplejack shoots were particularly welcome whenever I could find them. <i>(May 23, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I'd been lassoed by a rope of bush lawyer, the vine tearing across my neck like barbed-wire. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,12</i>) [NOTES: 1994 edition]

>  There, his diet consisted of such things as bush lawyer berries (from the same genus, rubus, as blackberries)... <i>(Apr 30, 2005,Dominion Post,E12</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> <i>Rubus Australis</i> [sic], the thorny strings of which scratch the hands and face, which the colonists, therefore, very wittily call the `bush lawyer'. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),135</i>)

>  The stringly tendrils of the aptly named taihoa, or 'wait-a-while native creeper, tough enough to trap and hold a horse. <i>(1948,Gumdigger, The,53</i>)

>  The next day I somehow get out of that difficult terrain and drop into the Waiau, wet through, cut-up by bush lawyer and totally knackered. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Perhaps you know it better by the name Bush Lawyer or simply Lawyer; and if you are a botanist, as Rubus australis. The Maoris call it Tataramoa - a heap of prickles. <i>(Nov 20, 1907,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  Shall I see the Ponga Fairy and the cross old Bush Lawyer? <i>(Dec 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Hour after hour we toiled on, sometimes making our way through .. the long and clinging bramble, called by colonists the 'bush-lawyer'. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,44</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> .. a bush lawyer had twined some of its long straggling prickly limbs through the scrub <i>(1881,Waitaruna,94</i>)

>  In the different species of bush-lawyers the prickles are well developed at an early stage. <i>(Sep 28, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Everywhere the self-asserting 'Bush Lawyer' which has gained its name from the tenacity of its prickly spikes <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,234</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> The most reviled, and at the same time one of the most beautiful, of the climbers is the 'bush lawyer'. It is armed, on stalks and leaves alike, with barbed hooks, by which it climbs, clutching everything within reach. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,83</i>)

### bush level
 
 <i>n.</i> bushline (cf. also bush limit)

>  ... that there is a pass below bush-level, at the head of the Hauroto Burn ... <i>(1897,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: 1897 AJHR Wilmot Report of a Journey in 1950 Reed and Reed Farthest West 185]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> It took us three hours to reach the open basin above the bush level ...<i>Ibid.</i> 51 ... the large open faces above the bush level. <i>Ibid.</i> 71 The snow that was still on the tops above bush level was the main reason why the deer were so plentiful. <i>Ibid.</i> 121 The high `tops' are practically free from snow, and the `waps' will be out above bush level seeking the young tussock growth. <i>Ibid.</i> 123 The bush-covered mountains reached from the water's edge up to the natural bush level, about three and a half thousand feet up. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,46</i>)

### bush licence
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a licence to serve alcohol in early European settlement days.

>  <b>MORGAN</b> Bush licenses in remote country districts do not answer the purpose for which the license is granted. <i>(Jun 17, 1857,Letter,I 278</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers]

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> .. Elmbranch had built a bush-licence accommodation house known as The Rest and Be Thankful. <i>(1973,Petticoat Pioneers,211</i>)

>  <b>KAY</b> The first 'bush license' at the Halfway was issued to John Lodge, who owned the third eight-acre strip of land there, alongside those of Anthony and John Whitehouse. Bush licenses were issued for the sale of liquor without any rules for accommodation. A Special Publican's License was issued to John McKain in 1849, followed by a 'bush license' a year later. <i>(1996,Anthony Wall, Settler of Porirua,39</i>)

>  A bush license has been refused to Mr Price, the owner of the accommodation house at the Mangawhero bridge. <i>(Jan 22, 1881,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  The following applications for bush licenses were granted:- J H Noding, Waipori, accommodation house. <i>(Dec 6, 1862,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  <b>BOLLINGER</b> Especially after the widespread granting of 'bush licences' in the 1860s, liquor was deliberately used by unscrupulous Pakehas as a means of debauching individual Maoris, especially chiefs, and swindling them of their tribal lands. <i>(1959,Grog's Own Country,17</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a licence to cut timber from native bush.

>  The interest that the bush licence or leasing system affords to the State is `nil'. <i>(1880,TrNZI,12/7</i>) [NOTES: (Lecoy)]

>  <b>JACOBSON</b> He took out a bush licence for splitting shingles and posts and rails. <i>(1893,Tales of Banks Peninsula,158</i>)

>  A number of questions have been asked within the last day or two about the issue of bush licenses. On and after the 1st January next, licenses will be issued to settlers, giving them the right to cut timber for their own use ... [bush license] <i>(Dec 15, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

### bush life
 
 <i>n.</i> back blocks life; life as a pioneer farmer.

>  My youthful spirits are required ... to keep Bessie [Domett] from drooping under the depressing influence of bush-life. <i>(Jan 24, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/473,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Maria Richmond)]

>  The party returned to Kaihu, after the dancing, with entirely new ideas of bush-life. <i>(Oct 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> I saw plenty of bush life and was twelve months at one time up the Totara River ... without once going to Ross. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,110</i>)

>  He was a deserter from the colonial forces ... and had already lived a bush life with the Piri-Rakau ... Tribe <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/14,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> Bush-life is just what you make it. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,56</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> When we had no birds, a small piece of bacon went into the billy to give some taste to our `billsticker's paste' as a prosaic member of our party called it. He did not understand the poetry of bush life. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,283</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 283]

>  <b>PRENTICE</b> The late proprietor [of the sheep-station] was particularly anxious to return to England without delay. The experience of one year's bush life as a pioneer not at all agreeing with his preconceived ideas of this land being a land flowing with milk and honey. <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,29</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> [He acquired] such a passion for a wandering bush life, that he afterwards threw up his billet at Mr Taylor's, and followed me all the way down the coast, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,36</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... I had bragged a little too much of the `glorious liberty' of bush-life. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,62</i>)

>  Bush life [in Dannevirke] is monotonous enough. <i>(Sep 23, 1905,NZ Truth,14/1/5</i>)

### bush lily
 
 <i>n.</i> one of the indigenous ground growing Astelia species. [also bush-lily]

>  The native fuchsia [fn <i> Fuchsia excorticata </i>.] - an exile from South America - and the fierce bush-nettle [fn <i> Urtica ferox </i>.] are conspicuous amongst the undergrowth, together with the bush-lily [fn Astelia nervosa.], but we see but few species of the characteristic New Zealand daisy-trees [fn Species of <i> Olearia </i>.] or of the bushy Veronicas (koromiko), which are so conspicuous in other parts of these islands. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>) [NOTES: Dendy]

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i> ASTELIA FRAGRANS </i>. Bush lily grows rather like flax in lowland to montane forest from about the latitude of Thames southwards ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,182</i>)

>  Astelia nervosa, kakaha or bush lily, has pronounced clear nerves running down the midribs of the silvery green leaves. These flaxy leaves may be up to 2.6m long and 10cm wide. <i>(Nov 27, 1997,Evening Post,C22</i>)

### bush limit
 
 <i>n.</i> = bushline (See [cf. bush level](bri-byo.md#bush-level))

>  <b>HARPER</b> The robin (<i>Miro albifrons</i>) is a constant companion in some localities, as far as the bush limit; ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,39</i>)

>  ... near the bush limit ... <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/443</i>) [NOTES: (Kingsley)]

>  The Nelson Land District comprises the north and north-western portion of the Middle Island, the greater part being high and mountainous, and on the western and inland ranges covered with dense forest to the bush-limit, at from 4,000 ft. to 4,500 ft. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,18</i>)

### bush living
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bush life](bri-byo.md#bush-life))

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> It is all very fine to read about bush-living, or listen to men talking of the `dampers' that they have made ... <i>(1881,Chequered Career,40</i>)

### bush loaf
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> After dinner, I negotiated an exchange [with my boundary-keeping neighbour] of half a loaf, as a sequel to my appreciative comments on the excellency of his bush loaf. <i>(Oct 1872,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,32,1935 ed.</i>)

### bush lokey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> The typical bush lokey of this early period was the four-wheel direct-drive type imported from England weighing between 5 and 10 onnes. <i>Ibid</i> 79 A special type of bush lokey was developed to operate over these newer lines which were built far beyond the alignment limits of 'normal' railways. Because more power was required, the special bush lokeys were bigger than those used previously. <i>Ibid.</i> 83 The introduction of the first bush lokeys in New Zealand was concurrent with the initial railway construction boom and the first steam-powered mineral railways. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,14</i>)

### bush looking
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> The above parties were no sooner satisfied, than a new company arrived on the land, ...; they were a wild bush looking race. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/209</i>)

### bush lore
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A great deal of the bush-lore of the Maoris has vanished with the wise old men and women ... <i>(Mar 10, 1921,Quick March,11</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> A great deal of the bush-lore of the Maori has vanished, but there remains an abundance of useful knowledge to be gathered and turned to account by scientific enquirers. <i>(1930,Maori, The,172</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> My father, who knew Maori bush-lore as well as the natives themselves, heard of the trouble [dysentery] and decided on treatment. <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,110</i>)

### bush manager
 
 <i>n.</i> one who manages the `bush' side of a sawmill's operations (See [cf. bush-boss](bri-byo.md#bush-boss))

>  <b>REED</b> Ted Thompson, who was bush manager for the K[auri] T[imber] C[ompany] at No Gum Camp, supervised this work, and attended to the cleaning up of all the old logs in the various driving creeks. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,263</i>)

### bush Maori
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Back home with my mates, though, I slip into our kind of bush Maori. It's mainly Maori but we throw in English at times just to entertain ourselves. <i>(Jun 1998,Mana,No. 22: 35</i>)

### bush margin
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a boundary or perimeter of a growth of native bush.

>  <b>SHARPE</b> .. in the bush margin there was a bit of feed ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,89</i>)

>  <b>WEBB</b> Ongaonga grows typically along bush margins, in disturbed forest, and in scrub in lowland areas. <i>(1990,Flowering Plants of NZ,100</i>)

>  "Hotspots" for flystrike include sheltered scrub-filled gullies, patches of thistles, bush margins, the lee of shelter belts and sheep camps. <i>(Feb 28, 2002,Fencepost.com,Expert farmer Livestock</i>)

>  <b>WELLINGTON REGIONAL COUNCIL</b> Don't plant exotic climbers close to bush margins. Old Man's Beard Habitat: Bush margins, open forest, gardens ... <i>(2000,Invasive Creepers and Climbers,4</i>)

### bush mattress
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We helped ourselves liberally to `bush mattresses' by stripping thick, heavy fleeces of dry moss from the trunks of old fallen trees. <i>(1937,Tramper,11(2):4</i>) [NOTES: The year is pure guesswork - check]

>  <i>Bush mattresses,</i> i.e. dry moss for bedding, I have not heard used, but it occurs in <i>The Tramper</i> for 1938. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,24</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Sep Ruth Mason `Track and Trail' in Tararua 24]

### bush medicine
 
 <i>n.</i> folk medicine or patent medicine for which a medical prescription is not required.

>  <b>Prince, W</b> They bought a good supply of provisions .. not omitting a bottle of Perry Davis' Painkiller, the latter a valuable bush medicine ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,17</i>)

>  While on the subject of bush medicine, I may as well mention a few others. <i>(Mar 10, 1921,Quick March,11</i>)

### bush mill
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> A 'Bush mill' was timber industry vernacular for a sawmill sited close to the bush working rather than sited close to 'civilisation' - usually meaning the local town or at least the local railway station. The rationale for establishing a bush mill was economic. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,58</i>)

### bush missionary
 
 <i>n.</i> missionary working in the bush (?among bush-natives; ?f. a bush station).

>  ... the labours and sufferings of a New Zealand bush-missionary. <i>(1858,Young Southern World,69</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an early settler intent on bringing Chistianity to New Zealand.

>  While her husband won honourable mention as one of the earliest bush missionaries, she, unhonoured and unsung, kept the small farm going ... <i>(Apr 2, 1956,NZ Dairy Exporter,128</i>)

### bush mixture
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cheap seed-mix for new pasture.

>  Bush Mixtures: Under the term "bush sowings" or "bush mixtures" hundreds of sacks of screenings and old low-germinating seeds are distributed in New Zealand every year. In a great many of cases, bush mixtures are stocked ready mixed and are offered to bargain hunters as good quality seed. <i>(Aug 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,94</i>)

### bush mother
 
 <i>n.</i> a mother figure on geological field trips

>  Harold's wife Joan played an important part and became a friend and 'bush mother' to generations of students. <i>(Jun 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,84/11</i>)

### bush musician
 
 <i>n.</i> the bellbird

>  A beautiful bush-musician in the South Island ... is the bell- bird (<i>korimako </i>or<i> makomako</i>). <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/339</i>)

### bush Native
 
 <i>n.</i> a `primitive', fierce Maori living in the bush, ?distinct from more or less civilised natives who had come into contact with Pakeha

>  It was rather a hazardous undertaking ... as it sometimes happens in these paths, straggling bush-natives attack and kill such persons who they fall in with, helpless. <i>(1827,Boultbee Journal,192,1979 ed.</i>)

>  Not a week has passed without my having had to make communication to Europeans living at a distance from the Waimate; and whatever might be the value of what was to be sent, I had no hesitation in giving it in charge to a bush-native, accustomed to Europeans, but has all his life resided among his own people. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),194 [fn]</i>)

>  It was rather a hazardous undertaking ... as it sometimes happens in these paths, straggling bush-natives attack and kill such persons who they fall in with, helpless. <i>(1827,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 192]

>  <b>YATE</b> Not a week has passed without my having had to make communication to Europeans living at a distance from the Waimate; and whatever might be the value of what was to be sent, I had no hesitation in giving it in charge to a bush-native, accustomed to Europeans, but has all his life resided among his own people. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),194 [fn]</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... from all I have seen in New Zealand, I am convinced that the formidable pictures of bush-natives which have been drawn are purely the result of imagination. The natives in general are much too civilised and social, and know their own interests too well, to live in a gloomy and inhospitable forest. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/86</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> They were not bush natives of the Ngaticahoon tribe. <i>(1839,Journal,148</i>)

>  [reference is to a very warlike tribe] They are bush natives and all heathens. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/17/221</i>) [NOTES: (W. Deans)]

>  Bush natives who know their advantage better than to live in an inhospitable bush. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/29/51</i>) [NOTES: (Dieffenbach)]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> Ekehu discovered signs of the place having been recently occupied by natives ... We were yet 75 miles from ... the Ngaitahu tribe ... Were they bush natives? Jacky and Etau appeared to think the vicinity of such individuals rather objectionable. <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Expedition to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 223]

>  It was rather a hazardous undertaking my going so unprovided, as it sometimes happens in these parts, that straggling bush-natives attack and kill such persons as they fall in with, helpless. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,75,1986 ed.</i>)

### bush nightie
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a Swanndri.

>  bush nightie: a bush shirt or Swanndri. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a black singlet

>  <b>PETER</b> bush nightie: a black bushman's singlet. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,2</i>)

>  bush nightie: black bush singlet. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### bush nurse
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a relieving housekeeper [working in rural areas usually under the administration of WDFF.]

>  The bush nurse has been simply invaluable for child-birth. <i>(Jun 4, 1925,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Probably the most lasting effect began with the early formation of the 'bush nurse' or emergency housekeeper scheme. <i>(Sep 24, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,Farming Today  36</i>)

### bush oat-grass
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass))

### bush orchestra
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a morning native bird-call.

>  <b>BATES</b> ( Another good night, no crashes ) and I was awakened by my bush orchestra which seemed to play an extra piece for my special benefit. <i>(1982,Bridge to Nowhere, The,50</i>)

### bush outfit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Packed up my boxes and prepared bush outfit. Took section up Dublin St. <i>(Aug 31, 1849,Torlesse Papers,100,1958 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [sc. for a surveyor]]

>  <b>BARBER</b> During operations in the bush the armed constables wore `bush outfits', and called themselves `shawl parties', referring to the woollen shawl used as a kilt, and easily discarded while crossing streams, thus avoiding the discomfort of wet trouser legs. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,48</i>)

### bush paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock containing bush stumps, scrub or shelter bush.

>  (Report on Grasses) In the North it is usually relied upon for the staple, especially on bush paddocks. <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/297</i>)

>  John & W Torkilsen went to see Potts at Takapau about purchasing the totara in one of his bush paddocks. <i>(Mar 4, 1908,Brabazon Diaries</i>)

>  Another experiment with a similar dressing was carried out in a bush paddock, two sheep and a young steer being placed on it. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,120</i>)

>  Bush paddocks are shut up in summer in preparation for shearing time, as shelter is paramount depending on weather conditions. <i>(Nov 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,44</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> That bit o' bush paddock I fall'd myself. <i>(1901,BLANK ENTRY,50</i>) [NOTES: in Woodhouse (1950)Farm and Station Verse 50]

### bush painkiller
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [horopito](hom-hyp.md#horopito))

>  Two plants that furnish "bush painkiller," the pepper tree and the pepper-wood, or horopito, both with leaves that have a hot taste, have been investigated. <i>(Jan 29, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### bush parrot
 
 <i>n.</i> indigenous parrot, the kaka [aka kaka]

>  NESTOR MERIDIONALIS, Gml. Kaka. Bush Parrot. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/64</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRIE</b> <i>Kaka</i>: bush parrot. <i>(1926,Treasury of NZ Verse,288</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The kaka, or bush parrot, was killed and eaten in large numbers. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,27</i>)

>  <i>Kaka Nestor meridionalis </i>(GMELIN, 1788) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Brown parrot, bush parrot; ... </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,244</i>)

### bush parson
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a preacher in early New Zealand bush settlements.

>  Mr McGillivray followed on the side of the Government. This is another Scotchman and a bush parson, with strong leanings to the Mosaic law and a very severe ritual. <i>(Sep 23, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

### bush party
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a group of people traversing or cutting tracks in the bush or working as bush-fallers.

>  The bush party have now been away a fortnight. <i>(1853,TrNZI,1/133,(1960) ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J. Maria Richmond)]

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> The bush party have [sic] now been away a fortnight and some days. <i>(Nov 11, 1853,Letter,133</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers]

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> Last night the bush party returned earlier than usual. There had nearly been a sad accident in felling a pine; in falling it struck another tree and the head split .. Hal and Arthur were quite buried ... <i>(Feb 5, 1854,Letter,142</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers]

### bush path
 
 <i>n.</i> a path or track through the bush; a bush track (cf. bush road) (Lush 1867 uses BUSH TRACK in the same entry with similar ref.).

>  The road had been a tolerable bush-path. <i>(May 1, 1850,McLean Papers,3/33</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> It is a very long time since I had such a thorough bush path to traverse, ... The forest was very beautiful and as I rode slowly along, pushing my way through the undergrowth, ... <i>(Mar 15, 1867,Waikato Journals,109,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> We got great sprays of clematis - and konini, and drove first through a bush path <i>(Nov 20, 1907,Letters,1/31,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> She and Philip walking together along bush paths ... [sic] long silences ... [sic] <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,200</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> She started to walk to the Domain [Auckland] by a little bush path which lanced the side of the reserve, coming out near a flagged lily-pond. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,245</i>)

### bush pen
 
 <i>n.</i> a pen made of materials from the bush; or ?a pen situated in the bush (?=brush yard; see note at bush fence).

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> ... one wether lamb staked in bush pen and after drowned. <i>(1849,Journal,275</i>)

### bush people
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> The way in which the bush people - the bushmen and their wives - stuck to one another is shown by the way they rallied round in the case of a family that was in need. A common method of raising money was called a `basket social'. A hall would be used for the function, and the people of the district would be invited to bring along baskets. Now these baskets were made of any material at all at hand, and it was remarkable the ingenuity and workmanship shown in making them. ... In the basket its maker put a supper, the food cooked at home ... [151] Now on the night of the basket social, all the baskets would be handed in ... They would then be auctioned, and the purchaser of the basket would have the pleasure and the honour of having supper with the provider of the basket. ... Now I've seen them pay twelve ... pounds in those days for a basket, just to make sure that the girl of the boy's choice saw that he really meant business. ... Every penny of that would go to the family in need ... <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,150</i>) [NOTES: (Joe Gasparich, Northland kauri country)]

### bush philosopher
 
 <i>n.</i> a self-trained philosopher living and working in bush; a moralising or philosophically inclined bushman (bush - may have a derog. sense as in bush baptist, carpenter 2, lawyer; or merely a locative sense as in bush carpenter 1, bush doctor, preacher. That is it may mean `an amateur philosopher perniciously fond of moralising'; or `one who lives in the bush and is an amateur philosopher').

>  <b>HAY</b> The Old Colonial is a bush-philosopher, and delivers himself of moral orations in the shanty of nights. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/127</i>)

>  [introduction] ... the moralisings of his bush philosopher Bill. <i>(1903,King Bill's Philosophy</i>)

### bush picnic
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mess or shambles.

>  bush picnic: a shambles. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a meal taken in forest or bush setting on a farm property.

>  When it begins to get chilly we wander homewards, all feeling refreshed after our bush picnic. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Dairy Exporter,54</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> A BUSH PICNIC [title] <i>(1905,Poems & Sketches,125</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> My mind kept returning to the events of that bush picnic. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,74,1975 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> It was obvious that Felicity was not at all an outdoor girl, nor was she dressed for a bush picnic, but the arrangements were too elaborate to be cancelled. <i>(1967,Empty Hills,49</i>)

### bush pig
 
 <i>n.</i> wild pork

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> Mutton down to the price of bush-pig. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Captain Cooker : (bush-, as well as having a locative sense, strongly implies both wild (`not domestic') and fierce: (See [bush dog](bri-byo.md#bush-dog),[bush-bee](bri-byo.md#bush-bee))



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild pig. (See [tusker](tot-tyr.md#tusker),[Captain Cooker](c-chi.md#captain-cooker),[Irish merino](../I#irish-merino),[scoofler](../S#scoofler))

>  The latest addition to our urban population is a two-months-old bush pig, who, having made friends with Mr H. Walsh somewhere in the Toko regions, was brought by him into town. <i>(Oct 6, 1890,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  He always did have a temper like a bush pig. <i>(Jan 2, 1963,Weekly News,32</i>)

>  <b>W WILLIAMS</b> Got from them a supply of pork as they had killed two bush pigs during their delay, and gave them in return some of our bread. <i>(Aug 11, 1847,Journal,438</i>) [NOTES: in Porter (1974) Turanga Journals]

>  Other parties out this way take considerable interest in the young lambs, namely the wild bush-pigs, which are very plentiful, and a great nuisance... <i>(Aug 14, 1900,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> We lived on potatoes, bread and butter, bully beef where there no bush pigs, little else. <i>(1977,Roughnecks, Rolling stones & Rouseabouts,51</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> We ought to have known that we would not come across bush pigs away down here. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,121</i>)

>  It appeared that one of the eldest ... was upset in the path by a wild bush-pig, at full gallop, running against him with its utmost force; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/313 Here, on the contrary, the Australasian pigs rove amid the bushes and plains, <i>free as the air</i>, until their presence is requested ... Many wild bush pigs have been brought to me weighing three hundred pounds. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/64</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The narrator had been hunting the cochon-marran [sc. wild boar] or bush-pig. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/270 [?2/270]</i>)

>  The bush pig is ... a formidable adversary. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/47/274</i>) [NOTES: [review of Jamieson's NZ, South Australia and NSW 336]]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> Our dogs scented a bush pig, which after a long chase they brought to bay. <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Expedition to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 246]

>  <b>POWER</b> In most places there are pigs of the tame species, which are bred in the bush, and which grow [76] to a large size, ... The bush-pig is occasionally hunted by both white men and Maories, but never with any other view than to obtain cheap pork. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,74</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> A person who could speak so was not civilised and was no better than a bush pig. <i>(1854,Journal,8/180</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Mawai compared him to a bush pig, which, when you think it is caught, suddenly slips out of your hand and escapes. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,274</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> ... some said it was only an old bush pig ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,239</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> Pork was the only Butcher's meat ... - the flesh of wild, or Bush pigs, and very good it was. <i>(1888,Fifty Years In Maoriland,28</i>)

>  The wild bush-pigs ... are the progeny of animals which escaped from the settlements and in part, it is said, of animals liberated by Captain Cook's party, which landed in Mercury bay in 1769. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.4 (N.S.) 5</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams, Coromandel)]

>  In the 1865 .. the native contingent .. captured .. some of the enemy, or, as the N.C. used to call them, the "Wild Mans", from their bushy and unkempt hair and wild, bush-pig look. <i>(Oct 27, 1888,Evening Post,3</i>)

### bush pigeon
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) (See [kereru](kee-kiw.md#kereru))

>  I allude to the pitiable sight of well-to-do settlers, and settlers' sons, hurrying to the steamer with back-loads and horse-loads of bush pigeons for sale. <i>(Jul 10, 1875,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  Investigation almost always shows that that the bird thus pursued is either the rare and useful "morepork" or owl, or a Native bush pigeon, both of which, by the way are at present supposed to be under the protection of the law. <i>(Jun 15, 1904,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> At Tautara ... the party was hospitably entertained ... with abundance of taro, potatoes and bush pigeon. <i>(1850,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1850 May McLean Journal in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 52]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Bush Pigeon --- Few, but increasing. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,240,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Bush Pigeon --- Few, but increasing. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,240,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> There are numbers of bush pigeons, tuis, and others of our beautiful native birds; ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,150</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> A bush pigeon sometimes sat in that tree in the quiet morning sunshine. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,8</i>)

>  The bush-pigeon (<i>carpophaga ovae-zealandiae</i>), known in the north by the Maori name <i>kukupa</i>, is fast becoming scarce. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/603</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Bush Pigeon - - - Few, but increasing. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,240,1954 ed.</i>)

>  He has had a rough time of it surveying a block on the ranges up the Wai-o-hine in the Wairarapa ...[sic] Once ...[sic] they had nothing to eat but a tui and a more-pork. ... They were stewing the two birds when a kuku-pa (bush pigeon) flew up and perched. Bob shot it ... This is not exactly what the Britishers call `sport' - but there is more of nature about it than 30 gents in pink and tops ... waiting for a wretched little fox who, when he is killed, is not equal for culinary purposes to a more-pork. Once coming up the Waikato in 1851 your Uncle James got a pot-shot at a lot of parera (grey duck) ... <i>(Mar 22, 1894,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/597,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

### bush pilot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fixed-wing aircraft pilot [employed to fly deer hunting parties into isolated valleys, landing on river flats.]

>  Following World War II a new generation of 'bush' pilots emerged in New Zealamd, founding many small entrepreneurial airlines often serving isolated areas. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/40</i>)

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> Crack bush pilots would fly in drums of gas for the choppers and extra hands for gutting ... <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,34</i>)

>  <b>FARMER</b> Ken Eden was another flier who developed into an excellent bush pilot. <i>(1994,Best Job Ever, The,86</i>)

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> Terry Belcher, to me, epitomises the typical Kiwi bush pilot, who flies because he loves to hunt. <i>(1987,Helicopter Hunters, The,100</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Campbell (Cam) Shepherd was good enough bush pilot to inspire the utmost confidence. <i>(1988,Hunting Experience, The,60</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> .. it epitomises the skill of the few West Coast bush pilots who specialise in this sort of mountain flying. <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,96</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> This combination of expert marketeer, bush pilot and hunters, ignited the furnace of what would soon become a booming, exciting and dangerous enterprise. <i>(1998,Sea of Gold,  A,92</i>)

### bush pioneer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bush settler who cuts a farm from bush.

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Mr McPherson was one of the real old time bush pioneers. He had felled trees on his section, pit-sawn the logs and built his own house with the timber. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,13</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> The bush pioneer depended on the bush for nearly everything in the way of furniture. <i>(1945,Pioneer Looks Back, A,12</i>)

### bush plant
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Some of the bush-plants of Otago cannot endure the winters of the more Northern Canterbury Plains ... <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,11</i>)

### bush plot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an allocated section of bush to be cleared for farming [often forty acres].

>  <b>WASWO</b> The proposed 40 acre bush plots posed problems since clearing such a large area was a major exercise. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,17</i>)

### bush poet
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an oral entertainer of verse in a rural setting.

>  The New Zealand bush poet - I speak of him in the singular, but his name is legion - is not dead, nor even seriously discouraged. <i>(Dec 18, 1890,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  He is widely recognised and honoured as 'Hobnail Henry' the bush poet. <i>(Jun 2002,Growing Today,40</i>)

### bush poetry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Another story is commemorated by a piece of rough bush poetry which still adorns the wall of the Glenthorne musterers' hut. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,105</i>)

### bush pole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PYKE</b> The bunks - rude structures of bush poles, with flour bag sacking - had been torn down; and clothing ... heaped pell-mell on the floor. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/iv/85,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

### bush polo
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a casual game of polo. (See [Sunday polo](../S#sunday-polo))

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> With them, supplemented by three or four station hacks, we started playing 'bush polo' on Sundays in the summer. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,76</i>)

### bush pool
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pond fringed by native vegetation.

>  <b>SALE</b> Below them, dragonflies dance over a bush pool ... <i>(1987,Country Diary,15</i>)

### bush pork
 
 <i>n.</i> wild pork

>  <b>MASTERS</b> I may here mention that bush pork, is superior to home fed, as the pigs live upon roots, berries ... <i>(1871,Autobiography,41</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> ... how thankful I was for a pannikin of tea and a slice of bush pork and damper ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,147</i>)

### bush porridge
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> She claimed to have built all her children up on smoky, burnt bush porridge. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,91</i>)

### bush post-office
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rural mailbox, situated far from civilisation.

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> In the middle of the [Canterbury] plains we drew up alongside a post, on which was nailed what looked like a small writing-desk. The driver leant out, lifted the lid, took out a small leather bag, and drove off. It was a bush post-office - a very private letter-box, belonging to some sheep-station. <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,196</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> It was a bush post-office - a very private letter-box, belonging to some sheep-station. <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,196</i>)

### bush pub
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rural public house.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> I travelled by rail as far as Mataroa and then by a special public works train to Ohakune, where I stopped the night at a bush pub. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,38</i>)

>  At 4 o'clock he had started with two two-gallon billies to fetch the beer from the nearest bush-pub. <i>(Mar 22, 1890,NZ Observer,9</i>)

>  At 4 o'clock he had started with two two-gallon billies to fetch beer from the nearest bush-pub. <i>(Mar 22, 1890,NZ Observer,9</i>)

### bush railway
 
 <i>n.</i> = bush tramway

>  I begin with an occasion when I had dinner in a house in the bush at Karekare on Auckland's west coast. We sat around a German coal range the couple had hauled by means of wood-track bush railway to the top of the hill on which they had built their house. On a nog in the unlined room was propped an old photograph. <i>(1979,Islands,25/267</i>) [NOTES: Stead (John Mulgan)]

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> In some cases steam engines were employed [to get logs out of bush], or locomotives running on a bush railway, or `tram'. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,161</i>) [NOTES: (Joe Gasparich, Northland kauri country)]

### bush range(s)
 
 <i>n.</i> range(s) covered with bush (See [cf. bush country](bri-byo.md#bush-country),[bush face](bri-byo.md#bush-face))

>  <b>GUDGEON</b> The men did little but scout and forage ... allowing the Hauhaus to e a strong pah in the bush-ranges. <i>(1879,Reminiscences of the War in NZ,70</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The floor and gently sloping sides were verdant with English and native grasses from the broad strath at our feet right up to where the cleared country narrowed and ran out in the higher ground of the bush range at the head. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,41,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> This seemed to make him very happy, and he continued, `My farm extends right to the top of those bush ranges,' he said proudly, ... <i>(1982,Myrtle and Sophia,26</i>)

### bush ranger
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a member of the settlers' militia

>  Artillery including howitzers, rockets and mortars were used to assist infantry attacking from the north and east while a force of bush rangers attacked from the ridge line above... After the ambush, a force of bush rangers set out in pursuit of the Maori party... <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/25</i>)

### bush rat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the native or New Zealand rat (introduced by early Maori immigrants), kiore.

>  <b>MONEY</b> This I did by the light of a good bush-fire, that.. showed me the only living animals that formed my society - grey bush-rats. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,31 or 81</i>)

>  On the habits of the New Zealand Bush-rat (Mus maorium) ... <i>(1889,TrNZI,22/301</i>)

>  <i>Arboreal Nests of Bush-rat</i> (Mus maorium) [title] <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/258</i>)

>  On Great Barrier Island, bush rats kiore [sic] and feral cats threaten the small colony [of Cook's petrel]. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,81</i>)

>  Arboreal Nests of Bush-rat (<i>Mus maorium</i>) [title] <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/258</i>)

>  On Great Barrier Island, bush rats kiore [sic] and feral cats threaten the small colony [of Cook's petrel]. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,81</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> I have heard a bush rat run across the dry leaves <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,146</i>)

>  She can be as cunning as a bush rat sometimes. <i>(Aug 21, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> found in the bush [the 1871 citation above may belong here]

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... I smoked my evening pipe. This I did by the light of a good bush-fire, that danced and flickered on the trees and scrub around me, and showed the only living animals that formed my society - grey bush rats. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,31</i>)

>  The bush-rats (<i>Mus rattus</i>), which depend for food ... on the ripe berries falling from the trees ... <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/207</i>)

>  The Bush Rat is a noted Egg Stealer. [caption] <i>(Nov 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,109</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> Two days after our arrival [at Hokitika in 1865] there was a flood, ... and bush rats were swimming in hundreds, to the fearsome delight of the youngsters. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,148</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> [?Norway rats] The bush-rats are experts in extracting the kernels from the nuts [of the tawa]. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,285</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> It is a pity if this [blue NZ] rat is extinct ... All the bush rats I have seen in the King Country, however, were the ordinary grey vermin. <i>(1950,My First 80 Years,107</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> The bush rat, with its great round nest of holly-bark on holly-tree or scrub-top was observed without hurry; [Southland c1900] <i>(1953,Chaslands,94</i>)

>  <b>ELLIOT& ADSHEAD</b> Then came a plague of bush rats, huge brutes that were eaten by the moreporks. Mr Thomas Nix, now in his 80s, told me that when the men were out bushfelling it was standard practice to give the pungas a hearty whack and then leap back to dodge at least a couple of rats ... <i>(1967,Cowshed to Dog Collar,16</i>)

>  <b>HEND</b> Bush rats at night sometimes made their way into our schooners when they were loaded with general merchandise, or back-loaded with small quantities of farm produce. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,122</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> anything to avoid the `Thirty-Mile Bush', a half-liquid marsh strewn with the skeletons of pack-horses, their bones picked clean by bush rats. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,118,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... Dad said seriously `I well remember in the timber camps, the bush-rats would come out at night so hungry they'd eat your toes.' <i>(1965,Power of Joy,173</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Those three [musterers] were `Bush Rats'. They came from the Alford Forest district, commonly known as `The Bush', and a district which has always been noted for the [27] good hill men it has produced. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,26</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (historical) the Norway rat ?

>  The bush-rats (<i>Mus rattus</i>), which depend for food ... on the ripe berries falling from the trees ... <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/207</i>)

>  [?Norway] The Bush Rat is a noted Egg Stealer. [caption] <i>(Nov 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,109</i>)

Mr Thomas Nix, now in his 80s, told me that when the men were out bushfelling it was standard practice to give the pungas a hearty whack and then leap back to dodge at least a couple of rats ... <i>(1967,Cowshed to Dog Collar,16</i>)

### bush reserve
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an area of native bush set aside for conservation and/or recreation purposes.

>  There's invariably a willing response to the 'all expenses paid trip to an island or bush reserve for a day', in exchange for manual labour. <i>(Jun 2006,Conservation Action,5</i>)

### bush ridge
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bush-covered ridge in hill country.

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> Northward, the nearer view hidden by the bush ridge up which we had come [bushridge] <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,47</i>)

>  <b>MCCONOCHIE</b> The odd trail appeared on the bushridges, which were well back from our farm clearing. <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,32</i>)

### bush rigged
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of tractors or similar machinery] fitted with extra equipment for heavy work.

>  Farm equipment for sale: HD6 Bulldozer, bush rigged, manual, good farm machine. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,25</i>)

### bush road
 
 <i>n.</i> (often used spec. in sawmilling for a logging road).

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> A bush road, tolerably level for a mile. Then ... on to a small <i>kainga,</i> on the banks of the Otawao; ... <i>(Dec 6, 1850,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Dec 6 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 59]

>  There is what we call a<i> good </i>bush road to Rata Nui but beyond it there are two miles of bush walking along what is called `a line'; a line is made by cutting the supple jacks and small shrubs with a bill hook ... [sic] time bush walking takes is very surprising to me ... [sic] <i>(Nov 11, 1853,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/133,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J. Maria Richmond)]

>  <b>ELWELL</b> Sledges were necessary for bush work, for the bush roads were too narrow and too full of stumps for a dray to be worked in them. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,238</i>)

>  The bird in question was captured in the Tadmor Valley, in [Nelson], by a man who stated that he saw it walking on an unfrequented bush-road. <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/190</i>)

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> Late on the evening of the 31st of August, after having waded through the bottomless bush road to Pukekohe, I stood before the Church of the settlement, now transformed into a fortress and garrisoned by the settlers. <i>(1864,Memoranda,23</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Pihoi (<i>Anthus N.Z.</i>) is the so-called native `lark.' It is a ground pipit, and may often be seen fluttering and chirping about a bush road. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Three grand days in the saddle, the last winding down the bush road that skirted the ... Ohura ... then `pikau' and foot-slog for the great roadless forest. <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,6</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> ... the two men and the two boys in that dog cart went their way along the long bush road past Marsden township towards Kumara. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,23</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a packtrack or dray road cut through native bush.

>  We'll wander again up the bush road with the morning ... <i>(Feb 2, 1942,NZ Dairy Exporter,44</i>)

>  The bush road between Tauranga and Ohinemutu is in a bad state.. <i>(May 7, 1883,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> There is what we call a good bush road to Rata Nui but there are two miles of bush walking along what is called a 'line' - a line is made by cutting the supplejacks and small shrubs ... <i>(Sep 24, 1851,Letter,I 133</i>) [NOTES: Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers]

>  Pigeons and pheasants are numerous in many parts of the bush, whilst there are a few to be obtained up any of the bush roads. <i>(May 19, 1888,Egmont Star,15</i>)

>  From different quarters we hear that the bush roads in this district are not nearly so bad this year as during winter months in past years. <i>(Jul 21, 1888,Egmont Star,7</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> ... we set out for Tapanui, and after ploughing through some five miles of bush roads, up to our ankles in mud and slush ... <i>(1922,Reminiscences of a New Chum in Otago,34</i>)

>  <b>REA</b> .. my father was killed instantaneously being thrown from a dray when the horse bolted down a steep bush road. <i>(1991,Honey and Cheese,11</i>)

>  <b>EWERS</b> No, we'll all go and then walk down the old bush road and home that way. <i>(1993,Lifetime on the Land, A,56</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a back-country, rough road, by no means necessarily through bush

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Some of the bush runs in Western Southland had no choice in the matter, as the country was unsuitable for sheep, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 56 [?thru the bush] They were on the bush road, curved like a bow; half-way, they turned and struck up over the hills, into a clear crest of morning sultried with yellow gorse. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,40</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> To describe ... so strange an assortment ... as the labourers on the bush-roads in the colonies would require the pen of Dickens. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,115</i>)

>  <b>TROLLOPE</b> The road [to Wakatip was] fairly good - for a "bush" road. The name must be taken in a colonial sense. There was hardly a tree to be seen throughout the journey but the word has made its way over from Australia, and the traveller when he is out of town is in the bush. A country road which is merely formed and not metalled is a bush road, though it pass across an open plain, or up a treeless valley. <i>(1874,New Zealand (various authors/titles),47</i>)

>  Is the road difficult? And the answer was, Not as bush-roads, so called, go. <i>(1895,Pictorial NZ,2</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>HYDE</b> <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,1</i>) [NOTES: incomplete]

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> Trollope states that `the road was fairly good for a bush road, i.e., a country road formed but not metalled; but there was hardly a tree to be seen on the whole journey.' <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,112</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> At first he conducted business from his farm house, about a mile and a quarter up the little bush road at Puketitiri. [1880's] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,33</i>)

### bush robin
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a small, round, tame and inquisitive little bird, one of the Petroica australis subspecies, known also as New Zealand robin. [<i>Petroica australis</i>, (SOUTH ISLAND) ROBIN]

>  No matter where anyone entered the forest he would have a bush robin as a mate and a friend. <i>(Dec 26, 1931,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [They saw only] a wretched bush-robin, which Arthur insisted on shooting ... which was so close that it was actually blown to pieces. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,150</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Tiffany, wooing bush robins, coteries of tiny wrens, swinging in supple-jack loops with a bellbird ringing its chime for her .. <i>(1938,Promenade,96</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On our arrival back at Hollyford late that afternoon we found our former camp friends, the bush robins waiting to greet us. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,286</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Bush robins, parakeets, tomtits, grey warblers, riflemen, pigeons and the bush hawk are still to be seen. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,129</i>)

>  <i>Robin Petroica australis</i> (SPARRMAN, 1788) ... <i>North Island Robin P.a. longpipes</i> (LESSON, 1828). <i>South Island Robin P.a. australis</i> (SPARRMAN, 1788). <i>Stewart Island Robin P.a. rakiura</i> FLEMING, 1950. OTHER NAMES: <i>Bush robin, toutouwai. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,282</i>)

### bush rug
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a basic, serviceable mat.

>  Bush rugs, 58 in X 68 in. fringed edges, good designs. <i>(Mar 28, 1931,NZ Dairy Exporter,32</i>)

### bush run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large area of bush land that can be developed in to farmed pasture.

>  <b>REA</b> Trembath brothers, about 1920, purchased a bush run up the top of the Big Waitaha river ... <i>(1991,Honey and Cheese,19</i>)

### bush sarsaparilla
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Rhipogonum scandens</i> cit. also at sarsaparilla =supplejack.

>  KARE-AO (<i>common supplejack</i> ...) The roots, after the outer skin is scraped off, are beaten into pulp, and steeped in a little water; ... (It has been called bush Sarsaparilla.) <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,742</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The roots of the Rhipogonum are used by bushmen as a medicine and the plant is sometimes called "Bush Sarsaparilla". <i>(1906,Plants of NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>LAING  & BLACKWELL</b> The roots of the Rhipogonum are used by bushmen as a medicine, and the plant is sometimes called `Bush Sarsaparilla' <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,93</i>)

### bush sawyer
 
 <i>n.</i> ?one who saws timber in the bush; or ? one who falls or saws bush for timber (See [bushman](bri-byo.md#bushman); [bushie](bri-byo.md#bushie))

>  <b>LINGARD</b> The main offenders, drunken bush-sawyers, had departed. <i>(1936,Prison Labour in NZ,8</i>)

### bush school
 
 <i>n.</i> a country or backblocks school

>  <b>MUNDY</b> The humble hedge-school - or rather bush-school ... and a crowd of flaxen ... children rushing from its porch. <i>(1852,Our Antipodes,61</i>)

>  Bush Schools: Low wages, hard graft, and unnecessary worry is the lot of the out-back schoolteacher. <i>(Jan 27, 1906,NZ Truth,32/4/4</i>)

### bush scone
 
 <i>n.</i> a scone baked in the bush over the embers (See [cf. damper](d-dif.md#damper))

>  <b>WILSON</b> That evening we baked bush scones on sticks to the delight of our friends. <i>(1950,My First 80 Years,141</i>)

### bush scout
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Soldiers and Maori warriors, missionaries, settlers, bush scouts, sailors, heroic women are the leading figures in these tales of true romance. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,xi</i>)

### bush section
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a parcel of forested land (See [cf. section](sa-sho.md#section))

>  One great blessing in a bush section is the abundance of firewood. <i>(Apr 20, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/95,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richmond)]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> [title] A `Bush' Section on Green Island, Otago (Fox Lodge). <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/239</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The early settlers of the Weber area, for a number of years after taking up their bush sections, had to rely on packhorse transport from Dannevirke for the delivery of mail and essential supplies. [1880's] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,141</i>)

>  He chose to pick his way back ... by way of the scrubby bush section down behind the sand dunes ... <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>) [NOTES: (File Mourning)]

>  It is a cosy ... home [in Titirangi] on a large bush section. <i>(Oct 29, 1989,Sunday Magazine (Auckland),8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an allotted area of bush land available for farming development.

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> One great blessing in a bush section is the abundance of firewood. <i>(May 18, 1851,Letter,I  95</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers]

>  54 acres bush section of which six are cleared and logged. <i>(Jun 23, 1880,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  An industrious and steady man of experience could start on a 100 acre deferred payment bush section. <i>(Jul 1887,NZ Farmer,196</i>)

>  Why, one good bush section here would beat that hollow. <i>(Apr 7, 1888,Egmont Star,9</i>)

>  <b>BARRER</b> They took up a bush section near Raetihi and rode through with pack horses. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,139</i>)

>  In 1858 he was attracted to Akaroa where he took up a bush section and became a settler, and married. The Norseman later on took up a 60-acre bush section up on the ranges <i>(1945,BLANK ENTRY,16</i>) [NOTES: in KEEGAN (1983) The First Generation]

### bush selection
 
 <i>n.</i> bush section (? an Aust. borrowing).

>  <b>GIPPS</b> ... on this spacious bush-selection. <i>(1907,Outward Bound,27</i>)

### bush sense
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering, hunting, tramping] an instinct to take a correct route when none is obvious.

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Some people are attributed 'bush sense', which is supposed to be an instinct for the right direction. <i>(1988,Hills, The,29</i>)

### bush settlement
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a district settled by bush farmers or foresters and their families.

>  The New Zealand fungus known to commerce is found upon various kinds of decayed timber in the North island, in what are called new bush settlements. ... It is used by the Chinese for mixing with food, and for medicinal purposes. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,151</i>)

>  The effect of the ... destructive elements may be seen on a wide scale in any of the larger bush-settlements. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/493</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

>  <b>BOREHAM</b> Whenever he came upon some rustic township, bush settlement or native <i>pa</i>, he at once pitched his tent, and threw all his energy into his ministration to the deepest needs of the people. <i>(1911,Selwyn,63</i>)

>  As a child I lived with my parents in a bush settlement, miles from the railway. <i>(Apr 29, 1932,NZ Dairy Exporter,72</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> The inner history of the bush settlements is one of hardship, isolation and privation unknown to the settlers of the open country. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,8</i>)

### bush settler
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who fells and farms bushland [usually in small holdings].

>  During late years the dairy industry has been one of the most popular ... industries of the bush settler. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/440</i>)

>  The bush settler generally owns a few head of cattle ... <i>(Sep 1883,Northern New Zealand Settler,262</i>)

>  It would depend a great deal upon whether the intending bush settler would be willing to roll up his sleeves. <i>(Jul 1887,NZ Farmer,196</i>)

>  A well-known bush settler had occasion to pay an order on him at a warehouse. <i>(Mar 10, 1888,Egmont Star,7</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> When these prominent men had to visit at the bush settlers' whares the trouble was always to find a place for the top hat, and then to give it a brush up when the visitor was leaving. <i>(1945,Pioneer Looks Back, A,19</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> It is difficult to realise in these days the price the pioneer bush settlers had to pay to gain their small and often uneconomic holdings. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,8</i>)

>  It had often been said that as the greater portion of the retiring members lived near Manaia, bush settlers were neglected and Manaia exceptionally favoured. <i>(Jun 25, 1883,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  There is plenty of good land in the Forty-Mile Bush. The bush settlers would be only too glad to avail themselves ... <i>(Aug 1888,NZ Farmer,289</i>)

### bush shanty
 
 <i>n.</i> a place where bushmen 1 or bush hands live together [cf. bush hut note that 1857 Hammond Wild N. Scenes is a North American citation (OEDS BUSH 10c] (See [cf. bush hut](bri-byo.md#bush-hut))

>  <b>GIBSON</b> Most bush shanties possess such a butt <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot,66</i>)

>  When he arrived at the little bush shanty, Mr Jones was away in the tall timber. <i>(1961,Puborama,94</i>)

>  <b>GIBSON</b> The monotony of bush-shanty life. <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a shanty (?unlicensed grog-shop) in the (?forested) outbacks.

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Scene a bush Shanty among the [West Coast] ranges. A number of diggers sitting Drinking and talking. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,30</i>) [NOTES: 1881 Douglas Field Book in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 30]

>  <b>HUTT</b> Also prominent in the newspapers of the time were sensationalist reports that the King Country prohibition was being widely flouted and that "lightning rod" (a mixture of methylated spirits and whisky and/or raspberry cordial) was being hawked in bush shanties. <i>(1999,Maori & Alcohol,103</i>) [NOTES: See Bollinger <i>Grog's Own Country</i> 1967, p. 104; Eldred-Grigg <i>Pleasures of the Flesh. Sex and Drugs in Colonial NZ</i>, 1984, p. 202,216]

### bush shirt
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Advt] The "Lamby" Bushshirt. A legend throughout the land. Made from quality 100% wool weave, wool lined, lined hood and lace-up front... Lamby bushshirt $139.00. <i>(Jun 1993,NZ Outdoor,36</i>)

>  [Caption] Bush-shirts, soldiers for the use of. <i>(May 24, 1943,NZEF Times,II/100 : 12</i>)

>  When I get my civvy clothes on No more bush shirts will I don. <i>(May 22, 1944,NZEF Times,III/152 : 7</i>)

>  "He can't stop if he wants to," said bush-shirt. <i>(Jul 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  The legendary Swanndri is an invention of the 1800s when William Broome devised it as the country's first all-purpose, hard wearing shower- proof bush shirt. <i>(Nov 30, 1999,Evening Post,Looking Back : 18</i>)

>  <b>THE SARGE</b> He went away in search of his <i>Groppi mokka</i> which consisted of a freshly pressed.. pair of slacks and a bush shirt. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,110</i>)

>  The witness said he had visited the same property four times last year, stealing four motorcycles and a bush shirt. <i>(Jul 21, 1992,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  <b>CHAVASSE</b> There were several four-wheel-drive vehicles there ... and maybe thirty or forty solid-looking young men in swandrys and colourful bush shirts getting ready to go. [fn] Waterproof woolen [sic] outer garment. <i>(1990,Integrity,25</i>)

>  It seemed strange to see him wearing a sort of bush shirt and rough trousers. <i>(Oct 17, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a protective, heavy woollen shirt usually of a checked or tartan patterned fabric and with a long tail.

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> .. the bush shirts and the moleskins that the old time shepherds wore. <i>(1975,More From the High Country,38</i>)

>  The first Swanndri was made by William Broome in 1871 and the design of the distinctive bush shirt has changed little. <i>(Mar 24, 2002,Sunday Star Times,E1</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> It's something to do with bush shirts and billies, roadends, the tops, blisters and always the bush. <i>(1988,Hills, The,7</i>)

>  Another [farmer] mourned the dumping of a bush shirt that he had "only had for 37 years." <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,1</i>)

### bush shooter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hunter who shoots deer and other feral animals in bush rather than in open country.

>  <b>AXBEY</b> After three shots it became obvious that John was a much better bush shooter than an open-top hunter ... <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,66</i>)

### bush shooting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the stalking and shooting of feral deer, pigs and possums in native forest.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> When bush shooting one soon learns to outwit the deer. <i>(1964,Deer Shooting Days,103</i>)

>  <b>FARMER</b> For bush shooting it helped to sweat a blob of Silfloss or brazing brass onto the foresight and shape it with a file. <i>(1994,Best Job Ever, The,97</i>)

### bush shop
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) matters to do with bush farming.

>  <b>COMBS</b> Men talked politics and bush 'shop.' <i>(1951,Growing Up in the Forty-Mile Bush,34</i>)

### bush shrub
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> It [<i>C. lucida</i>] is one of the many Otago bush shrubs or trees, whose introduction in Britain is worthy ... of experiment. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,71</i>)

### bush sickness
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Dr Reakes and Mr Aston.. have given such hidebound opinions a rude shock in the matter of the bush sickness of the North Island. <i>(Jul 17, 1912,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  Over a small extent of wooded country between Waikato and Tauranga, a percentage of cattle kept on clearings become affected with what is locally called "bush sicknesss, " recovering when removed. <i>(1897,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,V/4</i>)

>  The mysterious wasting condition locally known as `bush sickness', which always eventually appears in ruminating herbivora pastured on certain areas of pumice country in the North Island ... <i>(1912,TrNZI,44/288</i>)

>  For long the "coasty sickness", "pluing", "vinguish" and "daising" of stock on King Island off the coast of Tasmania, in the Kedong Valley of Nairobi, and among the Cheviot Hills of the North of England were not associated with New Zealand "bush sickness" or "the skinnies" as it is graphically labelled by the farmers of this country. <i>(1911,NZ Journal of Agriculture,III/394</i>)

>  The curious ailment of cattle peculiar to certain parts of New Zealand, known as "bush sickness", was reported on to the *** <i>(Mar 18, 1924,Otago Witness,5</i>) [NOTES: Check & complete quotation]

>  For long the "coasty sickness", "pluing", "vinguish" and "daising" of stock on King Island off the coast of Tasmania, in the Kedong Valley of Nairobi, and among the Cheviot Hills of the North of England were not associated with New Zealand "bush sickness" or "the skinnies" as it is graphically labelled by the farmers of this country. <i>(Jun 22, 1928,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

>  Cobalt.. is the only substance which has been definitely proved to cure bush sickness. <i>(May 6, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,33</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Cobalt.. is the only substance which has been definitely proved to cure bush sickness. <i>(1967,Farm & Station Verse,33</i>) [NOTES: Original citation 1950]

>  Development of former bush-sick areas in Tauranga county... The discovery that cobalt was a cure for bush sickness came at a most opportune moment. <i>(Jul 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,67</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep and cattle disease caused by cobalt-deficient soil [known by several names such as Morton Mains disease, the skinnies, Hope disease, Tauranga disease, bush disease and Mairoa dopiness.]

>  All the evidence gathered points to the condition being one of malnutrition rather than of disease, properly speaking, and I have therefore adopted the term 'bush sickness' in connection with it, instead of 'bush disease' as heretofore. <i>(Aug 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,118</i>)

>  Later New Zealand investigations showed that similar-appearing diseases in this country (bush sickness, Mairoa dopiness, and Morton Mains disease) could also be cured and prevented by cobalt. <i>(Mar 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,239</i>)

>  <b>LANDS & SURVEY</b> The last name 'Bush-sickness' persisted long after it was known that the native bush had really nothing to do with the disease. <i>(1975,Prosperity from Pumice Land,7</i>)

### bush singlet
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a black, working singlet; a thick, black, woollen, sleeveless singlet worn by bushmen and other manual workers (because it soaks up sweat without chilling the wearer, and does not show sweatstain and other grime). [c. 1910 p.c. W.H.B.Orsman (Marlborough). As long as I can remember and certainly before the First World War, black woollen singlets were always called, and sold in stores as, 'bush singlets'.] (See [bushman's singlet](bri-byo.md#bushman's-singlet))

>  Singlets. New, navy blue, all-wool athletic-type singlets for outdoor winter workers. <i>(Jul 28, 1965,Weekly News,19</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Over a well-trimmed hedge and neat flower garden was a man in his forties, white hair cropped short, a black bush singlet, shearers' pants, heavy work boots that scraped on the porch floor as he stood up. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,99</i>)

>  Navy Bush Singlets, M., 8s 3; O.S., 8s 9d. <i>(Feb 8, 1944,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  At my golf club there is a large notice (abbreviated): 'T-shirts, bush singlets, beach shorts, jeans, jandals, sneakers are not acceptable.' <i>(Mar 3, 1991,New Zealandia,3</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Got the missus sewing up my best bush singlet and the kids polishing up the seaboots. <i>(1981,Fish Factory,157</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> In fact, standing there in his black bush singlet and leather apron, turning the handle of the forge,... <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,40</i>)

>  Hey Colonel, you reckon ... two grunds, two woollen shirts, long trou, bush singlet, two jerseys, long johns, balaclava, over trou, swanee, puttees and parka will be enough [for a tramping trip]. <i>(1971,VUWTC'71,49</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> [In the changehouse the miner could] wash his working clothes - consisting of a bush singlet, a pair of tough denim trousers, and a pair of thick socks - <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,211</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Bits of drapery too, the black bush singlets, work trousers and shirts, singlets - <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,200</i>)

>  G. W. Johnson's working life 1940-46 Dress... changed a lot ... Bush singlet - navy blue flannel or woollen singlets either short or long-sleeved - and Parma nap trousers. <i>(Nov 12, 1983,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Wellington 9.25 am: Spectrum Documentary:]

>  <b>CASEY</b> Facing him was a man the size of a gorilla with his chest tearing his bush singlet open. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,98</i>)

>  The small village of Waitotara boomed for a while with the sale of axes, slashers, sharpening stones, boots and bush singlets. <i>(Dec 31, 1964,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  NZ Farmer July 14. 45 bush singlet: the all-time New Zealand favourite made to last. <i>(Jul 14, 1983,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

>  <b>NGAWHIKA</b> My bush singlet was slipped over his torso and tied in place to try and prevent further damage to his intestines. <i>(1996,Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting,155</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> A man came out wearing nothing but a bush singlet, black hairy legs planted, tossle and cods hanging free. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,79</i>)

>  I rather liked Uncle Jacko sporting sexy black underwear, namely his black bush singlet. <i>(Aug 2010,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,31</i>)

### bush skiddy
 
 <i>n.</i> [see also skiddy, skiddie, skidworker, skidman]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Thus, a <i>bush skiddy</i> is a man who works on the cleared tracks which carry logs to the mill. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,85</i>)

>  WANTED 2 bush skiddies. <i>(Feb 20, 1904,Bush Advocate,3</i>)

>  2 Bush Skiddies. Single accommodation. <i>(Aug 25, 1945,Evening Post,3</i>)

### bush skids
 
 <i>n.</i> see skids

>  BUSH SKIDS A platform made or logs and used to assist in loading logs or trees on to vehicles. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### bush snail
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... where ... woodhens once stalked in leafy damp and probed, and bush snails made slow and thoughtful circles in time ... <i>Ibid.</i> 133 The great bush snails hold in, in brief innocence and ignorance! <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,127</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Students of the large bush snails (<i>Paryphanta </i>) have found this an interesting area ... <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,27</i>)

>  <b>12</b> Those great crashing bush-drives, when one tree was aimed to hit another partly-cut tree, which in turn smashed into another half-severed tree, and so on smashing and roaring downhill to the gully's bottom, ... the bush snails dying, the woodhens confounded, the brown pebbled creek and all its reflections from the beginning shrinking back in dismay. <i>(Feb 3, 1985,NZ Times</i>) [NOTES: (Jim Henderson)]

### bush sock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering and hunting] a very thick woollen boot sock.

>  <b>NGAWHIKA</b> Cutting one of my bush socks in half, I slipped it over Caesar's head like a long woollen collar to slow the bleeding and keep the throat wound clean. <i>(1996,Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting,155</i>)

### bush soil
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) earth fortified with wood ash and humus from native bush.

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> .. so green with its pasture paddocks built upon the rich bush soils. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,118</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> We raked the wood ashes out over the rich bush soil with a homemade leveller. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,119</i>)

### bush sow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild sow.

>  There is a tamed bush-sow on the farm, a most ungainly looking brute ... <i>(Aug 14, 1900,Hawera Star,4</i>)

### bush spider
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PETERSOLN</b> ... little Jim would proudly bring me a fury bush spider spanning his palm ... <i>(1981,Glasshouses,26</i>)

### bush sprig
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rubber boot sprig.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They wore what were known as ferntights, boots which, instead of the ordinary hobnails, had bush sprigs. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,245</i>)

### bush stain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a charcoal stain in fleecewool of sheep grazed on a bush-burn.

>  <i>Bush Stain</i>: Discoloration of wool with charcoal from bush burns. More commonly referred to as log stain. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,53</i>)

### bush stalking
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the stealthy movement through bush for deer or other wildlife.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> One of the pleasures of bush stalking is to walk along a few chains ... <i>(1964,Deer Shooting Days,103</i>)

### bush station
 
 <i>n.</i> a (missionary) station in the bush (See [cf. bush-missionary](bri-byo.md#bush-missionary))

>  <b>YOUNG</b> Mr Whiteley, who occupies a "bush" station ... <i>(1858,Southern World,14</i>)

### bush store
 
 <i>n.</i> a rural store

>  <b>HAY</b> When enough [gum] has been collected and scraped it is carried down to the nearest bush-store or settlement, where it is at once sold. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/27</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> I had time to walk down the street [?] and see some of the typical bush-stores, with their disordered medley of merchandise ... <i>(1914,Woman in the Antipodes, A,86</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> There were nine of us who fell in ... at Harry Tanner's little bush store and accommodation-house, and each ... picked out a horse from the little mob, of Maori-bred animals that the packer brought along ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small shop for isolated bushworkers and local farmers.

>  <b>HAY</b> When enough has been collected and scraped it is carried down to the nearest bush-store or settlement, where it is at once sold. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,II 27</i>)

>  <b>SPENCER</b> We got it all from the bush store. [bush-store] <i>(1992,Waioeka Pioneering Saga,208</i>)

### bush story
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TINNE</b> Among other bush-stories, some of which savoured a trifle of Munchausenism, one especially tickled my fancy. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,7</i>)

### bush stream
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Kokopu] ... is generally found in bush-streams where trout are rare or absent. <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/294</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

### bush stretcher
 
 <i>n.</i> a rough or makeshift bedstead (stretcher) consisting of a rectangular frame covered with planks, lathes, branches or wire-netting, the whole supported by four (often folding) legs (See [cf. stretcher](../S#stretcher),[camp bed](c-chi.md#camp-bed))

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I didn't have a bed, so I knocked up a bush stretcher, with some rails, and nailed sacks across. <i>Ibid.</i> 87 He knocked up a bush stretcher to sleep in. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,5,1975 ed.</i>)

### bush style
 
 <i>n.</i> a rough, makeshift way, as in the bush

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> The table was laid in regular bush style, with tin plates[,?] and pannikins, iron forks and spoons ... <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,141</i>)

### bush swag
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of swag (or pack) best suited to use in the bush

>  <b>McHUTCHESON</b> The horse-shoe [swag] won't do in the bush ... it's always catching on something. I'll show you the "bush" swag ... when the compact knapsack had been slung between our shoulders, leaving the arms quite free, and no projecting ends to catch, we recognised how convenient the bush swag was. <i>(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland,46</i>)

### bush talk
 
 <i>n.</i> backblocks speech

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Then Steve's heart leapt in him at the sound of the bush-talk, which is a language all of its own. <i>(1907,Tracks  We Tread,87</i>)

>  But mostly, back at Ruatoki, we use our slangy bush talk. <i>(Jun 1998,Mana,No. 22: 35</i>)

### bush tea
 
 <i>n.</i> billy tea

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Bush tea is ... boiled in a can. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,43</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tea ... Billy-tea</i>, or <i>Bush-tea</i>. Tea made in a <i>billy</i> (q.v.) There is a belief that in order to bring out the full flavour it should be stirred with a gum-stick. <i>New Zealand tea.</i> Tea made of the leaves of the <i>manuka</i> (q.v.). See <i>Tea-tree. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,462</i>)

### bush teal
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILMOT</b> Tonight I saw 3 bush teal ... [151] At the river we came across four bush teal. <i>(1897,Journal,137</i>) [NOTES: 1897 Wilmot Journal in 1950 Reed Farthest West 137, 151]

### bush telegraph
 
 <i>n.</i> an informal but well organised system of word-of-mouth communication transmitting plans and movements of the police: grapevine 3. unofficial information: rumour; informal process of word-of-mouth communication, usually of rumour or gossip (cf. grapevine, jungle telegraph). [ca1953 Frank O'Brien "I heard it by bush telegraph; they wouldn't tell me straight to my face."]

>  [Hdg] Bush Telegraph on Play Values <i>(Nov 30, 1955,NZ Truth,28</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bush-telegraph</i>, ... Confederates of bushrangers who supply them with secret information of the movements of the police. <i>(1898,Austral English,72</i>)

>  <b>1/507</b> <i>(1878,Australian, The</i>)

>  The latter, however, got a message by medium of a bush telegraph, and slid out of town. <i>(Oct 24, 1903,NZ Bulletin,19</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... derivatives ... commonly used both in Ne Zealand and Australia [include] ...<i> bush telegraph</i>. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,15</i>)

>  <b>LAWSON</b> ... even men working in lonely gullies got word by bush telegraph. <i>(1951,Gold in their Hearts,117</i>)

>  <i>bush telegraph n.</i> confederates of bushrangers who supply them with movements of police. Now synonymous with <i>grapevine. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  <i>bush telegraph n.</i> confederates of bushrangers who supply them with movements of police. Now synonymous with "grapevine". <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> He was also one of those back-country types, who, by magic of bush telegraph, acquire, and pass on all the latest news. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,85</i>)

>  ... word had got round on the bush telegraph about the woolshed baby ... <i>(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,37</i>) [NOTES: (Joy Cowley)]

>  <i>bush telegraph n. Inf</i>. a means of spreading rumour, gossip, etc. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,148</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 96; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 64]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) gossip or informal news-spreading.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The bush telegraph is a powerful instrument and it's working all the time. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,16</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> He sent word around by bush telegraph. <i>(1977,Roughnecks, Rolling stones & Rouseabouts,103</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> The 'bush telegraph' spread the news that there was work for local men ... <i>(1993,Colonial Outcasts: A Search for the Remittance Man,139</i>)

### bush telegraphy
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bush telegraph](bri-byo.md#bush-telegraph))

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Bush telegraphy is a mystic phenomenon and I pondered and wondered how the news of the visit to this distant village could have reached these hospitable people when only late the night before was the visit suggested. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,119</i>)
