>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> I have an elaborate spy system, In Wellington as well as here ... Bureaucracy and bush-telegraphy go together. <i>(Jul 25, 1947,Letters,167,1981 ed.</i>)

### bush term
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> HEEL HORSE: Last horse in single file team (bush term). <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,186</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 186]

### bush terrace
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a level piece of land in mountainous country, covered in native flora.

>  <b>AXBEY</b> A couple of hours later, the others arrived back with a rifle and I followed the track out on to a bush terrace. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,30</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> It was only midday when we arrived so we had plenty of time to hunt some interesting bush terraces. <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,109</i>)

### bush township
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BELL</b> ... the promoters of these bush townships - whether it were the Government, private companies [21] or individuals, or a supplementing of the former by the latter - made the boundaries of them wide enough ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,20</i>)

### bush track
 
 <i>n.</i> a track through or in the bush (modern implication often a scenic track) (See [cf. bush path](bri-byo.md#bush-path),[bush road](bri-byo.md#bush-road),[Maori track](m-mar.md#maori-track))

>  Another forde, another bush track. <i>(May 13, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,184,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Wishing to reach the Wairoa Road, Mr Hill assured me there was a bush track which would take me there in 6 miles. ... <i>(Mar 15, 1867,Waikato Journals,108,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> On most bush tracks men follow each other - Indian file - and all excepting the leader pay little attention to the road they are going; those who would become good bushmen would do well to lead ..<i>Ibid.</i> 281 After dinner we rode along a bush track through the forest to call on Mr. Page ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,231</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> It [sc a badly made bale] makes an ugly and bulky bundle of merchandise, ... [eliciting] criticism by all and sundry, starting with the bullocky who has to [97] load and cart it over the natural bush tracks ... [to] the made roads. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,96,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... the landlord ... asked me if I had searched the bush track leading from his shanty to the inn from whence I had started. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,83</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... I found afterwards, the so-called bush-track was so horribly bad, that no description could give any idea of it. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,83</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> "This bush track had been specially cut in order to get supplies to Mr. Adams camp at Paengaroa, on the Main Trunk Line." <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,47</i>) [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 47]

>  <b>BULLER</b> At the time ... this place could be only reached by a canoe journey ... from Foxton, or by a rude bush-track - one of the Maori war-paths of former times. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/162</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> He guided us through the bush track by the river. <i>(Nov 20, 1907,Letters,1/31,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOREHAM</b> On the Lone Bush Track [chapter heading] <i>(1911,Selwyn,48</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> Donald McCormick ... walked along a bush track from their father's station four miles further down the Sound. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,17</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> When I was eleven my ... brother and I were walking ... round the bush track to school. We left early to carry the billies of milk ... to the settlement to the homes of children. <i>Ibid.</i>31 Trampers who walk the four-mile bush track from Halfmoon Bay ...come out on the open green patch ... <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,15</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> There would be miles of carrying their gear through bush tracks before they reached and found `home', just a few survey pegs in the bush. <i>Ibid.</i>24 Fortunately, there was a timber mill operating nearby. By borrowing horses, they were able to snig the timber home through the bush track. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,13</i>)

>  <b>GASCOYNE</b> There in an open spot in [sic] <i>manuka</i> I left my horse and saddle (for the bush track was impassable for a horse in the dark), and soon found that it would take me all night to find the way on foot. <i>(1916,Soldiering in NZ,26</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> In spite of the naval patrol ... the hostile Maoris maintained their communication with Te Rauparaha ..., either by canoe at night or by the bush tracks on the northern side of the Paua-taha-nui arm. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/117</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> From the Thames a romantic bush-track leads some 30 miles to Coromandel, ... <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,80,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> ... it was great fun ... riding over bush tracks, seing a new generation carry a step further inland the work their grandparents had begun on the coast. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,59</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> Toward the end of 1860 a young native named Eriata was found one morning lying dead from gunshot wounds on a bush track, ... <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,128</i>)

>  She carried with her a flax kit of kumaras, ... and struggled through the bush tracks, loaded with her heavy kits, taking three days to reach the school. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,33,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... derivatives ... commonly used both in New Zealand and Australia [include] ... <i>bush track.</i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,15</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> A forest path is a bush track. <i>(1945,If You'd Care to Know,99</i>)

>  Even those later scientists who use <i>forest</i> as a general rule usually write of <i>bushmen, bush tracks, patches of bush</i>, and the<i> bushline</i>. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,23</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason "Track and Trail"]

>  <b>BACON</b> "Where? There's only been one turn-off since we spoke to him, and that was only a bush track. This must be the right-road." I pointed to the map. "... Here's where we turned off the main road before we started to climb. ..." <i>(1963,In the Sticks,2</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Then there were the twins, Uncle Ed and Aunt Margaret ; they'd been born in the fern by the side of a bush-track before she could get to hospital. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,102</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Corduroying bush tracks was not the only lesson the American backwoods could teach. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,359,1962 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rough path through a forested area.

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> I climbed the mountain and went round the top, coming down the bush track and going over the lower burn. <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,201</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> .. having to traverse three huge ridges and miles of bush track, the memory is still vivid 76 years later. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,133</i>)

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> This is a bush track where two cannot walk abreast in most places. <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,193</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Most of us had to ride our horses, [to dances] sometimes ten or twelve miles through bush tracks. <i>(1997,Pigs and Me: Tales of the Bygone Bush.,77</i>)

### bush tractor
 
 <i>n.</i> [1981 Dept of Labour Bush tractor canopies [title]]

### bush tram
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the engine or train of a railway system, usu. with wooden rails, for carrying logs from the bush to the sawmill; a loci

>  BUSH TRAM , usually steam driven, locomotive hauling, bogeys of logs from the bush to mill, usually on wooden rails. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  BUSH TRAM , usually steam driven, locomotive hauling, bogeys of logs from the bush to mill, usually on wooden rails. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> The cart track petered out and became the track of the old bush trams. They could see the wooden rails along which the logs had been brought. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,125</i>)

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> There were once over 500 bush trams in New Zealand... The opening of the first bush tram has not been identified but records show they were in use in the 1850s. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,12</i>)

>  The discovery of gold on the West Coast demanded transport, which was by means of wooden-railed track serving the shanties and settlements with crudely built horse trams taking passengers and freight on 'bush trams'. <i>(Aug 2003,NZ Memories,No. 43/41</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  BUSH TRAM A light logging railway, usually on wooden rails. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### bush tramline
 
 <i>n.</i> = bush tramway

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> It was an extraordinary journey, mostly on one of those amazing bush tramlines built for timber trucks by amateur engineers who worked in the mills. <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,174</i>)

### bush tramway
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also tramway](tot-tyr.md#tramway))

>  <b>MOORE</b> Sometimes it was milled in a bush mill at that point [sc. the hauler]. Ofte[n] it was `fleeted up' on the skids ready for loading on to a bush tramway, the motive power for which was supplied either by draught animals or small steam locomotives. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,38</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> ... baulks of kauri descending, to the [233] accompaniment of squealing brakes, the self-acting incline from the end of the bush-tramway on the hill south of the battery. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,232</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> It [sc. timber] had come out ... horse-drawn through virgin forest to a bush tramway, ... <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,43</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> The little mill unions ... were always in the most inaccessible parts of the Coast and ... you had to ride by rickety bush tramway ... <i>(1984,Blackball 08,161</i>)

>  We crossed the Routeburn [Otago] on a good swing bridge and picked up an old bush tramway which led us well up the river. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,32</i>)

>  The words "bush tramway" always bring a smile to the face of an old tramping hand, because translated it means" "flat, benched, graded, and above all, easy". <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> However, there as no direct English 'bush tramway' experience to call upon, and so all the adaptations to bush conditions occurred in the new land...An overseas forester reported in 1877: "The universal use of the tramway forms a marked feature of the treatment of New Zealand forests." <i>Ibid.</i> 78 Steam locomotives designed for normal railways were not suitable for the rugged conditions on bush tramways and gradually special designs evolved. <i>Ibid.</i> 83 1871 Opening of William Brownlee's Mahakipawa-Kaituna valley tramway on 4'0" (1219 mm) gauge. This was New Zealand's first steam-worked bush tramway and used a British 0-4-0 vertical boiler locomotive. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,12</i>)

>  <b>LEITCH</b> Here [sc. on the Rotorua main line, the NZR has one of the last substantial bush tramways in the country <i>(1972,Railways of NZ,221</i>)

### bush tucker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) food that can be gleaned freshly from the bush environment or cooked in a basic, simple way.

>  If you have the inclination, tree and roots can be cooked up to provide you with some solid bush tucker. <i>(Jul 24, 2002,Capital Times,25</i>)

>  The concept of a Maori restaurant is great, and a whole galaxy of New Zealand bush tucker awaits imaginative treatment by contemporary Maori chefs. <i>(Aug 10, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  But if hairy-chested Kiwi bush tucker isn't your thing, there's less challenging fare on offer too. <i>(Feb 4, 2007,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),40</i>)

>  Food safety officials are taking a look at icons of Kiwi culture - bush tucker such as wild pork and venison, summer "harvests" of blackberries, wild mushrooms and indigenous foods such as puha and whitebait. <i>(Jul 23, 2004,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

>  On p 40 of the October issue, in Bush tucker by Neville Pomare it is incorrectly stated that the flesh of the karaka berry is poisonous. <i>(Mar 2001,Growing Today,5</i>)

>  The fame of Hokitika's Wildfood Festival and its gourmet bush tucker is spreading. <i>(Mar 6, 2002,Dominion,3</i>)

>  There's more disturbingly delicious delicacies than ever on offer (100 stalls this year) at this West Coast tribute to the wildest bush tucker. <i>(Mar 2006,North & South,38</i>)

### bush turkey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BROOKS</b> School games such as hopscotch, marbles, etc.. held no place in the lives of little "bush turkeys" like we were. <i>(1994,How Green Was Our Valley,35</i>) [NOTES: quote c.1908-1909]

### bush vegetable
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> A rather good bush vegetable is the <i>pikopiko</i>, the curled shoots of the <i>mauku</i> fern. The natives have long given up the use of these bush foods;... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/428,1983 ed.</i>)

### bush veil
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> ... a world which now and again, behind the bush-veils and the mist-veils of the New Zealand hills, had silenced their childhood with a memory ... <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,111</i>)

### bush vine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> The foot of the <i>pekerangi,</i> about 15 feet high, was strengthened with a facing of rocks and [42] stones ...; this was a variation of the usual Maori method of leaving the foot of the <i>pekerangi</i> open for the garrison's fire. ibid. 51-52 The outer wall, the <i>pekerangi</i>, or curtain, was formed of stout timbers ... sunk deeply in the ground at short intervals, with saplings and split timbers closely set between the larger posts, all bound firmly [52] together with cross-rails and <i>torotoro</i>, or bush-vines <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/41</i>)

### bush wagon/waggon
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large dray.

>  <b>WEBB</b> Peter used to take the milk to the factory, [19] driving Dolly in a bush wagon - the low, four-wheeled springless cart so popular in the bush districts of New Zealand. <i>(1925,Miss Peters' Special,18</i>)

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> Paid saddler GBP4.5.6 for repairs and second hand set of harness for bush waggon. Paid GBP1.2.6. to J Collinge, amount of his bill and GBP14 for bush waggon. John and Mr Beckett brought down in bush waggon, lawn tennis roller and rolled a piece in front for lawn tennis. <i>(Oct 3, 1905,Brabazon Diaries</i>)

### bush walk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Wayne Mason's "Nature" is less evocative of a meditative bushwalk than a battle with the neighbour's stereo. <i>(Jun 12, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  A sign on the corner of the shelter points to the left, announcing the bushwalk... But if you'd prefer the bushwalk and a look at the Manawatu River, go straight ahead and through the "wriggle gate" (the sort designed to keep out cyclists) and turn right down the hill. <i>(Jan 7, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Keeley's Bush Farmyard Holiday Park runs night-time bush walks where tourists see wallabies and possums, or Kiwi bears as they tell visitors. <i>(Jun 2, 2001,Dominion,00</i>)

### bush warbler
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  <b>BULLER</b> GERYGONE SYLVESTRIS. (BUSH-WARBLER.) ... The above Warbler, of which unfortunately no specimen exists ..., was first made known by Mr. T.H. Potts [in] .. `The Ibis' in 1872 (p.325), but without proposing any name for it. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/50</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Gerygone, n.</i> ... In New Zealand they are called Bush-warblers. <i>(1898,Austral English,159</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,85</i>)

>  "Kakarikis are absent this summer, and, strange to say, the silver-eye, and the charm of the hedge, the teetotum, and other bush warblers, are not to be seen or heard within many miles of this " <i>(Feb 14, 1879,Otago Daily Times,4</i>)

>  The "chough, chough" of the tui and the little bush warbler, with its one note, brings back early recollections of my life in the Westland forests. <i>(May 4, 1903,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  That bush warbler usually warns you of rain to come. <i>(Aug 8, 1931,Evening Post,16</i>)

### bush warfare
 
 <i>n.</i> guerilla Maori warfare in the bush (See [bush fighter](bri-byo.md#bush-fighter),[bush fighting](bri-byo.md#bush-fighting),[bushranger](bri-byo.md#bushranger))

>  [The natives'] present mode of <i>bush</i> or guerilla warfare <i>(1843,NZ Journal,104/327</i>)

>  ... bush warfare ... <i>(Dec 15, 1870,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> It was typical bush warfare for a few minutes. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/157</i>)

### bush weed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  .. (ranging from anywhere from $200 -$350 - or more for super 'my friend smuggled this himself from whereverthefuckistan' bud (normally bush weed from Wainuiomata anyway)) <i>(Apr 6, 2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.norml.org.nz/postt136.html]

### bush week
 
 <i>n.</i> modern slang or coll. idiom, often humorous and exclam., to describe or explain a rough, disordered state of affairs, a barbarous appearance, or makeshift arrangements, etc.: an extended (and fig.) use of bush `rough, makeshift'. [ca1946 University undergraduates at supper. Somebody has used all the milk. The person who has to go without says "No bloody milk! Man'd think it was bush-week!" ca1950 (in a hotel disorganised with renovations) "We'll fit you in somewhere but you can see we're in the middle of bush-week"]]

### bush weta
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tree-dwelling native insect, the cricket-like Stenopelmatidae.

>  I am speaking of the bush weta, and I don't hesitate to say the bush weta does bore into certain trees, manuka in particular. <i>(May 15, 1933,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> There are four kinds: the bush-weta, the cave-weta .. the rock-weta and the ground-weta ... <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,452</i>)

>  <b>PEAT</b> I have seen a Rock and Pillar weta, a very dead one, in Otago Museum. In general form it is similar to the large bush weta. <i>(1992,Falcon and the Lark, The,35</i>)

### bush wether
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wether grazed in bush, on bushburn or in rough country.

>  100 2-tooth ewes from up-river, 40 18 month steers, off rough country, 300 forward bush wethers ... <i>(Jan 31, 1905,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  Livestock sales: two and four tooth bush wethers. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,409</i>)

### bush whare
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small bush worker's hut located in native bush or forest.

>  He [Joe Green] had lived most of his life at Kawhia in a bush whare making a living from his one or two cows, vege garden and fishing. <i>(Oct 1980,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,50/86</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> The three young men lived in a bush whare and by 1919 had 600 acres cut, fenced and in grass. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,140</i>)

### bush whisky
 
 <i>n.</i> illicit, home-made, whisky; hokonui.

>  Quite a lot of people do not seem to like hare. They seem to think it is like bush whisky: "the flavour is pretty strong". <i>(May 17, 1899,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  Bush whisky makes the boozer think the world is upside down. After three glasses of a brand retailed by a down South publican ... <i>(Apr 7, 1906,NZ Truth,42/1/3</i>)

>  <b>CLARK</b> ... vile bush whiskey. <i>(1918,My Erratic Pal,45</i>)

### bush wind
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME</b> In the city,I won't hear the sea, the bush-wind or the birds. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,120</i>)

### bush work
 
 <i>n.</i> the work done (by a bushman) in the bush

>  <b>MARSHMAN</b> There are other kinds of unskilled labour going on as road-making, fencing, bush-work etc. <i>(1864,Canterbury, NZ, in 1862,48</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> Sledges were necessary for bush work, for the bush roads were too narrow and too full of stumps for the dray to be worked in them. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,238</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> It was his first acquaintance with our [sc. missionary] bush-work ... [sc. travelling] <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,316</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Desirous that no delay should occur with the surveys, Colonel Wakefield ... had despatched the <i>Carbon</i> ... having on board Messrs. Richard Nicholson and Albert Allom, who were surveying cadets, and seven men selected for their knowledge of bush work. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,59</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> They couldn't walk in the bush and carry a rifle, far less fight in the bush. The practised bushmen among us, like myself - I had done pioneering bush-work before the Waikato war began - had grown to look on a tree as a friend. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/210,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> 1850 - as bush work proved too heavy the father returned to Waikouaiti ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,45</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> And when everything was sorted out, the Old Man came out of that contract without a zak to show for it. ... at the end of seven months' bushwork we didn't have a brass razoo.<i>Ibid.</i> 76... months of bushwork, with hardly a cracker coming from it, made me carry a swag. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,68</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

### bush worker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The higher wages paid to bush workers are accounted for partly by the more exacting nature of the work, longer hours, remoteness and consequent lack of social intercourse, and the less favourable living conditions. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,590</i>)

>  Did you ever hear a New Zealand bushworker talk like that? <i>(Jan 10, 1910,Triad, The,43</i>)

### bush workings
 
 <i>n.</i> an area where bush is being, or has been, worked or logged for timber.

>  ... a fire ran through the old bush-workings ... BUSH <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/412</i>) [NOTES: (Bagnall)]

### bush wren
 
 <i>n.</i> a small bird; Acanthisitta chloris or rifleman, and Xenicus longipes or matuhi.

>  <b>BULLER</b> XENICUS LONGPIPES. (BUSH-WREN.) ... <i> Native names </i>. Matukituki, Piwauwau, {Puano,} and Huru-pounamu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,115,{1888 2nd edn 1/108} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Bush-wren [<i> Xenicus longpipes </i>] ... is generally met with singly or in pairs. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand</i>) [NOTES: ALSO vol. i. p. 108: A full description].]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i> Bush-wren </i>. see <i> Wren </i>. <i>(1898,Austral English,72</i>)

>  <i> Wren n. [Add.] Bush wren. (Bot.),</i> a passerine bird of New Zealand <i> (Xenicus longipes). </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2034</i>)

>  Amongs[t?] the creepers there are the bush-wren, the rock-wren, the rifleman and the yellow-headed native canary. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,195</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> Another tiny bird is the Bush Wren; this, on the contrary, has hardly any tail at all, ... <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,70</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i> Houtuutu,</i> n. <i> Acanthidositta chloris, bush wren. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,75</i>)

>  Fulton (1908) [TrNZI 40/485-500] states that the `rifleman and bush wrens abound wherever are our native trees.' <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science,6/87</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> ... green bush wrens <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,25,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> As elsewhere, as in the case of the Saddleback <i> (Creadion carunculatus)</i> and Bush Wren <i> (Xenicus longpipes) </i>... restriction of food ... may have reduced an originally large clutch. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,74</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>(Xenicus)</i> were hopping around, ... <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,85</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i> Houtuutu </i>, n.<i> Acanthidositta chloris, rifleman </i>, a bird. <i>Ibid.</i> 195 [also green wren] <i> Matuhituhi ... Xenicus longpipes, bush wren. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,63</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> In addition to the rock wren, there is another species, <i> Xenicus longipipes </i>, the bush wren or matuhi. This bird is also very small, of darker green colouring and with a white throat. It is found in damp rimu and beech forests on the West Coast of the South Island, and builds its nest in hollow trees and, like the rock wren, lines it with feathers. <i>Ibid.</i> 3/705-12 Wren, bush .. .. matuhi .. .. <i> Xenicus longipes </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/689</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BUSH WREN <i> Xenicus longpipes Other names:</i> Matuhi, Tom Thumb Bird (Stewart Is.). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,192</i>)

>  <i> Bush Wren Xenicus longipes </i>(GMELIN, 1789) ... This tiny, inconspicuous bird may be extinct; ...<i> South Island Bush Wren X.l. longpipes </i> (GMELIN, 1789)<i> North Island Bush Wren X.l. stokesii </i> GRAY, 1862. <i> Stead's Bush Wren X.l. variabilis </i> STEAD, 1936. OTHER NAMES: <i> Matuhituhi, green wren, Tom Thumb bird. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,265</i>)

>  <i> bush wren </i> small NZ forest bird. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>MCCONOCHIE</b> On comparing observations that evening, Alex casually mentioned seeing a small colony of the large green bush wren, familiar to us in our early boyhood days at Glenhope .. they were subsequently catalogued by Dr Oliver as a hitherto unknown species, under the name of the 'Fiordland wren.' <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,119</i>)

>  <i>Xenicus longipes</i> (Bush Wren). Common some distance up the bushy slopes, where the vegetation is not so dense as near the lake [Brunner]. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/214</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> As in the case of the .. Bush Wren (Xenicus longipes) .. restriction of food .. may have reduced an originally large clutch. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,74</i>)

>  It is nevertheless a useful bird, and might be tried in New Zealand to further supplement the bush wren (Tenicus longipes) and the grey warbler (Gerygone flaviventris), both becoming scarcer. <i>(Jan 3, 1893,Auckland  Star,3</i>)

### bush yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a makeshift stockyard. (See [scrubyard](../S#scrubyard))

>  <b>MACNICOL & TROTTER</b> The sheep were kept there for the night by a bush yard, made of scrub cut and heaped up to a circular pen, it was only for one night. <i>(1987,I, Duncan from Skippers,88</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> We built temporary bush yards and killed and skinned the lot [scabby sheep]. <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,81</i>)

### bush-bash
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There's one bush bash on every trip. <i>(Dec 3, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C12</i>)

>  ..with one possible 'bush-bash' above Bristed Stream, where the river runs hard against the bank. <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,208</i>)

>  Once I reached the river the bush-bash should have been over. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to tramp off the formed track through the bush

>  I knew he'd been joking, but when we bush-bashed our way through the forest to the possies on the river's edge, I received the answer I'd been looking for... <i>(Oct 30, 2004,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

>  <b>MARRINER</b> At a certain bemd, if you bush-bash across the northerly ridge, you hit farmland. <i>(2001,Stonedogs,250</i>)

>  The ridge walk had become impassable and they had to bush-bash through the leatherwood on the tops. <i>(Jan 9, 1994,Sunday Times (NZ),19</i>)

>  But half the track had been washed away so we decided to `bush bash' to get to Cone Ridge. I was last in the party. We were cutting a trail into the bush. <i>(Dec 6, 1967,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  ...Crockett's team bush-bashed through hectares of rugged terrain searching for signs that the [Chatham Island] taiko must still be alive... <i>(Aug 2009,Forest & Bird,333/22</i>)

>  Toward the end these paddlers did decide to walk a rapid or two..we bush-bashed up the bank to the bottom bridge... <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.huttkayakers.org.nz/tripIndex.php *Accessed 17/04/06. No date for site construction or posting.]

>  <b>PICKERING</b> So to cope with the rituals of tramping, its pains and camaraderie, a slang language has developed to suit. Lots of quick short words to fit in the breathless pauses as you pant uphill. `Crud' (cloud), `bush-bash', a `gunn' tramper (which must have originated from Davy Gunn's famous trek up the Hollyford Valley to help victims of a plane crash in 1936; he travelled 40 km by rowing boat, horse and foot in 21 hours), `windfall' (a collapsed pile of trees), `pit' (the tramper's true friend, a sleeping bag), `pog' (porridge), `bastard grass' and `lawyer' (two unpleasant plants), `piker' (one who stayed home), `Huey' (all-powerful god of weather), and many, many more. <i>(1988,Hills, The,8</i>)

>  All three of these intrepid suburban pupil-carriers can bush-bash with the best of them and I have to admit that I did get even the Lexus very dirty. <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Dominion Post,H1</i>)

>  They teetered on platforms, loafed in Gucci, bushbashed through Burger King in oversized camel Timberlands. <i>(Sep 22, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  The trail plan called for me to bush-bash a few kilometres across the flanks of Mount Kaihinu... <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

### bush-bashed
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  I bush-bashed down to the river but found no trail, only kilometres of bush and precipitous river spurs between me and my destination. <i>(Aug 15, 1998,Dominion,21</i>)

>  I bush-bashed down to the river but found no trail, only kilometres of bush and precipitous river spurs between me and my destination. <i>(Aug 15, 1998,Dominion,21</i>)

### bush-basher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Bush-bashing comes easily in Suzuki's dinky little wagon [heading] ... It has always been a source of amazement to true-blue four wheel drive fanatics, just how many people use the little Suzuki off roader as a round-town hack. Obstensibly designed as a mini bush-basher, the Suzuki has nevertheless proved to be a winner among the rich and trendy people from places such as Parnell, Herne Bay or Eastbourne. <i>(Apr 28, 1985,NZ Times,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The 4WD is no longer just a bush basher. These days they're more likely to be shuttling around town with a woman at the wheel. <i>(Mar 24, 1999,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  Bush-bashers go navigating, with compasses and maps and call each other on radios (except Dave who'll call his mum) to find targets or bush-blobbers. <i>(Mar 29, 1993,Salient,25</i>)

### bush-bashing
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  We had the alternatives of facing a wet crossing but then having a good track up the other bank, or staying on our own side and perhaps getting involved in a bush-bashing session. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,39</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> travelling (with difficulty) through the bush

>  Almost every car maker has a 4x4 machine which will go bush-bashing to a wilder or milder extent depending on how capacious your wallet happens to be. <i>(Aug 27, 2003,Norwester,12</i>)

>  Some of the proposed links weren't in place, so that meant an occasional bit of bush-bashing. <i>(Aug 15, 1998,Dominion,21</i>)

>  Beyond here is only a marked route requiring some bush-bashing and boulder-hopping beside the river, with bluffs to bypass beyond Kakapo Creek. <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,119</i>)

>  The following day our bush-bashing commenced. ... A good deer track came to an abrupt end at a cliff face ... It was a case of `up and over'. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,43</i>)

>  Bush-bashing comes easily in Suzuki's dinky little wagon [heading] ... It has always been a source of amazement to true-blue four wheel drive fanatics, just how many people use the little Suzuki off roader as a round-town hack. Obstensibly designed as a mini bush-basher, the Suzuki has nevertheless proved to be a winner among the rich and trendy people from places such as Parnell, Herne Bay or Eastbourne. <i>(Apr 28, 1985,NZ Times,29</i>)

>  Yesterday morning, they were lifted out from the Totara Flat hut by helicopter, after losing their way, going in the wrong direction, bush-bashing till 3am on Saturday morning, ... <i>(Feb 2, 1981,Dominion,3</i>)

>  ...who'll buy the car? ...those who've bought a heavy duty off-roader with fond dreams of bush-bashing then find they don't so opt for something that handles both their on road duties and ski-field antics. <i>(Apr 20, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B11</i>)

>  It was bush bashing, blizzards, just horrific stuff. Just a weekend out with the boys. <i>(Nov 5, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B20</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> The remaining two hours to Grey's Hut.. is more strenuous and requires some bush- bashing and boulder-hopping. <i>(1991,NW Nelson Tramping Guide,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> metaphorically or physically breaking new ground

>  Western Southland Cretaceous-Cenozoic Project is nearly through the bushbashing stage and synthesis of data has commenced. <i>(Feb 1984,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,63/23</i>)

### bush-blanketed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  His mother, Dawn, soon shifted to the Waitakeres, the bush-blanketed hills west of Auckland, where she became part of an avant-garde alternative community. <i>(Mar 2004,North & South,71</i>)

### bush-bled
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of gum) bled from the living kauri tree (See [cf. gum-bleeding](gra-gyp.md#gum-bleeding),[tap n](t-ten.md#tap))

>  <b>REED</b> This harvest of the living [kauri] tree is called `bush-bled gum'. <i>(1948,Gumdigger, The,78</i>)

### bush-blobber
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Bush-bashers go navigating, with compasses and maps and call each other on radios (except Dave who'll call his mum) to find targets or bush-blobbers. <i>(Mar 29, 1993,Salient,25</i>)

### bush-bordered
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The tourists ... never see the wood-chopping at the bush-bordered showgrounds at Tuatapere ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,227</i>)

### bush-bound
 
 <i>adj.</i> bounded (`fenced in') by bush; surrounded or enclosed by bush

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... I had previously shot two or three on every clearing while travelling up the bush-bound valleys ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,100</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> . . . they had also been leasing Baton Tops a block of high rugged bushbound country between the Baton headworks and a branch of the Karamea. ...<i>Ibid.</i> 28 These [ewes] could not be let go in the Red Hills block with safety [because of lack of fences] so he put them out on some bush-bound tops away in behind the Mataki country; . . . <i>(1973,Big Country,12,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... I had a go at the wild cattle at Lake Coleridge. These cattle, all cleanskins, were mainly in the bush-bound Harper country, ... <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,74</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Poulter and Riversdale country is practically all bushbound and, with those tracts of matagouri as well, it is difficult to get clean musters. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,120</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of grazing or hunting land] surrounded by bush.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> It's [summer country] completely bush-bound. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,33</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> These cattle, all cleanskins, were mainly in the bush-bound Harper Valley. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,74</i>)

### bush-bound country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) pastoral land surrounded by bush.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It is bushbound country, and although it is a real death trap in the winter, could summer a tremendous number of sheep .. It is completely bushbound and the only access is up Andrews creek itself.. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,140</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Cora Lynn tops, bush-bound country with big open basins, was great going, and the mustering simplicity itself. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,148</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The bushbound country beyond the run boundaries and the headworks of the Ant and Esk, were also a great possie for wild cattle. [bush-bound] <i>(1952,High Country Journey,146</i>)

### bush-bowered
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> The canoe came from the mouth of a bush-bowered river, and turned ... toward the cliff-set <i>pa</i> where the watchers stood. <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,30</i>)

### bush-bred
 
 <i>adj.</i> reared in the bush

>  His mare was one of those wiry bush-bred ones. <i>(1896,Tom Bracken's Annual,39</i>)

>  ... the long grey moss which hangs in festoons and beards from some of the trees in the heavy bush [especially in mountain country in the North Island]. One kind of the moss ... is almost white and very conspicuous in the gloom of the bush. The settlers' name for it in my part of the country is `new-chum's pigeon'. It is so called because of the imagined resemblance of the mossy bunches to the white breast of the wild pigeon; ... I'm hanged if it would deceive any New Zealander country-born or bush-bred. <i>(May 10, 1920,Quick March,39</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> There were three stalwart brothers ...men of bush-bred strength and hardihood. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,225</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The musterers were descendants of whaler and Maori and were mounted on tough, bush-bred ponies, ... <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,43</i>)

### bush-browned
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> An equal distance farther back was McMurray's Creek, the bush-browned waters of which idled along ... parallel with the more rapid and clear river flow, ... <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,63</i>)

### bush-burning
 
 <i>n.</i> the process of bringing land into cultivation by clearing bush or logs by fire (See [cf. bush-firing](bri-byo.md#bush-firing))

>  <b>BELL</b> The season promised to be a good one for bush-burning; and those who had bush-felled were congratulating themselves on the prospect of getting excellent `burns,' while many, who, ... had been unfortunate in getting bad `burns,' were now hopeful of clearing off much of the timber by means of a second fire. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,198</i>)

>  [the reference is to sowing blocks with grass seed] I hadn't heard the term `bush-burning' for ages and I thought it a thing of the past. <i>(Dec 22, 1980,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: (David Buxton) 2YA Farm Programme 12.50 p.m.]

### bush-burnt
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  It is more than likely that much of the damage caused to grass in recently bush-burnt country ... will be found to be due to the little green cockchafer. <i>(Dec 16, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,585</i>)

### bush-canopied
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Trek in beech forest leaves lasting memory of bush-canopied trails <i>(Jul 30, 1987,Dominion Sunday Times,24</i>)

### bush-carpentered
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> By eye-metry the house measures 10 ft. by 20 ft.; the walls and roof are of split palings, bush-carpentered, but storm excluding. ... A slab chimney, if put there for ornament, is a mistake, but otherwise the blend is perfection. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,296</i>)

### bush-clad
 
 <i>adj.</i> covered with bush A1; =bushed, =bushy aj.

>  ... buttressed by spurs and terraces, forest-clad, lit up by the evening sun, ...] <i>(Sep 1, 1857,Letter</i>) [NOTES: [1857 Sep 1 Harper Letters from Canterbury (1914) 19]

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> It dates back many years to when the whole district was bush-clad ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,158</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> ... on the horizon were ramparts of bush-clad hills. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,43</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> I roamed in the bush-clad hills. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,161,1949 ed.</i>)

>  .. a good deal of the country at present bush-clad was comparatively bare. <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/454</i>) [NOTES: (Mulgan)]

>  <b>McINTOSH</b> About two-thirds of this consists of bush-clad hills and narrow valleys in the Sounds ... <i>(1940,Marlborough,289</i>) [NOTES: [c1890's, Report of Commissioner of Crown Lands] in 1940 McIntosh Marlborough 289]

>  <b>BARkER</b> ... miles of bush-clad mountain ... <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,2</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> For miles above Maraetai the steep, bush-clad hills come down almost to the left bank of the river, ... <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,112</i>)

>  Even the hardy miner, who in his search after gold has penetrated the dark bush-clad and rugged ranges of the west coast of this Island ... has avoided this wilderness, ... <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/350</i>) [NOTES: (Park)]

>  <i>(1928,TrNZI,58/412</i>) [NOTES: (Speight)]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Herds of wild cattle were encountered in the rugged bush-clad areas, which was something they had not reckoned on, and resulted in their having the wind put up them ... <i>Ibid. </i>143 ...; [they] set off along the rugged, gorge intersected and bush-clad country at the foot of the Ruahines, ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,17</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> creeks flowed into the sea on either side of the headland. <i>Ibid. </i>141 Incidentally, Leisure Hour was a beautiful bush clad gorge not far from Weber, ... <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,17</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> In the first winter snows of 1863 two men ... descended through a long line of difficult bush-clad gorges to the West Coast. <i>(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,9</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> ... Sam regarded the high opposite bank of the river. It was bush clad. <i>(1979,Pallet on the Floor,139</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> ... the basic pattern of military operations in the Northern War was a series of British forays into the hilly and bush-clad interior. <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,29</i>)

>  Laing calls her Titirangi home `my saviour' and ... guards her privacy in their bush-clad house. ... It is a cosy ... home on a large bush section. [as a journalist cliche gone astray] <i>(Oct 29, 1989,Sunday Magazine (Auckland),8</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> The rugged panorama, still bushclad in places, is cradled in an extensive mountain basin of great charm. <i>(1971,Moonlight,6</i>)

>  We also have <i>bush burns,</i> areas where the bush has been recently burnt off, and hills are <i>bush-clad.</i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,24</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Sep Ruth Mason `Track and Trail' in Tararua 24]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> This heavy bush-clad country was a completely new experience for me, as most of my previous shooting had been done on more open tussocky country. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,42</i>)

>  The saddle is about one mile wide ... with a flat bushclad floor, ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,45</i>)

>  <b>GORE</b> A light southerly ruffled the waters of Cook Strait and stirred into restless eddies a whispy mist, parting it occasionally to reveal glimpses of bush-clad hills guarding the entrance to Port Nicholson. <i>(1956,Levins,1</i>)

>  That leaps with laughter from a wave I know,/ Higher to the bush-clad hills above the bay. <i>(May 25, 1942,2NZEF Times,9</i>) [NOTES: 1942 May 25 NZEF Times 9 in 1979 Cleveland Iron Hand 23]

>  [usually <i>forest-clad </i> in this publication] ... the balance, 1,898,776 acres, comprising the ordinary bush-clad country, much of which is quite inaccessible to stock. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,22</i>)

>  [usually <i>forest-clad </i> in this publication] ... the balance, 1,898,776 acres, comprising the ordinary bush-clad country, much of which is quite inaccessible to stock. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,22</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> Low scrub-covered hills walled it in ; beyond rose great bush-clad ranges, sharply outlined against the silvery sky. <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,5</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> The mere protection of a few thousand acres of bush-clad hills will no more save for posterity the native fauna of a country than will a National Park in America preserve herds of bison from extinction. <i>(1909,Murihiku,16</i>)

>  ... in those low ridges and bush-clad hills roamed herds of red deer and thousands of `Captain Cookers'. <i>(Sep 10, 1921,Quick March,23</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> ... it was lost in the bush-clad lower slopes of the gullies below. <i>(1927,Follow the Call,111,1975 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land and landforms] covered in native bush.

>  <b>WHYTE</b> But even with this cutting up of country, we still have the irregular contours, the rugged hills and the bush-clad slopes to contend with ... <i>(1927,Sheep-Dog, The,114</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> .. most mountaineers prefer to climb from huts, and shun bush-clad gorges when there are more certain ridges and snow basins on the Otago side. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,144</i>)

>  <b>JANES</b> There are big areas of fern, second growth and heavy bush-clad country. <i>(1994,Great New Zealand Hunting Stories,51</i>)

### bush-clerking
 
 <i>n.</i> the occupation of bush-clerk

>  <b>GIBSON</b> Bush-clerking [c1890] requires but little brains. <i>(1924,Gentleman's Magellan,71</i>)

### bush-climber
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A bush-climber; apparently rare. <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/36</i>)

### bush-clothed
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [cf. bush-clad](bri-byo.md#bush-clad))

>  <b>HAY</b> So we see as much of the place as we can, ... the bush-clothed heights upon the further shore. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/280</i>)

### bush-covered
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [cf. bush-clad](bri-byo.md#bush-clad),[bushy](bri-byo.md#bushy))

>  <b>POWER</b> On two sides it was nearly perpendicular bush-covered mountain; and in front was the rising ... crest of the hill, densely covered with forest, ... <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,19, 24</i>)

>  <b>ST JOHN</b> The dark bush-covered hills of the Hunua ... <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands,5/67</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Picture the rough grassy slopes, ... the background of bush-covered heights, ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/322</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> At their base, bush-covered hills, down whose sides shower countless cascades ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,152</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Jan 31 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 152]

>  [They] form the high bush-covered ranges on the east side of the river-valley. <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/361</i>)

>  Looking to the right is seen perhaps the most extended stretch of hill-country in the whole of New Zealand. Rounded hills, bush-covered for the most part, extend as far as one can see [on the East Coast]. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/513</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> You draw rein at the top of the mountains & round you everywhere are other mountains - bush covered <i>(Nov 20, 1907,Letters,1/31,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> So on their way back they again sailed into the beautiful, bush-covered bay ... <i>(1930,NZ Memories,87</i>)

>  The little beaches ... bush-covered all the way. <i>(1945,Centennial Treasury of Otago Verse,71</i>) [NOTES: 1945 D.W.C.Jones Diversions of a Professor in Centennial Treasury of Otago Verse 71]

>  <b>McDONALD</b> ... `There ye are now!' and pointed to the cottage at the edge of the bush-covered rise. <i>(1954,Stinson's Bush,56</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> Furthermore, ... these mammoth birds wandered in flocks through the bush-covered foothills of the Southern Alps, ... <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,116</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> The bush-covered hills slide past ... <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,121</i>)

>  The counties which constitute the lowest group [of fenced land] were to be found primarily in the bush-covered or formerly bush-covered areas of the western portions of both islands. <i>(Oct 1965,NZ Geographer,154</i>) [NOTES: (Hargreaves `Farm Fences')]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He feels for them as he roves through bushcovered hillside and gully, ... <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,44</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Bob was obviously on the far side of a bush-covered rise ... no more than sixty yards away. <i>(1984,Razorback,85</i>)

>  The banded kokopu is another whitebait species reaching 26 cm. Its habitat is a pool in a small bush-covered stream which may be a considerable [73] altitude and distance from the sea. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,72</i>)

>  On their arrival in 1866 the Schischkas had to battle not only with dense bush-covered land, but a steep access and an often flooded creek. <i>(Mar 1, 1986,NZ Herald,1</i>)

### bush-crash
 
 <i>v.</i> bush-bash

>  Leaderless, the five raced and bush-crashed up the bank of the Wartangau River to the Waikato hut site. <i>(Jul 2006,Footprints,24/2</i>)

>  <i>(1952,Tararua,68</i>) [NOTES: (R. Mason)]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> We followed a faint track as far as it went, then we [33] just bush-crashed. We explored gullies and clambered along ridges. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,32</i>)

### bush-crashing
 
 <i>n.</i> bush-bashing; making a way through thick bush without a track.

>  However, a spot of bush-crashing remedies this and we are down to oggling at the views from Don McLean's summit. <i>(May 1967,Footprints,24/1</i>)

>  By late afternoon, after stream-bashing and ridge-climbing with a bit of bush crashing, [sic] we safely traversed a formidable barrier in the form of a somewhat shaky wire fence and reached a clearing ... <i>(May 1968,Footprints,25/1</i>)

### bush-crowned
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>REED</b> Over to the north-east, ... can be seen a bush-crowned hill, the site of a famous pa, Ruapekapeka, ... <i>(1946,Farthest North,15</i>)

>  <b>SUMMERS</b> ... honeysuckle ... wafted its scent to me and over in the low bush-crowned hills some bird or other seemed to pluck its breast two or three times <i>(1978,Islands,21/239</i>)

### bush-cutter
 
 <i>n.</i> ?=bush-faller

>  <b>BAKER</b> New Zealand originals include BUSH-CUTTER and BUSH-CUTTING (both used before 1863) <i>(1941,NZ Slang,15</i>)

### bush-cutting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (in cit.) ?track-cutting, cutting a path through bush.

>  <b>MUELLER</b> The work consists chiefly of bush-cutting, which certainly is as bad as can be, owing to the many creeping plants, supplejacks, &c., growing in these old forests. <i>(Oct 10, 1865,My Dear Bannie,56,1958 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?=bush-falling

>  <b>BAKER</b> New Zealand originals include BUSH-CUTTER and BUSH-CUTTING (both used before 1863) <i>(1941,NZ Slang,15</i>)

### bush-dark
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COLE</b> He stood for an instant surveying the bungalows, their blistered white paint reflecting the hard sunlight, and in the background the purple bush-dark hills. <i>(1949,It Was So Late,32,1978 ed.</i>)

### bush-draped
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  [headline] Barry's big day had 'em bushed! Being flipped about in a four-wheel drive ... high above bush-draped ravine [sic] made things just authentic enough for `townies' to think they'd gone native. <i>(Nov 16, 1986,Sunday News,5</i>)

### bush-dweller
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Anyway, whoever heard of a bush-dweller going home with an unblown cheque in his pocket. <i>(Jan 10, 1920,Quick March,42</i>)

### bush-fashion
 
 <i>adj.</i> roughly (as in the bush (See [cf. bush-style](bri-byo.md#bush-style))

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> The ducks were roasted, bush fashion; . <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,30</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> <i>Bush fashion.</i> [page heading] ... He [the Northland sawmiller] wore what is called a `blue shirt', that is, a sailor's shirt of serge, fitting very closely, and a pair of moleskin trousers, very much torn ... from walking through harsh fern and `tea-tree' scrub, ... and a wideawake hat ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,39</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> For a traveller who intends to live bush-fashion, three natives, for bearers, are a sufficient complement. <i>(1853,Auckland,125</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> For he had so allowed his hair to encroach upon his face in the customary bush fashion, that the more indicative features ... were entirely concealed from observation. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,2/ii/53,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> We travelled in the usual bush fashion, each man with his swags strapped before and behind his saddle ... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,55</i>)

### bush-fed
 
 <i>adj.</i> of cattle, allowed to feed on leaves and other green-feed from the bush A1 (See [cf. bush-feed](bri-byo.md#bush-feed))

>  <b>HAY</b> But the beef is of first-rate quality; and as bush-fed beasts are in good condition at the end of the dry season, when pasture-raised cattle are poor, we do as well by them as could be desired. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/207</i>)

### bush-fell
 
 <i>v.</i> fell; =fall (bush a1).

>  <b>BELL</b> The season promised to be a good one for bush-burning; and those who had bush-felled were congratulating themselves on the prospect of getting excellent `burns,' while many, who, ... had been unfortunate in getting bad `burns,' were now hopeful of clearing off much of the timber by means of a second fire. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,198</i>)

### bush-free
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] carrying no native forest or shrubbery.

>  Practically the whole of the 100 acres of bush-free country on the place had been infested with blackberry and gorse, with some fern ... <i>(Nov 21, 1927,NZ Journal of Agriculture,295</i>)

### bush-fringed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>11</b> Two communities at the end of five kilometres of bush-fringed gravel road in Golden Bay are fed up with raids on their quiet valley by police squads searching for cannabis. <i>(Dec 10, 1985,Dominion</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Whether it were through the bush-fringed Christchurch road or along the straight stretches of road that led from Stafford and the goldfields of Goldsborough to Kumara when old Fanny pulled the family buggy, those drives were a joy .. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,30</i>)

### bush-happy
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of bush workers and hunters] oppressed by isolation.

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> No wonder [the Wild Life Division's] cullers got bush-happy and once every month or so had to be carried off down to Poronui for a long weekend bombing up the sika [deer]. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,42</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> The season had been bad - too much rain, too few possums and a 'mate' who to use his expression was going 'bush-happy.' <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,22</i>)

### bush-hiking
 
 <i>n.</i> bush-walking, tramping. [coinage from Eng. `hike' - NZ idiom now usu. tramp, but `bush-walking' was earlier used.]

>  [At Milford Sound] we were rewarded by some `bush-hiking' for quite a long stretch. ... It was a mixture of boulder-scrambling and `bush-hiking' interspersed with a few shingle-flats. <i>(1937,NZ Alpine Journal,7/24/57</i>)

### bush-like
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> ... the prevailing species of trees are remo [rimu], totara, and tall manuka, all indicative of inferior soil.... The bush-like woods on the east side are of white birch, likewise no recommendation of the soil, but the chief objection to the district is, that the prairie soil affords scarcely any food for cattle ... it grows only the wiry tree grass [tussock] ... and <i>Phormium tenax</i> [flax], no aniseed, perennial groundsel, or milk thistle, and very little couch grass. <i>(Aug 16, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Aug 16 Tuckett's Diary in 1898 Hocken contributions 223-224]

### bush-raised
 
 <i>adj.</i> Sc. fed on leafage, karaka etc

>  <b>HAY</b> But the pasture-fed cattle of the South are not in prime condition ... during the dry season. Our bush-raised beasts are, and this gives us a pull. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/200</i>)

### bush-roughing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the traversing of bush-covered land.

>  <b>MONEY</b> They had neither of them seen much of real bush-roughing before, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,81</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> They had neither of them seen much of real bush-roughing before ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,81</i>)

### bush-scented
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The air is clean again, the bush-scented air of the Coast. <i>(1980,Canterbury Tale, A,152</i>)

### bush-scouring
 
 <i>n.</i> [1879 citation is of verb to scour (no entry)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> ... Captain Jackson's Forest Rangers ... The Rangers had most perilous duty to perform. If the troops advanced, the Rangers scoured the country in front. <i>(1879,Waikato War,72</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> At its fullest, the `bush-scouring' theory entailed a `flying column' of a few hundred men, untrammeled by a large supply train,hunting down the Maoris in the bush, and forcing them to fight by attacking their villages and cultivations. ... [214] Bush-scouring was not a panacea for all circumstances, based on the inherent abilities of settler-frontiersmen, but a military method appropriate only in a certain context ... One assumption behind the bush- scouring theory was that the Maoris were evasive guerilla fighters ... [215] Masking the <i>pa</i>, and bush-scouring against the Maori base areas ... would be a long and slow process. <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,213</i>)

>  The scorched earth policy of stripping the land of Maori houses and crops that had been applied since the beginning of the war in 1863 was supplemented by 'bush-scouring'. This startegy aimed to take the fight to the Maori in the bush. <i>(1996,Waitangi Tribunal Report,143 : 97</i>)

### bush-scouting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Memories of old Ben Biddle and Donald Sutherland ... telling the tales of their bush scouting.<i>Ibid.</i> 223In his crippled old age he was cheered now and again by a visit from some old friend of his bush-scouting, bush-clearing, bullocking days when one could march all day with a seventy-pound swag ... <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,x</i>)

### bush-scrubber
 
 <i>n.</i> [1896. Modern. Up country manservant on seeing his new mistress: -->"My word! a real lady! she's no bush-scrubber!"]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bush-scrubber</i>, ... a bushman's word for a boor, bumpkin, or slattenly<i>person. See</i> Scrubber. <i>(1898,Austral English,72</i>)

### bush-shacking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The story belongs to an earlier day when after two and a half years ranging on or about the Northland gumfields, scrub-cutting, bush-shacking and farm-labouring, I decided to follow my star south. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,111,1963 ed.</i>)

### bush-shadowed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COLE</b> Here the Public Works gangs still use the pick and shovel. The rattle of their implements, and the occasional echoing wham of a charge of gelly, and the deepening engine note of a passing vehicle as it changes into low gear on the ... slippery bush-shadowed bends, seem only to nibble at the vast silence. <i>(1949,It Was So Late,63,1978 ed.</i>)

### bush-shooting
 
 <i>n.</i> shooting in the bush A1 (as distinct from in the open) (See [cf. bush-stalking](bri-byo.md#bush-stalking))

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Some say the open sight is better for snap or bush shooting and the aperture more suitable for long range shooting ...<i>Ibid.</i> 44 In bush shooting our range would vary from a few yards up to seventy or eighty depending on the thickness of the undergrowth ...<i>Ibid.</i> 47 Some days you may shoot several deer while bush-shooting, and at other times you may stalk for a full day and get none... When bush-shooting, you are usually sure of the locality of the deer, as the hill slopes give you an idea of his approximate location. Some days you may shoot several deer while bush-shooting, and at other times you may stalk for a full day and get none, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 119 When bush-shooting, one has [sic] to make sure of each shot, as the deer would be gone after the first shot was fired. <i>(1952,Deer Stalker,26</i>)

### bush-shrouded
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>McDONALD</b> ... perhaps even now he was in some secluded hut drinking the potent whisky which was the secret of the bush-shrouded hills. <i>(1954,Stinson's Bush,216</i>)

### bush-shy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> What a life for them ... Could anything be lonelier, harder, less rewarding? You'd soon get bush-shy like that. <i>Ibid.</i> 242 bush-shy shy of people from being too long isolated <i>(1984,Blackball 08,74</i>)

### bush-sick
 
 <i>adj.</i> of an animal

>  Today, Tokoroa farmland is in the same category as the lush pastures of the Waikato, but back then it was still deemed to be within the boundaries of the 'bush-sick' country, deficient in cobalt where pasture was poor and livestock failed to thrive. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/39</i>)

>  The family dairy farm was on formerly bush-sick land near Putararu. <i>(Jan 2000,North & South,50</i>)

>  ... a steer ... was killed ... as being a typical bush-sick animal. <i>(1912,TrNZI,44/288</i>)

>  ... a steer ... was killed ... as being a typical bush-sick animal. <i>(1912,TrNZI,44/288</i>)

>  Development of former bush-sick areas in Tauranga county... The discovery that cobalt was a cure for bush sickness came at a most opportune moment. <i>(Jul 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,67</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land and stock] suffering a lack of cobalt.

>  Lime .. has an injurious action on the feeding-quality of pasture on 'bush-sick' lands. <i>(Aug 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,123</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The land was sick - 'bushsick,' they called it. <i>(1966,Sixty Million Muster,66</i>)

>  The amount of cobaltised fertiliser used .. would be sufficient to correct cobalt deficiency in 940,000 acres of bush-sick pasture. <i>(Jan 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,31</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Sixty Million Muster 66 Soon he noticed his bush-sick cattle crowding round the vehicle, licking the mud spattered along its sides with the eagerness of a youngster setting to work on an icecream sundae. <i>(1966,Sixty Million Muster,66</i>)

>  <b>ADDENBROOKE</b> My cousin Leo Addenbrooke had a six hundred acre farm at Lichfield in the area that was partly bush sick, and I was able to look around from there. <i>(1992,Home from the Hill,105</i>)

### bush-sick country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land lacking cobalt [that without top-dressing, will cause bush sickness in cattle and sheep.]

>  The experience gained indicates clearly that milch animals, even when young and consequently most susceptible, will retain a condition of health in bush-sickness country. <i>(Nov 20, 1915,NZ Journal of Agriculture,378</i>)

>  This will probably be superior to either constituent applied singly, especially on "bush-sick" country. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,312</i>)

>  The following analyses of grasses grown on the bush-sick country show that their content of iron may sink below even the small amounts quoted for clovers. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,84</i>)

>  .. it affords a distinguishing difference between 'bush-sick' country and 'dopy' country. [bush-sickness country.] <i>(Aug 20, 1931,NZ Journal of Agriculture,121</i>)

### bush-singleted
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He cannot be visualised as an uneloquent slang-spattered nasal bush-singleted clumping Kiwi - few of us are ... <i>(1978,Soldier Country,135</i>)

### bush-sited
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  A newspaper advertisement offers for auction a bush-sited home with an "irreplaceable Northcote beach frontage" ... <i>(May 13, 1985,National Business Review,6</i>)

### bush-stalk
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to hunt animals in bush or forest.

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The deer were numerous enough that we rarely entered the rainforest to bush-stalk animals. <i>(1998,Sea of Gold,  A,52</i>)

### bush-stalking
 
 <i>n.</i> stalking deer in the bush

>  <b>ORBELL</b> Stalking generally takes two different forms:- (1) High tops open country stalking: (2) Bush stalking. <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,50</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Most of our shooting was a fairly close range in this district, due to the bush-stalking and the many small clearings at regular intervals on the valley floor. <i>Ibid.</i> 119 Bush-stalking in the large valley between the Arm and the Waiau Rivers was actually a pleasure after climbing over rough, rocky tops for so long. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,44</i>)

### bush-surrounded
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> Potatoes, kumeras, taros, and water melons were the chief crops grown in the ... humid bush-surrounded plots ... <i>(1951,Confessions,24</i>)

### bush-trained
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> The bush-trained settlers of Papkura, Hunua, and the Wairoa were the dependable nucleus of the corps ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/268</i>)

### bush-travel
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Visitors and colonists `bush travelling' in New Zealand, generally provide themselves with a native attendant, as guide ... New Zealand `bush travelling' ... beats a Highland tour by a hundred per cent., ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/251</i>)

### bush-traveller
 
 <i>n.</i> one who travels in, or through, the bush A1.

>  Sunrise, after which no bush-traveller ought to be in bed. <i>(1847,NZ Journal,7/201/240</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> [Pigs] keeping in the deepest recesses of the forest, are seldom or ever seen except by bush travellers or pig-hunter. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/124</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> But a lengthened expedition into the interior of a new country, cannot of course be undertaken without some preparation ... Nor should a stranger, or novice in bush travelling ... be advised to start alone. On the contrary he should, if possible, secure as a companion some experienced bush traveller. <i>(1853,Auckland,123</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... [supple-jack] sometimes catches you by the neck, the most temper-provoking and the most anathema-producing thing that ever tried the temper of a bush traveller, but so useful withal in its proper places. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/3/155</i>)

### bush-travelling
 
 <i>n.</i> travelling in or through the bush (See [cf. bush-hiking](bri-byo.md#bush-hiking),[-walking](../-#-walking))

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Visitors and colonists `bush travelling' in New Zealand, generally provide themselves with a native attendant, as guide ... New Zealand `bush travelling' ... beats a Highland tour by a hundred per cent., ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/252</i>)

>  We have with us tea and sugar which are considered indispensable in bush travelling. <i>(1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/83,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richmond)]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> "Bush-Travelling in New Zealand" [chapter title] To the sportsman New Zealand offers no attraction; but, for the active and adventurous, a pedestrian excursion into the interior of the country affords an agreeable recreation; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 123 What wonder that bush-travelling, then, should be a holiday amusement? <i>(1853,Auckland,115</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Great fault is found with me for wearing one of the common felt hats which having very broad brims is well adapted for bush travelling. <i>(Apr 12, 1853,Journal,8/97</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Mr. Mason, who has been twenty years in the country and accustomed to bush travelling, borrowed a knife ...and said he would soon get me some luncheon. ...We speedily spied a young <i>Nikau</i>, the native palm tree, and cutting off the upper end he cut out the inside. . . .It tasted like sweet turnip. <i>(1971,Auckland Journals,150</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> With great regret I parted with this gentleman, who had ... been accustomed for years to New Zealand bush travelling, and possessing ... a quick eye for discerning readily the topographical features of the country. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,58</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> I found rapid bush travelling by no means easy of accomplishment. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,85</i>)

>  <b>DOUGLAS</b> ... a great deal more Bush travelling. <i>(Mar 7, 1891,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Mar 7 Douglas Diary in 1957 Mr Explorer Douglas 137]

>  ... three of them (strong and determined, and used to heavy bush-travelling) arranged to visit that out-of-the-way spot ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/88</i>)

>  The old-timer was talking of bush-trvelling and the difficulaty in rough forest country of maintaining one's direction ... <i>(Dec 10, 1921,Quick March,9</i>)

>  <b>ORBELL</b> The old pipe-line gives direct altitude and eliminates the worst of the bush travelling. <i>(1935,Handbook Arthur Pass National Park,39</i>)

### bush-type
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  KOKOPU (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>) is a sluggish native fish found ... in most "bush-type" streams throughout New Zealand. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/235</i>)

### bush-walk
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <i>bush-walk NZ & Austral.</i> go tramping or camp in bush. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### bush-walker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>bushwalker</i>: a man who walks in the backwoods or BUSH. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,20</i>)

>  Bushwalker mix-up gets police attention [headline of a party of schoolchildren lost in bush]. <i>(Dec 9, 1982,Evening Post,44</i>)

>  Rambling, hiking, wanderlust, walking and bushwalkers' clubs are foreign terms to us, and the Australian bushwalkers' clubs are some years younger than ours. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,28</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason "Track and Trail"]

>  [Hdg] Bush walkers warned. <i>(May 1, 2003,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

### bush-walking
 
 <i>n.</i> walking in or through the bush (See [cf. bush-hiking](bri-byo.md#bush-hiking),[-travelling](../-#-travelling))

>  We came soon by a little bush-walking ... to Okewi. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/18/232</i>) [NOTES: (W.D.B. Mantell)]

>  There is what we call a <i>good</i> bush road to Rata Nui but beyond it there are two miles of bush walking along what is called `a line'; a line is made by cutting the supple jacks and small shrubs with a bill hook ... [sic] time bush walking takes is very surprising to me ... [sic] <i>(Nov 11, 1853,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/133,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J. Maria Richmond)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> No paths but real bush-walking. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,179,1974 ed.</i>)

>  Private walkways now offer a bushwalking experience that combines quality food and wine with comfortable accommodation... <i>(Nov 9, 2002,Dominion Post,F12</i>)

### bush-wandering
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MONEY</b> In considering upon the compliment paid me by Mr. Money, when he asked me to write a preface to this record of rough and ready bush-wandering, I think of the `Pioneer' of that dinner party, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,vi</i>) [NOTES: 1871 Marcus Clarke in Money Knocking About in NZ vi]

### bush-working
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  One shop [at Thames] specialized in the manufacture of sawmilling and bush-working machinery, log-haulers in particular. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,26</i>)

### bushcraft
 
 <i>n.</i> skill in, or knowledge of, matters pertaining to life or survival in the bush A1.

>  <b>MONEY</b> He gave me my first lessons in bushcraft, such as a knowledge of edible herbs and roots, modes of crossing rivers, snaring birds, and many other invaluable `wrinkles.' <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,29</i>)

>  A young settler, who in addition to being a son of the soil, was well-skilled in bush-craft, assured me that he once met with a Kokako's nest ... <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/186</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> [The parrakeet] is an inhabitant of the woods, and ... its plumage is so suited to its natural surroundings ... that ... it is rarely seen, and except to the lovers of nature and bush-craft its very existence is almost unknown to the colonists. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/144</i>)

>  <b>McHUTCHESON</b> Mac proved himself an adept .. at all other wrinkles of bush-craft. <i>(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland,42</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... the white and Maori explorers experimented with such onga-onga poison antidotes as their bushcraft suggested. <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,8</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> There is still room for skill and bushcraft which is acquired only after years of experience. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,201</i>)

>  <i>Bushcraft</i> refers to those skills necessary for anyone to find his way around and to exist comfortably in bush country. It was in use [24] in Australia in 1897. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,23</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Sep Ruth Mason `Track and Trail' in Tararua 23-24]

>  It was the final weekend of my bushcraft course... It had seemed a good idea on New Year's Eve when, dancing on a barrel at a Greek restaurant, I vowed: "This year I will drink less, eat more veges, and join a bushcraft course."... So, with the jeers of friends and flatmates ringing in my ears (apparently "bushcraft" carries connotations of hairy legs, bushy beards and unsavoury hygiene), I enrolled for a bushcraft course. <i>(Apr 11, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

>  He regarded the skill and ability in bushcraft of Johnston and Crump as extremely high. <i>(Feb 5, 1970,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  <b>ORBELL</b> It is good bushcraft to find a place to build a fire and a log you can sleep under. <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,58</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> skill in, or knowledge of, matters pertaining to the occupation of bushman (bushfaller).

>  <b>REED</b> ... he had to find dependable answers to problems peculiar to kauri bushcraft ... [122] they took a pride in their work, and knew all there was to know about the bush-craft of their day - cross-cutting and jacking. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,120</i>)

### bushcrafter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I had arranged to meet two of my fellow bushcrafters for moral support as I trekked through Wellington to our departure point at the railway station. <i>(Apr 11, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

### bushed
 
 <i>adj.</i> exhausted, tired out

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> BUSHED, BE 2 to be tired out. .;`After the long drive in the heat, well, I was a bit bushed, I will admit.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],141</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bushed </i>exhausted; lost; eg `Can we take a breather? I'm bushed.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,23</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> ?lost

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... the secret of safely finding their way through densest forest ... was theirs, better than their adults they could track a `bushed' calf and return it to its proper mother. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,101</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) exhausted; doggo.

>  <b>KERR</b> I was bushed, sick of bouncing over papa and looking at sheep's arses ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,174</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> caught by nightfall, or lost in, the bush A1; exhausted; covered with bush A1 =bush-clad, bush-covered, bushy (but cf. bush in bush-face, -country) (See [also heavily-bushed](hea-hom.md#heavily-bushed),[lightly-bushed](../L#lightly-bushed))

>  She got "bushed." <i>(Mar 16, 1912,NZ Free Lance,14</i>)

>  We moved along the shores of Lakes Katherine and Sumner, the latter a very fine lake ... and bushed down to the waters [sic] edge. <i>(Apr 15, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,174,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> High, heavily bushed hills appeared to rise straight out of the water, ... <i>(1930,NZ Memories,89</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> As the months went by, I kept poking around the bushed spurs and the high, open basins shooting a few deer each day. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,116</i>)

>  From South Ohau, the track leaves the river ... before easing off to a more gentle gradient up the bushed ridge to Te Mataurai Hut. ... some ... hours later we had negotiated the last bushy knob and were descending the final slope towards the hut ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,68</i>)

>  <b>BALE</b> The following morning he found himself in a thickly bushed gully with a small stream at the bottom. <i>(1971,Maratoto Gold,106</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> The land at the junction of the Matakitaki and Buller which was not heavily bushed was lined and later surveyed in sections ... <i>(1976,Difficult Country,47</i>)

>  <b>HOOPER</b> ... the dark, bushed hills seemed to be pushing the town into the river and the sea. <i>(1981,Goat Paddock,193</i>)

>  ... when the streams came from heavily bushed country. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/468</i>) [NOTES: (Farquharson)]

>  <b>REES</b> (et passim) ... densely bushed hills. <i>(1924,Heather of the South,211</i>)

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> I was utterly `bushed', - literally `lost in the bush' ... <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,135</i>)

>  <b>COTTLE</b> Next time you get bushed stick to the spot till someone comes to find you, for you are an awful duffer in the bush. <i>(1891,Frank Melton's Luck,117</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Even the oldest bushman may find himself temporarily `bushed,' but a good man can ... generally find his way out somewhere ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,32</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> ... you had to follow down a track or run the risk of being bushed. <i>Ibid.</i> 117 Of course such a start in so thickly bushed a country was everything in the robbers' favour. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,43</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> [Wherever have you been?] `Bushed,' he answered, ... `Got into this infernal New Zealand bush of yours, and, egad, I was nearly spending the night in it, too; ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,191</i>)

>  ... late at night, having been temporarily `bushed', he wandered, footsore and gumless, into camp. <i>(Mar 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,429</i>)

>  <b>SKINNER</b> In the southern part of the island the bush was so dense that two members of Mr.Percy Smith's party, surveying the district in 1868, were `bushed' and very nearly lost their lives. <i>(1923,Morioris of the Chatham Islands, The,11</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> One night the Bishop and the Doctor were `bushed' and forced to bivouac where they were till morning. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,1938,24 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CLARK</b> If you ever get bushed, boy, follow a creek./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,92</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Frequently they had shouted out Roddy's name. ... `He must be hurt,' Ken said suddenly. `Or bushed,' said Sam. <i>(1984,Razorback,78</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> BUSHED, BE 1 To lose one's way. .in 4;`We had to turn back. We were bushed when the fog came down.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],140</i>)

>  <b>56</b> Before long he [a tramper] was fair-dinkum bushed. He had a compass ... but it was puckeroo'd. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> 'Now that,' I declared.. 'was hard. I went down into the gorges and got bushed,' 'Gosh.' One of the English tourists off the Kiwi Experience bus looked up from her <i>New Idea</i> magazine and went back to her reading. 'Golly, really,' said her companion, then went back to writing her letter home. <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,113</i>)

>  One party attempted Mt. Greenlaw, but got well "bushed." <i>(1932,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,I/47</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> "You wouldn't suppose a fellow could get lost on those Plains, would you? I was bushed here once!" " After that you'd get bushed in an empty packing case." <i>(1900,Tales of the Colonial Turf,197</i>)

>  We were.. cautious about getting "bushed" among the wire fences in the dark. <i>(Jun 17, 1924,Otago Witness,68</i>)

>  <b>ORBELL</b> [Title] Getting Bushed. <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,57</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> I was utterly `bushed', - literally `lost in the bush' ... <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,135</i>)

>  <b>COTTLE</b> Next time you get bushed stick to the spot till someone comes to find you, for you are an awful duffer in the bush. <i>(1891,Frank Melton's Luck,117</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Even the oldest bushman may find himself temporarily `bushed,' but a good man can ... generally find his way out somewhere ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,32</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> ... you had to follow down a track or run the risk of being bushed. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,43</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> [Wherever have you been?] `Bushed,' he answered, ... `Got into this infernal New Zealand bush of yours, and, egad, I was nearly spending the night in it, too; ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,191</i>)

>  ... late at night, having been temporarily `bushed', he wandered, footsore and gumless, into camp. <i>(Mar 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,429</i>)

>  <b>SKINNER</b> In the southern part of the island the bush was so dense that two members of Mr.Percy Smith's party, surveying the district in 1868, were `bushed' and very nearly lost their lives. <i>(1923,Morioris of the Chatham Islands, The,11</i>)
