>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> One night the Bishop and the Doctor were `bushed' and forced to bivouac where they were till morning. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,24</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> If you ever get bushed, boy, follow a creek./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,92</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Frequently they had shouted out Roddy's name. ... `He must be hurt,' Ken said suddenly. `Or bushed,' said Sam. <i>(1984,Razorback,78</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> BUSHED, BE 1 To lose one's way. .in 4;`We had to turn back. We were bushed when the fog came down.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],140</i>)

>  Before long he [a tramper] was fair-dinkum bushed. He had a compass ... but it was puckeroo'd. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... the secret of safely finding their way through densest forest ... was theirs, better than their adults they could track a `bushed' calf and return it to its proper mother. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,101</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> beaten (at sports, games, etc.)

>  Second-half burst leaves Bay [Hawkes Bay rugby team] bushed. <i>(Sep 11, 1973,Dominion,18</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land and landforms] covered with native forest and shrubs.

>  To make one's way through the heavily bushed country towards the lake will take at least two days. <i>(Aug 13, 1904,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We struggled out of the basin on to the bushed ridge ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,166</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> .. they left the bullock at Blairlogie, and set off across lightly-bushed country ... <i>(1875,BLANK ENTRY,50</i>) [NOTES: in Cresswell (1949) Early New Zealand Families]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> As the months went by, I kept poking around the bushed spurs and the high, open basins ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,116</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Apparently the beasts were making towards the bushed faces. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,162</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> An inland journey to the bushed mountain lake of Waikaremoana and long coastal walks ... <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,37</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> (extended use) lost (anywhere) (possibly occas. bush C4, cf. bluffed a.).

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> I live in a country where drought is unknown,/ Where the poor stockman, bushed, need be never athirst. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,45</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> I was bushed here [on the Plains] once. ...[202] ... Geting bushed among the tussocks on the Canterbury Plains. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,197</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Bushed</i> Lost (of people) whether in the bush or anywhere else. (A taxi-driver got `bushed', he said, in finding a street) <i>(1933,Sheep-station Glossary</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> `Have you any idea where we are?' `We're bushed behind the enemy lines about a hundred miles from nowhere.' <i>(1944,Troop Target,45</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> `You've got me bluffed properly,' I said. `I'm properly bushed.' Each tree, each fallen log, was the same as the last to me, but gradually I became aware of a slight thinning of the bush, ... <i>(1963,In the Sticks,64</i>)

>  <i>bushed ... 3. Austral & N.Z</i>. lost or bewildered, as in the bush. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,147</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 96; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 64]]

>  [Hdg] Barry's big day had 'em bushed! Being flipped about in a four-wheel drive ... high above bush-draped ravine [sic] made things just authentic enough for `townies' to think they'd gone native. <i>(Nov 16, 1986,Sunday News,5</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i> confused; ?distraught.

>  [A prisoner speaks] Something seemed to move behind him; he jumped. Nothing. Legs trembling slightly he slid quickly down the ladder, slamming the bolt on the door. `Now, don't go getting bushed', he said. <i>(1964,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51/1365 p.5</i>) [NOTES: [check ref. from main MS]??]



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i> baffled, stumped (by a question, etc) [ca1950 Wellington That question completely bushed me.] (See [cf. also bluffed](bla-bri.md#bluffed))

>  Bushed Lost or astray anywhere. One who loses his way among city streets will say he is bushed. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),55</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> BUSHED, BE 3 To have no information on that matter, or, to be puzzled. .in 4;`I understand what you meant up to a point, but now I'm bushed. You'd better explain it all from the start. I'll get the gist of it, I just have to take my time.' W3 <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],142</i>)

>  <b>LAWSON</b> The deeper you read ... about things that end in ism ... the more likely you are to get bushed. <i>(1900,Over Sliprails</i>) [NOTES: OEDS]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bushed quasi past participle</i>, lost in the bush; then, lost or at a loss. 1861. T. McCombie, `Australian Sketches,' p. 115:] <i>(1898,Austral English,72</i>)

### bushfaller
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAYTON</b> I watched the noble game of `Euchre' being played by some bush-fallers and Scandinavians. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,48</i>)

>  <b>BROOKES</b> Immigrants ... were not long in becoming ... good axemen and bush-fallers. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,11</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> `Then, Maurice,' said Ashwin, `sing us that bushfaller's song of Flash Harry's. ...' <i>Ibid.</i> 48 `... You're getting some more bush down on that section of yours in the Arataki block ...?' `I have just let fifty acres to some chaps, and hope to get down another fifty or hundred acres next season if all's well. ... Those bushfallers ... were asking where they could get a regular supply of good butter, ...' <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,13</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bush-faller, n.</i> One who cuts down timber in the bush. 1882. `Pall Mall Gazette,' June 29, p. 2, col. I: <i>(1898,Austral English,70</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> In the mountain's deep gulch .../ Once the bushfaller's camp, now the deer-slayer's haunt -/ There Hughie resides ... <i>(1912,Castle Gay,63</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> In a bush-faller's whare ... <i>(1912,Mate,469</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... bush-faller ... <i>(1901,Thousand Pities, A,123</i>)

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> the bushfallers fled, were fired upon ineffectually at the same time that the sentry was shot, and the arms and pouches grasped by the Maoris. <i>(1864,Memoranda,13</i>)

### bushfalling
 
 <i>n.</i> the felling or cutting down of trees; or timber, bush-felling; the felling of bush.

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> I found a haven with Mr. Wilson at his bush farm, which was only connected with the outside world by water. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,63,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MURRAY</b> Bush Falling is never done in quantity. <i>(1866,Description Province Southland,37</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> We worked steadily at bush-falling. <i>Ibid.</i> 187 When bush-falling is performed by hired labour, it usually goes by contract. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/184</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> This was Maurice McKeown, employed ... to assist ... in the work of the farm, in attending to the stock, in logging up and clearing, in fencing, and also ... in doing a little bushfalling, though the latter work was usually let by contract, one of which was ... being carried out by ... Flash Harry, and his mates. <i>Ibid.</i> 94 This is one of the most awkward mischances ... in the work of bush-[95]falling, ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,9</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... bush-falling ... <i>(1901,Thousand Pities, A,123</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> ... I class the men who follow up bushfalling as physically the finest ... on the colonies. <i>(1913,Country Work,29</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Bushfalling had barely been started. <i>(1921,Tutira,203</i>)

>  <b>VOGEL</b> There is still a great demand for labour. Bush falling, road making and harvest. <i>(1879,Land and Farming in NZ,68</i>)

>  <i>bush falling NZ & Austral.</i> felling of trees in the bush; <i>bush-faller</i> person who does this. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Never `bush-felling' <i>(1933,BLANK ENTRY</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BELL</b> `I know who you are - you're one of the [86] bushfalling chaps that get butter here - ' <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,85</i>)

>  Bill was cook of a bushfalling camp ... <i>(Jan 10, 1920,Quick March,41</i>)

### bushfarer
 
 <i>n.</i> worker? in the bush

>  <b>BELL</b> But de'il tak' the hour I agreed to gang amang the cockatoos wi' ye, Bill; an' I could wish mysel' back amang the squatters again. Your wee bit farmers are a measly lot ... To be sure, ane is aye made at hame in a bush fa'ers' or roadmen's camp to a share o' what's goin'; ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,7</i>) [NOTES: [a swagger speaks]]

### bushfeller
 
 <i>n.</i> one who falls bush

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> The bush-fellers, having come down from the front, have proceeded to Wairoa, to commence widening the road in that district. <i>(Oct 31, 1863,Journal,107,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> This post was also the headquarters of the bush fellers, who under Mr. Martin's superintendence were cutting down the forest on each side of the road, ... <i>(1864,Memoranda,25</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Two were paid-off bushfellers; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,229</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> At McGregor's store a party of native bush-fellers were at work extending and burning the clearing, ... <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,353</i>)

>  The subject of these notes ... was discovered by some bushfellers ... <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/500</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The bushfellers were rescued by the advance-guard of a convoy escort from Drury ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/261</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> That wouldn't have mattered, for the town, to which many bushfellers and men from the outback came when they drew their pay cheques, was accustomed to small troubles. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,145</i>)

>  BUSHFELLER [=]BUSH WHACKER <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> When the bushfellers came to take timber from a pure kauri stand, they worked the bush on a face. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,149</i>)

### bushfelling
 
 <i>n.</i> the falling or cutting down of timber trees or bush A1; =bush-falling (bush-falling is the common term; bush-felling is uncommon, and would seem slightly pedantic or `Englishy'. (See [cit. bush-falling 1933](../C#cit.-bush-falling-1933))

>  This industry gives employment to a large number of persons, both directly to those engaged in breeding, shearing, freezing, and shipping, and indirectly to those who labour at bushfelling, grass-seed sowing, fencing, and otherwise opening up new country. <i>(1902,Settlement of New Plymouth,11</i>)

>  Bush-felling is expensive, but the establishment of dairy factories is already raising the price of land and giving a needed stimulus to the clearing of the forest. <i>(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.1 (N.S.) 15</i>) [NOTES: (Bell, Hokitika)]

>  ... in the process of clearing the land the necessary burning which follows the bush-felling often destroys such reserves as may have been left for the sake of the timber contained. <i>(1910,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.9 (N.S.) 4</i>) [NOTES: (Adams, Poverty Bay)]

>  <b>DOBSON</b> ... but on reaching Lake Sumner, the bush began, and we commenced bush felling. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,168</i>)

>  The impression I gathered by personal inspection of some of the lands and from representations made at crowded meetings in seven settlements, where we met some 3000 of the northern tribes, was that they were doing more than any other section of the race - not excepting the East Coast - for themselves. Road-making on a 50/50 basis with the local bodies, ohu or mahi-apu [ed fn: A group of volunteer workers] in relation to scrub-cutting, bush felling, splitting posts and battens, gave ample evidence of self-help. <i>(May 22, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/34,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> However, he could make no headway ... without first clearing the land of forest, so they were obliged to continue the ... work of bush felling. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,107</i>)

>  Bush-felling thus provides a useful means of supplementing the farm income, and accumulating capital for farming purposes. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,589</i>)

>  <b>ELLIOT& ADSHEAD</b> Then came a plague of bush rats, huge brutes that were eaten by the moreporks. Mr Thomas Nix, now in his 80s, told me that when the men were out bushfelling it was standard practice to give the pungas a hearty whack and then leap back to dodge at least a couple of rats ... <i>(1967,Cowshed to Dog Collar,16</i>)

>  He and another man were engaged in bushfelling near Reweti ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/221</i>)

>  The best tools for bush-felling are an American axe, about five pounds weight, and a light slash-hook with a thin edge. <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In [63] mining and bushfelling country bad news travels quickly. <i>(1978,Listener Short Stories,2/62</i>) [NOTES: 1962 Vogt in 1978 Listener Short Stories Vol 2 62-63]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> I was on a bush-felling contract, back of Gisborne, in the winter of 1914, when the war began. <i>(1988,Voices of Gallipoli,60</i>)

### bushie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a bushman or forestry worker; a country person (See [bushman](bri-byo.md#bushman))

>  Some bushies at the back [of the Taihape theatre] objected. <i>(Aug 25, 1906,NZ Truth,62/1/4</i>)

>  <b>GIBSON</b> The unlucky `bushie' whose mannerisms and objectionable traits attract overmuch attention from his mates ... <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot,66</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> The lone hand digging gum/ and the starving bushie outback/ girls from the stews and the slum/ <i>(1963,Collected Poems,85</i>) [NOTES: [check outback spg] 1936 (`Youth at the Dance')]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The same method of word formation [by adding -y] gives, in both [Australia and New Zealand] <i>yachty</i> (yachtsman) ... <i>bushy</i> (bushman) ... <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,88</i>)

>  A bushman [speaks] ... `Look here', said the bushie ... said the man from the outback. <i>(Nov 11, 1905,NZ Truth,21/7/1</i>)

>  <i>bushie n.</i> rough yokel; equivalent of Indian <i>jungle wallah. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural, informal) a bushman or forestry worker.

>  So Jim talked to himself in fluent Maori (which he spoke as well as any native) about the loneliness of a bushie's life and about the care and comfort which awaited any wahine who cared to share his camp. <i>(Sep 1962,Te Ao Hou,40:18</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> In the first year Woody let a contract, against his better judgement, to a 'bushie' named Kelly from Opotoki. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,153</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> One of the Omaruparoa bushies did leave a memory to posterity ... <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,185</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> The country man finds trouble as he goes about his job, the 'bushie' with his chainsaw and the drover with his mob. <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,41</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> On one occasion Cecil had signed up a couple of young bushies to cut a block ... <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,208</i>)

### bushing
 
 <i>n.</i> living (or surviving) in the bush (See [cf. bush it](bri-byo.md#bush-it))

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> There being no material for erecting a shelter, we had to hoist our blankets for a shed, but found a year's bushing had made a sad alteration in their waterproof qualities. <i>(Apr 12, 1848,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Apr 12 Brunner Exped. Middle Isd in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 313]

>  <b>J J SYMONDS</b> ... these Mokau natives seem more apt to bushing than most I have seen owing to the rough country they inhabit. <i>(Jan 26, 1850,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Jan 26 J.J.Symonds Journal in 1983 Pascoe Explorers and Travellers 31]

>  <b>ED. WARD</b> Robert ... proved his inexperience in `bushing' by insisting on taking a cup and saucer and teaspoon - a duster ... and other most unnecessary articles for tent life. <i>(Apr 7, 1851,Journal,164,1951 ed.</i>)

### bushing it
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) camping out without tent or normal supplies.

>  <b>ADAMS</b> A little more experience in 'bushing it' would have taught us to have left the bush at once to its own fate. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,40</i>)

### bushism
 
 <i>n.</i> a bushman's idiom or usage (implying `rough, uncultivated').

>  He explained that `tinned cow' was a `bushism' for condensed milk. <i>(1916,Wowser, The,90</i>)

### bushlands
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>21/209</b> I have seen most of the great bush-lands of New Zealand ...[213]... and leave the higher bush-lands for the lower valleys. <i>(1889,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Smith)]

>  ... when bush-lands are put up for sale ... <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/155</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The fallen bush lands and some of the scrub lands have been surface sown in English grasses ... <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,106</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The fallen bush lands and some of the scrub lands have been sown in English grasses ... <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,119</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The pastures being encircled by unhunted bushlands, signs of pigs moving in from the ranges had to be watched for constantly. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,69,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> They inhabited the rough broken country fringing the western boundary of the station and ... infested the wild bushlands that lay beyond it. <i>(1984,Razorback,11</i>)

>  Deer, chamois, and thar ... in themselves are an obstacle to the preservation of bushlands. <i>(May 27, 1950,Evening Post</i>)

>  All that winter cold held the bush-lands in a fierce unrelenting grip. <i>(Aug 1957,Forest & Bird,Np. 125</i>) [NOTES: (Hugh Ross)]

>  <b>RIDGWAY</b> [New comers] are puzzled by what is said about <i>fern-lands </i>and <i>bush-lands.</i> <i>(1860,Voices from Auckland,66</i>)

### bushline
 
 <i>n.</i> the timber-line; the upper limit of the growth of bush A1 on a mountain; height at which bush ceases to grow (cf. timber-line (OED 1835) is occas. used in N.Z; cf. and cp. snow-line, which is more often used in the SI mountain-country. Whether one uses bush-line or snow-line depends upon the locality of the speaker, or the point of view of the speaker, e.g. a bushman would be more likely to speak of the bush-line, a mountaineer of the snow-line. bush-line and snow-line are only occas. synonymous. e.g. in mountain-country, in the summer, when snow is lying high or has disappeared, the bush-line stands out clearly, but is often called (locally and colloquially) `the snow-line': (cf. also scrub-line).

>  Although above the bush line, there will be abundance of wood within convenient distance. <i>(Feb 13, 1899,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  To be considered a true alpine plant, a species must grow in a zone which lies between the bushline - the altitudinal limit beyond which no trees can grow - and the snowline. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/43</i>)

>  South of Mt Hector the route crosses a series of other sub-alpine peaks, reaching the bushline near Alpha Hut. <i>(2008,NZ Geographic,94/28</i>)

>  There is any amount of bush all along this line. In fact, the line is generally referred to as the "bush line." <i>(Jan 15, 1924,Otago Witness,64</i>)

>  We may speak of kauri forest or beech forest, but we get lost in the bush, fight bush fires, and climb above the bushline. The kauri forest, like the mixed podocarp forest, the sub-tropical rain forest, ... and such-like are terms that come from the botanist and forester, not from the layman - or from the bushman. ... Even those later scientists who use <i>forest</i> as a general rule usually write of <i>bushmen, bush tracks, patches of bush</i>, and the <i>bushline. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 24 <i>Bushline</i>, the upper limit of the bush, would be the <i>timberline</i> in North America. Hudson [G.V. Hudson 1890 TrNZI 2/181] used it in 1890 as if it were then well established. It does not seem to have been noted in any dictionary and is probably a word indigenous to New Zealand. <i>(1958,Tararua,23</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <b>CRUMP</b> His place was spread against one side of a leading ridge, along the top of which was the bushline, with his boundary fence hard against the bush. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,82</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Bush-line</i> or sometimes [161] <i>forest-line</i> (for American<i> timber-line</i>, English <i> tree-line</i> and Australian <i> tree-line or forest-line), bush-clad, bush burns</i> (areas where the bush has recently been burnt off) are tramping as well as general terms, as is the use of <i> shingle</i> for angular as well as rounded stones in <i> shingle slide</i> , or (earlier) <i>shingle slip</i> , also recently called scree as in Scotland and northern England. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,160</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> He followed the greenstone to his camp ..., keeping above the bushline, headed for the sea. <i>(1968,Early Fiordland,77</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> The ... New Zealand cedar ... stands over a skeletal mountain neinei <i> Dracophyllum traversii</i> ) near the bushline in Heniker Creek. <i>Ibid.</i> 164 Mountain cottonwood (<i> Cassinia vauvilliersii</i> ) and<i> Senecio bennettii</i> are also common plants in plants in the region immediately above the bushline. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,163</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Above the bushline the environment is less inviting. The bush is generally a friend to trampers as it provides the two crucial necessities of shelter and fuel. Only when you emerge from the forest onto the bare tops do you realise how generous a protector the bush is. ... Traditionally all the landforms above the bushline have been regarded as tops country. <i>(1988,Hills, The,43</i>)

>  <b>BERTRAM</b> Sheep on the hills, and a grazing horse by the ragged/ Edge of the bushline./ <i>(1971,Occasional Verses,11</i>)

>  <b>SHERSON</b> There luck seemed to desert them and ... they were beginning to wonder if they would be able to find their way out [of the bush] again. ... As they reached the bushline they came across one more pig ... <i>(1981,Kahikatea, Cabbage Trees and Koromiko,56</i>)

>  <b>PAULIN</b> Fresh snow ... came down to within 2000 feet of sea level - <i> i.e</i>., considerably below the bush-line. <i>(1889,Wild West Coast,119</i>)

>  In this paper Hudson uses <i>forest(s)</i> except in the following:] ... where the track was soon discovered, and followed up until the bush-line was passed, at about 4,000 ft. <i>(1890,TrNZI,22/181</i>) [NOTES: (Hudson)]

>  ... led to the open hill above the bush-line. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/326</i>) [NOTES: (Brown)]

>  From the bush-line to the top of the mountain the ... ascent is very easy. <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/336</i>)

>  ... I observed vast swarms ... flying over the birch-trees at the bush-line on one of the western slopes ... <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/335</i>)

>  ... a steep climb of about 2,500 ft. over burnt-forest area brings you to the sharply defined bush-line. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/119</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  I do not think I have ever heard one above the bush-line (about 3,000 ft.). <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/329</i>)

>  <i>Alsophila Colensoi</i> grew above the bushline on Flagstaff, where it still grows, but of the others few remain. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/280</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... if we are above the bush line, snow or creeks wet us daily ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,151</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Above the bushline it reared in innumerable ridges. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,134</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The high cathedral peaks midway up the lake on our right were a most impressive sight, with the snow still covering the high places above the bush line, the distance giving them a blue to purple toning. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,41</i>)

>  <b>DOUGLAS</b> perhaps the scarcity of grass on the high ranges and height of the Bush line is the cause. <i>(Jun 8, 1892,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1892 June 8 Douglas Letter to Strauchon in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas p? ...]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> We sorted our packs on a river flat at the foot of a mountain whose snow-grass tops showed alluringly above the bushline under a clear blue sky. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,164,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> They set out up a ridge.. and within five hours reached Table Top, a vantage point above the bushline, from whuch they could study the surrounding country. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,84</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> A hut above the bush-line of the Southern Alps. <i>(1955,Fire and the Anvil, The,58</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a delineation separating pasture and bush in hill or high country or separating bush and sparse ground cover in high, mountainous areas.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Above the bush line the country opened into great carpet-grass basins. <i>(1949,High Country Days,140</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> These [cattle] had strayed into the scrub above the bushline ... <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,184</i>)

>  <b>WARDLE</b> Timberline, or bush line as we usually call it, is a conspicuous feature on high mountains of New Zealand which still carry their original forest ... <i>(1972,BLANK ENTRY,23 31</i>) [NOTES: in Tussock Grasslands and Mountain Review]

### bushlover
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  As a bushlover I regret the practice. <i>(Feb 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

### bushman
 
 <i>n.</i> an expert or one experienced in the bush

>  <b>REEVES</b> Even in the North Island the average bushman will speak of the pukatea tree as "bucketeer", and not to call the poroporo shrub a "bull-a-bull" would be considered affectation. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,404</i>)

>  A female huia had been seen recently by an old-time bushman in the Wellington district. <i>(Sep 4, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/167 : 10</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> These wet-day holidays were known as "Buckley's" but says an old bushman, "under some contractors and most "subbers", they were a bit hard to come by". <i>(1964,New Story of the Kauri, The,85</i>)

>  The balance pole.. was abandoned in favour of the kawa, which corresponds to the bushman's swag straps and to the soldier's knapsack. <i>(Jul 17, 1924,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Her dad was a bushman. Her mum was pretty busy being mum. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 68</i>)

>  After coming back to civilisation from long periods in the bush, it would sometimes be difficult to get to sleep on soft beds - bushmen were just as likely to sleep on the floor in a hotel room. <i>(Mar 2, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The woollen blanket ... I had sewed up ... into a kind of sack ... - an excellent invention of experienced `bush-men'. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),285</i>) [NOTES: CHECKED DEC 1989 AGAINST AND/OED2: nf references on bush checkfile]

>  [Caption] Bushmen dwarfed by a giant Kauri tree at Tokatoka near Kaipara. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,No. 51/60</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Like most bushmen, his axe-work was a mighty necessary part of his makeup (and Old Dad was a `top hand'). If he wasn't a good man on the end of a saw - some blokes were `jockeys', they rode it - well, he wouldn't last long in the bush as far as his work was concerned ... <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,62</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A bushman always wore a special type of boot known as "fern-tights", named because the upper part of the boot was sewn inside the lower leather, preventing sharp stakes from penetrating the seam and tearing the stitching. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,61</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Mountaineers were amazed to see that a bushman with a heavy swag could slash a track just about as fast as they could walk. <i>(1958,Great Days in NZ Mountaineering,132</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> He couldn't swim a stroke, you know - a lot of the bushmen couldn't. <i>(1986,Southern Cross (Wgton),37</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) forestry worker or cutter.

>  <b>BARKER</b> Armed with the necessary document, the next step was to engage 'bushmen' or woodcutters by profession ... <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,3</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> A gang of bushmen would get a permit when they had finished clearing a patch of bush, and bring in a whole lot of grog. <i>(1997,Pigs and Me: Tales of the Bygone Bush.,76</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who establishes pasture from bush on his own land or on contract.

>  <b>BARKER</b> The 'bushmen' - as the men who had bought 20 acre sections and settled in the bush are called - had scattered English grass seed. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,180</i>) [NOTES: Station Life in NZ (1870)]

>  A bushman named Mannix was badly hurt yesterday whilst cattle-shooting in the bush behind Manaia. <i>(Jun 29, 1883,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  <b>HOBHOUSE</b> His sole dwellingroom is his store containing everything needful for the service of a station from a horseshoe to a Bushman's monkeyjacket. <i>(Nov 4, 1859,Letter</i>)

>  <b>PRATT</b> I have previously referred to a contract that was taken up by two of the settlers, first rate bushmen ... <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,126</i>)

### bushman's friend
 
 <i>n.</i> a wren

>  He had seen.. the little wren known as 'the bushman's friend'. <i>(Jan 26, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,47</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Bushman's friend, or rangiora, was not only used for letter writing and toilet paper. Tom Paul shows how the Maori used the leaves for a bandage tied with flax. <i>(Jan 4, 1985,Evening Post,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Solanum</i> spp.

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> [Flannelweed, <i>Solanum mauritianum</i>] is a handsome plant with big, pointed, pale-green leaves (`the bushman's friend, as a [200] tramper once called it, although this vulgar name has been applied to other large-leaved New Zealand plants - for practical reasons readers will guess). <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,199</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering, hunting, tramping] rangiora, an indigenous plant with a flat, flexible leaf used in place of toilet paper.

>  Bushman's friend,or rangiora, was not only used for letter writing and toilet paper. <i>(Jan 4, 1985,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bushman's friend: rangiora, the leaves of which are used as toilet paper in the bush. <i>(1980,Loughnan Glossary,14</i>)

### bushman's jacket
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Feeling an enormous sense of loss, Bob reached into the deep pocket of his bushman's jacket and took out his packet of tobacco and papers. <i>(1984,Razorback,17</i>)

### bushman's mile
 
 <i>n.</i> an exceedingly conservative rough estimate of a distance; a distance that turns out to be longer than has been estimated; an `Irishman's mile'.

>  <b>FIRTH</b> If you are ever in this Colony, let me advise you, if you are pressed for time, to beware of the `bushman's mile' or an `Irishman's quarter of an hour'. <i>(1890,Nation Making,253</i>)

### bushman's minnow
 
 <i>n.</i> a stick of dynamite

>  Then we started being accused of using bushman's minnows. A bushman's minnow was a stick of dynamite that was lit thrown into the deep holes. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,87</i>)

### bushman's pie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> Bushman's pie. Another fine example of New Zealand's haute cuisine. <i>(1982,200 Years of NZ Food & Cookery,115</i>)

### bushman's saw
 
 <i>n.</i> a frame handsaw, with a narrow or ribbon blade, of several sizes, for use in lopping off small branches, cutting firewood, etc. [ca1940 at least, Havelock]

### bushman's shirt
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EDMOND</b> ... Mr McLachlan, his red-checked bushman's shirt with the sleeves rolled up, ... <i>(1984,High Country Weather,33</i>)

### bushman's singlet
 
 <i>n.</i> a black, working singlet; a thick, black, woollen, sleeveless singlet worn by bushmen and other manual workers (because it soaks up sweat without chilling the wearer, and does not show sweatstain and other grime). [c. 1910 p.c. W.H.B.Orsman (Marlborough). As long as I can remember and certainly before the First World War, black woollen singlets were always called, and sold in stores as, 'bush singlets'. ca1900 at least, the Havelock store for a long time sold `bushmen's singlets'.] (See [bush singlet](bri-byo.md#bush-singlet))

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We wear saddletweed trousers and the sleeveless woollen dark navy-coloured "bushmen's singlet". <i>(1974,Open Country Muster,187</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> Their [Maori bushmen's, 1920s] usual grab was a sleeveless knitted bushman's singlet, and denim or palmer-nap pants. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,49</i>)

>  <b>Bacon</b> He laughed heartily, his great stomach wobbling under his black bushman's singlet. <i>(1964,Along the Road,47</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> In his ragged bushman's singlet/And no backside in his breeks,/ He's the roughest- looking cocky/ That ever left the sticks./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,83</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A bushman's singlet is a black, heavy woollen sleeveless garment that keeps the body warm when it is soaking wet. The same type of singlet worn by shearers is called a "Jackie Howe." <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,61</i>)

### bushman's soap
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  She demonstrates bushman's soap (the crushed leaves on the kumarahou plant). <i>(Dec 18, 2005,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),40</i>)

### bushman's sprigs
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A bushman always wore a special type of boot known as `ferntights', named because the upper part of the boot was sewn inside the lower leather, preventing sharp stakes from penetrating the seam and tearing the stitching. Round-headed hobnails were replaced with square-headed "bushman's springs" [sic].It was essential when standing on roots to have a firm footing and "bushman's sprigs" helped to prevent us losing our foothold on a slippery bank. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,61</i>)

### bushman's staggers
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANNABELL</b> About an hour after the second boil up for the day the affected person would feel an unnatural weakness stealing over him, his axe would grow excessively heavy, every blow would be an effort and a feeling of unreality as if working in a dream and a faint tremnbling of the limbs would assail him. Usually he would sit down and sharpen his axe for a minute or two then carry on chopping, the feeling slowly fading away in about half an hour. Among ourselves, to give this affliction an important sounding name, we always called it "Hydrophobia" but it is more generally called "Bushman's staggers," and is supposed to be caused by overworking the stomach at the same time as overworking the muscles. <i>(1984,Ngamatapouri,51</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a muscle weakness due to inadequate diet and over-exertion in bush-clearing.

>  <b>COWAN</b> We had to make sure we fed ourselves well or we would suffer from the bushman's staggers. <i>(1997,Pigs and Me: Tales of the Bygone Bush.,72</i>)

### bushman's steak
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRANDON</b> <i>BUSHMAN'S STEAK </i>... For two men ... steak on a long stick ... roasted to a nice brown ... pour little water into pan; add ... flour ... vinegar ... pepper ... salt ... pour over steak. <i>(1905,'Ukneadit'. Home for Incurables Bazaar,102</i>)

### bushman's toilet paper
 
 <i>n.</i> rangiora, Brachyglottis repanda

>  <b>CROWE</b> Rangiora.. <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i> Bushman's toilet paper... The more practical and better known use of rangiora is described by the plant's European name. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,48</i>)

### bushman-settler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> Day after day Atkinson led out his war-party of practised bushmen-settlers and scoured the forest and the native tracks, and soon had the country free from hostiles for a radius of many a mile from New Plymouth. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/225</i>)

### bushmanlike
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> Led by Julian with his slash-hook ... - bushmanlike figure enough was `Wirihana' for he ... travelled bare-legged in Maori fashion all that week's tramp. <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,9</i>)

>  Led by a chainman ... with his slasher, and Wirihana with his tupara - a bushmanlike figure was the Boss, for he cannily packed his trousers in his swag and travelled bare-legged, Maori fashion ... <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

### bushmanship
 
 <i>n.</i> a capability for or knowledge of bushcraft

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bushmanship</i>, ... knowledge of the ways of the bush. 1882. A. J. Boyd, `Old Colonials,' p. 261: <i>(1898,Austral English,71</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> Arthur had been much struck with [?O'Brien's] ... bushmanship. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,44</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> A friendship based on bushmanship. <i>(1934,Windy Island,147</i>)

>  <i>bushman n</i>. one skilled in bush lore, and able to find his way in the bush; whence bushmanship, skill in bushcraft. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

### bushmen's trousers
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  These are extra heavy underpants, dark colour, hip pocket, and cost 10/-. ca1954 R.Mason notes: `According to two old bushmen, Joe Gibbs and Sandy Donaldson, bushmen wore (and may still wear) long, red, woollen underpants at work. They do not get wet as quickly as trousers, do not flap uncomfortably around the legs when wet, and keep the legs warm. Trampers often do likewise in snow and in cold wet windy weather when it is too cold for shorts. <i>(1939,Tararua,5(12)/2</i>) [NOTES: check year]

>  A <i>bushman</i> is one who lives and works in the bush, or sometimes one skilled in bushcraft. Judging by a remark in the <i>Tramper</i> of 1933 there were articles known as <i>bushmen's trousers</i> on sale. These were a type of garment pretty much the same as long woollen underpants, but made for outer wear and provided with pockets. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,24</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

### bushrange
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  I had a skirmish with Arthur, who tho' off duty ... wished to go as he scented fighting. ...I have let him bush range for months uncomplainingly ... <i>(Aug 7, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/117,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Jane M. Atkinson)]

### bushranger
 
 <i>n.</i> (Transf. or extended uses of Aust. bushranger-->; `bush bandit' [AND 1801];the primary sense (OED 1817), though widely known, was used infreq. in NZ contexts before the major gold discoveries, and then mainly referring to goldfields robberies (e.g. Maungatapu Murders 1866) of the "hold-up" type. The word has always been Aust. from c1806;. cf. Elder Goldseekers and Bushrangers in New Zealand.)

>  <b>ELDER</b> Great numbers of them [sc. Tasmanian aboriginals] have been shot like beasts of prey by our people, and the bushrangers who make their escape from the settlement have (for fear of being discovered by the settlers) killed them without mercy. <i>(Apr 23, 1814,Marsden's Lieutenants,54,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Apr 23 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 54]

>  <b>ELDER</b> He had been formerly amongst the bushrangers at Van Diemen's Land, and had of course nothing to say on his behalf. <i>(Jan 21, 1817,Marsden's Lieutenants,133,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1817 Jan 21 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 133]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Capt Anderson gave a fearful account of the audacity of the Bushrangers all over New South Wales. <i>(Apr 23, 1840,Diary</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The sight of "mounted police", - men whose business it was, in the penal colonies to apprehend escaped convicts and hunt down "bush-rangers" - patrolling the beach [at Wellington] with fetters in their hands, for the intimidation of the inhabitants, served but to prepare them [the Company settlers] for the still harsher usage they were to meet from local government. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,9</i>)

>  [cit. form Melbourne paper] Two bush-rangers, named Wilson and Coyle, have been apprehended, and the charge of highway robbery being substantiated against them, they were committed for trial. <i>(Jul 15, 1851,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  ... a notorious Australian bushranger has been caught here [at Timaru]. <i>(May 6, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,86,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Talk [in Hokitika] was running on the capture and trial of a gang of Australian bushrangers, who have been lurking in [93] the forest ... <i>(Oct 6, 1866,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,92,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FARJEON</b> We're not good gentlemen - we're bushrangers. <i>(1866,Grif: a Story of Colonial Life,233</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> The great break or rift ... was named <i>`McKenzie's Pass'</i>, after a bush-ranger who ... tried to push through with a thousand sheep which he had succeeded in carrying off ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,136</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bushranger</i>, ... one who ranges or traverses the bush, far and wide; an Australian highwayman; in the early days usually an escaped convict. Shakspeare uses the verb `to range' in this connection. <i>(1898,Austral English,71</i>)

>  Some excitement was caused on the road, when in 1865, two bushrangers, Davis and Everest, stuck up Fricher's Hotel ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,169,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... I saw that the supposed bushranger was a compact bush of matakoura or wild Irishman. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,27</i>)

>  <b>SHAW & FARRANT</b> In the spring of 1861 drama stalked on to the scene - bush-rangers were reported in the district under the leadership of Garrett, a character known to the Australian police. <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,36</i>)

>  Bushranger The Australian highwayman of the early days . - <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),55</i>)

>  <i>bushranger ...n.</i> originally an escaped convict, then any highway robber or hold-up man frequenting the bush; <i>bushranging</i>, the practice of the bushranger. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> On August 18, 1863, five bushrangers attempted a robbery of gold at [the] hotel at Eden Creek. <i>(1948,Down the Years in the Maniototo,42</i>)

>  I put several miles between myself and the would-be bushrangers. <i>(Oct 30, 1890,Otago Witness,32</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a black cricket or puharanga.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The black cricket, <i>puharanga</i> - "bush-ranger" - of the natives ... is reputed to have reached New Zealand either in matting from the islands, or in the bedding of troops from India. <i>(1926,Tutira,319</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The black cricket, puharanga - 'bush ranger' of the natives, whose faint musical trills tells us that autumn has come once more ... <i>(1921,Tutira,340</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a supervisor of bush gangs engaged in bushfelling.

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> One of the bush gangs could not get on with the ranger. (A ranger is the man I employ to look after the bushmen and their work ... ) <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,87</i>)

>  <b>DAHM</b> A bushranger, a man who understood all aspects of bush felling, was then called in to put a felling price on each block. <i>(2000,People of the Axe, The,94</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep or cattle beast that evades muster and hides in areas of bush.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Of Newton's 4000 sheep, 1200 or so would have died .. about 200 of them would have became what we used to call 'bushrangers' ... <i>(1921,Tutira,139</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a renegade Ma. `bandit' infesting or `ranging' the bush; a runaway slave who takes to the bush.

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Near Matukutuki near the Mokau] The natives say that until lately these remote forests were infested with `Parau' native bushrangers or slaves who had run away from their masters and were guilty of every enormity, and woe to the unfortunate traveller who went alone through these forests. <i>(Feb 5, 1850,Journal,7/62</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARK</b> Consequently her thoughts went: `Two shillings for that smoked snapper! That Solivich is a bushranger. Oh, that's a bonzer hat. ...' <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,190</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> All Sun reporters were `bush-rangers' - wandering hither and thither to pick up local incident, tint with local colour, and present in `snappy' style. <i>(1934,Journalese,189</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> an elusive sneak-thief

>  <b>BARLOW</b> ... a sort of a amateur bushranger started business in the district, about eighteen months ago, and upset all our feelings of security <i>Ibid.</i> 162-163 This lollipop-sucking bushranger for several [163] weeks completely baffled all efforts ... leaving behind him ... jam tins, lolly bottles, pie dishes, ... and rural policemen. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,159</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a sheep which has taken to the bush and missed a shearing; a sheep which has escaped into the bush.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> ... of Newton's 4000 sheep, 1200 or so would have died ...; about 200 of them would have became what we used to call "bushrangers"; from 500 to 700 would have straggled off the run, ... <i>(1926,Tutira,139</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRERETON</b> Roger Salisbury had killed a big bushranger wether and dogs and men had a good meal. <i>(1947,No Roll of Drums,120</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Bushranger tragedies held us spellbound. Towards the end of one blood-curdling melodrama the heroine was in dire distress. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,34,1968 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1880s (Kumara) in Seddon The Seddons (1968) 34]

### bushrangering
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> Nearly everyone in the new district was sedate, except old Tim O'Keefe, the harness-maker, who was a Bolshie, and had in his shop window a large, unsteadily-lettered notice, `No Bushrangering Done Here.' <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,77,1970 ed.</i>)

### bushrangers
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> In the last years of the 19th century a battle game of `bushrangers' was popular. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### bushranging
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> The risks of travel were considerable: `Sticking-up', and other features of `Bush-ranging' ... were rife in Otago <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,6</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bush-ranging</i>, ... the practice of the <i>Bushrangers</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,72</i>)

>  <i>bushranger ...n.</i> originally an escaped convict, then any highway robber or hold-up man frequenting the bush; <i>bushranging</i> the practice of the bushranger. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The first instance of bushranging was when Garrett stuck up persons travelling over Maungatua Range. <i>(Jan 22, 1924,Otago Witness,7</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> ?of a wild boar

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Meanwhile men spoke of this [sc. a wild boar's] bushranging nuisance ... in crowded bars - of some footprints, ... or a recent raid on sheep. <i>(1955,By Reef and Range,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> wandering in the bush.

>  <b>GOLDER</b> Let's go a bushranging, thou fairest of lassies;/ Let's go a bushranging, and visit each scene,/ Whose beauties unchanging which nought eye surpasses/ While clad in mantles of gray [?gay] evergreen. <i>(1852,NZ Ministrelsy</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> guerilla-patrolling in the bush (as done by the bush rangers n2)

>  Paraded at 9 when the men were picked out for the bush ranging. <i>(Jul 7, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/53,(1960) ed.</i>)

>  ... leave to do bushranging granted to the Civilian Force. <i>(1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/281</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> The night-watch was once more told off: and let me tell you young men so eager for the eight shillings per day for bushranging, that ... you had better think twice before you venture on such work; ... <i>(1879,Waikato War,38</i>) [NOTES: [quoting a correspondent with the Forest Rangers c? 1863-64]]



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> The importance of bushranging corps ... has been urged repeatedly. <i>(Dec 1863,Journal,135,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The patient Colonel probably did not venture to inform his wife that the Rangers preferred their bush-ranging costume, and would not wear trousers if they had them - on active service at any rate. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,234</i>)

### bushscape
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McHUTCHESON</b> ... The most remarkable piece of "bushscape" any of us had ever been privileged to see. <i>(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland,74</i>)

### bushwack
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bushwhack](bri-byo.md#bushwhack))

### bushwacker
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bushwhacker](bri-byo.md#bushwhacker))

### bushwards
 
 <i>adv.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> And I've seen the owners strutting proudly bushwards with each little compartment bulging and a mass of billies and other gear bouncing about merrily on the back. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,28, 160</i>)

### bushwhack
 
 <i>v.</i> to cut (plug tobacco) roughly )(cf. timber `splinters of stem in fine-cut cigarette tobacco') [reconsider: may be treat merely as a metaphor.]

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> ... cuttin' good terbaccer as if you was bushwackin' <i>(1907,Return of Joe,287</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to cut or make one's way through rough bush [cf. bush-bashing ca1940 This letter has got me bushwhacked; I can't make head nor tail of it.] (See [cf. bush-bashing](bri-byo.md#bush-bashing))

>  Since 1948 six expeditions have bushwhacked into the 685-square mile zone [in Fiordland]. <i>(Mar 1952,National Geographic Magazine,397</i>) [NOTES: Smith "Finding an Extinct' New Zealand Bird"]

>  <b>MacMORRAN</b> Her future husband ... was working there as a `bushwacker' for a mill when Helen ... first arrived. <i>(1977,In View of Kapiti,156</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Old Con Kelly says you can always tell a bushwhacker by the shape of his arm muscles ... <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,36</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... old Naish Cook, a great old bushwhacker and scrubcutter and shearer in his day but now a bit ringbarked himself, ... <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,129</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Bushwhackers, sugar-bag men, tramps, swaggers, rousabouts - all these expressions became familiar as I grew older, but I had always thought of these wanderers as tramps and swaggers only. <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,114</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> fire upon (game) from a point of vantage



 
<b>4</b> <i>v.</i> to dwell in; prowl or search among, the bushes, or forest (1868- 1889).

>  <i>bushwhack ... 3. (intr.) Also Austral. & N.Z.</i> to range or move around in woods or the bush <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,148</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD but see 96]]

### bushwhacked
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) exhausted.

>  <b>MCGILL</b> bushwhacked - exhausted: eg 'No more for me. I'm utterly bushwhacked.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,23</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> occas. modern coll. from an extension of the orig. (widely known) U.S. usage `kill by ambushing' cf. similar sense and use whacked; of which bushwhacked in this sense, is phs an elaboration, e.g. I was bushwhacked on the question of 10 o'clock closing; to be, get (a person), bushwhacked, to be, get beaten (in argument, a contest etc); bewildered, puzzled (by a problem etc) [?Is this merely an extension or elaboration of <i>whacked. </i>]

### bushwhacker
 
 <i>adj.</i> rough, homely; makeshift; rough-and-ready

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> After the meal the whole Crummer tribe squat around the big bushwhacker chimney-place and roll cigarettes. <i>Ibid.</i> 109 Jake thinks how nice it is after Uncle Ted's bush-whacker style to see a table with a crisp white cloth and crystal and all the fittings. <i>Ibid.</i> 148 How nice after Uncle Ted's bushwhacker style fire to see a table with a crisp white cloth. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,57 or 70</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The sawmill hands were there almost to a man, and many also of the "bush-whackers" from away back. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,145</i>)

>  They were Bush-Whackers, and they lived by Bush-Whacking. <i>(Jul 15, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),XV</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Remember too another toiling host, the bushwhackers, those who opened up New Zealand. <i>(1998,From Top to Bottom : Reinga to Bluff,60</i>)

>  If we.. wish to protect [our forests] from the axe and saw of the modern bushwhacker.. then we must turn to other forms of construction. <i>(Apr 2, 1952,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  And so, with the philosophy of the average bush-whacker and stockman, they contentedly went to sleep. <i>(Oct 30, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/5 : 104</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> [He had been] the most skilful bushwhacker in the district. <i>(1905,Return of Joe,257</i>)

>  It was too far away for them to see.. [the biggest forest trees] left by the bushwhackers, who clear the smaller trees and undergrowth for burning, and leave these giants. <i>(Nov 25, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> While the chopped bush is drying under the hot sun during the summer months, the bushwhacker is never idle. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,72</i>)

>  There was also a "bush-whacker's" camp at the back of the place. <i>(Nov 4, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> one who falls bush A1 for a living; professional axeman; bush-faller, bushman 2 (prob. taken from US bushwhacker , `backwoodsman' in a NZ - note there is no composition in the earliest uses of BUSH + whack). A timber feller; bushwhack, to fell timber.

>  <b>BELL</b> Accidents [sc. a broken axe-handle] will happen, you know. Davie isn't qualified as a bush whacker yet. <i>Ibid.</i> 95 `Hallo! ... Harry, my experienced bushwhacker, you didn't carry all before you this time.' <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,18</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> I was a bush whacker. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> [He was] the most skilful bush-whacker in the district. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,257</i>) [NOTES: [287 OEDS]]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The labourer is worthy of his hire, whether it be as a Member of Parliament or as a bush whacker, mechanic or any other kind of workman. <i>(1910,Reminiscences,29</i>)

>  He said it was the practice of many bush-whackers to set the big hollow ratas ablaze whenthey cut out a bush ... <i>(Feb 10, 1920,Quick March,37</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> The bushwhacker with his axe had become the spearhead of civilization. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,59</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> A man who fells trees is not a lumberjack or a woodsman but a bushwhacker. <i>(1945,If You'd Care to Know,99</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> My opponent was a tall, strapping young bushwhacker, a good deal younger than me ... <i>(1946,There Was Gold,123</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> ... the chance for a woman or a blowout, the release from routine monotony, in much the same way as bushwackers [sic] in peacetime looked forward to their yearly trip to town with the cheque. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,344</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>bush-whacker </i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Some gate-crashing bush-whackers had picked on one of their blokes. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,164</i>)

>  BUSHWHACKER A workman engaged in clearing forest for settlement. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> `What is your job, McLatchie?' `Bushwhacking, Sir.' `What, exactly, is a bushwhacker?' `A man who fells bush.' [ca1900] <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,100</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... there were the usual handful of farming people, mill workers, bush whackers, navvies ... . <i>(1973,Once Is Enough,80</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a worker who clears land of scrub and timber.

>  <b>BELL</b> Davie isn't qualified as a bush whacker yet. <i>(1898,BLANK ENTRY,18</i>) [NOTES: In Shadow of Bush]

>  .. I was told to see to the burning-off, a bushman named Ginger was sent to assist me. Ginger, a typical bush-whacker of that time, had been several weeks on the spree ... <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,44</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Huge gangs of bushwhackers were employed ; the bush was felled and burnt and the area fenced and sown down. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,65</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> A fairly broad term for someone who cuts timber, even if he is only clearing scrub with a slasher. [bush-whacker] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,14</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> George .. is an old bushwhacker who every now and then gets an urge to get back into the game. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,40</i>)

>  <b>O'CONNOR</b> The settler often began work on his block of land in the winter, armed with not much more than a tent and an axe. If possible he hired a 'bush-whacker' or co-operated with a neighbour. <i>(1970,Growth of NZ Farming 1890-1918, The,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a person from the bush

>  <i>bushwhacker</i>, n. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> `But princess,' he drawled, `us sex-starved bushwhackers buried out here in the sticks have to make do with what we can get.' `Queen Street bushwhackers from a commune in Auckland, more likely. I'm sure you don't talk like a milk-bar cowboy at the commune.' <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,45</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> one who ranges the bush

>  <i>bushwhacker n. 1. U.S., Austral. & N.Z</i>. a person who travels around or lives in thinly populated woodlands. <i>2. Austral. & N.Z. sl.</i> an unsophisticated person. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,148</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 96; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 64]]

>  <i>bushwhacker n</i>. dweller in the outback, often used humorously for any country vistor to the city. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <i>bushwhacker ... n.</i> dweller in the outback, often used humorously for any country visitor to the city. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  <b>ROGERS</b> [?a country person?] Hal's teeth flashed ... `Hislops are holding a <i>tangi</i> tonight. Tiare invited all of us. ... I'm taking Concertina Charlie over with me. Special invitation to bushwhackers.' <i>(1960,Long White Cloud, The,13</i>)

### bushwhacking
 
 <i>n.</i> bush-falling; clearing bush with an axe [one or two citations below belong with the verb to bushwhack]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Grave's daughter published a record of his bush-whacking and mountaineering that added up to genuine exploration. <i>(1971,Explorers and Travellers,150,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> ... I was exceptionally hungry after my first day's bushwhacking. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,60</i>)

>  Reaching the North Island, he started bush whacking, being one of a gang of contractors, among whom he held his own with the best, his activity standing him in good stead. <i>(Sep 7, 1904,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  They were Bush-Whackers, and they lived by Bush-Whacking. <i>(Jul 15, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),XV</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I'm out at Whangamomona at present, bush whacking. <i>(1936,Follow the Call,155</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> .. bushwhacking was the refuge of one type of destitute ... <i>(1955,Land of My Children,7</i>)

>  <b>ELKINGTON</b> Bushfelling, or as it is termed, bush-whacking was a favourite pastime of mine. <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,XVI 262</i>)

>  <b>SMYTH</b> Don't you like saw-mills and bush whacking and all that. <i>(1930,Wooden Rails,39</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> He'd ... had a go at everything - mining, bullock-driving, bush-whacking, shearing, ... <i>(1959,No Remittance,56</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> In fact, bush-whacking was the refuge of one type of destitute who could hardly ... have been induced to take work in shop, gravel-pit, railway or any of the recognized avenues of trade or labour, ... <i>(1955,Land of My Children,7</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> `What is your job, McLatchie?' `Bushwhacking, Sir.' `What, exactly, is a bushwhacker?' `A man who fells bush.' [ca1900] <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,100</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Bushwhacking is men's work if ever there was. <i>(1984,Tales of the Coast,124</i>)

>  Clerks don't, as a rule, tear their collars out by the roots when the slump comes, and take to bushwhacking. <i>(Oct 8, 1904,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> "That's like all 'o you new chums, cuttin' good terbacker as if you was bushwackin', and wasting 'arf of it." <i>(1905,Return of Joe,287</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> He.. went bushwhacking in the Rangitikei district, but the contract did not pay a dividend. <i>(1900,Tales of the Colonial Turf,40</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  ... it's the only way I feel safe in this blasted bushwhacking job. <i>(Nov 10, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The new tracks were not maintained and ... in the thirties ... the Whitcombe Pass was considered to be one of the really tough bushwhacking feats. <i>(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,14</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Outside bushwhacking contracts and shearing had given them cash. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,148</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the struggling through scrubby undergrowth in native bush

>  <b>PASCOE</b> They alternated with boulder scrambling and bush-whacking. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,161</i>)

### bushwoman
 
 <i>n.</i> a white woman living in the bush A1.

>  <b>BAINES</b> Those demons in human form - I allude to the white bushwomen - creatures of mature age, hideous to look upon, without an apparent good quality, always drunken when opportunity was found..... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,192</i>) [NOTES: [check ths and see notes]]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> THE BUSHWOMAN [title] They're bidding me to town./ They say they are ashamed/ That I should live alone - / My sons' wives will be blamed./ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,103</i>) [NOTES: 1940 Eileen Duggan in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 103]

### bushwood
 
 <i>n.</i> (Fire)Wood from felled bush trees

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The bush-wood after it is cut down is generally disposed of by burning. <i>(1860,Rough Notes of Travel,55</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> J.P. for a dray load of bushwood. [sc. firewood] <i>(Jul 30, 1869,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,191</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> The lapping of the sea mingled with the bell notes of the moko moko and tui and ... that of the burning bush wood in the boiling of the `billy'. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,70</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> [A homesick Maori says] I longed for the smell of burning bush-wood. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,243</i>)

### bushy
 
 <i>adj.</i> bush-clad, bushed, bush aj

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> Then there's what we call a <i>gully</i>, which of course we take to mean/ Just a small and narrow valley, in which bush is sometimes seen;/ <i>Bushy gully</i> suits me better, serves my purpose just as well;/ ... <i>Bush</i>, too, means the native forest; you will never, I'm afraid,/ Hear a self-respecting bushman call a bush a leafy glade,/ ... <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,3</i>) [NOTES: (`An Old Chum on New Zealand Scenery')]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> ... down into bushy valleys. <i>(1920,Prelude,17</i>) [NOTES: [recheck this]]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> Beyond, on the coastal hills, litle cotton wool clouds hide the bushy valleys. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,28,1979 ed.</i>)

>  From South Ohau, the track leaves the river ... before easing off to a more gentle gradient up the bushed ridge to Te Mataurai Hut. ... some ... hours later we had negotiated the last bushy knob and were descending the final slope towards the hut ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,68</i>)

>  ... many of the sheep had run into bushy gullies during the fire. <i>(Aug 11, 1958,Christchurch Star-Sun,5</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Only when the warming face of the sun cleared a bushy ridge ... did he turn away. <i>(1980,Stag,76</i>)

>  <b>YEREX</b> Deer can be caught in bushy terrain, or on bush edges where it is difficult for helicopters to reach. <i>(1982,Farming of Deer,93</i>)

>  <i>Bushy sites soon on sale</i> [headline] The first 15 sections of a development on Lower Hutt's Western Hills, which could yield up to 185 sections, ... <i>(Jan 7, 1986,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  He said ... many other islanders had claimed that reversion of Island Hill inot scrub was part of a plan by `greenies' and the department to turn Stewart Island into `a big green bushy paddock'. <i>(Jan 10, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,3</i>)

>  <b>BARNICOAT</b> ... surrounded by wooded or bushy hills. [176] ... bushy Islands. <i>(1845,Journal,168</i>)

>  That day ... we followed the track ... to the bushy gorge of the Pureora. <i>(Mar 6, 1855,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Mar 6 Sidebottom's letter in 1958 Gillespie South Canterbury (1971) 459]

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Continued on our course up the river - a very bushy sideling of a steep mountain gorge, with the white foam of the river some hundreds of feet below us - ... <i>(Apr 30, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Apr 30 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 409]

>  ... bushy gullies ...some bushy gully. <i>(1870,TrNZI,3/181</i>) [NOTES: (Potts and Gray)]

>  MOHOUA OCHROCEPHALA, Gml. Mohoua. Canary. ... as soon as one ascends the bushy gullies of the hills, the Canary is sure to pay a reconnoitering visit; ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/57</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  ... the once silent woods now resound with the blows of the felling-axe ... the Wattle-bird is no longer to be found in its old haunts; it seeks shelter amongst the higher parts of the bushy gullies ... <i>(1874,TrNZI,6/146</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  ... common on the bushy slopes of the mountains. <i>(1888,TrNZI,21/219</i>) [NOTES: (Smith)]

>  <i>Xenicus longipes</i> (Bush Wren). Common some distance up the bushy slopes, where the vegetation is not so dense as near the lake [Brunner]. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/214</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>McLEAN</b> Travelled over a rough bushy road. <i>(Nov 7, 1845,Journal,1/</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a timber feller

>  <b>McDONELL</b> ... Observations on NZ (Geog. Soc. Reprint) ... the friendly visits of the bush-rangers [in N.S.W.] ... <i>(1834,Journal</i>)

>  Two bushies, passing, helped the first to carry / To his lost home this legal luminary. <i>(Feb 15, 1890,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  A resident of Tauranga.. who describes himself as a "bushy" or "wayback" has sent some notes on the habits of shags. <i>(Jul 28, 1917,NZ Herald,Suppl. 1</i>)

>  These are the "bushies" and "sailors" on horseback who have no idea of riding, but simply mean to stick there by brute force. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(20) : 28</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] bush-covered.

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The daring of Ross and Fyfe was a stimulus for other young New Zealanders to fossick for themselves in unknown corners of their rocky, bushy, snowy land. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,165</i>)

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> They clean up this rough 'flag', break through bushy and rushy obstacles and thus purify the pasture for the sheep. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,15</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> When the rest of the gang caught up, the long strings of sheep began to converge and gather where some narrow spurs, divided by deep bushy gullies, led downwards to a gate at the bottom. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,89</i>)

### Business New Zealand
 
 <i>n.</i> an amalgamation of the Manufacturers' Federation and the Employers' Association

>  Business NZ is the cockpit for, and the victim of, these fights. <i>(Sep 19, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

### Business Roundtable
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Business Roundtable a sudden unrivalled power in the land . . . <i>(Feb 14, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  The Business Roundtable is strongly committed to a New Zealand where average incomes are high and unemployment is low. <i>(Apr 15, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Hide playfully threatened to get his Business Rountable mates in for a waiata of the song "Money Makes the World Go Round." <i>(Mar 15, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  the Chairman of the Business Roundtable, / the Governor of the Reserve Bank, / Oliver Burl who delivered her babies, <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  One surprise has been the four-year flirtation between Business Roundtable and this Labour Government. They made an odd couple: on one hand the group of free enterprise privateers and, on the other, the suppposedly socialist representatives of Her Majesty. <i>(Jul 2, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Today, the Business Roundtable, which represents the elite of the country's rampart [sic] business sector, is the most significant of all the lobby groups. <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### Business Roundtabler
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of the Business Roundtable

>  Even Business Roundtablers might find the Bentley a little too rich. For this car brings back a handcrafted opulence that existed long before motorised mobility reached the masses. <i>(Oct 27, 2000,Evening Post,Autopost : 2</i>)

### bust
 
 <i>n.</i> a spree, a burst q.v.

>  A great number of sailors and firemen used to get a bed at that place and used to have a "bust" ashore. <i>(Dec 11, 1924,Christchurch Star,11</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to spend

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Have a good time with it. Bust it any way you like. <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,181</i>)

### bust one's billy
 
 <i>phr.</i> to exert oneself; analogous to bust one's boiler, both probably euphemisms for bust one's gut; or poss. merely a coincidental half-rhyme on <i>boiler</i> rather than a semantic connection.

### bust up
 
 <i>v.</i> as busted up: gone broke, ruined

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> With stations mostly busted up that once was pretty fair. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,30</i>) [NOTES: (`The Swagger')]

### bust-and-offload
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Umaga showed all too clearly last weekend that he is without equal as a bust-and-offload player at second five-eighth and will probably be retained there for the northern hemisphere tour. <i>(Oct 1, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,2</i>)

### bust-up
 
 <i>n.</i> a party

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> ... Our days in the coaster ..., the old port-to-port beat with a bust-up in every port and Ted Cullers matching me on every boozy run. <i>(1966,Friend of the Family,10</i>)

### busted
 
 <i>adj.</i> done up (with exertion)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I'm nearly busted <i>(1946,That Summer,127</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> busted: exhausted <i>(1964,Collected Stories,298</i>)

### buster
 
 <i>n.</i> a fall; a cropper, purler etc. (esp. come a buster) and transf. or fig. use.. [1953 15 M A23 Thames DHS 16 BUSTER (a fall) -->1953 15 M A28 Thames DHS 16 BUSTER]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> ... the ferns were sufficiently thick to break his fall [from his horse]. He appeared to be more mortified at missing the dogs than by the buster he got. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,156,1935 ed.</i>)

>  What a buster I came on that stone. <i>(1902,NZ Illustrated Magazine,V/488</i>)

>  You will meet him when you're busted/ When you're broken by the `books'/ And he knows you've come a buster ... <i>(Aug 26, 1905,NZ Truth,10/7/8</i>)

>  <i>buster n</i>. a fall; (<i>phrase</i>). come a buster, fall down. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <i> buster ... n.</i> a fall; <i>buster (Phr.) come a buster</i>, fall down. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  <i>buster n. NZ & Austral. </i>(also <i>southerly buster</i>) cool southerly gale; <i>come a buster colloq.</i> come a cropper. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 64]]

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Hearing of the buster at Fern Flats, Jack put together a par. concerning young Steuart. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,183</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> short for cheese buster, a biscuit or pastry flavoured with cheese; a cheese straw (See [also cheese buster](c-chi.md#cheese-buster))

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>BUSTERS</i>. Two cups flour, dessertspoon butter, pinch salt, small cup milk and water. ... roll out [dough] half at time very thin, cut into strips or large blocks. <i>(1915,Amuri Cookery Book,112,1927 ed.</i>)

>  Busters. Two cups wholemeal, 2 teaspoons baking powder, 1 cup milk, rub in 2ozs butter, salt. Stiff dough. Roll very thin and cut into long strips. <i>(1936,Cookery Book of NZ Women's Institutes,154</i>) [NOTES: [also in 1955 edn p.109]]



 
 <i>n.</i> come a buster: (of a season, weather) to blow up cold and windy

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Then winter came a buster, with its wind and driving snow. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a (usu. small) local newspaper; a country newsheet or newspaper (containing mostly local news and advertisements) (usually, owing to its local significance, referred to as `the buster' [ca1922 R. Mason `a small local newspaper, a weekly.'; c.1900 Havelock The Buster, The Pelorus Buster The Pelorus Guardian newspaper..]

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> How vain the thought to get such grace/ Within the roll of Fame's bright muster,/ Whose rhymes are scarce permitted space/ Of print within the local <i>Buster! </i> <i>(1905,Poems & Sketches,139</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> I bought the gold myself. I gave the information to the `buster.' <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown,39</i>)

>  <b>GIBSON</b> ... the Chronicle, his own paper, a typical country `buster', consisting mainly of advertisements and stale telegrams. <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot</i>)

>  <b>FROBISHER</b> Whose rhymes are scarce permitted space, / Of print within our local "Buster." <i>(1893,Sketches of Gossipton,73</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> short form of southerly buster q.v. (See [southerly buster](sho-spa.md#southerly-buster))

>  <b>AYTON</b> SW buster all night until ... today with hail and Snow Squalls. <i>(Nov 4, 1899,Diary,12,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> In March a `buster' blew up from the South, a foot and a half of rain falling in three days. <i>(1926,Tutira,394</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> If, when a change is evidently coming up from the south, no mist rests on its rounded top, the change will pass off as a `dry souther,' a skiff of big cold drops blown up in fierce raw gusts; even when rain continues and the fatal cloud cap remains away, our auguries are hopeful, and though half an inch or so may fall, we do not aticipate a `buster.' When the cloud cap settles heavy rain always follows. <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,26</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> `Pray for sou'-westerly to turn into a buster more like,' said little Blue, ... <i>(1964,Watersiders,57</i>)

>  <i>buster n. NZ & Austral.</i> (also <i>southerly buster</i>) cool southerly gale; <i>come a buster colloq</i>. come a cropper. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 64]]

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> [Otago's] winds ... include all forms between the cold gales locally known as `Busters', and the hot dust winds that sweep across Australia ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,28</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> The young man had gained the reputation of being `weather-wise' ... On the present occasion he predicted `a buster' ... <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,268</i>)

>  <i>buster n. NZ & Austral.</i> (also<i> southerly buster</i>) cool southerly gale; <i>come a buster colloq.</i> come a cropper. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 64]]

>  <b>HAY</b> The quarter from which we got the most rain was the south-west. It came in what we called "busters". <i>(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury,83</i>)

>  The great objection is exposure to the South-westers 'Busters' as they call them. <i>(1980,Journal of Henry Sewell,I : 148</i>) [NOTES: Original journal entry15 Feb 1853]

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> The mountain - up Cooper mountain / for a buster from the brine <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,164</i>)

### buster-cut
 
 <i>n.</i> a boyish close crop (? fashionable in late 1920s) (perhaps from prec. buster `boy, fellow'; <i>buster-cut</i>: (hair) `cut like a boy's').

>  <b>HOOD</b> I cut off my hair in a buster cut. <i>(1988,Sylvia,49</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from Sylvia Ashton-arner, about 1926]

>  <b>GLEN</b> Though it was not a buster-cut the effect was really pretty. <i>(1926,Uncles Three,134</i>)

>  She had her hair short in a buster-cut. <i>(1950,Landfall,13/15</i>) [NOTES: (Gaskell)]

>  I thought you hated buster cuts. <i>(Dec 12, 1924,Christchurch Star,Xmas No. : 5</i>)

>  You would want to read about the latest buster-cut. <i>(Mar 27, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,30</i>)

### bustered
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of hair) cut in a buster-cut

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Her bustered yellow hair stood out from her head in a beautiful crisp frizz. <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,78</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> After I'd had my hair 'bustered' and slicked down with bay rum, he said I looked like a wet ferret. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,45</i>)

### busting
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>McHUTCHESON</b> There's a full head of steam in, an' bustin'-power in the toobes. <i>Ibid.</i> 26 "Full bustin'-power" [became] our recognised rallying-cry. <i>(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland,21</i>)

### busy as a blowfly
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>PARK</b> The two bartenders [140] ... were all as busy as blowflies ... <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,139</i>)

### busy as a one-armed paperhanger
 
 <i>phr.</i> [cf. like a one-armed paperhanger]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>busy as a one-armed paperhanger</i> bustling busy; c. 1939, also Canadian. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> I had been as busy, as the phrase runs, as a `one-armed paperhanger with the itch'. <i>(1952,Faring South,191</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Ya gotta work though. Yeah tis too. I been as busy as a one-armed paper-hanger <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,147</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> He's as busy as a one-armed paperhanger, and about as useful. <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,26</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Gaskell `Pig and Whistle' in 1978 All Part of the Game 26]

### busy-bothering
 
 <i>n.</i> [also: UK]

>  .. on the warpath against people who smoke in the public parks of such hotbeds of social busy-bothering as Hawera, Kaponga, Eltham and Patea. <i>(Aug 19, 2005,NZ Herald,A2</i>)

### but
 
 <i>adv.</i> (at end of sentence) however

>  I've grown fond of the innovative Kiwi "but" meaning "I must admit" at the end of a sentence. TV watchers must have all seen that high-country musterer detailing the delights of a new girlfriend, then reassuring his mate with, "She doesn't drink Speights, but". Now we have test driver Greg Murphy judging a new four-wheel drive after a spectacular run: "She's tough. But." <i>(Aug 17, 2002,Dominion Post,F9</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> There is no local grammar. The local usage which comes to mind, and it is not widespread, is the use of <i>but</i> as an adverb at the end of a sentence, an equivalent of <i>however.</i> ... The man who took our luggage in the air terminal at Sydney ... agreed [the Sydney climate] was good, then added `We've got central heating, but'. I first heard this usage in 1945 in Auckland among students at a Teacher's College, where it was a bit of an affectation or craze rather than established unconscious usage, but. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,113</i>)

>  <b>C. CORNE</b> but - `though', sentence / clause finally. `Jeez, this is strong piss! Nice, but'. `X's are good cars, I wouldn't buy one, but' Whangarei, widely used in 50s and 60s. Also current in Hawaian Creole English [see also Beaton] <i>(Oct 14, 1983,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (Auckland)]
