>  [(schoolchildren's) as a sentence ending] I didn't believe it first time a Whistler kid said he had two mothers. It's true but. ... Caleb still caught untold kutus that year but. <i>(1986,New Outlook,21/49</i>) [NOTES: Macrae (The Kingsland Kid)]

### butcher
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rough shearer.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Remember that one hears some shearers called butchers. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,4</i>)

### butcher bats
 
 <i>n.</i> a game with insects (See [aka butcher boys](../A#aka-butcher-boys),[penny doctor beetles](../P#penny-doctor-beetles))

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> <i>Penny Doctor</i> beetles, also known as `Butcher bats' is a game widely reported by Wellington Training College students as being played in many parts of the North Island. In this game straws moistened by spittle are pushed down the hole of the appropriate beetle. The beetle seizes the end of the straw. When the player feels the straw moving he or she flicks out the beetle. The player who can catch the most beetles wins the game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,328</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The most widely known game with insects is a game named variously by T.C. students as Butcher Boys (Gisborne, New Plymouth, Kaponga, [405] Te Pohu, Newall, Sth. Wellington), Butcher Bats (Ohau, Takaka), Penny Doctor Beetles (Tauranga, Pipiriki, Rotorua), or Fishing (Morunui). It is a game in which straws which have been suitably moistened with spittle are placed in an insects [sic] hole and are flicked out when the insect bites the end of the straw. Most of the reports of this practice come from the North Island, though it was known in the South as early as 1900 (Waikouaiti). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,405</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> A universal animal play in the North Island, but known also in the South Island, was a game variously termed Butcher Bats, Butcher Boys, or Penny Doctor Beetles. In this game, straws suitably moistened at the end with spittle were placed down insect holes and flicked out when the insect bit the end of the straw. The player won who could flick a beetle up first. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,128</i>)

### butcher lamb
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a lamb offered for processing for the local market.

>  Lamb and cattle prices ease: hvy butcher lambs $73-$80 .. butcher lambs $76-$80. <i>(Jun 6, 2001,Dominion,8</i>)

### butcher shop
 
 <i>n.</i> dysphemism for (school) dentist

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> There is a tendency to refer to the game-dentist (and to the real school dentist) in an exaggerated manner. `Here's for the butcher shop', or `I'm off to the horror house', (Waddington [19]49) or `Here's my grave number.' (Forbury), These games are reported mainly by children of mid-primary years. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,1/382</i>)

### butcher's board
 
 <i>n.</i> a marble game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> In the nineteenth century the ring game seems to have been the most popular marble game. (In more recent years marble games with holes have had the greater role.) There was Big Ring, in which a circle a yard or more in diameter was drawn on the ground; there was the mullibar variation, in which an outsize taw was catapulted amongst the marbles in this circle. There were Little Ring, Liney, Follows, Follow the Taw, Holey, and then, on a less important scale, Butcher's Board, in which the marbles were bowled through the holes in a board, Eye-Drop, Knock-Backs, Leggings-Out (or Leggin's-Out), Span-'Em, and Backits. The last was more usually a girls' game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### butcher's prime
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) well-fed live stock for sale for the local meat trade.

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> .. how to tell the 'freezers' from a line of 'butcher's prime.' <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,18</i>)

### butcher(s) ewe/butchers hogget
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep offered for slaughter on local, not export, market.

>  C.S.Walter, Onewhero, received $34 for a pen of freshly-shorn butcher ewes. <i>(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,37</i>)

>  Butchers' hoggets fetched $46 to $48. <i>(May 12, 1993,NZ Farmer,41</i>)

>  Heavy shorn butcher ewes from CJ and S Hughes Waiuku realised $26.50 ... <i>(Jul 20, 1995,NZ Farmer,39</i>)

>  Prime butchers ewes were also in demand. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,39</i>)

### butcher-bee
 
 <i>n.</i> as butcher-boy

>  <b>STEWART</b> A butcher-bee is a small, black, busy insect, perhaps really a kind of wasp rather than a bee, which lives in a hole in a clay bank. You fished for them by poking a straw down the hole, whereupon we believed, the butcher-bee would seize it and you could pull him out, still clinging to it. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,72</i>) [NOTES: From 1991 edition]

### butcher-bird
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TOMTIT <i>Petroica macrocephala Other names:</i> Miromiro (North Island), Ngirungiru (South Island), Wheedler, Butcher-bird (Taranaki). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,200</i>)

### butcher-boy
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also cits at butcher bats](../A#also-cits-at-butcher-bats))

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Catching butcher-boys was good fun ... You chose a piece of footpath where there was no tar-sealing and looked for little round holes in the earth. When you found a hole, you pushed the soft end of a grass stalk into it ... As soon as it moved you pulled it out and caught a small white grub clinging to the end with its nippers. <i>(1959,Stone, The,29</i>)

>  As a young child I would play around the sturdy old house and among the trees and in the small creek below, or sit on the veranda playing with my dolls or with pieces of grass, fish for butcher boys in the holes in the clay path. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/26</i>)

### bute
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of phenylbutazone

>  Racecourse investigators stumbled on a real whodunnit when they started checking out why Arahiwi Prince returned a positive test to the anti-inflammatory drug phenylbutazone... The committee said it was impossible to escape the conclusion that Arahiwi Prince was nobbled (sic) considering there was more bute in the horse on raceday than when another sample was taken.. one day after the horse was injected with the same drug to treat an infected hoof. <i>(Apr 22, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B12</i>)

### butt
 
 <i>n.</i> a crust (sc. of bread) (?from butt-end of a loaf). [ca1934 at least: Dunedin and Otago children's specific usage, of ? p.c. A. Campbell]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the skin end of a sheep fleece.

>  <b>COOP</b> With the standard December shearing, this weakened July-August growth comes about one-third of the way along the staple from the butt. But with August shearing the weakened portion comes right at the butt or the tip of the staple ... <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,22</i>)

>  <b>SQUIRES</b> Butt - Opposite end of the wool staple to the tip. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,11</i>)

>  Butt - The opposite end of a wool staple to the tip. <i>(2001,Woolpro Glossary of Wool Terms,5</i>)

### butt-ender
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [burster](bri-byo.md#burster))

>  <b>SHAW</b> In the Straits [we ran into] an opposite burster. Some of these winds come on gradually until they reach their greatest force ... others burst upon the crew with all their fury and then gradually decline. This latter has received the name `butt-ender' from its resemblance to that part of a gun which is the heaviest, strongest and broadest. <i>(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,184</i>)

### butt-spanker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They were labelled "sheep-shaggers", "butt-spankers" and "Christ College third-form faggers".. Over the public address system. <i>(Feb 8, 1999,Evening Post,1</i>)

### butter box tree
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the white pine or kahikatea, considered free of tainting of taste and odour, used for making boxes for transporting butter to Britain. [also butterbox, butter box timber, butterbox timber, butter box wood, butterbox wood]

>  We would also refer again to the importance of having the butter-boxes lined with grease-proof paper, so as to avoid its being touched by the wood, which is a most important part in the packing. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,130</i>)

>  ... kahikatea for buildings, butter-boxes, and packing-cases. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,118</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> There was still some demand for white pine from the butter-box industry, ... <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,35,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> Much of the White Pine cut here was used in the making of butter boxes, while the remainder went to builders in Gore. <i>(1966,In the Lee of the Hokonuis,281</i>)

>  .. the immediate demand for butter box timber is so great that it is shipped away before it is seasoned ... <i>(Nov 12, 1907,Taranaki Herald,7</i>)

>  As it is essential that all butter box wood be thoroughly seasoned, the danger of splitting when nailed must also be taken into account. <i>(May 18, 1914,Wanganui Chronicle,8</i>)

>  In view of the shortage in New Zealand of kahikatea - the splendid white pine sometimes referred to as the 'butter box tree' - the government some time ago decided to prohibit exportation, except by permit. <i>(Sep 1, 1937,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

### butter cheque
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Decided to put the Chudleigh Estate on the market. ... I also start then [?them] with 20 cows apiece at a fixed price to be paid for out of the butter cheque. <i>(Mar 31, 1919,Chudleigh Diary,466,1950 ed.</i>)

### butter conditioner
 
 <i>n.</i> a refrigerator compartment for keeping butter spreadable

>  The Nelson man was so incensed to find new fridges no longer had butter conditioners that he took out an advertisement in a magazine accusing New Zealand appliance giant Fisher & Paykel of being "anti-Kiwi".... That was partly why Fisher & Paykel did away with the butter conditioner. <i>(Apr 22, 2004,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> Taranaki.

>  Billy Murphy now knocking out a crust in the butter country. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/7/0</i>)

### butter factory
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It is true that cheese factories are becoming numerous in Otago and Southland, with a few butter-factories. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,128</i>)

>  <b>WEBB</b> We have no wealthy capitalists, ... no manufactories save the butter factory, which takes our milk, and perhaps a couple of sawmills. <i>(1925,Miss Peters' Special,123</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> When I started to the butter factory ... he went to sleep between the shafts of the dray ... <i>(1927,Gus Tomlins,91,1977 ed Sturm ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> A little later a butter factory was opened between Katui and Aranga. <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,97,1956 ed.</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> A large white buttercup, the largest known, found in the mountain regions of the South Island; Ranunculus insignis. (See [Mount Cook lily](mob-mys.md#mount-cook-lily))

>  <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: (And)Turner Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino in AJHR]

The exact connection between the "Rockwood Lily" and a Canterbury sheep station presents an interesting puzzle... Rockwood Station, owned by Henry Phillips, was one of the earliest established homesteads... However, the precise connection with Rockwood.. has yet to be discovered. <i>(Feb 11, 1988,Press, The,10</i>)

>  There, nestled among the scree, is a delicate yellow bloom - the flower of one of our endemic alpine buttecups, <i>Ranunculus godleyanus</i>, found only in the central parts of the Southern Alps between Aoraki/Mt Cook and Arthur's Pass national parks. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/36</i>)

### butterfat
 
 <i>n.</i> The essential fats in milk, used in NZ since 1892 as the basis for calculating the monthly payment to dairy factory suppliers, hence of prime importance to the dairy farmer; now replaced by MILKFAT.

>  To counteract the present waste of butterfat sent into cheese factories, the logical issue seems to be more butter-making for Taranaki. <i>(Jun 27, 1928,NZ Free Lance,36</i>)

>  It was the sharemilker and dairy co-op director and its accountant revealed that next season their industry was facing an almost 50 per cent drop in payments to farmers on their production's butter fat content. <i>(Jul 12, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  The paying for milk delivered at cheese- and butter-factories according to the amount of butter-fat contained is a question of great importance to the dairymen of New Zealand. <i>(1895,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,III/73</i>)

>  Butter-fat was only 7d per lb. <i>(Aug 28, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,11</i>)

>  "Butter fat" is a word that will be heard frequently in the House this session... <i>(Jul 3, 1917,Stratford Evening Post,4</i>)

>  It was resolved that 16d per lb be paid for butter-fat during the coming season. <i>(Oct 2, 1918,Otago Witness,23</i>)

>  A second payment.. would be made at the rate of 1d for 1lb. butter-fat. <i>(Sep 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,26</i>)

>  No other part of the world.. is producing so much butter-fat under the same conditions of dairying. <i>(Oct 26, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,31</i>)

>  Is [efficiency] to be determined on pounds of butterfat averaged per cow milked? <i>(Jan 5, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,65</i>)

>  <b>BELSHAW</b> The practice in New Zealand is to pay the dairy farmer on a butterfat basis, whether he is supplying a cheese, butter or any other factory, and it has, therefore, become general to speak in terms of butterfat production rather than gallons of milk. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,446</i>)

>  There appears to be little material change in the aggregate quantities of butterfat now being supplied to the factories. <i>(Nov 2, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,84</i>)

>  It was proved quite early by experiment that farmers could not pick the animals most productive of butter-fat in their own herds with anything approaching scientific accuracy. <i>(1941,Farmer in New Zealand, The,107</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses]

>  <b>19</b> Ken wants the butterfat price to be up in '97, and he hopes the government gives a good price for wool. <i>(Jan 18, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  [Farmers' representatives] are out for a refund of the butter-fat tax, which the Government says is not a tax. <i>(Jul 3, 1917,Stratford Evening Post,4</i>)

>  [Hdg] Butter-fat payments. <i>(Sep 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,26</i>)

>  Less hired labour is employed on Dominion dairy farms to-day than was the case when low butterfat prices and correspondingly lower wagers were ruling prior to the advent of the guaranteed price. <i>(Sep 28, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,78</i>)

>  <b>BELSHAW</b> The butterfat production for the month is calculated at the test recorded on the testing officer's monthly visit. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,347</i>)

>  <b>ALLEY & HALL</b> The butterfat production for the month is calculated at the test recorded on the testing officer's monthly visit. <i>(1941,Farmer in New Zealand, The,108</i>)

>  To get a fair test of a cow's capabilities in the cream of the butterfat line she must be milked dry. <i>(Oct 20, 1917,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  From the Government viewpoint standardisation was viewed as probably lessening considerably the difficulties attendant on cheese-manufacture from high-butterfat-testing milk. <i>(Sep 23, 1931,Dominion,7</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but of hist. sign.] milk solids or solid fat in milk.

>  All scientific efforts to increase the proportion of butterfats over the casein in milk, by feeding, have been unsuccessful .. there ought to be a larger proportion of butterfats on warm fair days than on cold, rainy ones. <i>(Nov 24, 1888,Egmont Star,11</i>)

>  The veriest novice in dairying keeps the heifer calves from his best butter-fat cows to fill the places of the 'boarders.' <i>(Aug 22, 1912,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  It is satisfactory to know that farmers are taking a great interest in herd-testing as a means for increasing the yield of butterfat. [butter-fat] <i>(Oct 1, 1926,NZ Farmer,1335</i>)

### butterfatter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Farmers' representatives] are out for a refund of the butter-fat tax, which the Government says is not a tax. <i>(Jul 3, 1917,Straight Furrow,4</i>)

### butterfish
 
 <i>n.</i> banded parrotfish

>  The Wrasse and Parrotfish are mostly caught outside among the kelp, and with the Spotty are indiscriminately named Kelp-fish by the fishermen, though the term Butterfish is also given to the smaller sorts. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/384</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Banded Parrotfish are also known as Butterfish in some areas. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,33</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BANDED PARROTFISH [<i>Pseudolabrus pittensis</i>] ... Also called kelpie, kelpfish, and sometimes butterfish. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,22</i>)

>  The Wrasse and Parrotfish are mostly caught outside among the kelp, and with the Spotty are indiscriminately named Kelp-fish by the fishermen, though the term Butterfish is also given to the smaller sorts. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/384</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]



 
 <i>n.</i> banded parrotfish; deepsea butterfish (fishshop name?).

>  PSEUDOLABRUS CINCTUS (Hutton). <i>Girdled Parrot-fish</i>. Odd examples are exposed for sale in Wellington during the later summer and winter months. Sold as `deep-sea butterfish.' ... Distribution: Unknown north of Napier. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/116</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GIRDLED PARROT-FISH <i>Pseudolabrus cinctus</i> (Hutton) ... / This fish was sold on the Wellington Fish Market as `deep sea Butterfish', inferring that it is the Greenbone (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>). It has no relation to Greenbone, nor is it as valuable a fish food as many other fishes. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,275</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> rockfish

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> ROCKFISH ... <i>Other names: Acanthoclinus quadridactylus</i>; rock cod, butterfish; taumaka (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,283</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> spotty

>  We caught ... a larger number of Butter-fish and Kelp-fish; the former with hook and line in Kaipipi bay; the latter in our nets ... <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>) [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]

>  [Native or Common Name] Marare, Butterfish ... [Scientific Name] <i>Labrichthys bothrycosmus</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 9/487) Butterfish is a misnomer. At least the fish so termed here is not the same as the one known by that name in the North. <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  16. The true butterfish was caught several times during the year. It must, however be considered a rare fish in our waters. 17. The spotty, or butterfish of our local fishermen, has been very plentiful this year ... <i>Ibid.</i> 11/384 The Wrasse and Parrotfish are mostly caught outside among the kelp, and with the Spotty are indiscriminately named Kelp-fish by the fishermen, though the term Butterfish is also given to the smaller sorts. <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/.329</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> SPOTTY (<i>Labrichthys bothrycosmus</i>). ... [92] According to Mr. Thompson's observations, the spotty is also known in Dunedin as the butter-fish, as when writing in 1877, he said: `The spotty, or butter-fish of our local fishermen, has been very plentiful this year, and had been brought to town in large numbers at times; was in the market 128 days, as against thirty-four days for last year.' ... adding in the most tantalizing manner possible, `There are two sorts of spotty - a big and a little.' And then, after saying the fishermen called them kelp-fish, writes: `Spotties in market 154 days'. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,91</i>)

>  In New Zealand waters there were two species of closely allied fish with similar feeding habits, locally known as butter-fish (<i>Coridodax pullus</i> and <i>Odax vittatus</i>). ... The New Zealand butter-fish was called `marare'. <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/556</i>)

>  <i>Pseudolabrus bothryocosmus</i>, Richardson. Locally known as `butter-fish' or `spotty'; very common. <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/551</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  PSEUDOLABRUS CELIDOTUS (Forster). <i>Spotty.</i> Common in all shallow water in rocky localities. Large examples are sold as `butterfish' in Dunedin. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/117</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> In Auckland the fish known to South Islanders as Spotty (<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>) is called Butterfish on account of its bright golden colouring on the lower parts of the body and fins fancifully resembling the colour of butter. <i>Ibid.</i> 272 SPOTTY (PAEKIRIKIRI) <i>Pseudolabrus (Lunolabrus) celidotus </i>(Bloch and Schneider) ... [<i>Ibid.</i> 275] The name Spotty is derived from the presence of spots on each side of the fish. The name Butterfish used in the North Island for them is from the yellow or buttery colouring on the undersurface. In Canterbury they are called Guffy, but for what reason I have yet to learn. It is a very pretty fish, more especially when seen in the water. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,261</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The popular name [spotty (<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>) however refers to the general spotty nature of these fishes, and is quite appropriate. It is frequently, but quite incorrectly referred to as the Butterfish, and in Canterbury it is generally known as the `guffy'. <i>Ibid.</i> Spotty Pseudolabrus celidotus Butterfish; Guffy <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,133</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SPOTTY ... <i>Other names: Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>; butterfish, kelpie, guffy. None is widely used. Paketi, pakirikiri (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,257</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SPOTTY [<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>] ... Other names include paketi, kelpie, guffy, butterfish and pakirikiri. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,22</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> SPOTTY The common little Spotty is known to every boy or girl who has even gone fishing along the tide line. ... Other names are kelpie, guffy, butterfish, pakirikiri and paketi (Maori). Gets its most common name from a large, black spot on its upper side. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,207</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Spotty Pseudolabrus celidotus</i> Alternative names include paketi, kelpie, and butterfish (the last two being misleading). Restricted to New Zealand ... The girdled wrasse, P. cinctus, is rather similar to the spotty but grows larger ... It occurs around the South Island but is not common. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,107</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> n2 (Stewart Island) Parochialism runs strong in most of us, and many will note indignantly the absence of a familiar local name. The difficulty is that Stewart Island butterfish = Wellington spotty, while Wellington butterfish = Stewart Island greenbone. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,207</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> any of a variety of fishes having a slippery coating of mucus ... 1880 Gunther.] A green-boned edible fish found most commonly in southern waters; also called greenbone, marari, and occasionally kelp-fish; greenbone, (southern) kelpfish, kelp-salmon, koaea, marari (marare). Any of several fishes having a slippery (`buttery') coating of mucus of the families Odacidae (the main use), Labridae (wrasses), and Centrolophidae (raftfishes). a. (i) [Name poss. reinforced in NZ by the light buttery (NZ butter is of a very light whitish yellow) colour and softness of the flesh.]Odax pullus, also listed as <i>Coridodax pullus, Odax vittatus</i> (juveniles), a seaweed-eating fish of rocky coasts, larger and more common in s. NZ; a fine food-fish; also called greenbone, kelp-fish, kelpie, kelp salmon, marari (marare).

>  I have just drowned myself in the same treatment given to soft, white, taste-of-the-sea butterfish fillets. <i>(Jun 5, 2004,Dominion,E8</i>)

>  This was feats enough but a boat working pots further offshore hailed us and slung a surprise present in a sack - two crayfish and a butterfish. <i>(Feb 5, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> He and Ted would spend hours.. fishing for 'spotties', other names are butterfish or kelpie or.. Pakirikiri. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,105</i>)

>  There are worse jobs, I cheerfully assured myself the other day, splattering my face and chest while wrenching the guts from a butterfish. <i>(Sep 21, 1999,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Butterfish</i>A small family (Odacidae) of Australasian shallow- water, weed dwelling and eating fishes, sometimes called kelpfishes or weed whitings. Internationally the name butterfish is used for the family to which our warehou belong, but in New Zealand the name is so firmly associated with the species described below that a change is unlikely. <i>Odax pullus</i> ... An alternative name is greenbone. Restricted to New Zealand ... [check also PAEKIRIKIRI 1843] <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,110</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 71. BUTTER FISH. ... The Marare of the natives, known as the Kelp Fish among the fishermen and the Butter Fish in the market (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>), is the fish most commonly sold in Wellington during the winter ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,114</i>)

>  16. The true butterfish was caught several times during the year. It must, however be considered a rare fish in our waters. 17. The spotty, or butterfish of our local fishermen, has been very plentiful this year ... <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/329</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>AYLING</b> BUTTERFISH (Greenbone, marari) Odax pullus (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>, Odax vittatus) ... Small individuals have a markedly different colour pattern from that of the adults and until recently were thought to belong to a separate species named the kelpfish, [266] Odax vittatus. These young fish are yellow-brown with a line of white dashes running down each side ... [Butterfish] are an excellent eating fish, with delicate tasty flesh that prompted the name butterfish ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,265,1984 ed.</i>)

>  Butterfish is a misnomer. At least the fish so termed here is not the same as the one known by that name in the North. <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/487</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  35. <i>Coridodax pullus</i> Forster. Kelpfish, Butterfish, or Greenbone. Maori: Marari <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/145</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i>: Butter-fish (Wellington and Marlborough), green-bone or kelp-fish (Otago). <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Forster). Greenbone (butterfish, kelp-fish, kelp-salmon). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/412</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Marari (or marare in other provinces) - the butter-fish, or kelp fish. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,61</i>)

>  <i>butterfish</i>: edible fish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> BUTTERFISH (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>), Marari of the Maoris, is a kelp fish found throughout New Zealand, but most commonly in the South. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,69</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BUTTERFISH ... <i>Other names: Coridodax pullus</i>; greenbone; marari, rari (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,256</i>)

>  <i>butter fish n</i>. edible fish, the kelp-fish of N.Z. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  Now his own children swim in the sea, fritter away the day, catch some butterfish for tea, go to bed early. <i>(Jan 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> There is a dark-coloured, ugly-looking fish called the butter-fish, known by the fishermen as the kelp-fish. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,297</i>)

>  <b>GUNTHER</b> The `butter-fish' or `kelp-fish' of the colonists of New Zealand (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>) is prized as food, and attains to a weight of four or five pounds. <i>(1880,Study of Fishes,533</i>) [NOTES: [also OED - with diff quot][Morris]]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> [check ?p.12] BUTTER FISH (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>). ... [14] Known by many as the kelp-fish, and by the fishermen in some places as the butter-fish. Dr. Knox, in the <i>Transactions of the New Zealand Institute</i>, gives the following interesting details of the marare of the Natives : `On the 2nd August, 1870, a large quantity of the kelp-fish were offered for sale in and about Wellington, and though by no means prepossessing in external appearance, being of a dingy-black colour, and covered with a slimy mucus, I found that the fish frequent Cook Strait, more especially off and around the Mana.' <i>Ibid.</i> 91<i>SPOTTY (Labrichthys bothrycosmus)</i>. ... [92] According to Mr. Thompson's observations, the spotty is also known in Dunedin as the butter-fish ... <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,13</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The greater part of the spoil was butter-fish, ... and it was certainly the first fish in the Colony ... worth eating, for, excepting frost-fish, [most have little flavour]. <i>(Jan 31, 1894,In the Land of the Tui,148</i>)

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are confined to the south. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  8.<i>Pencarrow Heads.</i> - Butter-fish (kelp-fish) ... <i>Ibid.</i> 204 12. <i>The Brothers (Cook strait).</i> - Rock-cod, spotty, butter-fish, red-cod. ... 16. <i>Moeraki</i>. - Rock-cod, gurnard, parrot-fish, butter-fish, sprats, conger-eel ... 24. Puysegur Point - Hapuku, kahawai, trumpeter, moki, pig-fish, trevally, rock-cod, perch, butter-fish, red-cod, dog-fish ...27. <i>Bligh Sound </i>- John-dory, rock-cod, wrasse, butter-fish. <i>Ibid.</i> 212 Butter-fish or Kelp-fish (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>). NUMBER OF FISH RECORDED - 365. LOCALITIES - Pencarrow Head, the Brothers, Moeraki, Taiaroa Head, Cape Saunders, Catlin's River, Dog Island, Centre Island, Puysegur Point (where it is almost the only fish recorded), Bligh Sound, Milford Sound. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  Moki are very plentiful, but we only get a few trumpeter now and then. Of course, the cod and groper are plentiful, also butter-fish and barracouta. ... When we see the mackerel splashing along we know they are after squid; the mullet, latris, and parrot-fish are always after them, and even the moki and butter-fish join the hunt. <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/53</i>) [NOTES: (Henry)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Butter-fish, n.</i> a name given in Australia to <i>Oligorus mitchellii</i>, Castln. (see <i>Murray Perch</i>); in Victoria, to <i>Chilodactylus nigricans</i>, Richards. (see <i>Morwong</i>); in New Zealand, to <i>Coridodax pullus</i> Forst., called also <i>kelp-fish</i>. The name is in allusion to their slippery coating of mucus. See <i>Kelp-fish</i>. 1850. <i>(1898,Austral English,74</i>)

>  In New Zealand waters there were two species of closely allied fish with similar feeding habits, locally known as butter-fish (<i>Coridodax pullus</i> and <i>Odax vittatus</i>). ... The New Zealand butter-fish was called `marare'. <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/556</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. <i>Ibid.</i> 73 The butterfish lives amongst kelp, or sea-weed, and is generally found where kelp is plentiful. Neither it nor the moki takes bait freely. <i>Ibid.</i> 74 ...the butterfish and the red cod, which are not known anywhere else. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Snapper, <i>warehou</i>, butterfish, trumpeter, <i>moki,</i> flat-fish (<i>patiki</i>), and other fine sea fish were plentiful. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,107</i>)

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i> ... Butter Fish. <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/227</i>)

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i> Forster. The butter-fish, kelp-fish or kelp-salmon, as it is variously called, is very common in Otago Harbour. It is wrongly identified in my previous list as <i>Odax vittatus</i> Solander. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/231</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  Butterfish. <i>Coridodax pullus</i> ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/271</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  CORIDODAX PULLUS (Forster). <i>Butterfish; Marari</i>. Sold as `greenbone' or `kelp-salmon' in Dunedin. An outside fish secured in the vicinity of kelp around the northern coasts during most months of the year. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/117</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Forster). BUTTERFISH, SOUTHERN KELP-FISH; MARARI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,40,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The Trumpeter is rare, but its close relative the Moki (<i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i>) is secured in good numbers near the kelp, as also are Butterfish (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>), and Trevally (<i>Longirostrum platessa</i>). <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,199</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 199]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GREENBONE, BUTTERFISH, KELP SALMON, or KELPIE (MARARI) <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... The name Greenbone is given on account of the bones being a bright green colour, which is so permanent as to with-stand boiling or bleaching. ... The name kelp-fish is given on account of its habit of feeding on kelp or seaweed, and also because it likes to make its home among kelp. In such a habitat it has both food and a favourite home. Kelp-salmon is given for two reasons. The first part, kelp, is explained in the last paragraph, while the second half, salmon, is because the fish is not unlike a Salmon in shape, so much so that new chums who caught this fish have been under the impression they had caught the protected introduced Salmon, but of that later in this chapter. But why this dark coloured fish should receive the name Butterfish I am unable to say. It is certainly not as some people suggest because it needs butter with it when cooked or eaten. In Auckland the fish known to South Islanders as Spotty (<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>) is called Butterfish on account of its bright golden colouring on the lower parts of the body and fins fancifully resembling the colour of butter. The name Kelpie, of course, is derived from its habit of living amidst kelp and it is easily understood how the `ie' was tacked on by fishermen. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,261</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Butterfish is very similar in form, and is related to, the Kelpfish (<i>Odax vittatus</i>), which is also a brightly-coloured species and found in similar situations, feeding on kelp and other marine plants in rocky localities. <i>Ibid.</i> 130 It is unfortunate that the Butterfish has been given a variety of local names which have originated either from their habit of associating with kelp, their body form, or from the colour of their bones, as for example, Kelpfish, Kelp-salmon, Greenbone, and several other names not so easy to explain. Butterfish or Greenbone are possibly the more generally used names for this fish but it should be remembered that in the North Island the Spotty (<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>), a very different fish, is related to the Butterfish there is superficially little resemblance between the two species. <i>Ibid.</i> 169 Butterfish Coridodax pullus Greenbone; Kelp Salmon; Kelpi; Kelpfish <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,129</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Butterfish, greenbone .. koaea, marari .. <i>Coridodax pullus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>butter fish n.</i> edible fish, the kelp-fish of New Zealand. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins)</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> [=GREENBONE (Stewart Island)] Parochialism runs strong in most of us, and many will note indignantly the absence of a familiar local name. The difficulty is that Stewart Island butterfish = Wellington spotty, while Wellington butterfish = Stewart Island greenbone. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,207</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BUTTERFISH [<i>Coridodax pullus</i>] ... The term greenbone is widely used in southern waters referring to the bone colour. The Maori names marari, and rari are seldom used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,28</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>BUTTERFISH</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Coridodax pullus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Butterfish; Greenbone. (c) Other common names: Southen Kelpfish. (d) Principal localities where taken: Wellington, Castlepoint, Makara, Paremata, Nelson, Picton, Bluff and Stewart Island. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,14</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> KELPIE A name given variously to the Spotty, Banded Parrotfish, Kelpfish, Butterfish and Greenbone. Generally regarded as the other proper name for Butterfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,140</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BUTTERFISH <i>Coridodax pullus</i> A common and delicious shallow-water coastal dweller the Butterfish is also well-known as the Greenbone. This because his bones (depending on where he has been feeding) can range in colour from green to almost coal black. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,33</i>)

>  <i>butterfish</i> NZ edible sea-fish with green bones. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,97</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 149; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 65]]

>  [species name] butterfish .. [scientific name] <i>odax pullus</i> .. [other name(s)] greenbone, southern kelpfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Butterfish Odax pullus</i> ... An alternative name is greenbone. Restricted to New Zealand ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,110</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> greenbone, (northern) kelpfish, wrasse; an edible kelp-feeding marine fish, <i>Odax pullus</i>, also known as kelpfish and marari.

>  Found in shallow water on the coast and offshore islands, and caught in small commercial quantities in Cook Strait, butterfish are cold-blooded, live in cold water and feed mainly on kelp. <i>(Mar 9, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i> Forster. The butter-fish, kelp-fish or kelp-salmon, as it is variously called, is very common in Otago Harbour. It is wrongly identified in my previous list as Odax vittatus Solander. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/231</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Kelpfish Odax vittatus Butterfish; Greenbone; Northern Kelpfish <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BUTTERFISH</i> (Greenbone, marari) <i>Odax pullus (Coridodax pullus, Odax vittatus</i>) ... Small individuals have a markedly different colour pattern from that of the adults and until recently were thought to belong to a separate species named the kelpfish, [266] <i>Odax vittatus</i>. These young fish are yellow-brown with a line of white dashes running down each side ... [Butterfish] are an excellent eating fish, with delicate tasty flesh that prompted the name butterfish ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,265</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Butterfish</i> A small family (Odacidae) of Australasian shallow- water, weed dwelling and eating fishes, sometimes called kelpfishes or weed whitings. Internationally the name butterfish is used for the family to which our warehou belong, but in New Zealand the name is so firmly associated with the species described below that a change is unlikely. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,110</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] butterfish 132, 1683, 1697, 1720, 1732, 1745, 1753-60 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Odax cyanoallix</i>. a n. sp. found only at Three Kings Islands, mature males having conspicuously blue-fringed fins.

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLUE-FINNED BUTTERFISH Odax cyanoallix</i> ... This unusual species of butterfish was first discovered in 1978 and has not yet been given a proper scientific name. <i>Iid.</i> 267 [267]One of [its] most unusual features ... is that it is ... found only around Three Kings Islands ... .[Name poss. reinforced by its yellowish undersurface.] <i>(1982,Collins Guide,266,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Schedophilus maculatus.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>PELAGIC BUTTERFISH Schedophilus maculatus </i>... Until recently the pelagic butterfish was known only from juvenile specimens less than 10cm long ... This species is apparently found in most southern subtropical seas but is nowhere common. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,303,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> ??a freshwater fish ?genus [may be a local or idiolectal name].

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The creeks [up-country from Raglan c1914-1018] were full of eels, butterfish and crawlies (freshwater crayfish), so we never lacked in a variety of foods. <i>Ibid.</i> 29 While washing was being completed we youngsters would catch butterfish and crawlies in the nearby creek to roast in the hot embers under the boiling clothes. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,27</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> occas. <i>Notolabrus fucicola</i>, also listed as <i>Pseudolabrus pittensis </i>(banded parrotfish); banded wrasse

>  The Wrasse and Parrotfish are mostly caught outside among the kelp, and with the Spotty are indiscriminately named Kelp-fish by the fishermen, though the term Butterfish is also given to the smaller sorts. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/384</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Banded Parrotfish are also known as Butterfish in some areas. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,33</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BANDED PARROTFISH [<i>Pseudolabrus pittensis</i>] ... Also called kelpie, kelpfish, and sometimes butterfish. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,22</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> The warehou family (Centralophidae) contains ... fishes known as raftfishes ... as ruffes ... or as butterfishes ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,136</i>)

>  ... with regard to their currency some of them live by flying butterflies, pieces of paper of peculiar form and stamp ... <i>(1868,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,67</i>)

### butterfly
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Monarch butterflies arrived in New Zealand shortly after their food - the swan plant (<i>Asclepias fruticosa</i>) arrived - maybe 150 years ago. <i>(Jun 15, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  New Zealand's orange and black monarch butterflies are at risk of starving after stripping bare their favourite fodder - swan plants - but garden owners have been told "not to worry". <i>(Feb 25, 2001,Dominion,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Larvae of the tussock butterfly <i>Argyrophenga janitae</i> are just one of the many species to feed in tussock. The adult butterflies are orange and brown with black and white spots on the wings. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/34</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> He found a fern, some snails and moths, or perhaps tussock butterflies, but not the specimens he wanted. <i>(1978,Acheron Journal,96</i>)

>  <b>GIBBS</b> These have been recognised as a result of recent studies on the variability within the tussock butterfly (<i>Argyrophenga</i>) ... . <i>(1980,NZ Butterflies,22</i>)

>  ...the larvae of tussock butterflies (<i>Argyrophenga</i> spp.) feed on silver and tall tussocks, whereas Butler's ringlets (<i>Erebiola butleri</i>) sticks to low alpine <i>Chionochloa</i> species in the wetter western South Island mountains. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/43</i>)

>  ...the larvae of tussock butterflies (<i>Argyrophenga</i> spp.) feed on silver and tall tussocks, whereas Butler's ringlets (<i>Erebiola butleri</i>) sticks to low alpine <i>Chionochloa</i> species in the wetter western South Island mountains. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/43</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  With the advent of warmer weather the white butterfly is reappearing in most parts of New Zealand. <i>(Nov 20, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/178 : 5</i>)

>  The marked absence of the white butterfly pest in Hawke's Bay this season is thought to be due to the destruction of the larvae by volcanic ash from Ruapehu. <i>(Dec 3, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/232 : 1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In its native Australia, it goes under the ugly name of "common eggfly", but, here, we call it a "blue moon" butterfly because we so seldom see it. <i>(May 24, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to split down the middle

>  <b>GREIG & HADDAD</b> Butterfly - To split a food, such as shelled prawns or piece of pork, down the centre almost completely through, and flattening it out so that the food resembles a butterfly <i>(2000,NZ Cook's Dictionary, The,72</i>)

>  Or butterflied roast lamb and parsnips on barley almond pilaf... <i>(Oct 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an oilskin cape, fitting over shoulders and leaving the arms free of movement ?short for butterfly cape. [ca1910 W.H.B. Orsman Havelock]

>  Spectrum Documentary: G.W. Johnson's working life 1940-46 Butterfly capes - still occasionally see them - just cover the back - straps cross across in front of you - they keep the back dry. <i>(Nov 12, 1983,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 2ZA Wellington 9.15 am:]

>  <b>H SMITH</b> A `butterfly' to shade them [sc. the miners] [fn] `An oilskin coat' <i>(1946,Poetical Works,261</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bent penny which does not turn over when thrown from a kip.

>  He was throwing butterflies. <i>(Oct 29, 1984,TV1</i>) [NOTES: 1984 Oct 29 Country GP TV1]



 
 <i>n.</i> a marble with coloured pattern thus like a butterfly through the marble



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> One species [of chiton] <i>Cryptoconchus porosus</i>, has beautiful greenish-blue internal valves. These are the `butterflies' so keenly sought by amateur collectors. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/340</i>)

 <i>n.</i> buddleia.

>  <b>10</b> <i>butterfly bush [=] buddleia</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds</i>)

### butterfly cake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On the cover of The Picnic Virgin there is a photograph of a butterfly cake - once a staple of suburban culture, now translated in to the realm of the kitsch and set up in the frame of the ironic. <i>(Sep 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  The most exotic of these little cakes were the butterfly cakes, with home-made jam, whipped cream and little wedges of sponge cake to represent the wings. <i>(Jan 12, 1993,Dominion,6</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Pterois volitans</i>, aka lionfish

### butterfly fish
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i>; (also name of a fish in Europe).

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 30. GASTEROCHISMA MELAMPUS. Rich. Butterfly Fish. <i>(1872,Fishes,20</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON & HECTOR</b> Butterfly Fish (G. melampus) in New Zealand only. <i>(1872,Fishes,20</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i> Rich. Butterfly-fish <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,301</i>)

>  <i>Butterfly-fish</i>, ... New Zealand sea-fish, <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i>, Richards, one of the <i>Nomeidae</i>. The ventral fins are exceedingly broad and long, and can be completely concealed in a fold of the abdomen. The New Zealand fish is so named from these fins; the European <i>Butterfly-fish, Blennius ocellaris</i>, derives its name from the spots on its dorsal fin, like the eyes in a peacock's tail or butterfly's wing. <i>(1898,Austral English,74</i>)

>  <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i> Richardson. The `butterfly-fish', as it is called when young, has been taken in Otago Harbour, and is now known to be the young of the scaled tunny, described as <i>Lepidothynnus huttoni</i> Gunther. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/230</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i> Richardson. BUTTERFLY-FISH. <i>(1927,TrNZI,45/230</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i> Richardson. BUTTERFLY-FISH. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,45,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Butterfly Fish <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i> Richardson) (Syn. <i>Lepidothynnus huttoni </i>Gunther) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,410</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 410]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BUTTERFLY-FISH or SCALED TUNNY <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i> Richardson <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,301</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE BUTTERFLY FISH <i>Gasterochisma melampus (Richardson)</i> ...The Butterfly Fish is also known as the Scaled Tunny, and is closely related to the Bonito and Albacore which all belong to the true Mackerel family the <i>Scombridae. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 169 Butterfly-fish Gasterochisma melampus Scaled Tunny <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,156</i>)

>  <i>butterfly-fish n</i>. New Zealand sea fish with broad ventral fins. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),612</i>)

>  <i>butterfly-fish n</i>. N.Z. sea fish with broad ventral fins. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  [species name] butterfly tuna .. [scientific name] <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i> .. [other name(s)] butterfly fish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] butterflyfish 2052-3 BUTTERFLYFISHES 1524-5 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### butterfly perch
 
 <i>n.</i> Also known as red perch; brightly and variably coloured; common around northern NZ. [<i>Caesioperca lepidoptera</i> aka red perch, red snapper, St. Peter's Fish, or bastard long-fin'] (See [butterfly perch](bri-byo.md#butterfly-perch))

>  Butterfly Perch Image by Vince Kerr .. Motukaroro - Whangarei Harbour Marine Reserve. Butterfly Perch (<i>Caesioperca lepidoptera</i>) feed on plankton - mainly small crustaceans and salps in the current. The rich reef and strong current at Motukaroro provides them with ideal habitat. <i>(Jul 17, 2007,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.marinenz.org.nz/index.php/resources/image_detail/butterfly_perch4/]

### butting order/bunting order
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the order cows develop for entering the cowshed, drinking at troughs etc.

>  .. cows lose the bunting instinct because they can not remember the bunting order any more and the boss cow system is broken down. <i>(Aug 1972,NZ Dairy Exporter,71</i>)

>  Butting order: the order in which cows line up for milking or feeding. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### buttinski
 
 <i>v.</i> to butt in or interrupt

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Buttinski</i>. v.i. To interrupt, esp. when one's presence is undesired: NZ soldiers': 1915. -->(also ca1941: a person who interrupts others); AC1924; W3; WF <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,115</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>buttinski</i> to butt in or interrupt, usually not welcomed; Kiwi soldiers 1915, a mock coinage; subsequently travelled to Australia, and heard by author in American TV series <i>MASH. </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)

### button
 
 <i>v.</i> to tout [as noun a person who acts as an enticer in swindles (so P8: 1840-1940)]

>  The [racing] club had one of its employees `buttoning' for the machine. He stood beside the machine's notice board informing all and sundry that `Now's yer time, only half a minute more,' and a bit of such vernacular. <i>(Jan 11, 1908,NZ Truth,124/2/6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the urethra of wethers.

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> Wethers should be shorn around the 'button' - too much wool about that organ frequently causes scald and subsequent inflammation. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,22</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the part of a lemon where the fruit is fastened to the stem.

>  Picking should be done by means of a specially designed sharp shears, and the stems of the fruit should be cut in such a way as to avoid injuring the "button" (at the same time avoiding the danger of injuring other fruit) by leaving no stem-spike. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,210</i>)

>  What about the picking bag? Do you empty it frequently or is it full of "buttons" and small twigs? <i>(Aug 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

### button fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Lastreopsis glabella</i> and the button fern (<i>Pellaea rotundifolia</i> are found among the roots of large trees and on rocky ground. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/76</i>)

### button-eye
 
 <i>n.</i> a silvereye

>  Button-eye, a wandering bird (Zosterops halmaturina), blight-bird; twinkie; button-eye; wax-eye; ring-eye. Tauhou means stranger, and this name was given by the Maori when the bird appeared, in 1856, in the central part of New Zealand, gradually spreading north and south till the the islands were occupied. It is an Australian bird, and was blown across the Tasman sea in the year mentioned. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Andersen)]

### button-pusher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Button-pushers, or `turkeys' as they are known in CB jargon, hold down the buttons on their CB sets and play music or use abusive language. Their actons prevent other CB operators from using the frequency. <i>(Jul 20, 1987,Evening Post,2</i>)

### buttoner
 
 <i>n.</i> the accomplice of a confidence man.

>  And the [Auctioneers Amendment] Bill could also have been improved by a clause or two dealing with `buttoners' the individuals employed, mostly at furniture sales, to run up prices. <i>(Nov 26, 1910,NZ Truth,283/4/6</i>)

### buttonhole
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>buttonhole</i> to crutch sheep's sensitive areas; from `buttonhole' as vagina or penis. <i>[Shepard] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to part-crutch.

>  The ewes and hoggets are button-holed and eye-clipped before the rams go out in April, and crutched in August, just before lambing. <i>(Oct 9, 1952,NZ Farmer,14</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> buttonhole: to crutch sheep's sensitive areas; from 'buttonhole' as vagina or penis. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> A flock so treated will remain sound in the feet and clean in the crutch through the autumn flush of feed. The same job, done in the yards when the ewes are 'button-holed' with the shears ... <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,35</i>)

### buttonholing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) part-crutching sheep, particularly around the belly area, prior to tupping.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. at one time they employed the full gang of ten men for the button-holing and second shearing, putting through anything up to 5,000 sheep a day. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,110</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> button-holing: part-crutching. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,171</i>)

>  <b>CROSS</b> If he could stick it he graduated to real work - dipping in February, dagging, footrotting, buttonholing (part crutch) when the rams went out ... <i>(1990,NZ Agriculture,59</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### butty
 
 <i>n.</i> a slice of bread and butter, a sandwich [also north of England]

>  I also have a report from the North Shore of the continued presence of jam butties (ie thickly-cut jam sandwiches), and one lady assures me that one of the great treats of her childhood was a cold-chip butty, which was a sandwich made with the left-overs of the Friday night fish and chips. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>) [NOTES: (I.A.Gordon)]

### Buy Kiwi Made
 
 <i>n.</i> (Commerce) a movement started in 1908 to promote the purchase of New Zealand-made goods and the use of Mew Zealand services.

>  This April marks 100 years of the Buy Kiwi Made movement, as well as the 20th birthday of private organisation Buy NZ Made (whose member companies label products with that familiar kiwi-in-the -triangle logo that tells shoppers goods are produced locally).. Buy Kiwi Made Government spokeswoman Sue Bradford says the campaign's biggest success has been the growing numbers of New Zealanders aware of their purchasing power. <i>(Apr 2008,North & South,84</i>)

### buyer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] stock agent who arranges purchase and sale of livestock or a meat buyer who arranges purchase of stock for slaughter.

>  person who selects stock for sale: buyer. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### buzz
 
 <i>n.</i> a kick, a thrill

>  Mr Scully said that, when Soutar was asked for an explanation, she stated she was `having a buzz'. <i>(Oct 2, 1987,Dominion,3</i>)

>  [Blue is a Mongrel Mob gang member] When he joined gang members used blades but today's weapon was the gun and 14-year-olds were not mature enough for this. `You do not want a 14-year-old boy with a gun in his hand. That is not the buzz,' he said. ... Blue said he thought the mobs' `buzz' was the best it had been for 20 years. `We have our own little buzz. You cannot describe it. It's living life to the hilt.' <i>(May 7, 1986,Evening Post,1</i>)

### Buzz Bar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  New Zealanders have a long standing love affair with the bewitching combination of chocolate and marshmallow. Many of our favourite sweets are based on this happy pairing: in addition to Easter eggs we have the Buzz Bar, the chocolate fish, the Toastie and the Pinky Bar. <i>(Apr 8, 2004,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  The Buzz bar bash - Steve McDowell versus Mark Bourneville - has been talked up all night. <i>(May 5, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Buzz Bars on the ice. Just as they always were, unwrapped, sticky, so good we send them halfway round the world. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,60</i>)

### buzz out
 
 <i>v.</i> be excited

>  When I was at the awards I was just buzzing out, sitting in the crowd with all these famous people... and then they called my name out and I had to walk down those big steps. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 51</i>)

### buzzer
 
 <i>n.</i> ?A nonce word, or a general word applied here to a motorbike, or spec. for an old motorbike.

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Gus hasn't got a lady friend just for the time, and sooner than go by himself, he wants me to ride in his car [other texts `cart'] with him to the next dance ... when Gus arrived with his buzzer at my gate, I just slipped on an overcoat over the rig-out, and stepped into the side-car. <i>(1924,Me and Gus</i>) [NOTES: c1924 Anthony Me and Gus (1977 Gus Tomlins ed Sturm) 11]



 
 <i>n.</i> a (school) dentist's drill [ca1938]

### Buzzy Bee
 
 <i>n.</i> a classic wooden New Zealand pullalong toy [NZ wooden toy check patents for date.]

>  Other picks [for inclusion] include a Buzzy Bee circling parliament by Geogre [sic] Baloghy... <i>(Jan 21, 2004,Capital Times,15</i>)

>  Buzzy bee, made from 1948 by Auckland company H.E. Ramsay, was copied from a toy brought from the United states, the Ramsay family confirmed yesterday... A worker at the factory in 1948 brought an American Buzzy bee toy to Mr Ramsay, and suggested something similar be made...The American Buzzy bee was flat-sided, about one and a half inches thick, made from timber with a metal clacker and covered by painted paper. That accurately describes the Fisher Price bee, which Oamaru man Gerald Lynch-Blosse bought in Arkansas about five years ago. <i>(Jul 9, 1999,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  The Buzzy Bee is our greatest national icon. It is in the great book of icons. It cannot now be un-iconised. <i>(Jul 10, 1999,Dominion,16</i>)

>  Although making it clear he didn't want a "buzzy bee in every shot" Hurst went after some of New Zealand's iconic images - "swathes of green hills, fence lines, Robin Morrison-type pictures". <i>(Apr 2000,North & South,25</i>)

>  You love those rarely-seen-on-TV curves, that determinedly New Zealand accent, that Cyndi-Lauper-meets-Buzzy Bee smile, her unabashed, well ... ordinariness. <i>(Nov 14, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),68</i>)

>  There are images as Kiwi as Marmite and Buzzy Bee. <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  A noisy wooden bug with a daft clicker, Buzzy Bee has enjoyed a 61-year reign as New Zealand's favourite toy. and it has the cult status of Weetbix, Marmite and gumboots. <i>(Sep 5, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

>  Mention culture, they say, and it's beginning to sound too important, the role of Buzzy Bee or Wattie's peas in our national psyche notwithstanding. <i>(Aug 3, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Meanwhile, the Historic Places Trust has given the mill the best protection it can, a category one listing. It says Sergeant Dan is as well-known an image as Buzzy Bee or the Edmonds Baking Powder tin. <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  The Buzzy Bee, you might think, is a simple enough toy... [He] had intended to make most of his returns on franchising the Buzzy Bee label. <i>(Aug 30, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,9</i>)

>  <b>RENeE</b> There were, no doubt, sniggerers at the launches of Buzzy Bee, Marmite and Swanndri, but look what happened to them. Icons, my dears, icons. <i>(1998,Yin & Tonic,38</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Buzzy Bee</i> although now made in China of some wood other than native tawa, this dinkum Kiwi wooden bumblebee, which has been pulled along by generations of toddlers who have delighted in its clacking wings (once wood, now plastic), was the creation of Auckland toymaker Hec Ramsay back c.1946. Once Prince William declared an interest it became a worldwide plaything. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,18</i>)

>  They reveal that Beehive matches are now made in Sweden and that our old mate Buzzy Bee, that venerated toy of thousands of New Zealand children and developed by Aucklanders Hec and John Ramsey, of Onehunga, is now being manufactured in China. <i>(Dec 1, 1989,NZ Truth,2</i>)

### by corry
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [see by korry/py korry]

>  <b>Stella Morice</b> By corry, Hori would give him a kit of kumara some day to make up for taking his nails. <i>(1944,Book of Wiremu, The</i>)

### by crikey
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  By Crikey, didn't a quid go fast over here. <i>(Sep 6, 1943,NZEF Times,III/115 : 6</i>)

>  I don't want to add to the damned grumbles that are around, but by crikey, it took a fair while!. <i>(Dec 10, 1999,Dominion,2</i>)

>  When asked for an explanation, he astonished his mentors by replying "By criky [sic], no, that's too much like the other job." <i>(Aug 21, 1939,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  "By crikey!" said one of them.. "that the dam good act." <i>(Mar 27, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/40 : 81</i>)

>  <b>6</b> When asked for an explanation, he astonished his mentors by replying "By criky [sic], no, that's too much like the other job." <i>(Aug 21, 1939,Taranaki Herald</i>)

### by gorry/py gorry
 
 <i>exclam.</i> (informal) stage Maori for by golly! (rather than <i>by God!</i>); (HO never heard this used seriously or unconsciously by a Maori) [also by korry/py korry]

>  [Facsimile letter from Maori Girl of 16 in 1892, in `pidgin', ending:] My sister say you fond of little boys and girls well py gorry bimeby I give you plenty /I send you xx/ Hohepa. <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot,(Frontispiece)</i>) [NOTES: [1892] 1924 Gibson Gibbie Galoot]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> When both parties had collected their fish the fishermen, for a joke, would say to the Maoris: `Got many Pigfish today, Ehoa? Let's have a look!' and on examining the kits a Flounder here and there would be seen in with the Pigfish; but the Maori women were not to be out-done and would exclaim with great vehemence: `By gorry, Mister, I never seen him go in, he must have jumped in when I not looking!' <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,355</i>)

>  <b>SANDYS</b> `By gorrie,' Wiri muttered ... <i>(1982,Love and War,153</i>)

>  "Wild," said the Maori, "by gorry, not half as wild as me." <i>(Jul 6, 1937,Morrinsville Star,14</i>)

### by hobnail express
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural, informal) [mustering and hunting] on foot.

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> 'How am I going to get to town? By hobnail express, of course.' <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,55</i>)

>  <b>FARMER</b> This feat was emulated later by helicopter hunters, but Max has the distinction of shooting all of his [deer] by 'hobnail express'. <i>(1994,Best Job Ever, The,10</i>)

### by hokey
 
 <i>exclam.</i> (esp. associated from the 1950s with Selwyn Toogood) also by the hokey

>  [Hdg] By hoki, where's the scampi? NZ First leader Winston Peters has failed to produce promised evidence of corruption in the scampi fishing industry but says he has five "telephone books" of evidence of corruption in 1986 hoki allocations. <i>(May 8, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  "By hokey, Selwyn, we are going to miss you. You were sure one out of the bag." <i>(Mar 5, 2001,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  There was, she says, "the usual biff. 'We don't know how we're going to get on without you Pam, but by hokey we're going to try." <i>(May 27, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  "It's good to be back," screamed an enthusiastic Julia Deans, and by hokey it was good to have her back. <i>(Oct 29, 2001,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  "Probably, though, all of us who were so close to the Royal car this Christmas Eve will remember the Queen and her husband always in New Zealand sunshine, against a delicate blaze of New Zealand flowers. By hokey, New Zealand. <i>(Feb 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `By the hokey,' replied Jake, `we should make Cape Brett before dark.' <i>(1948,Taina,46</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> Sailed out of Papeete with supplies for some of the outer group and, by hokey, what happens? <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,128</i>)

>  By hoki, its good stuff [a play on the fish-name, hoki] <i>(May 1983,NZ Clubman,24</i>)

### by Joves
 
 <i>exclam.</i> Usu. with -s termination in NZ, as a kind of pl. for SE coll (1570); Jove! By Jove!. (RM).

### by korry/py korry
 
 <i>exclam.</i> (informal) a stage-Maori representation of 'by golly' [various spellings b/p k/c/g, rry/rri (etc.); also my korry - see below] (See [by gorry/py gorry](bri-byo.md#by-gorry/py-gorry))

>  <b>FUSSELL</b> My korry te Poneke people werry glad to see Henare Tikitanu in to noo uniform. Ibid. My korry when the ship lie down and get up again all the kai no stop inside. <i>(1917,Letter from Private Henare Tikitanu,5</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> My korry, Maritana.. why you buy t'e frock we see you coming harf-a-mile, allasame like t'e big red mota-bus. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,124</i>) [NOTES: 1989 edition]

>  Py korry, him the very fine, splendid. <i>(Dec 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  The recent Christchurch sittings of the Supreme Court found an astonished Maori charged with an offence against a half-caste girl of sixteen, but the defence put up by Lawyer Donnelly ... so impressed the jury that they returned a verdict of not guilty. Py Korry! <i>(May 27, 1911,NZ Truth,309/4/7</i>)

>  Pai Korry, you do t'e good work. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,161</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,32</i>)

>  <b>REID</b> <i>(1961,Kiwi Laughs, The,12</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,71</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `Netta, you're not a fool, but pai kare! you talk like one sometimes.' <i>(1960,Maori Girl,158</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> `Py korry Bill,' said Ugly Pete. <i>(1964,Watersiders,73</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,200</i>)

>  <b>43</b> This appealed to the Maori, who ... bawled, `By Korri - the blurry good sulky eh?!!' <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm</i>)

>  `The good <i>kai</i>? Jonathan was dismayed. `Py korry, <i>e hoa</i>, I'm very sorry about that.' <i>(1982,Myrtle and Sophia,43</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>py korry</i> by God; pidgin; eg `Py korry, that's a good drop, eh?' NZA, and perhaps only ever in writers' minds. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,89</i>)

>  Py korry, that strange. <i>(Mar 23, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  [Hdg] Pai Corri, te fine Work. <i>(May 10, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/43 : 152</i>)

>  <b>VOGEL</b> Py Kolly ... which represents the Maori's `By Golly' ... <i>(1898,Maori Maid,41</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The `Digger': `One pair, Hori?' `No, by korry, just the one trouser I want ...' <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,112</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> But, by korry! the Maori these days likes better a good jazz band, and electric stoves for cooking the kai. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,30</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> By korry .... (I use the expression that has become traditional in European versions of Maori humour, but I do not vouch for its frequency) <i>(1939,First With the Sun,28</i>)

>  <b>MYERS</b> `By corry!' shouted Huoto ... <i>(1941,Valiant Love,45</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> The huge Maori put the Yank down ... `By Kori,' he said with a kind smile. `That was a cracker yarn, mate. Why not tell that beauty about yer girl-friend in New York again?' <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,41</i>)

>  Come on, Dad, the Pai Korry's are on again. <i>(May 22, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,17</i>)

### by the limping cricket
 
 <i>exclam.</i> (informal)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>by the limping cricket</i> agreeable or exasperated oath akin to `by thunder' or `by crikey', used by Kaipara area 1920s; eg `By the limping cricket, you're a useless darned goory.' <i>[Jean and Jim Henderson] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)

### by the thundering sardine
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>by the thundering sardine</i> agreeable or mock horror oath of the `by crikey' variety; eg `By the thundering sardine, that's a crop and a half of spuds you've got this year.' <i>[Jean Henderson] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)

### BYO
 
 <i>abbr.</i> bring your own: the usual notice at restaurants not fully licensed to sell liquor.

>  Most of our cities and even small towns have restaurants that offer and wine service and / or allow patrons to bring their own wine. Not many diners know that the BYO concept originated in Australia and New Zealand during the 1960s and 70s and is rarely found elsewhere. The antipodean origin is acknowledged in the authoritative Oxford Companion to Wine. <i>(Apr 25, 1999,NZ Catholic,17</i>)

>  The interior recalls an almost vanished era - that of the BYO bistro. <i>(Nov 30, 1999,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  We stopped BYO for larger groups two years ago. All they were interested in doing was bringing in a couple of cases of booze and having a party at our expense... You want BYO, he says, then you go to a restaurant, probably an ethnic restaurant or cafe, where you get good cheap food that doesn't take a great deal of preparation and few staff. That's the sort of restaurant that can afford to do BYO. <i>(Dec 18, 1999,Dominion,23</i>)

>  They had that BYO sort of look about them and let me bare all. It's that sort of place. <i>(Jul 19, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

>  I don't want to buy into a punch-up over the BYO syndrome, but this tell-tale brown-bag routine is something I shy away from. It's pretty much an Australian and New Zealand thing to ferry our own drinkies into restaurants, sometimes even transporting them in a designer bag, suitably labelled, and drawing attention to the fact that l'il ole me is bringing my own. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Dominion,23</i>)

>  Inside, on the walls, are black and white photographs of early Plimmerton, and the cafe is one of the last of a species fast facing extinction in central Wellington - the BYO restaurant. Twenty-five years ago, "BYO restaurant" had exciting, avant-garde connotations of a new breed of restauranteur, intent on providing a hip, casual alternative to the handful of stiff, expensive, fully licensed fine dining restaurants which, aspart from hotels and a scattering of tired old steak and chip grill-roomns, previously comprised the sum total of the New Zealand dining-out experience. <i>(Jun 5, 2003,Dominion Post,D4</i>)

>  <b>WILKINS</b> "What is it, Henry?" she said. "Irene and her b.y.o. handbag?" <i>(1990,Veteran Perils,139</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> There was a BYO restaurant, but [152] I had not brought my own booze. <i>(1985,G'day Country,151</i>)

>  BYO ad functions illegal, police say ... Senior Sergeant Max Moore said this when commenting on recent advertisements on radio and newspapers in Oamaru advertising functions as `bring your own liquor.' <i>Ibid.</i> 14 Dining with members of the Collard family at Chatterleys byo restaurant in Auckland recently was a truly gastronomic and vinous experience. <i>(Jul 11, 1986,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> A ute boot sale, BYO gumboot throwing, raffles ... [at School Gala day]. <i>(1988,More from Moaville,45</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>BYO</i> acronym for bring your own, usually meaning the wine you take to an unlicensed restaurant. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)

>  FED UP. 244 Ponsonby Rd. BYO. No bookings. Small, crowded with drabbies. Menu long overdue for a change, but still good value. <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,20</i>)

>  <i>Progressive Dinner:</i> Begins at 5 Mount St at 6 pm for Drinks and then to Kohanga for Mains and on and on!! BYO and $2.00 to cover costs. <i>(Mar 21, 1986,Victoria Univ. Weekly Staff Circular,No., 782</i>)

>  On 22 February at 11 am the Victoria Child Care Centre is holding a party brunch at the Centre to farewell Maggie Haggerty ... All past and present children and adults welcome. Please B.Y.O. and a plate of nibbles. <i>(Feb 13, 1987,Victoria Univ. Weekly Staff Circular</i>)

>  Sale of Liquor Act... 61. Liquor permit for unlicensed restaurant... The Chairman of the Licensing Committee.. may at any time grant to any person conducting the business of a restaurateur in any unlicensed restaurant.. a permit authorising the consumption of liquor in the restaurant by any person who is there for the purpose of partaking of a meal and who brings the liquor with him into the restaurant. <i>(1978,NZ Statutes,I 53/681</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a BYO restaurant

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> The breakaway group hadn't been able to decide whether to go to a licensed restaurant or a B.Y.O ... <i>(1985,Secret Diary of a Telephonist,157</i>)

>  There have been times when I have spent a whole five minutes deciding which BYO to go out to, and another half hour deciding which wine to take. <i>(Sep 8, 1986,Campus News,5</i>)

>  <b>4</b> It has always surprised me that more restaurants don't open them - particularly the smaller BYOs which are mainly owner-operated. <i>(Dec 8, 1989,Contact (Wgtn)</i>)

>  The menu of Le Saucier, the new BYO in lower Cuba Street, more or less speaks for itself. <i>(May 1986,Wellington City Magazine,86</i>)

### BYOB
 
 <i>abbr.</i> bring your own bottle. [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  <b>LELAND</b> B.Y.O.B. on a party invitation, means BRING YOUR OWN BOTTLE. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,20</i>)

### BYOG
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Bring Your Own Grog

>  <i>B.Y.O.(G.) n. Austral. & N.Z.</i> an unlicensed restaurant at which diners may drink their own wine, etc. [C20: <i>bring your own (grog)] </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,150</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 98; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 65]]

>  The need for a provision of this nature - sometimes known as BYOG, "bring your own grog" - has been widely recognised and provides a clear enough example of a case where the present law has fallen into disrepute because of its unreasonableness. <i>(1976,NZ Parliamentary Debates,407 : 3561</i>)
